\chapter*{About this document}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{About this document}
Chapter 1 states the assumptions and the main results. Chapter 2 proves them, following the argument from the hypotheses to the conclusion in order. Chapter 3 proves the supporting lemmas, each before its first use. The proofs are written for readers with an undergraduate background in analysis, linear algebra and probability; definitions are recalled where they are first used, and each inequality is justified.

\section*{Mathematics and its Lean formalization}
Every result is followed by a grey box headed \emph{Lean}. The box reproduces the exact statement of the corresponding Lean declaration, read from the source file and line range named in the box, and then explains how the Lean proof follows the mathematical argument: which hypothesis encodes which assumption, and which formal step carries out which step of the proof. The mathematics can be read without the boxes; the boxes connect each claim to the statement that Lean has checked.

\section*{Checking the proofs}
The results are verified by Lean 4 with the Mathlib library, at the versions pinned in the repository \url{https://github.com/odakyildiz/LangevinLean}. After installing the Lean toolchain manager elan and cloning the repository, run from its root:
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{lake\ exe\ cache\ get}
\leanline{0}{lake\ build\ ProximalLangevin}
\leanline{0}{lake\ env\ lean\ ProximalLangevin/Check.lean}
\end{leancode}
The second command checks every proof in the library. The third prints the axioms on which the main theorems depend; they are only the standard axioms of propositional extensionality, choice and quotient soundness.

Prepared by the LangevinLean Project with AI assistance, these notes form part of an ongoing effort to formalize Langevin algorithms comprehensively. This is draft work and may contain mistakes.

\section*{Literature and scope}
The algorithms are from the cited paper. The corrected bounds and proofs here are unpublished. They establish the results stated below; some rates and constants printed in the paper are not proved here.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Paula Cordero Encinar, Francesca Romana Crucinio, and Omer Deniz Akyildiz (2025). \emph{Proximal Interacting Particle Langevin Algorithms}. Proceedings of the Forty-first Conference on Uncertainty in Artificial Intelligence, Proceedings of Machine Learning Research 286, 1220–1265. \url{https://proceedings.mlr.press/v286/encinar25a.html}.
\end{enumerate}
Results from the earlier reports \cite{LangevinLean2026ULASC,LangevinLean2026ULALSI,LangevinLean2026IPLA} are used below. Each application cites the section containing the required result.

\chapter{Assumptions and main results}
\section{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA: parameter error}{MYIPLA: parameter error}}


Write \(U=g_1+g_2\) on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\). Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex for \(m>0\), and has \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient. Assume \(g_2\) is finite-valued, convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz. Define its proximal map and Moreau envelope by


\[P_\eta(x)=\operatorname*{argmin}_p\left\{g_2(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\eta}\right\},\quad
g_2^\eta(x)=g_2(P_\eta(x))+\frac{\|P_\eta(x)-x\|^2}{2\eta},\]
\[V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta,\qquad L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1},\qquad\eta>0.\]


Let \(N\ge1\), \(d_\theta,d_x\ge0\), and assume the initial particle state in scaled coordinates has a finite second moment. The Gaussian vectors are standard, independent over coordinates and time, and are independent of the initial state. Denote the initial distribution in these coordinates by \(\rho_0\), and denote the Gibbs measure associated with the sum of the \(N\) copies of \(V_\eta\) by \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\). Let \(\theta_U\) and \(\theta_\eta\) maximize the parameter marginals corresponding to \(g_1+g_2\) and \(V_\eta\). The proof establishes the existence of both maximizers under these assumptions.



Precisely, the scaled state, lifted potential, and normalized target are


\[z=(\theta,x^1/\sqrt N,\ldots,x^N/\sqrt N),\qquad
W_{N,\eta}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^N V_\eta(\theta,\sqrt N z_i),\]
\[\Pi_{N,\eta}(dz)=Z_{N,\eta}^{-1}e^{-W_{N,\eta}(z)}dz.\]


A marginal maximizer of a pair potential \(V\) maximizes \(\theta\mapsto\int e^{-V(\theta,x)}dx\).



MYIPLA is the Gaussian IPLA recursion for \(V_\eta\), using the proved identity \(\nabla g_2^\eta=(I-P_\eta)/\eta\):


\[z_{n+1}=z_n-\frac\gamma N\nabla W_{N,\eta}(z_n)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi_n.\]


For \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\) and every \(n\in\mathbb N\), the parameter iterate satisfies


\[\begin{aligned}
\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}
+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}\]


The first term bounds \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\). The second bounds the root mean square distance from a parameter drawn from \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\) to \(\theta_\eta\). The remaining two terms bound the distance between the numerical distribution and \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\). The smoothing estimate is \(O(\sqrt\eta)\), and the discretization term depends on \(\eta\) through \(L_\eta\). These are the bounds proved here; the paper's linear-in-\(\eta\) smoothing expression is not a conclusion of this argument.

\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPGLA: parameter error}{PIPGLA: parameter error}}


Write \(U=g_1+g_2\) on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\). Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex for \(m>0\), and has \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient. Assume \(g_2\) is finite-valued, convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz. Define its proximal map and Moreau envelope by


\[P_\eta(x)=\operatorname*{argmin}_p\left\{g_2(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\eta}\right\},\quad
g_2^\eta(x)=g_2(P_\eta(x))+\frac{\|P_\eta(x)-x\|^2}{2\eta},\]
\[V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta,\qquad L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1},\qquad\eta>0.\]


Let \(N\ge1\), \(d_\theta,d_x\ge0\), and assume the initial particle state in scaled coordinates has a finite second moment. The Gaussian vectors are standard, independent over coordinates and time, and are independent of the initial state. Denote the initial distribution in these coordinates by \(\rho_0\), and denote the Gibbs measure associated with the sum of the \(N\) copies of \(V_\eta\) by \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\). Let \(\theta_U\) and \(\theta_\eta\) maximize the parameter marginals corresponding to \(g_1+g_2\) and \(V_\eta\). The proof establishes the existence of both maximizers under these assumptions.



Precisely, the scaled state, lifted potential, and normalized target are


\[z=(\theta,x^1/\sqrt N,\ldots,x^N/\sqrt N),\qquad
W_{N,\eta}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^N V_\eta(\theta,\sqrt N z_i),\]
\[\Pi_{N,\eta}(dz)=Z_{N,\eta}^{-1}e^{-W_{N,\eta}(z)}dz.\]


A marginal maximizer of a pair potential \(V\) maximizes \(\theta\mapsto\int e^{-V(\theta,x)}dx\).



For PIPGLA assume also \(m\le L_1\), take any proximal parameter \(\lambda>0\), and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). First apply the smooth-gradient step with its averaged parameter drift and Gaussian innovation:


\[\begin{aligned}
\widetilde\theta_n&=\theta_n-\frac\gamma N\sum_i\nabla_\theta g_1(\theta_n,x_n^i)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi_n^0,\\
\widetilde x_n^i&=x_n^i-\gamma\nabla_x g_1(\theta_n,x_n^i)+\sqrt{2\gamma}\,\xi_n^i.
\end{aligned}\]


Then set \((a_i,b_i)=P_\lambda(\widetilde\theta_n,\widetilde x_n^i)\), \(\theta_{n+1}=N^{-1}\sum_i a_i\), and \(x_{n+1}^i=b_i\). Let \(\theta_\gamma\) maximize the marginal for \(V_\gamma\). The actual iterates satisfy


\[\begin{aligned}
\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}
\le{}&\frac{K|\lambda-\gamma|}{m\gamma}+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+\sqrt{(1-m\gamma)^n W_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N}.
\end{aligned}\]


All constants are explicit. Put \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\), \(a=Nm\), \(b=NL_1\), \(c=NK\),


\[G=\sqrt{2b^2D/a+2c^2}+c,\qquad\sigma=\sqrt{2D},\]
\[C_N=\frac{4b^2}{a^2}\left(\frac{G^2}{a}+\sigma^2\right)+\frac2a(Gc+c^2).\]


The first term bounds the difference caused by using \(P_\lambda\) in place of \(P_\gamma\). The next two terms bound smoothing of the parameter maximizer and the second moment of the smoothed Gibbs parameter marginal. The square-root term follows from a coupling of the splitting recursion with a stationary comparison process, followed by a recurrence for their squared distance. The constants in this estimate differ from those printed in the paper.

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYPGD: parameter error without parameter noise}{MYPGD: parameter error without parameter noise}}


Write \(U=g_1+g_2\) on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\). Assume \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex for \(m>0\), and has \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient. Assume \(g_2\) is finite-valued, convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz. Define its proximal map and Moreau envelope by


\[P_\eta(x)=\operatorname*{argmin}_p\left\{g_2(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\eta}\right\},\quad
g_2^\eta(x)=g_2(P_\eta(x))+\frac{\|P_\eta(x)-x\|^2}{2\eta},\]
\[V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta,\qquad L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1},\qquad\eta>0.\]


Let \(N\ge1\), \(d_\theta,d_x\ge0\), and assume the initial particle state in scaled coordinates has a finite second moment. The Gaussian vectors are standard, independent over coordinates and time, and are independent of the initial state. Denote the initial distribution in these coordinates by \(\rho_0\), and denote the Gibbs measure associated with the sum of the \(N\) copies of \(V_\eta\) by \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\). Let \(\theta_U\) and \(\theta_\eta\) maximize the parameter marginals corresponding to \(g_1+g_2\) and \(V_\eta\). The proof establishes the existence of both maximizers under these assumptions.



Precisely, the scaled state, lifted potential, and normalized target are


\[z=(\theta,x^1/\sqrt N,\ldots,x^N/\sqrt N),\qquad
W_{N,\eta}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^N V_\eta(\theta,\sqrt N z_i),\]
\[\Pi_{N,\eta}(dz)=Z_{N,\eta}^{-1}e^{-W_{N,\eta}(z)}dz.\]


A marginal maximizer of a pair potential \(V\) maximizes \(\theta\mapsto\int e^{-V(\theta,x)}dx\).



MYPGD has the MYIPLA drift, with zero Gaussian noise in the parameter coordinates and unchanged Gaussian noise in the latent coordinates:


\[z_{n+1}=z_n-\frac\gamma N\nabla W_{N,\eta}(z_n)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,M\xi_n,\]


where \(M\) is the orthogonal projection onto latent coordinates. Assume \(m\le L_\eta\) explicitly and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\). For every \(n\in\mathbb N\), the actual finite-particle recursion satisfies


\[\begin{aligned}
\bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n^{\rm PGD}-\theta_U\|^2\bigr)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}}+\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}\]


The additional \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\) bounds the distance caused by setting the parameter noise to zero. It is obtained by comparing the two recursions with the same latent Gaussian variables. The displayed estimate concerns the finite-particle algorithm; it does not establish the paper's stronger estimate for the mean-field distance or its constants.

{\small\color{gray}Supporting proof: Section~\ref{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean}.\par}
\chapter{Mathematical argument}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Proximal optimality and differentiability of the envelope}{Proximal optimality and differentiability of the envelope}}



For \(\eta>0\), add quadratic penalty \(\|p-x\|^2/(2\eta)\) to the finite convex function \(g_2(p)\). Under the stated lower bound the objective is coercive and strictly convex, so it has unique minimizer \(P_\eta(x)\). Its residual support at two inputs and subgradient monotonicity give

\[\|P_\eta(x)-P_\eta(y)\|^2\le
\langle P_\eta(x)-P_\eta(y),x-y\rangle.\]

Expansion of residual differences also makes \(I-P_\eta\) nonexpansive. Compare the envelope objective at \(x+u\) using its minimizer at \(x\), and conversely. The resulting two-sided quadratic remainder proves

\[\nabla g_2^\eta(x)=\frac{x-P_\eta(x)}\eta.\]

Residual nonexpansiveness gives gradient constant \(\eta^{-1}\). Convexity of the envelope means \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\) retains curvature \(m\) and has gradient constant \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). These established properties meet the Gaussian Euler transport theorem \cite[Section~3.45.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.




For the remainder calculation put \(e(x)=g_2^\eta(x)\) and \(r(x)=(x-P_\eta x)/\eta\). Evaluation at \(P_\eta x\) gives \(e(x+u)\le e(x)+\langle r(x),u\rangle+\|u\|^2/(2\eta)\). Convex support at \(P_\eta x\), followed by minimization over the competitor for the envelope at \(x+u\), gives \(e(x+u)\ge e(x)+\langle r(x),u\rangle\). Hence the remainder after subtracting the linear functional lies between zero and \(\|u\|^2/(2\eta)\). Division by \(\|u\|\) makes it tend to zero, which is the definition of differentiability with derivative \(u\mapsto\langle r(x),u\rangle\). Residual nonexpansiveness gives \(\|r(x)-r(y)\|\le\eta^{-1}\|x-y\|\), so this derivative is continuous. \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau}}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 143–144}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ gradient\_moreau\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ gradient\ (moreau\ f\ lam)\ x\ =\ residual\ f\ lam\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau} identifies the actual gradient by applying the uniqueness property \texttt{.\allowbreak{}gradient} to \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At}. The underlying derivative proof has already obtained the two-sided remainder from the same minimizing-point comparisons described above. The section assumptions \texttt{hf}, \texttt{hb} and \texttt{hlam} are convexity, a global lower bound and a positive proximal scale. \texttt{residual} is the scaled displacement from the proximal minimizer, so the conclusion is the displayed envelope-gradient formula. Smoothness follows in the subsequent declarations by combining that identity with nonexpansiveness of the residual map.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{The finite Moreau potential-gap estimate}{The finite Moreau potential-gap estimate}}



Competitor \(p=x\) in the minimizing objective gives \(g_2^\eta(x)\le g_2(x)\). Conversely the value Lipschitz bound gives, for all \(p\),

\[g_2(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\eta}
\ge g_2(x)-K\|p-x\|+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\eta}
\ge g_2(x)-\frac{\eta K^2}{2}.\]

The last step completes a scalar square in \(\|p-x\|\). Evaluation at the proximal minimizer therefore gives

\[V_\eta\le U\le V_\eta+\delta_\eta,\qquad\delta_\eta=\eta K^2/2.\]

Exponentiation reverses potential order and latent integration preserves the resulting nonnegative-function order. Taking negative logarithms of the positive finite marginal integrals gives, for \(F_U,F_\eta\), the relation \(F_\eta\le F_U\le F_\eta+\delta_\eta\). The function \(U=g_1+g_2\) is continuous because \(g_1\) is differentiable and \(g_2\) is Lipschitz. The comparison gives Gaussian domination locally uniform in the parameter; dominated convergence therefore proves continuity of the original marginal. Its decay as the parameter norm tends to infinity gives existence of a marginal maximizer.




The square completion is \(-Kr+r^2/(2\eta)=(r-\eta K)^2/(2\eta)-\eta K^2/2\), valid for \(\eta>0\). The two pointwise potential bounds imply \(e^{-\delta_\eta}e^{-V_\eta}\le e^{-U}\le e^{-V_\eta}\). Their latent integrals obey the same inequalities. Since these integrals are positive, applying the increasing logarithm and then changing sign gives \(F_\eta\le F_U\le F_\eta+\delta_\eta\) with precisely the same gap. A parameter-independent Jacobian from scaled coordinates cancels before this comparison is used for the marginal centers. \(\square\)

\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{0\ ≤\ f\ x\ -\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ ∧\ f\ x\ -\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ ≤\ lam\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} proves both halves of the potential gap. For the lower half it invokes \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}le}, the competitor-at-the-input inequality. For the upper half it invokes \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lower\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, which performs the scalar square completion using \texttt{h\allowbreak{}K}, and rearranges the resulting inequality. The assumptions retained from the section are convexity \texttt{hf}, a global lower bound \texttt{hb} and positive scale \texttt{hlam}. The conclusion bounds a function-value difference; marginal and center comparisons are separate subsequent applications.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Marginal-potential comparison and center displacement}{Marginal-potential comparison and center displacement}}



Let \(\theta_U\) and \(\theta_\eta\) minimize \(F_U\) and \(F_\eta\). Marginal strong convexity \cite[Section~3.12.3]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} makes \(F_\eta\) \(m\)-strongly convex. Quadratic growth, the uniform gap, and minimality of \(\theta_U\) imply

\[\begin{aligned}
\frac m2\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2
&\le F_\eta(\theta_U)-F_\eta(\theta_\eta)\\
&\le F_U(\theta_U)-F_\eta(\theta_\eta)\\
&\le F_U(\theta_\eta)-F_\eta(\theta_\eta)
\le\delta_\eta.
\end{aligned}\]

Taking square roots yields \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le K\sqrt{\eta/m}\) for every positive \(\eta\). The argument does not differentiate the original nonsmooth potential. Its order is \(\sqrt\eta\); the proved result therefore does not support replacing this term by a linear smoothing rate.




The quadratic-growth inequality follows even without a derivative of the marginal: apply strong convexity to \((1-t)\theta_\eta+t\theta_U\), use minimality at \(\theta_\eta\), divide by \(t>0\), and let \(t\downarrow0\). This gives \(m\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2/2\le F_\eta(\theta_U)-F_\eta(\theta_\eta)\). The upper bound by \(\delta_\eta\) then yields \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2\le2\delta_\eta/m=\eta K^2/m\). Nonnegative square roots give the stated expression, including \(K=0\). The hypotheses specify both centers as marginal maximizers; the comparison does not assume uniqueness of the original nonsmooth model's center. \(\square\)

Substitution of the previously proved Moreau gap gives the smoothing-scale expression without differentiating the nonsmooth potential.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The MYIPLA bound about the original marginal maximizer}{The MYIPLA bound about the original marginal maximizer}}



MYIPLA equals IPLA for \(V_\eta\). The summed-potential statements \cite[Section~3.1.3]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} and \cite[Section~3.1.4]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} give curvature \(Nm\), gradient constant \(NL_\eta\), and dimension \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\). Step \(\gamma/N\) gives contraction \(1-m\gamma\). Apply Gaussian Euler transport \cite[Section~3.45.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC} and parameter projection \cite[Section~3.48.9]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.



The sharp marginal estimate Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean-1} gives radius \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Minkowski adds the center displacement. The four contributions are

\[K\sqrt{\eta/m},\quad\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}},\quad
(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta}),\quad
\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{\gamma\left(d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}\right)}.\]

A smaller \(\eta\) reduces the first contribution while increasing \(L_\eta\) and restricting the admissible step. Thus smoothing and discretization choices must satisfy the displayed joint conditions. The estimate is for the stated Gaussian particle law.




At the final projection step use \(\theta-\theta_U=(\theta-\theta_\eta)+(\theta_\eta-\theta_U)\) under the MYIPLA output probability. Minkowski bounds its root second moment by the smoothed-center root second moment plus the constant center displacement. The square-integrability of the projected output is established from the finite initial second moment and Lipschitz updates before taking this root. Substituting the sharp concentration, the transport theorem and the center comparison yields the four displayed terms. Every scale is positive and the step must satisfy the smoothness-dependent restriction with \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\); a small smoothing scale cannot be chosen independently of that step. \(\square\)

\section{\texorpdfstring{The equal-scale PIPGLA transport estimate}{The equal-scale PIPGLA transport estimate}}



Take \(\lambda=\gamma\). Apply the smooth noisy forward update and then the pairwise proximal maps, averaging their parameter outputs. Couple its noise with the stationary Langevin reference for \(V_\gamma\) supplied by \cite[Section~3.42.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}. Keep the proximal support inner products in the squared-error expansion until their energy terms are combined.



Put \(s=\gamma/N\), \(a=Nm\), \(b=NL_1\), and \(c=NK\). Drift and noise moment bounds are \(G=\sqrt{2b^2D/a+2c^2}+c\) and \(\sigma=\sqrt{2D}\). The local increment is bounded by \(Gs+\sigma\sqrt s\). Squaring with \((u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2\) and using \(s\le1/a\) bounds the higher powers by the coefficient

\[C_N=\frac{4b^2}{a^2}\left(\frac{G^2}{a}+\sigma^2\right)+\frac2a(Gc+c^2).\]

For gap \(\delta=N\gamma K^2/2=sc^2/2\), the one-step forcing is

\[\mathcal F=(1+(as)^{-1})[bs(Gs+\sigma\sqrt s)]^2+2s\delta+2s^2Gc+s^2c^2.\]

Weighted Young controls the smooth defect. Convex support, stationarity, and zero expected pairings with future Brownian increments give \(2s\delta+2s^2Gc\) for the energy cross term. The remaining term is the proximal quadratic remainder. Under \(s\le1/a\) these estimates imply \(\mathcal F\le as^2C_N\). The recurrence coefficient is \(1-a s=1-m\gamma\), so the initial coupling infimum and geometric sum give

\[W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2
\le(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N.\]

Projection adds the sharp equilibrium radius to the square root of this transport bound, and the center comparison adds \(K\sqrt{\gamma/m}\). Since the recurrence controls squared transport, its transient factor occurs inside the final square root.




For the scalar absorption, put \(R=Gs+\sigma\sqrt s\). Since \((Gs-\sigma\sqrt s)^2\ge0\), \(R^2\le2G^2s^2+2\sigma^2s\). The restriction \(s\le1/a\) makes this at most \(2s(G^2/a+\sigma^2)\). Consequently \((1+(as)^{-1})b^2s^2R^2\le2b^2s^2(s+1/a)(G^2/a+\sigma^2)\le4b^2s^2(G^2/a+\sigma^2)/a\). The gap contribution is \(2s\delta\le s^2c^2\). Adding the other two terms gives \(\mathcal F\le4b^2s^2(G^2/a+\sigma^2)/a+2s^2Gc+2s^2c^2=as^2C_N\). Dividing the constant forcing in the squared-error recurrence by its gap \(as\) leaves \(sC_N\). \(\square\)

\leanline{4}{(hdelta\ :\ delta\ ≤\ h\ *\ K\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2)\ :}
\leanline{4}{splittingCouplingForcing\ m\ h\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ delta\ ≤}

The identity \texttt{heq} and bound \texttt{htwice} control its coefficient; \texttt{hdelta'} inserts the potential gap. Finally \texttt{h\allowbreak{}C} expands the proposed constant and \texttt{hfirst} bounds the weighted smooth-error term. Unfolding \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing} leaves precisely the scalar inequality stated above. The actual law recurrence is established separately in the splitting endpoint module.

\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPGLA comparison at two positive proximal scales}{PIPGLA comparison at two positive proximal scales}}



For a convex \(K\)-Lipschitz penalty, proximal optimality and nonexpansiveness at a shifted input give

\[\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

Apply this estimate to every pair. Jensen for the parameter average and the exact latent scaling give the same full-state bound \(K|\lambda-\eta|\) per step. Gradient-step contraction \cite[Section~3.10.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC} has factor \(q=1-m\gamma\), and proximal nonexpansiveness preserves it when both chains use the same innovation.



From common initialization, geometric summation bounds their difference by \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(1-q)=K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\) uniformly over finite iteration counts. Set \(\eta=\gamma\) and combine this comparison with the equal-scale bound. Minkowski adds the same constant to the parameter root mean square error.




Let \(d_k\) be the state distance between the two common-noise chains, with \(d_0=0\). One update gives \(d_{k+1}\le qd_k+K|\lambda-\eta|\), because the same-scale map contracts by \(q\) and the change of scale contributes the second term. Induction gives \(d_k\le K|\lambda-\eta|\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}q^j\le K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). This is pointwise for every shared noise sequence. Parameter projection has norm at most one; taking the root second moment and using Minkowski yields the same additive constant for the output root mean square errors about any fixed center. Independence of the noise is not required for this comparison itself. \(\square\)

Both proximal scales are positive; this comparison does not require the noises themselves to be independent Gaussian vectors.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The finite-particle MYPGD–MYIPLA comparison}{The finite-particle MYPGD–MYIPLA comparison}}



Extend any finite-cost coupling of the current MYPGD and MYIPLA laws by an independent standard Gaussian vector. Both updates use its latent component; only MYIPLA uses its parameter component. That omitted component is centered and has squared \(L^2\) norm \(2\gamma d_\theta/N\). Independence makes its mixed expectation with the deterministic discrepancy zero.



The smooth contraction theorem \cite[Section~3.10.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC} gives factor \(1-m\gamma\). The constructed output coupling thus has squared cost at most \((1-m\gamma)^2\) times the input squared cost plus the omitted variance. Taking the input coupling infimum, for \(E_n=W_2(\rho_n^{\mathrm{PGD}},\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYIPLA}})^2\), gives

\[E_{n+1}\le(1-m\gamma)^2E_n+\frac{2\gamma d_\theta}{N},\qquad E_0=0.\]

The initial law is common, and finite moments justify all transport squares. Step admissibility gives \(0\le m\gamma\le1\) and therefore \(1-(1-m\gamma)^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\ge m\gamma\). The geometric sum gives \(E_n\le2d_\theta/(Nm)\). Parameter contraction \cite[Section~3.1.6]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} and projected root mean square inequality \cite[Section~3.48.9]{LangevinLean2026ULASC} consequently add \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\) to the MYIPLA parameter bound.



The comparison radius is a finite-particle upper bound and remains for finite \(N\). This result does not assert the stronger mean-field metric estimate; mean-field integral constructions and their particle comparisons are established separately.




Write the full Gaussian innovation as independent parameter and latent blocks. Conditional on both current states, the deterministic update discrepancy and shared latent innovation have zero cross expectation with the centered parameter innovation. Expanding the squared norm therefore adds only \(2\gamma d_\theta/N\). The denominator in the sum is \(1-q^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\), where \(q=1-m\gamma\). The admissible step gives \(0\le m\gamma\le1\), so \(2-m\gamma\ge1\), and division by this positive gap gives \(E_n\le2d_\theta/(Nm)\). Taking nonnegative square roots and applying the parameter transport projection proves the stated comparison radius. \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}actual\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}}

\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{ULA.wasserstein2}
\leanline{6}{(mypgdLaws\ Q\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ η)\ η\ γ\ initial\ (fun\ k\ =>\ noise\ k)\ n)}
\leanline{6}{(myiplaLaws\ Q\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ η)\ η\ γ\ initial\ (fun\ k\ =>\ noise\ k)\ n)\ ≠\ ⊤\ ∧}
\leanline{4}{ULA.wasserstein2Real}
\leanline{6}{(mypgdLaws\ Q\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ η)\ η\ γ\ initial\ (fun\ k\ =>\ noise\ k)\ n)}
\leanline{6}{(myiplaLaws\ Q\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ η)\ η\ γ\ initial\ (fun\ k\ =>\ noise\ k)\ n)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{2\ *\ (dθ\ :\ ℝ)\ /\ ((N\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ m)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}actual\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq} first uses \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}map\_\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}iff} and \texttt{Lp.\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp initial} to transfer square integrability to the initial distribution. It applies \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}, the transition-law coupling theorem containing the centered-noise cancellation and geometric recurrence. The two rewrites \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws} and \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws} identify these transition iterates with the actual algorithms under \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Gaussian}. Its conclusion includes finite transport before the ordinary real squared-distance bound. The following declaration \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}myipla} applies projection to that finite bound.
\end{leanbox}
\chapter{Supporting proofs}
The following sections give the definitions and prove the lemmas used in the convergence arguments.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The update maps of MYIPLA, PIPULA and PIPGLA}{The update maps of MYIPLA, PIPULA and PIPGLA}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean}


This section defines the three proximal interacting particle algorithms as deterministic maps of a state and an innovation, and proves the algebraic identities relating them. The state space is \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times(\mathbb R^{d_x})^N\) with scaled coordinates \(z=(\theta,z_1,\dots,z_N)\) (physical particles \(x_i=\sqrt Nz_i\)), \(J_iz=(\theta,\sqrt Nz_i)\) is the \(i\)-th parameter–particle pair, and \(F_g(z)=\sum_{i=1}^Ng(J_iz)\) is the lifted potential of a pair function \(g\). For a map \(P\) of the pair space, \(\mathcal P\) denotes the pairwise map that applies \(P\) to every pair, averages the parameter outputs and divides the latent outputs by \(\sqrt N\). With step \(\gamma\), smoothing parameter \(\eta\) and innovation \(\xi\) in the state space:


\[\begin{aligned}
\text{PIPULA:}\quad&z\mapsto\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi,\\
\text{MYIPLA:}\quad&z\mapsto\Bigl(1-\frac\gamma\eta\Bigr)z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\frac\gamma\eta\,\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi,\\
\text{PIPGLA:}\quad&z\mapsto\mathcal P\Bigl(z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi\Bigr).
\end{aligned}\]


Each algorithm is the recursion \(Z_{n+1}=T(Z_n,\xi_n)\) driven by a given innovation sequence. We prove that PIPULA is MYIPLA with \(g_1=0\) and \(\eta=\gamma\), write MYIPLA in parameter and physical-particle coordinates, and prove that if \(\nabla e=\eta^{-1}(I-P)\) (as for \(e=g_2^\eta\) and \(P=P_\eta\)) then MYIPLA coincides, iterate by iterate, with the Euler scheme (IPLA) for the smooth potential \(g_1+e\). PIPGLA applies the proximal map after the noisy gradient step and has no such identity; it is analysed separately. No probabilistic assumption enters this section.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.4]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The pairwise map in coordinates}{The pairwise map in coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-1}


\textbf{Definition.} Let \(P\) be any map from the pair space \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times\mathbb R^{d_x}\) to itself, and write \(P(w)=([P(w)]_\theta,[P(w)]_x)\). The \emph{pairwise map} \(\mathcal P\) on the state space is defined by


\[(\mathcal Pz)_\theta=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_\theta,\qquad(\mathcal Pz)_i=\frac1{\sqrt N}\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_x\quad(i=1,\dots,N).\]


\textbf{Claim.} For every parameter coordinate \(j\le d_\theta\), every particle \(i\) and every latent coordinate \(j\le d_x\), the coordinates of \(\mathcal Pz\) are \(N^{-1}\sum_i[P(J_iz)]_{\theta,j}\) and \(N^{-1/2}[P(J_iz)]_{x,j}\).




\textbf{Proof.} This is the definition read coordinatewise. \(\square\)




All \(N\) pairs are formed with the same current parameter \(\theta\). The \(N\) proposed parameters \([P(J_iz)]_\theta\) are averaged into one new parameter, while the latent output of pair \(i\) is a physical particle and is divided by \(\sqrt N\) to return to scaled coordinates. When \(P\) is a proximal map, \(\mathcal P\) need not be the proximal map of any function on the state space.

\leanline{4}{(z\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx\ :=}
\leanline{2}{WithLp.toLp\ 2\ (Sum.elim}
\leanline{4}{(fun\ j\ ↦\ (N\ :\ ℝ)⁻¹\ *\ ∑\ i\ :\ Fin\ N,\ P\ (IPLA.joint\ i\ z)\ (Sum.inl\ j))}
\leanline{4}{(fun\ ij\ ↦\ (Real.sqrt\ N)⁻¹\ *\ P\ (IPLA.joint\ ij.1\ z)\ (Sum.inr\ ij.2)))}
\end{leancode}

\texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map P z} is defined as \texttt{With\allowbreak{}Lp.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Lp 2 (Sum.\allowbreak{}elim … …)}: the coordinate function on \texttt{Fin d\ensuremath{\theta} ⊕ (Fin N \ensuremath{\times} Fin dx)} is given by \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}elim}, which uses the first function on parameter indices \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inl j} and the second on latent indices \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inr (i, j)}, and \texttt{With\allowbreak{}Lp.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Lp 2} regards the coordinate function as an element of the Euclidean space \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}State N d\ensuremath{\theta} dx}. The parameter coordinate is \texttt{(N : \ensuremath{\mathbb R})\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} * ∑ i : Fin N, P (IPLA.\allowbreak{}joint i z) (Sum.\allowbreak{}inl j)} and the latent coordinate is \texttt{(Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt N)\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} * P (IPLA.\allowbreak{}joint ij.\allowbreak{}1 z) (Sum.\allowbreak{}inr ij.\allowbreak{}2)}; here \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}joint i z} is \(J_iz\) and \texttt{(N : \ensuremath{\mathbb R})} is the particle number cast to a real number. The two lemmas \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}parameter} and \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}particle} state these coordinates and are proved by \texttt{rfl} (they hold by definition); being tagged \texttt{@[simp]}, they are used automatically by the simplifier in later proofs.

Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The PIPULA, MYIPLA and PIPGLA update maps}{The PIPULA, MYIPLA and PIPGLA update maps}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-2}


\textbf{Definitions.} Let \(P\) be a map of the pair space, \(g_1\) a real function on the pair space, \(\gamma,\eta\in\mathbb R\), and \(z,\xi\) states. The gradient of the lifted potential \(F_{g_1}(z)=\sum_ig_1(J_iz)\) is taken on the state space with its Euclidean inner product. The three update maps are


\[\begin{aligned}
T_{\mathrm{PIPULA}}(z,\xi)&=\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{N}}\,\xi,\\
T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)&=\Bigl(1-\frac\gamma\eta\Bigr)z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\frac\gamma\eta\,\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{N}}\,\xi,\\
T_{\mathrm{PIPGLA}}(z,\xi)&=\mathcal P\bigl(S_\gamma(z,\xi)\bigr),\qquad S_\gamma(z,\xi)=z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{N}}\,\xi .
\end{aligned}\]


\(S_\gamma\) is the scaled Euler (IPLA) step for the potential \(g_1\) with step \(\gamma\). In PIPULA the proximal pairwise map is applied to the current state and the Gaussian increment is added afterwards. In MYIPLA the proximal output enters through the term \(\frac\gamma\eta(\mathcal Pz-z)\), which for \(P=P_\eta\) is \(-\gamma\) times the scaled Moreau residual (see the last step), and \(\eta\) and \(\gamma\) are independent. In PIPGLA the proximal pairwise map is applied after the noisy gradient step, so the increment passes through \(\mathcal P\). For the same input the three maps generally give different outputs, and their analyses are separate.




The definitions involve no hypothesis on \(P\), \(g_1\), \(\gamma\) or \(\eta\); the results that use them assume \(\gamma>0\) (and \(\eta>0\) for MYIPLA) wherever the noise coefficient or the ratio \(\gamma/\eta\) matters.

\leansource{\texttt{pipulaUpdate} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Algorithms.lean, lines 41–43}
\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(γ\ :\ ℝ)\ (z\ ξ\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx\ :=}
\leanline{2}{pairwiseMap\ P\ z\ +\ Real.sqrt\ (2\ *\ γ\ /\ N)\ •\ ξ}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{myiplaUpdate} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Algorithms.lean, lines 46–50}
\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(P\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ (η\ γ\ :\ ℝ)}

\leanline{2}{(1\ -\ γ\ /\ η)\ •\ z\ -\ (γ\ /\ N)\ •\ gradient\ (IPLA.potential\ g₁)\ z\ +}
\leanline{4}{(γ\ /\ η)\ •\ pairwiseMap\ P\ z\ +\ Real.sqrt\ (2\ *\ γ\ /\ N)\ •\ ξ}

\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(P\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ (γ\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(z\ ξ\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx\ :=}
\leanline{2}{pairwiseMap\ P\ (IPLA.scaledUpdate\ g₁\ γ\ z\ ξ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{pipula\allowbreak{}Update P \ensuremath{\gamma} z \ensuremath{\xi}} is \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map P z + Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt (2 * \ensuremath{\gamma} /\allowbreak{} N) \ensuremath{\bullet} \ensuremath{\xi}}. \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Update g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} P \ensuremath{\eta} \ensuremath{\gamma} z \ensuremath{\xi}} is \texttt{(1 -\allowbreak{} \ensuremath{\gamma} /\allowbreak{} \ensuremath{\eta}) \ensuremath{\bullet} z -\allowbreak{} (\ensuremath{\gamma} /\allowbreak{} N) \ensuremath{\bullet} gradient (IPLA.\allowbreak{}potential g\ensuremath{{}_{1}}) z + (\ensuremath{\gamma} /\allowbreak{} \ensuremath{\eta}) \ensuremath{\bullet} pairwise\allowbreak{}Map P z + Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt (2 * \ensuremath{\gamma} /\allowbreak{} N) \ensuremath{\bullet} \ensuremath{\xi}}, where \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}potential g\ensuremath{{}_{1}}} is \(F_{g_1}\) and \texttt{gradient} is the gradient in the Euclidean state space (the vector representing the Fréchet derivative). \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Update g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} P \ensuremath{\gamma} z \ensuremath{\xi}} is \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map P (IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Update g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} \ensuremath{\gamma} z \ensuremath{\xi})}, and \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Update U h z \ensuremath{\xi}} is defined in the IPLA development as \texttt{z -\allowbreak{} (h /\allowbreak{} N) \ensuremath{\bullet} gradient (potential U) z + Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt (2 * h /\allowbreak{} N) \ensuremath{\bullet} \ensuremath{\xi}}, which is \(S_\gamma\). The function \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt} is total and returns \(0\) on negative arguments, so the definitions make sense for every real \(\gamma\). The map \texttt{P} is an arbitrary function at this stage; later sections substitute \texttt{prox g\ensuremath{{}_{2}} \ensuremath{\eta}}.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.1.7]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Recursions driven by an innovation sequence}{Recursions driven by an innovation sequence}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-3}


\textbf{Definition.} Let \(X\) be a set, \(T:X\times X\to X\) an update map, \(z_0\in X\) and \((\xi_n)_{n\ge0}\) a sequence in \(X\). The chain driven by \((\xi_n)\) is the sequence defined recursively by


\[Z_0=z_0,\qquad Z_{n+1}=T(Z_n,\xi_n)\quad(n\ge0).\]


By the recursion theorem this defines a unique sequence. The MYIPLA, PIPULA and PIPGLA chains are the chains of \(T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}\), \(T_{\mathrm{PIPULA}}\) and \(T_{\mathrm{PIPGLA}}\) on the state space.




\textbf{Claim.} \(Z_0=z_0\) and \(Z_{n+1}=T(Z_n,\xi_n)\) for every \(n\).




\textbf{Proof.} These are the two defining equations. \(\square\)




The definition is deterministic: the innovations are arbitrary elements of the state space, and no distribution, independence or measurability is assumed. When the initial state and the innovations are random variables, the \(n\)-th iterate is the composition of \(n\) applications of \(T\) with these inputs, and its law is determined by the joint law of \((z_0,\xi_0,\dots,\xi_{n-1})\) once \(T\) is measurable; these probabilistic statements are proved in later sections.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{algorithmChain}, \texttt{algorithmChain\_\allowbreak{}zero}, \texttt{algorithmChain\_\allowbreak{}succ} and 3 more}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{PIPULA is MYIPLA without smooth part and with equal scales}{PIPULA is MYIPLA without smooth part and with equal scales}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-4}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(P\) be a map of the pair space and \(\gamma\in\mathbb R\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item For every state \(z\), innovation \(\xi\) and parameter coordinate \(j\),
\[T_{\mathrm{PIPULA}}(z,\xi)_{\theta,j}=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_{\theta,j}+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi_{\theta,j}.\]

\item If \(\gamma\ne0\), then \(T_{\mathrm{PIPULA}}(z,\xi)=T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)\) for all \(z,\xi\), where \(T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}\) is formed with \(g_1=0\) and \(\eta=\gamma\).

\item If \(\gamma\ne0\), the PIPULA chain and the MYIPLA chain with \(g_1=0\) and \(\eta=\gamma\), started at the same \(z_0\) and driven by the same innovations, coincide: \(Z_n^{\mathrm{PIPULA}}=Z_n^{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}\) for every \(n\).

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) is the parameter coordinate of \(\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi\) given by the previous coordinate formula.




(b) With \(g_1=0\) the lifted potential \(F_{g_1}\) is identically \(0\), so its gradient vanishes. With \(\eta=\gamma\ne0\), \(1-\gamma/\eta=0\) and \(\gamma/\eta=1\). Hence \(T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)=0\cdot z-0+1\cdot\mathcal Pz+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi=T_{\mathrm{PIPULA}}(z,\xi)\).




(c) By (b) the two update maps are equal as functions, so the two recursions with the same initial value and the same innovations define the same sequence; formally, induction on \(n\) with base case \(Z_0=z_0\) for both chains and inductive step \(Z_{n+1}=T(Z_n,\xi_n)\) with the common map \(T\). \(\square\)




The hypothesis \(\gamma\ne0\) is needed for \(\gamma/\gamma=1\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA in parameter and physical-particle coordinates}{MYIPLA in parameter and physical-particle coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-5}


For a differentiable pair function \(g_1\), the chain rule applied to \(F_{g_1}(z)=\sum_ig_1(\theta,\sqrt Nz_i)\) gives the block formulas


\[\nabla_\theta F_{g_1}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^N\nabla_\theta g_1(J_iz),\qquad\nabla_{z_i}F_{g_1}(z)=\sqrt N\,\nabla_xg_1(J_iz),\]


proved in \cite[Section~3.1.5]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} and \cite[Section~3.1.5]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(g_1\) be differentiable, \(P\) a map of the pair space, \(\eta,\gamma\in\mathbb R\), and \(z,\xi\) states; write \(z^+=T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item For every parameter coordinate \(j\),
\[z^+_{\theta,j}=\Bigl(1-\frac\gamma\eta\Bigr)\theta_j-\frac\gamma N\sum_{i=1}^N\partial_{\theta_j}g_1(J_iz)+\frac\gamma\eta\cdot\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_{\theta,j}+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi_{\theta,j}.\]

\item If \(N\ge1\), then for every particle \(i\) and latent coordinate \(j\), with \(x_{i,j}=\sqrt Nz_{i,j}\) and \(x^+_{i,j}=\sqrt Nz^+_{i,j}\),
\[x^+_{i,j}=\Bigl(1-\frac\gamma\eta\Bigr)x_{i,j}-\gamma\,\partial_{x_j}g_1(J_iz)+\frac\gamma\eta\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_{x,j}+\sqrt{2\gamma}\,\xi_{i,j}.\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) Take the parameter coordinate of each term of \(T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}\): the first gives \((1-\gamma/\eta)\theta_j\), the second gives \(-(\gamma/N)\sum_i\partial_{\theta_j}g_1(J_iz)\) by the parameter block formula, the third is \(\gamma/\eta\) times the parameter coordinate of \(\mathcal Pz\), and the fourth is \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi_{\theta,j}\).




(b) Take the \((i,j)\) latent coordinate of each term and multiply by \(\sqrt N\). The first term gives \((1-\gamma/\eta)\sqrt Nz_{i,j}\). By the latent block formula the second gives \(-\frac\gamma N\sqrt N\cdot\sqrt N\,\partial_{x_j}g_1(J_iz)=-\gamma\,\partial_{x_j}g_1(J_iz)\), since \((\sqrt N)^2=N\) and \(N\ne0\). The third gives \(\frac\gamma\eta\sqrt N\cdot N^{-1/2}[P(J_iz)]_{x,j}=\frac\gamma\eta[P(J_iz)]_{x,j}\), since \(\sqrt N\ne0\). The fourth gives \(\sqrt N\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi_{i,j}=\sqrt{2\gamma}\,\xi_{i,j}\), because \(\sqrt N\sqrt{2\gamma/N}=\sqrt{N\cdot2\gamma/N}=\sqrt{2\gamma}\) (the product rule \(\sqrt a\sqrt b=\sqrt{ab}\) for \(a\ge0\)). \(\square\)




In physical coordinates, the parameter moves by the average force \(N^{-1}\sum_i\nabla_\theta g_1\) times \(\gamma\) with noise of variance \(2\gamma/N\) per coordinate, while each particle moves by its own force times \(\gamma\) with noise of variance \(2\gamma\) per coordinate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA is the Euler scheme for the smoothed potential}{MYIPLA is the Euler scheme for the smoothed potential}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Algorithms-lean-6}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\), let \(e\) be a differentiable function on the pair space, \(P\) a map of the pair space and \(\eta\ne0\) such that


\[\nabla e(w)=\eta^{-1}\bigl(w-P(w)\bigr)\qquad\text{for every pair }w.\qquad(1)\]

\begin{enumerate}


\item For every state \(z\), \(\mathcal Pz=z-\frac\eta N\nabla F_e(z)\).

\item In a complete real inner-product space, if \(f\) and \(g\) are differentiable then \(\nabla(f+g)(x)=\nabla f(x)+\nabla g(x)\).

\item If moreover \(g_1\) is differentiable, then for all \(\gamma\) and all states \(z,\xi\),
\[T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)=z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1+e}(z)+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi,\]
the scaled Euler step for the potential \(g_1+e\).

\item Under the same hypotheses the MYIPLA chain coincides with the scaled Euler chain for \(g_1+e\) with step \(\gamma\), started at the same \(z_0\) with the same innovations, at every iteration \(n\).

\end{enumerate}



The main application is \(e=g_2^\eta\) and \(P=P_\eta\) for a convex, bounded-below \(g_2\) and \(\eta>0\): then (1) is the Moreau gradient formula, and MYIPLA is the Euler scheme for \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\).




\textbf{Proof.} (a) By (1), \(P(w)=w-\eta\nabla e(w)\). Parameter block: using \([J_iz]_\theta=\theta\) and the parameter block formula of the previous step,


\[(\mathcal Pz)_\theta=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl(\theta-\eta\nabla_\theta e(J_iz)\bigr)=\theta-\frac\eta N\sum_{i=1}^N\nabla_\theta e(J_iz)=\theta-\frac\eta N\nabla_\theta F_e(z).\]


Latent block \(i\): using \([J_iz]_x=\sqrt Nz_i\) and the latent block formula,


\[(\mathcal Pz)_i=\frac1{\sqrt N}\bigl(\sqrt Nz_i-\eta\nabla_xe(J_iz)\bigr)=z_i-\frac{\eta}{\sqrt N}\nabla_xe(J_iz)=z_i-\frac\eta N\sqrt N\,\nabla_xe(J_iz)=z_i-\frac\eta N\nabla_{z_i}F_e(z),\]


where \(1/\sqrt N=\sqrt N/N\) because \((\sqrt N)^2=N>0\).




(b) The Fréchet derivative of a sum of two functions differentiable at \(x\) is the sum of the derivatives, and the vector representing a functional depends linearly on the functional.




(c) The lifted potential is additive: \(F_{g_1+e}(z)=\sum_i\bigl(g_1(J_iz)+e(J_iz)\bigr)=F_{g_1}(z)+F_e(z)\), and both summands are differentiable (compositions of differentiable functions with the linear maps \(J_i\)), so by (b) \(\nabla F_{g_1+e}=\nabla F_{g_1}+\nabla F_e\). Substituting (a) into the definition of \(T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}\),


\[\begin{aligned}
T_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(z,\xi)&=\Bigl(1-\frac\gamma\eta\Bigr)z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\frac\gamma\eta\Bigl(z-\frac\eta N\nabla F_e(z)\Bigr)+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi\\
&=z-\frac\gamma N\bigl(\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\nabla F_e(z)\bigr)+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi=z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1+e}(z)+\sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}N}\,\xi,
\end{aligned}\]


where \(\frac\gamma\eta\cdot\frac\eta N=\frac\gamma N\) uses \(\eta\ne0\).




(d) Induction on \(n\). Both chains equal \(z_0\) at \(n=0\). If they agree at \(n\), then at \(n+1\) both are obtained by applying the respective update to the same state and the same innovation \(\xi_n\), and these updates agree by (c). \(\square\)

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}step} assumes \texttt{h\allowbreak{}N : 0 < N}, \texttt{he : Differentiable \ensuremath{\mathbb R} e}, \texttt{h\ensuremath{\eta} : \ensuremath{\eta} ≠ 0} and \texttt{hgrad : \ensuremath{\forall} x, gradient e x = \ensuremath{\eta}\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} \ensuremath{\bullet} (x -\allowbreak{} P x)}, which is (1). The local fact \texttt{h\allowbreak{}P} rewrites (1) as \(P(x)=x-\eta\nabla e(x)\), and \texttt{hroot} is \((\sqrt N)^{-1}=\sqrt N/N\). After \texttt{ext a}, \texttt{rcases a with j | \ensuremath{\langle}i, j\ensuremath{\rangle}} splits into a parameter coordinate and a latent coordinate; each case is simplified with the coordinate lemmas, \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}potential\_\allowbreak{}parameter} or \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}potential\_\allowbreak{}particle}, and closed by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} (and, in the latent case, \texttt{linear\_\allowbreak{}combination} with the identity \texttt{hs2}, \((\sqrt N)^2=N\)). The equality is an identity of maps, valid for every state.

\item \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}differentiable} is (b), stated for a complete real inner-product space (\texttt{[Complete\allowbreak{}Space E]} is needed for the gradient to be defined through the Riesz isomorphism); the proof unfolds \texttt{gradient} and uses \texttt{fderiv\_\allowbreak{}fun\_\allowbreak{}add} and additivity (\texttt{map\_\allowbreak{}add}) of the Riesz isomorphism.

\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Update} is (c): \texttt{hpot} is the additivity \(F_{g_1+e}=F_{g_1}+F_e\) (\texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}distrib}), and the rewrite chain uses \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}step}, the definition \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Update}, \texttt{hpot} and \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}differentiable} with differentiability of both lifted potentials (\texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}potential\_\allowbreak{}differentiable}); the remaining coordinate identity is closed by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} and \texttt{ring}.

\item \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Chain} is (d), proved by \texttt{induction n}: the \texttt{zero} case is \texttt{rfl}, and in the \texttt{succ} case the induction hypothesis \texttt{ih} and \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Update\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Update} rewrite the next iterate, using \texttt{algorithm\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}succ} and \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}succ}.

\end{enumerate}



These are identities of explicit maps. The probabilistic consequences (equality of laws of the iterates) are drawn later from these pointwise identities.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Proximal points and the subgradient optimality condition}{Proximal points and the subgradient optimality condition}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Proximal-lean}


Throughout this section \(E\) is a real inner-product space with inner product \(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\) and norm \(\|\cdot\|\), \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) is a real-valued function and \(\lambda>0\). The \emph{proximal objective} at an input \(x\in E\) is \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)=f(p)+\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\), and a \emph{proximal point} of \(f\) at \(x\) is a global minimizer of \(Q_{\lambda,x}\). A \emph{subgradient} of \(f\) at \(p\) is a vector \(s\) such that the affine function \(y\mapsto f(p)+\langle s,y-p\rangle\) lies below \(f\) on all of \(E\).




We prove: (i) subgradients are monotone; (ii) \(p\) is a proximal point at \(x\) whenever the scaled residual \((x-p)/\lambda\) is a subgradient of \(f\) at \(p\), and, for convex \(f\), conversely; (iii) for convex \(f\), proximal points satisfy the firm nonexpansiveness inequality \(\|p-q\|^2\le\langle x-y,p-q\rangle\), so they are unique and both \(x\mapsto p\) and \(x\mapsto x-p\) are \(1\)-Lipschitz; (iv) if \(E\) is finite-dimensional and \(f\) is convex and bounded below, a proximal point exists at every input. The proximal map \(P_\lambda\) is then the map sending \(x\) to its unique proximal point.




These facts are used for the Moreau envelope in the next section, whose gradient is \((x-P_\lambda(x))/\lambda\), for the energy inequalities of the pairwise proximal particle updates, and for the contraction and parameter-sensitivity estimates of the splitting scheme.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Proximal objective, proximal points and monotone subgradients}{Proximal objective, proximal points and monotone subgradients}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Proximal-lean-1}


\textbf{Definitions.} Let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\), \(\lambda>0\) and \(x\in E\). The proximal objective is


\[Q_{\lambda,x}(p)=f(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\qquad(p\in E).\]


A point \(p\in E\) is a \emph{proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\) with parameter \(\lambda\)} if \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\) for every \(y\in E\), that is, if \(p\) is a global minimizer of \(Q_{\lambda,x}\). A vector \(s\in E\) is a \emph{subgradient of \(f\) at \(p\)}, written \(s\in\partial f(p)\), if


\[f(p)+\langle s,y-p\rangle\le f(y)\qquad\text{for every }y\in E.\qquad(1)\]


Inequality (1) says that the affine function \(y\mapsto f(p)+\langle s,y-p\rangle\) lies below \(f\) on all of \(E\) and agrees with \(f\) at \(p\). Neither definition requires convexity or differentiability of \(f\).




\textbf{Claim (monotonicity of subgradients).} Let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be any function, let \(p,q\in E\), \(s\in\partial f(p)\) and \(t\in\partial f(q)\). Then


\[\langle s-t,p-q\rangle\ge0.\]


\textbf{Proof.} Apply (1) for \(s\) at \(p\) with \(y=q\), and (1) for \(t\) at \(q\) with \(y=p\):


\[f(p)+\langle s,q-p\rangle\le f(q),\qquad f(q)+\langle t,p-q\rangle\le f(p).\]


Since \(q-p=-(p-q)\), linearity of the inner product in its second argument gives \(\langle s,q-p\rangle=-\langle s,p-q\rangle\). Adding the two inequalities and cancelling the real number \(f(p)+f(q)\) from both sides gives


\[-\langle s,p-q\rangle+\langle t,p-q\rangle\le0.\]


By linearity in the first argument the left-hand side equals \(-\langle s-t,p-q\rangle\). Hence \(\langle s-t,p-q\rangle\ge0\). \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{proxObjective}, \texttt{IsProxPoint}, \texttt{IsSubgradient} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{proxObjective} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 30–31}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ proxObjective\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (x\ p\ :\ E)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{f\ p\ +\ ‖p\ -\ x‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ (2\ *\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{IsProxPoint} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 34–35}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ IsProxPoint\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (x\ p\ :\ E)\ :\ Prop\ :=}
\leanline{2}{∀\ y,\ proxObjective\ f\ lam\ x\ p\ ≤\ proxObjective\ f\ lam\ x\ y}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{IsSubgradient} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 38–39}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ IsSubgradient\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (p\ g\ :\ E)\ :\ Prop\ :=}
\leanline{2}{∀\ y,\ f\ p\ +\ {\leanfallback ⟪}g,\ y\ -\ p{\leanfallback ⟫}\ ≤\ f\ y}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{IsSubgradient.\allowbreak{}monotone} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 42–49}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsSubgradient.monotone\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{p\ q\ g\ h\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hp\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ p\ g)\ (hq\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ q\ h)\ :}
\leanline{4}{0\ ≤\ {\leanfallback ⟪}g\ -\ h,\ p\ -\ q{\leanfallback ⟫}}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{prox\allowbreak{}Objective f lam x p} is \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\), the parameter \(\lambda\) being written \texttt{lam}. \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam x p} is the proposition \texttt{\ensuremath{\forall} y, prox\allowbreak{}Objective f lam x p \ensuremath{\le} prox\allowbreak{}Objective f lam x y}, and \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient f p g} is \texttt{\ensuremath{\forall} y, f p + {\leanfallback ⟪}g, y -\allowbreak{} p{\leanfallback ⟫} \ensuremath{\le} f y}, which is (1) with \(s=g\). Here \texttt{{\leanfallback ⟪}u, v{\leanfallback ⟫}} denotes the real inner product (made available by \texttt{open scoped Real\allowbreak{}Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space}), and the ambient space is a type \texttt{E} with instances \texttt{[Normed\allowbreak{}Add\allowbreak{}Comm\allowbreak{}Group E] [Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E]}, i.e. a real inner-product space; completeness is not assumed. The definitions accept every real \texttt{lam}: Lean's convention \(a/0=0\) makes \texttt{prox\allowbreak{}Objective} a total function, but every theorem of this section that uses it assumes \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam}, so only \(\lambda>0\) matters.




In \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient.\allowbreak{}monotone} the hypotheses \texttt{hp : Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient f p g} and \texttt{hq : Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient f q h} are the two supports; \texttt{hpq := hp q} and \texttt{hqp := hq p} are the two displayed instances of (1). The rewrite \texttt{show q -\allowbreak{} p = -\allowbreak{}(p -\allowbreak{} q) by abel} followed by \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}right} turns \(\langle g,q-p\rangle\) into \(-\langle g,p-q\rangle\); \texttt{simp only [inner\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}left]} expands the goal \(\langle g-h,p-q\rangle\) into \(\langle g,p-q\rangle-\langle h,p-q\rangle\), and \texttt{linarith} adds the two instances exactly as in the proof.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The residual subgradient condition characterizes proximal points}{The residual subgradient condition characterizes proximal points}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Proximal-lean-2}


Recall that \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) is \emph{convex} if \(f((1-t)p+ty)\le(1-t)f(p)+tf(y)\) for all \(p,y\in E\) and all \(t\in[0,1]\).




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(\lambda>0\), \(x,p\in E\) and \(f:E\to\mathbb R\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item (Sufficiency.) If \(\lambda^{-1}(x-p)\in\partial f(p)\), then \(p\) is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\). No convexity of \(f\) is needed.

\item (Necessity.) If \(f\) is convex and \(p\) is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\), then \(\lambda^{-1}(x-p)\in\partial f(p)\).

\end{enumerate}



Consequently, for convex \(f\) and \(\lambda>0\),


\[p\text{ is a proximal point of }f\text{ at }x\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{x-p}{\lambda}\in\partial f(p).\]


\textbf{Proof of sufficiency.} Fix \(y\in E\). Writing \(y-x=(p-x)+(y-p)\) and expanding the squared norm,


\[\|y-x\|^2=\|p-x\|^2+2\langle p-x,y-p\rangle+\|y-p\|^2.\qquad(1)\]


The hypothesis, tested at \(y\), reads \(f(p)+\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,y-p\rangle\le f(y)\). Since \(\langle x-p,y-p\rangle=-\langle p-x,y-p\rangle\), this is


\[f(y)-f(p)\ge-\frac1\lambda\langle p-x,y-p\rangle.\qquad(2)\]


Using (1) and then (2),


\[\begin{aligned}
Q_{\lambda,x}(y)-Q_{\lambda,x}(p)&=f(y)-f(p)+\frac{\|y-x\|^2-\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\\
&=f(y)-f(p)+\frac1\lambda\langle p-x,y-p\rangle+\frac{\|y-p\|^2}{2\lambda}\\
&\ge\frac{\|y-p\|^2}{2\lambda}\ge0 .
\end{aligned}\]


Hence \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\); as \(y\) was arbitrary, \(p\) is a proximal point. \(\square\) The computation also gives the quadratic growth \(Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\ge Q_{\lambda,x}(p)+\|y-p\|^2/(2\lambda)\).




\textbf{Proof of necessity.} Fix \(y\in E\). It suffices to show that


\[f(p)+\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,y-p\rangle\le f(y)+\varepsilon\qquad\text{for every }\varepsilon>0,\]


because a real number that is at most \(f(y)+\varepsilon\) for every \(\varepsilon>0\) is at most \(f(y)\). Fix \(\varepsilon>0\) and put


\[t=\min\Bigl(1,\ \frac{2\lambda\varepsilon}{\|y-p\|^2+1}\Bigr).\]


Then \(0<t\le1\) and \(t(\|y-p\|^2+1)\le2\lambda\varepsilon\); since \(t>0\), in particular


\[t\|y-p\|^2\le2\lambda\varepsilon.\qquad(3)\]


Let \(y_t=(1-t)p+ty\). Convexity of \(f\) with weights \(1-t\ge0\) and \(t\ge0\) gives


\[f(y_t)\le(1-t)f(p)+tf(y).\qquad(4)\]


Since \(y_t-x=(p-x)+t(y-p)\), expanding the squared norm gives \(\|y_t-x\|^2=\|p-x\|^2+2t\langle p-x,y-p\rangle+t^2\|y-p\|^2\). Minimality of \(p\) gives \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y_t)\); cancelling \(\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\) and applying (4),


\[f(p)\le f(y_t)+\frac{2t\langle p-x,y-p\rangle+t^2\|y-p\|^2}{2\lambda}\le(1-t)f(p)+tf(y)+\frac{t}{\lambda}\langle p-x,y-p\rangle+\frac{t^2}{2\lambda}\|y-p\|^2.\]


Subtracting \((1-t)f(p)\) from both sides and multiplying by \(2\lambda>0\) gives


\[t\Bigl(2\lambda\bigl(f(p)-f(y)\bigr)-2\langle p-x,y-p\rangle-t\|y-p\|^2\Bigr)\le0.\]


Because \(t>0\), the bracket is nonpositive. Dividing the bracket by \(2\lambda>0\) and using (3) divided by \(2\lambda\),


\[f(p)-\frac1\lambda\langle p-x,y-p\rangle\le f(y)+\frac{t\|y-p\|^2}{2\lambda}\le f(y)+\varepsilon.\]


The left-hand side equals \(f(p)+\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,y-p\rangle\), which proves the reduced claim. \(\square\)




The equivalence is the conjunction of the two implications. The choice of \(t\) replaces the limit \(t\downarrow0\) of the directional-derivative argument by a single admissible value of \(t\) for each \(\varepsilon\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient}, \texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}isProxPoint}, \texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}subgradient}}
\leansource{\texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 52–71}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ isProxPoint\_of\_subgradient\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)}
\leanline{4}{\{x\ p\ :\ E\}\ (hp\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ p\ (lam⁻¹\ •\ (x\ -\ p)))\ :}
\leanline{4}{IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}isProxPoint} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 74–113}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ subgradient\_of\_isProxPoint\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ \{x\ p\ :\ E\}\ (hp\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p)\ :}
\leanline{4}{IsSubgradient\ f\ p\ (lam⁻¹\ •\ (x\ -\ p))}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}subgradient} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 116–119}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ isProxPoint\_iff\_subgradient\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ \{x\ p\ :\ E\}\ :}
\leanline{4}{IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p\ ↔\ IsSubgradient\ f\ p\ (lam⁻¹\ •\ (x\ -\ p))}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient} assumes \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam} and \texttt{hp : Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient f p (lam\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} \ensuremath{\bullet} (x -\allowbreak{} p))}, where \texttt{lam\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} \ensuremath{\bullet} (x -\allowbreak{} p)} is the scalar multiple \(\lambda^{-1}(x-p)\); there is no convexity hypothesis. After \texttt{intro y}, \texttt{h := hp y} is the support inequality at \(y\), and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}left} moves the scalar \(\lambda^{-1}\) out of the inner product. \texttt{heq} is the expansion (1), obtained from \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real} (\(\|u+v\|^2=\|u\|^2+2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2\)), and \texttt{hi} is the sign change that turns the hypothesis into (2). The goal is multiplied by \(2\lambda>0\) with \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos\_\allowbreak{}right}; \texttt{h'} is (2) multiplied by \(\lambda\), and \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} followed by \texttt{nlinarith [sq\_\allowbreak{}nonneg \ensuremath{\Vert}y -\allowbreak{} p\ensuremath{\Vert}]} performs the displayed computation, the extra term being \(\|y-p\|^2\ge0\).




\texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point} assumes \texttt{hf : Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ f} (convexity of \(f\) on the whole space \texttt{univ}), \texttt{hlam} and \texttt{hp : Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam x p}.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{apply le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}forall\_\allowbreak{}pos\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}add} reduces the goal to the inequality with \(+\varepsilon\) for every \texttt{eps} \(>0\).

\item \texttt{t} is defined by the same minimum; \texttt{ht}, \texttt{ht1} and \texttt{hteps} are \(t>0\), \(t\le1\) and (3), the last one from \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}iff\ensuremath{{}_{0}}} applied to \texttt{min\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}right}.

\item \texttt{hconv} is (4): it is the second field \texttt{hf.\allowbreak{}2} of the convexity hypothesis, applied with weights \(1-t\) and \(t\). \texttt{hmin} is minimality of \(p\) tested at \(y_t\), and \texttt{heq} is \(y_t-x=(p-x)+t(y-p)\); the rewrites \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul} and \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos ht} expand \(\|y_t-x\|^2\).

\item \texttt{hstep} is the inequality \(f(p)-\lambda^{-1}\langle p-x,y-p\rangle\le f(y)+t\|y-p\|^2/(2\lambda)\). Its inner \texttt{have} is the product inequality with the factor \(t\); \texttt{nonpos\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}nonpos\_\allowbreak{}right} (from \(ab\le0\) and \(a>0\) infer \(b\le0\)) removes the factor \(t\), giving \texttt{hcancel}.

\item \texttt{herr} is (3) divided by \(2\lambda\), and \texttt{linarith} combines it with \texttt{hstep}.

\end{enumerate}



\texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}subgradient} packages the two implications as an equivalence \texttt{↔}, using convexity only for the forward direction.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Firm nonexpansiveness and uniqueness of proximal points}{Firm nonexpansiveness and uniqueness of proximal points}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Proximal-lean-3}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(f\) be convex, \(\lambda>0\), let \(p\) be a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\) and \(q\) a proximal point of \(f\) at \(y\), both with parameter \(\lambda\). Then


\[\|p-q\|^2\le\langle x-y,p-q\rangle,\qquad(1)\]
\[\|p-q\|\le\|x-y\|,\qquad\|(x-p)-(y-q)\|\le\|x-y\|.\qquad(2)\]


If \(x=y\), then \(p=q\); that is, the proximal point at a given input is unique.




\textbf{Proof.} By the necessity part of the previous step, \(\lambda^{-1}(x-p)\in\partial f(p)\) and \(\lambda^{-1}(y-q)\in\partial f(q)\). Monotonicity of subgradients gives


\[0\le\bigl\langle\lambda^{-1}(x-p)-\lambda^{-1}(y-q),\,p-q\bigr\rangle=\lambda^{-1}\bigl\langle(x-p)-(y-q),\,p-q\bigr\rangle.\]


Since \(\lambda^{-1}>0\), \(\langle(x-p)-(y-q),p-q\rangle\ge0\). Writing \((x-p)-(y-q)=(x-y)-(p-q)\) and using \(\langle p-q,p-q\rangle=\|p-q\|^2\), this is \(\langle x-y,p-q\rangle-\|p-q\|^2\ge0\), which is (1).




\emph{Uniqueness.} If \(x=y\), the right-hand side of (1) is \(\langle0,p-q\rangle=0\). Hence \(\|p-q\|^2\le0\), so \(\|p-q\|=0\) and \(p=q\).




\emph{Nonexpansiveness.} By (1) and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality \(\langle x-y,p-q\rangle\le\|x-y\|\,\|p-q\|\),


\[\|p-q\|^2\le\|x-y\|\,\|p-q\|.\]


If \(\|p-q\|>\|x-y\|\), then \(\|p-q\|>0\) and multiplying the strict inequality by \(\|p-q\|\) would give \(\|p-q\|^2>\|x-y\|\,\|p-q\|\), a contradiction. Hence \(\|p-q\|\le\|x-y\|\).




\emph{Residual nonexpansiveness.} Put \(u=x-y\) and \(v=p-q\), so that \((x-p)-(y-q)=u-v\) and (1) reads \(\|v\|^2\le\langle u,v\rangle\). Expanding the square,


\[\|u-v\|^2=\|u\|^2-2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2\le\|u\|^2-2\|v\|^2+\|v\|^2=\|u\|^2-\|v\|^2\le\|u\|^2.\]


Both norms are nonnegative, so \(\|u-v\|\le\|u\|\), which is the second inequality in (2). \(\square\)




Inequality (1) is the firm nonexpansiveness of the proximal map. By (2), the proximal map \(x\mapsto p\) and the residual map \(x\mapsto x-p\) are both \(1\)-Lipschitz.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}firm}, \texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}unique}, \texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}firm} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 122–133}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsProxPoint.firm\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ \{x\ y\ p\ q\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hp\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p)\ (hq\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ y\ q)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖p\ -\ q‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ {\leanfallback ⟪}x\ -\ y,\ p\ -\ q{\leanfallback ⟫}}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}unique} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 136–142}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsProxPoint.unique\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
