\leansource{\texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 145–151}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsProxPoint.nonexpansive\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ \{x\ y\ p\ q\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hp\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p)\ (hq\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ y\ q)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖p\ -\ q‖\ ≤\ ‖x\ -\ y‖}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{IsProxPoint.\allowbreak{}residual\_\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 154–162}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsProxPoint.residual\_nonexpansive\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ \{x\ y\ p\ q\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hp\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p)\ (hq\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ y\ q)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖(x\ -\ p)\ -\ (y\ -\ q)‖\ ≤\ ‖x\ -\ y‖}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The four theorems assume \texttt{hf : Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ f} and \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam} and take proximal points \texttt{hp : Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam x p} and \texttt{hq : Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam y q}; in \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}unique} both are at the same input \texttt{x}. The dot notation \texttt{hp.\allowbreak{}firm hf hlam hq} applies \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}firm} to \texttt{hp}.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}firm} is (1). Its \texttt{h} applies \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient.\allowbreak{}monotone} to the two residual subgradients produced by \texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point}. \texttt{rw [← smul\_\allowbreak{}sub, real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}left] at h} factors \(\lambda^{-1}\) out of the inner product, and \texttt{nonneg\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}right h (inv\_\allowbreak{}pos.\allowbreak{}mpr hlam)} removes the positive factor (the lemma states: \(0\le ab\) and \(0<a\) imply \(0\le b\)). \texttt{heq} is the regrouping \((x-p)-(y-q)=(x-y)-(p-q)\); \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}left} and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}self\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq} rewrite \(\langle (x-y)-(p-q),p-q\rangle\) as \(\langle x-y,p-q\rangle-\|p-q\|^2\), and \texttt{linarith} concludes.

\item \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}unique} applies (1) with equal inputs; \texttt{simp only [sub\_\allowbreak{}self, inner\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}left]} turns the right-hand side into \(0\), \texttt{nlinarith} gives \(\|p-q\|=0\), and \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero} and \texttt{sub\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero} give \(p=q\).

\item \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} combines (1) with the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}norm}; \texttt{nlinarith}, supplied with \(\|p-q\|\ge0\) and \(\|x-y\|\ge0\), performs the contradiction argument of the proof.

\item \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}residual\_\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} rewrites with the same regrouping, expands \(\|u-v\|^2\) with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real} (\(\|u-v\|^2=\|u\|^2-2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2\)), and concludes with \texttt{nlinarith} from (1) and nonnegativity of the two norms.

\end{enumerate}
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Existence of proximal points and the proximal map}{Existence of proximal points and the proximal map}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Proximal-lean-4}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(E\) be finite-dimensional, let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be convex and bounded below, say \(f(z)\ge b\) for all \(z\in E\) and some \(b\in\mathbb R\), and let \(\lambda>0\). Then for every \(x\in E\) there is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\). Consequently the \emph{proximal map}


\[P_\lambda(x)=\text{the unique proximal point of }f\text{ at }x\qquad(x\in E)\]


is well defined, and \(Q_{\lambda,x}(P_\lambda(x))\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\) for all \(x,y\in E\).




\textbf{Proof.} Fix \(x\in E\). Since \(f(x)\ge b\), the number \(s=\sqrt{2\lambda(f(x)-b)}\) is a well-defined nonnegative real, and \(R=s+1>0\).




\emph{Continuity.} A convex real-valued function on a finite-dimensional real normed space is locally Lipschitz, in particular continuous. The map \(p\mapsto\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\) is continuous as a composition of continuous maps. Hence \(Q_{\lambda,x}\) is continuous.




\emph{A minimizer on a ball.} In a finite-dimensional normed space closed balls are compact (Heine–Borel). The closed ball \(\bar B=\{p\in E:\|p-x\|\le R\}\) is therefore compact, and it is nonempty because it contains \(x\). A continuous real function on a nonempty compact set attains its minimum, so there is \(p\in\bar B\) with \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\) for every \(y\in\bar B\). Taking \(y=x\),


\[Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(x)=f(x).\qquad(1)\]


\emph{Competitors outside the ball.} Let \(\|y-x\|>R\). Then \(\|y-x\|>s\ge0\), so \(\|y-x\|^2\ge s^2=2\lambda(f(x)-b)\); dividing by \(2\lambda>0\) gives \(f(x)-b\le\|y-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\). Using \(f(y)\ge b\) and then (1),


\[Q_{\lambda,x}(y)=f(y)+\frac{\|y-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\ge b+\bigl(f(x)-b\bigr)=f(x)\ge Q_{\lambda,x}(p).\]


Together with the minimality on \(\bar B\), this gives \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(y)\) for every \(y\in E\), so \(p\) is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\).




\emph{The proximal map.} By the uniqueness proved in the previous step, the proximal point at each input is a single element of \(E\), denoted \(P_\lambda(x)\); by definition it minimizes \(Q_{\lambda,x}\). \(\square\)




The lower bound is used only to make the ball radius finite; convexity is used for continuity, for uniqueness and, through the previous step, for all later properties of \(P_\lambda\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}proxPoint}, \texttt{prox}, \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}isProxPoint}}
\leansource{\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}proxPoint} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 166–195}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ exists\_proxPoint\ [FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ ∃\ p,\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{prox} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 198–200}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ prox\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ E\ :=\ by}
\leanline{2}{classical}
\leanline{2}{exact\ if\ h\ :\ ∃\ p,\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ p\ then\ Classical.choose\ h\ else\ 0}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}isProxPoint} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Proximal.lean, lines 203–211}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_isProxPoint\ [FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ lam\ x\ (prox\ f\ lam\ x)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}prox\allowbreak{}Point} assumes the instance \texttt{[Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E]}, \texttt{hf : Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ f}, the lower bound \texttt{hb : \ensuremath{\exists} b, \ensuremath{\forall} z, b \ensuremath{\le} f z} and \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam}, and concludes \texttt{\ensuremath{\exists} p, Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam x p}. The proof follows the paragraphs above.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{R} is \(\sqrt{2\lambda(f(x)-b)}+1\), written with \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt}; \texttt{hxb} is \(f(x)-b\ge0\) and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}R} is \(R>0\).

\item \texttt{hcont} is continuity of the objective. The term \texttt{hf.\allowbreak{}locally\allowbreak{}Lipschitz} is the Mathlib theorem \texttt{Convex\allowbreak{}On.\allowbreak{}locally\allowbreak{}Lipschitz} (a convex function on all of a finite-dimensional space is locally Lipschitz), and the penalty is continuous by \texttt{continuous\_\allowbreak{}id.\allowbreak{}sub continuous\_\allowbreak{}const}, the norm, the square and \texttt{.\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}const}.

\item \texttt{(is\allowbreak{}Compact\_\allowbreak{}closed\allowbreak{}Ball x R).\allowbreak{}exists\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Min\allowbreak{}On} provides a minimizer \texttt{p} of the objective on \texttt{closed\allowbreak{}Ball x R}, which is nonempty by \texttt{mem\_\allowbreak{}closed\allowbreak{}Ball\_\allowbreak{}self}. Compactness of closed balls holds because finite-dimensional real normed spaces are proper.

\item \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hy : y \ensuremath{\in} closed\allowbreak{}Ball x R} splits the competitors. Inside the ball, \texttt{hmin hy} is the minimality. Outside, \texttt{hpx} is (1), \texttt{hy\allowbreak{}R} is \(R<\|y-x\|\), \texttt{hsqrt} is \(s^2=2\lambda(f(x)-b)\) (from \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}sqrt}), \texttt{hsq} and \texttt{hquot} are the two displayed consequences, and \texttt{linarith [hb y]} adds the lower bound \(f(y)\ge b\).

\end{enumerate}



The definition \texttt{prox f lam x} is \texttt{if h : \ensuremath{\exists} p, Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f lam x p then Classical.\allowbreak{}choose h else 0}. When a proximal point exists it returns one chosen by the axiom of choice (\texttt{Classical.\allowbreak{}choose}); otherwise it returns the value \(0\). Under the hypotheses of \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}prox\allowbreak{}Point} the first branch is taken, and by \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}unique} the chosen element is the unique proximal point, so \texttt{prox f lam x} is \(P_\lambda(x)\). Every later result that uses \texttt{prox} assumes convexity, a lower bound, finite dimension and \(\lambda>0\), so the fallback value is never used. \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point} states that \texttt{prox f lam x} is a proximal point: \texttt{dite\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}left h} selects the first branch of the definition, and \texttt{Classical.\allowbreak{}choose\_\allowbreak{}spec h} is the defining property of the chosen element.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Moreau envelope: gradient, smoothness and uniform approximation}{Moreau envelope: gradient, smoothness and uniform approximation}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean}


Let \(E\) be a finite-dimensional real inner-product space, \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) convex and bounded below, \(\lambda>0\), and \(P_\lambda\) the proximal map of the previous section. The \emph{Moreau envelope}


\[f^\lambda(x)=\min_{p\in E}\Bigl(f(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\Bigr)=f(P_\lambda(x))+\frac{\|P_\lambda(x)-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\]


is shown to be convex and continuously differentiable with gradient \(\nabla f^\lambda(x)=r_\lambda(x)=(x-P_\lambda(x))/\lambda\), and this gradient is \(\lambda^{-1}\)-Lipschitz. The proof rests on the two-sided bound \(0\le f^\lambda(y)-f^\lambda(x)-\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle\le\|y-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\); no differentiability of \(f\) is assumed.




If moreover \(f\) is \(K\)-Lipschitz, then \(\|\nabla f^\lambda\|\le K\), \(\|x-P_\lambda(x)\|\le\lambda K\) and \(0\le f-f^\lambda\le\lambda K^2/2\) on all of \(E\). The gradient formula identifies the smoothed particle algorithms of the later sections with Euler schemes for \(g_1+g_2^\lambda\), and the uniform gap \(\lambda K^2/2\) controls the distance between the smoothed and the original marginal maximizers.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The envelope, the residual and two value bounds}{The envelope, the residual and two value bounds}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-1}


\textbf{Setting.} In this section \(E\) is a finite-dimensional real inner-product space, \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) is convex and bounded below, and \(\lambda>0\). By the previous section every \(x\in E\) has a unique proximal point \(P_\lambda(x)\), the minimizer of \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)=f(p)+\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\). The \emph{Moreau envelope} of \(f\) with parameter \(\lambda\) and the \emph{proximal residual} are


\[f^\lambda(x)=Q_{\lambda,x}\bigl(P_\lambda(x)\bigr)=\min_{p\in E}Q_{\lambda,x}(p),\qquad r_\lambda(x)=\frac{x-P_\lambda(x)}{\lambda}.\]


\textbf{Claim.} (a) \(f^\lambda(x)\le f(x)\) for every \(x\in E\). (b) If \(b\in\mathbb R\) satisfies \(f(z)\ge b\) for every \(z\in E\), then \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\ge b\) for all \(x,p\in E\); in particular \(f^\lambda(x)\ge b\). Part (b) uses only \(\lambda>0\) and the lower bound.




\textbf{Proof.} (a) \(P_\lambda(x)\) minimizes \(Q_{\lambda,x}\); the competitor \(p=x\) gives \(f^\lambda(x)\le Q_{\lambda,x}(x)=f(x)+0=f(x)\). (b) Since \(\|p-x\|^2\ge0\) and \(2\lambda>0\), \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)=f(p)+\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\ge f(p)\ge b\). Taking \(p=P_\lambda(x)\) gives \(f^\lambda(x)\ge b\). \(\square\)




Thus \(f^\lambda\) is a real-valued function with \(b\le f^\lambda\le f\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau}, \texttt{residual}, \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}le} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 24–25}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ moreau\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{proxObjective\ f\ lam\ x\ (prox\ f\ lam\ x)}
\end{leancode}

\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ residual\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ E\ :=}
\leanline{2}{lam⁻¹\ •\ (x\ -\ prox\ f\ lam\ x)}

\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_le\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ ≤\ f\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}moreau} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 42–44}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ le\_moreau\ \{b\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hbf\ :\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ b\ ≤\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The section variables are the instance \texttt{[Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E]} and the hypotheses \texttt{hf : Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ f}, \texttt{hb : \ensuremath{\exists} b, \ensuremath{\forall} z, b \ensuremath{\le} f z} and \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam}. The command \texttt{include hf hb hlam} makes these three hypotheses arguments of every theorem of the section, and a line \texttt{omit … in} removes some of them from the single theorem that follows it.




\texttt{moreau f lam x} is defined as \texttt{prox\allowbreak{}Objective f lam x (prox f lam x)}, the objective evaluated at the selected proximal point, and \texttt{residual f lam x} is \texttt{lam\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} \ensuremath{\bullet} (x -\allowbreak{} prox f lam x)}. Under the section hypotheses \texttt{prox f lam x} is the unique proximal point \(P_\lambda(x)\) (by \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point}), so these definitions are \(f^\lambda\) and \(r_\lambda\).




\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}le} is (a): it is \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point hf hb hlam x} tested at the competitor \texttt{x}, simplified by \texttt{simpa} since \(\|x-x\|^2/(2\lambda)=0\). \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}moreau} is (b). Its line \texttt{omit [Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E] [Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E] hf hb in} removes the inner-product structure, finite dimension, convexity and \texttt{hb}, so the theorem holds for every function with the lower bound \texttt{hbf : \ensuremath{\forall} z, b \ensuremath{\le} f z} and every \(\lambda>0\). The proof chains \texttt{hbf \_\allowbreak{}} with \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}right}, the nonnegativity of the penalty being proved by \texttt{positivity}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Global supports, the quadratic upper bound and convexity}{Global supports, the quadratic upper bound and convexity}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} For all \(x,y\in E\),


\[f^\lambda(x)+\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle\le f^\lambda(y)\le f^\lambda(x)+\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\|y-x\|^2}{2\lambda}.\qquad(1)\]


In particular \(r_\lambda(x)\) is a subgradient of \(f^\lambda\) at \(x\), and \(f^\lambda\) is convex.




\textbf{Proof of the lower bound.} Write \(p=P_\lambda(x)\) and \(q=P_\lambda(y)\). By the residual subgradient condition of the previous section (here convexity of \(f\) is used), \(\lambda^{-1}(x-p)\in\partial f(p)\). Testing this support at \(q\),


\[f(q)\ge f(p)+\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,q-p\rangle.\qquad(2)\]


Put \(a=q-y\) and \(c=p-x\). Then \(q-p=a-c+(y-x)\) and \(x-p=-c\), so


\[\langle x-p,q-p\rangle=-\langle c,a\rangle+\|c\|^2+\langle x-p,y-x\rangle.\]


Insert this into (2), add \(\|a\|^2/(2\lambda)\) to both sides and subtract \(\|c\|^2/(2\lambda)+\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,y-x\rangle\):


\[f(q)+\frac{\|a\|^2}{2\lambda}-\Bigl(f(p)+\frac{\|c\|^2}{2\lambda}+\frac1\lambda\langle x-p,y-x\rangle\Bigr)\ge\frac{\|a\|^2-2\langle a,c\rangle+\|c\|^2}{2\lambda}=\frac{\|a-c\|^2}{2\lambda}\ge0.\]


Since \(f(q)+\|a\|^2/(2\lambda)=f^\lambda(y)\), \(f(p)+\|c\|^2/(2\lambda)=f^\lambda(x)\) and \(\lambda^{-1}\langle x-p,y-x\rangle=\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle\), this is the lower bound in (1).




\textbf{Proof of the upper bound.} Since \(P_\lambda(y)\) minimizes \(Q_{\lambda,y}\), the competitor \(p=P_\lambda(x)\) gives \(f^\lambda(y)\le Q_{\lambda,y}(p)=f(p)+\|p-y\|^2/(2\lambda)\). Writing \(p-y=(p-x)-(y-x)\),


\[\|p-y\|^2=\|p-x\|^2-2\langle p-x,y-x\rangle+\|y-x\|^2,\]


and therefore


\[Q_{\lambda,y}(p)=f(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}+\frac1\lambda\langle x-p,y-x\rangle+\frac{\|y-x\|^2}{2\lambda}=f^\lambda(x)+\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{\|y-x\|^2}{2\lambda}.\]


This is the upper bound in (1); it uses only the minimality of \(P_\lambda(y)\).




\textbf{Proof of convexity.} Let \(x,y\in E\), \(a,b\ge0\) with \(a+b=1\), and \(z=ax+by\). The lower bound in (1), based at \(z\) and evaluated at \(x\) and at \(y\), gives


\[f^\lambda(z)+\langle r_\lambda(z),x-z\rangle\le f^\lambda(x),\qquad f^\lambda(z)+\langle r_\lambda(z),y-z\rangle\le f^\lambda(y).\]


Multiply the first inequality by \(a\ge0\), the second by \(b\ge0\), and add. The inner-product terms combine to \(\langle r_\lambda(z),a(x-z)+b(y-z)\rangle\), and \(a(x-z)+b(y-z)=ax+by-(a+b)z=z-z=0\). Since \((a+b)f^\lambda(z)=f^\lambda(z)\), the sum reads \(f^\lambda(ax+by)\le af^\lambda(x)+bf^\lambda(y)\). \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}support}, \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}upper}, \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}convex}}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}support} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 47–66}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_support\ (x\ y\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ +\ {\leanfallback ⟪}residual\ f\ lam\ x,\ y\ -\ x{\leanfallback ⟫}\ ≤\ moreau\ f\ lam\ y}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}upper} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 69–79}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_upper\ (x\ y\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{moreau\ f\ lam\ y\ ≤\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ +\ {\leanfallback ⟪}residual\ f\ lam\ x,\ y\ -\ x{\leanfallback ⟫}\ +}
\leanline{6}{‖y\ -\ x‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ (2\ *\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}convex} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 82–103}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_convex\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ (moreau\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}support} is the lower bound in (1). With \texttt{p := prox f lam x} and \texttt{q := prox f lam y}, \texttt{h} is (2): \texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point} applied to \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point hf hb hlam x} and tested at \texttt{q}. \texttt{hd} is the decomposition \(q-p=(q-y)-(p-x)+(y-x)\), and the rewrites \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}left} and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}self\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq} expand the inner product as in the proof. \texttt{hs} is \(\|a-c\|^2\ge0\), expanded by \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real} and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}comm}. The goal, displayed by \texttt{change}, is multiplied by \(2\lambda>0\) using \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos\_\allowbreak{}right}; \texttt{h'} is (2) multiplied by \(\lambda\), and \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} and \texttt{nlinarith} combine \texttt{h'} with \texttt{hs}.




\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}upper} is the upper bound in (1). \texttt{h} is the minimality of \texttt{prox f lam y} tested at the competitor \texttt{prox f lam x}; \texttt{h.\allowbreak{}trans\_\allowbreak{}eq} reduces the goal to the displayed identity for \(Q_{\lambda,y}(p)\), which follows from the rewrite \(p-y=(p-x)-(y-x)\), \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}left}, a sign change, \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} and \texttt{ring}.




\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}convex} proves \texttt{Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ (moreau f lam)}. This structure has two components: \texttt{convex\_\allowbreak{}univ} (the domain is convex) and the convexity inequality for weights \texttt{a}, \texttt{b} with \texttt{ha : 0 \ensuremath{\le} a}, \texttt{hb' : 0 \ensuremath{\le} b} and \texttt{hab : a + b = 1}. \texttt{hx} and \texttt{hy} are the two supports based at \texttt{z}; \texttt{hi} states that the weighted inner-product terms sum to zero, using \texttt{hz}, the identity \(a(x-z)+b(y-z)=0\), and \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}right}. \texttt{h1} and \texttt{h2} are the weighted supports, \texttt{hm} is \(af^\lambda(z)+bf^\lambda(z)=f^\lambda(z)\), and \texttt{nlinarith} adds them.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz bounds for the proximal map and the residual}{Lipschitz bounds for the proximal map and the residual}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-3}


\textbf{Claim.} For all \(x,y\in E\),


\[\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\lambda(y)\|\le\|x-y\|,\qquad\|r_\lambda(x)-r_\lambda(y)\|\le\frac1\lambda\|x-y\|.\]


\textbf{Proof.} The first inequality is the nonexpansiveness of proximal points from the previous section, applied to \(p=P_\lambda(x)\) and \(q=P_\lambda(y)\). For the second, \(r_\lambda(x)-r_\lambda(y)=\lambda^{-1}\bigl((x-P_\lambda(x))-(y-P_\lambda(y))\bigr)\). By homogeneity of the norm and \(\lambda^{-1}>0\),


\[\|r_\lambda(x)-r_\lambda(y)\|=\lambda^{-1}\bigl\|(x-P_\lambda(x))-(y-P_\lambda(y))\bigr\|\le\lambda^{-1}\|x-y\|,\]


where the inequality is the nonexpansiveness of the residual map \(x\mapsto x-P_\lambda(x)\) from the previous section. \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, \texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}}
\leansource{\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 106–110}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_lipschitz\ :\ LipschitzWith\ 1\ (prox\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 113–120}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ residual\_lipschitz\ :\ LipschitzWith\ lam⁻¹.toNNReal\ (residual\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With K g} means \(\operatorname{dist}(g(x),g(y))\le K\operatorname{dist}(x,y)\) for all \(x,y\), with \(K\) a nonnegative real number (type \texttt{NNReal}, also written \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0}); in a normed space \(\operatorname{dist}(u,v)=\|u-v\|\) (\texttt{dist\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sub}).




\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} states \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With 1 (prox f lam)}. After \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With.\allowbreak{}mk\_\allowbreak{}one} the goal is the inequality between distances, which is \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} applied to the two proximal points given by \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point}. \texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} states \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With lam\ensuremath{{}^{-1}}.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}NNReal (residual f lam)}, where \texttt{lam\ensuremath{{}^{-1}}.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}NNReal} is \(\lambda^{-1}\) regarded as a nonnegative real. \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With.\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le'} reduces it to \(\|r_\lambda(x)-r_\lambda(y)\|\le\lambda^{-1}\|x-y\|\); the rewrites \texttt{← smul\_\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}abs} and \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos (inv\_\allowbreak{}pos.\allowbreak{}mpr hlam)} extract the factor \(\lambda^{-1}\), and \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}left} multiplies \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point.\allowbreak{}residual\_\allowbreak{}nonexpansive} by it.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The gradient of the envelope is the scaled residual}{The gradient of the envelope is the scaled residual}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-4}


Recall that a function \(g:E\to\mathbb R\) has gradient \(v\in E\) at \(x\) if \(g(y)-g(x)-\langle v,y-x\rangle=o(\|y-x\|)\) as \(y\to x\): for every \(c>0\) there is \(\rho>0\) such that


\[|g(y)-g(x)-\langle v,y-x\rangle|\le c\|y-x\|\qquad\text{whenever }\|y-x\|<\rho.\]


Equivalently, \(g\) is Fréchet differentiable at \(x\) with derivative \(h\mapsto\langle v,h\rangle\); \(v\) is then denoted \(\nabla g(x)\).




\textbf{Claim.} For every \(x\in E\), \(f^\lambda\) has gradient \(r_\lambda(x)=\lambda^{-1}(x-P_\lambda(x))\) at \(x\). Consequently \(f^\lambda\) is differentiable on \(E\), \(\nabla f^\lambda=r_\lambda\), and \(f^\lambda\) is continuously differentiable. No differentiability of \(f\) is assumed.




\textbf{Proof.} Fix \(x\) and put \(R(y)=f^\lambda(y)-f^\lambda(x)-\langle r_\lambda(x),y-x\rangle\). By (1) of the previous step but one,


\[0\le R(y)\le\frac{\|y-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\qquad(y\in E).\qquad(1)\]


Let \(c>0\) and \(\rho=2\lambda c>0\). If \(\|y-x\|<\rho\), then by (1)


\[|R(y)|=R(y)\le\frac{\|y-x\|}{2\lambda}\,\|y-x\|\le\frac{\rho}{2\lambda}\,\|y-x\|=c\,\|y-x\|.\]


Hence \(f^\lambda\) has gradient \(r_\lambda(x)\) at \(x\); in particular it is differentiable at every point and \(\nabla f^\lambda(x)=r_\lambda(x)\).




For the continuity of the derivative, the derivative at \(x\) is the linear functional \(h\mapsto\langle r_\lambda(x),h\rangle\). The map \(v\mapsto\langle v,\cdot\rangle\) from \(E\) to its dual is a linear isometry (Riesz representation), hence continuous, and \(r_\lambda\) is \(\lambda^{-1}\)-Lipschitz by the previous step, hence continuous. Their composition \(x\mapsto\langle r_\lambda(x),\cdot\rangle\) is continuous. A function that is differentiable everywhere with continuous derivative is continuously differentiable. \(\square\)




By the previous step the gradient \(\nabla f^\lambda\) is \(\lambda^{-1}\)-Lipschitz.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}hasGradientAt}, \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}differentiable}, \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}hasGradientAt} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 123–136}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_hasGradientAt\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ HasGradientAt\ (moreau\ f\ lam)\ (residual\ f\ lam\ x)\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}differentiable} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 139–140}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_differentiable\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ (moreau\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 143–144}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ gradient\_moreau\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ gradient\ (moreau\ f\ lam)\ x\ =\ residual\ f\ lam\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}contDiff} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 147–153}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_contDiff\ :\ ContDiff\ ℝ\ 1\ (moreau\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{Has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At g v x} states that \(g\) is Fréchet differentiable at \(x\) with derivative \(h\mapsto\langle v,h\rangle\), and \texttt{gradient g x} is the vector representing the derivative of \(g\) at \(x\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At}: \texttt{has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Little\allowbreak{}O} rewrites the goal as the statement that \(R(y)\) is little-o of \(y-x\) along the neighbourhood filter \texttt{{\leanfallback 𝓝} x}, and \texttt{Asymptotics.\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Little\allowbreak{}O\_\allowbreak{}iff} turns this into: for every \(c>0\), eventually \(|R(y)|\le c\|y-x\|\). \texttt{filter\_\allowbreak{}upwards [Metric.\allowbreak{}ball\_\allowbreak{}mem\_\allowbreak{}nhds x …]} restricts to the ball of radius \(c\cdot(2\lambda)\), which gives \texttt{hdist}. \texttt{hlo} (\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}support}) and \texttt{hhi} (\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}upper}) are the two sides of (1); \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg} removes the absolute value using the lower side, and \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}iff\ensuremath{{}_{0}}} with \texttt{nlinarith} performs the final estimate.

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}differentiable} applies \texttt{.\allowbreak{}differentiable\allowbreak{}At} to the gradient statement at each point, and \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau}, the identity \(\nabla f^\lambda(x)=r_\lambda(x)\), is \texttt{Has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At.\allowbreak{}gradient} applied to it.

\item \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}cont\allowbreak{}Diff} proves \texttt{Cont\allowbreak{}Diff \ensuremath{\mathbb R} 1 (moreau f lam)}. By \texttt{cont\allowbreak{}Diff\_\allowbreak{}one\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}has\allowbreak{}FDeriv\allowbreak{}At} it suffices to give a continuous family of Fréchet derivatives. The family is \texttt{Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Dual \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E (residual f lam x)}, the functional \(\langle r_\lambda(x),\cdot\rangle\); it is continuous as the composition of the continuous map \texttt{Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Dual \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E} with the residual, which is continuous by \texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, and each derivative statement is \texttt{(moreau\_\allowbreak{}has\allowbreak{}Gradient\allowbreak{}At hf hb hlam x).\allowbreak{}has\allowbreak{}FDeriv\allowbreak{}At}.

\end{enumerate}
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Bounded gradient and bounded proximal displacement}{Bounded gradient and bounded proximal displacement}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-5}


\textbf{Claim.} Suppose in addition that \(f\) is \(K\)-Lipschitz for some \(K\ge0\), that is, \(|f(u)-f(v)|\le K\|u-v\|\) for all \(u,v\in E\). Then for every \(x\in E\),


\[\|r_\lambda(x)\|\le K,\qquad\|x-P_\lambda(x)\|\le\lambda K.\]


\textbf{Proof.} Put \(p=P_\lambda(x)\) and \(g=r_\lambda(x)\). By the residual subgradient condition, \(g\in\partial f(p)\). Testing the support inequality at \(y=p+g\) gives \(f(p)+\langle g,g\rangle\le f(p+g)\), so, by the Lipschitz property,


\[\|g\|^2\le f(p+g)-f(p)\le|f(p+g)-f(p)|\le K\|g\|.\]


If \(\|g\|>K\), then \(\|g\|>0\) and \(\|g\|^2>K\|g\|\), contradicting the display; hence \(\|g\|\le K\). Since \(x-P_\lambda(x)=\lambda g\) and \(\lambda>0\), \(\|x-P_\lambda(x)\|=\lambda\|g\|\le\lambda K\). \(\square\)




Because \(\nabla f^\lambda=r_\lambda\), the first inequality bounds the gradient of the envelope by \(K\), uniformly in \(\lambda>0\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}displacement\_\allowbreak{}le}}
\leansource{\texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 156–167}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ residual\_norm\_le\ \{K\ :\ NNReal\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖residual\ f\ lam\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}displacement\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 170–176}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_displacement\_le\ \{K\ :\ NNReal\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖x\ -\ prox\ f\ lam\ x‖\ ≤\ lam\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The Lipschitz hypothesis is \texttt{h\allowbreak{}K : Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With K f} with \texttt{K : NNReal}, and \texttt{(K : \ensuremath{\mathbb R})} is the coercion of \(K\) to a real number. In \texttt{residual\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{hs} is the support from \texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point} tested at \texttt{p + g}; \texttt{add\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}cancel\_\allowbreak{}left} simplifies \((p+g)-p\) to \(g\) and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}self\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq} rewrites \(\langle g,g\rangle\) as \(\|g\|^2\). \texttt{hd} is the Lipschitz bound \texttt{h\allowbreak{}K.\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul (p + g) p}, rewritten with \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}eq} (on \(\mathbb R\), \(\operatorname{dist}(a,b)=|a-b|\)), and \texttt{hdiff} drops the absolute value using \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}abs\_\allowbreak{}self}. \texttt{nlinarith}, given \(\|g\|\ge0\) and \(K\ge0\), carries out the case argument.




\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}displacement\_\allowbreak{}le} rewrites the residual bound with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}abs} and \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos}, giving \(\lambda^{-1}\|x-P_\lambda(x)\|\le K\); it multiplies this by \(\lambda\ge0\) (\texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}left}), and \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} cancels \(\lambda\lambda^{-1}\).
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The uniform gap between a Lipschitz function and its envelope}{The uniform gap between a Lipschitz function and its envelope}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Moreau-lean-6}


\textbf{Claim.} (a) Let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be \(K\)-Lipschitz with \(K\ge0\), and \(\lambda>0\). Then for all \(x,p\in E\)


\[Q_{\lambda,x}(p)=f(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\ge f(x)-\frac{\lambda K^2}{2};\]


in particular \(f^\lambda(x)\ge f(x)-\lambda K^2/2\). Part (a) uses neither convexity nor a lower bound. (b) If \(f\) is in addition convex and bounded below, then for every \(x\in E\)


\[0\le f(x)-f^\lambda(x)\le\frac{\lambda K^2}{2}.\]


\textbf{Proof.} (a) Let \(d=\|p-x\|\). The Lipschitz property gives \(f(x)-f(p)\le|f(x)-f(p)|\le Kd\), i.e. \(f(p)\ge f(x)-Kd\). Completing the square in \(d\),


\[\frac{d^2}{2\lambda}-Kd=\frac{(d-\lambda K)^2}{2\lambda}-\frac{\lambda K^2}{2}\ge-\frac{\lambda K^2}{2},\]


because \((d-\lambda K)^2\ge0\) and \(2\lambda>0\). Hence \(Q_{\lambda,x}(p)\ge f(x)-Kd+d^2/(2\lambda)\ge f(x)-\lambda K^2/2\).




(b) The inequality \(f(x)-f^\lambda(x)\ge0\) is part (a) of the first step, and \(f(x)-f^\lambda(x)\le\lambda K^2/2\) is part (a) of this step with \(p=P_\lambda(x)\). \(\square\)




The gap is uniform in \(x\) and linear in \(\lambda\). For any function \(g_1\), writing \(U=g_1+f\) and \(V_\lambda=g_1+f^\lambda\), it gives \(V_\lambda\le U\le V_\lambda+\lambda K^2/2\) on all of \(E\); this two-sided comparison is the form in which the gap enters the comparison of marginal potentials.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lower\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lower\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 180–190}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_lower\_of\_lipschitz\ \{K\ :\ NNReal\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{f\ x\ -\ lam\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2\ ≤\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Moreau.lean, lines 193–200}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_gap\_of\_lipschitz\ \{K\ :\ NNReal\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{0\ ≤\ f\ x\ -\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ ∧\ f\ x\ -\ moreau\ f\ lam\ x\ ≤\ lam\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lower\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} is preceded by \texttt{omit [Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E] [Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E] hf hb in}, so its only hypotheses are \texttt{hlam} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}K}. It therefore holds for every \(K\)-Lipschitz function and for whichever point \texttt{prox f lam x} denotes, which is the content of (a). \texttt{hdist} is the Lipschitz bound between \texttt{x} and \texttt{prox f lam x}, with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}rev} orienting the norm, and \texttt{hdiff} removes the absolute value. After multiplying the goal by \(2\lambda>0\) and \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}, \texttt{nlinarith} uses \texttt{hdiff'} (\texttt{hdiff} multiplied by \(\lambda\)) and the square \((d-\lambda K)^2\ge0\), supplied as \texttt{sq\_\allowbreak{}nonneg (\ensuremath{\Vert}prox f lam x -\allowbreak{} x\ensuremath{\Vert} -\allowbreak{} lam * (K : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}))}: this is the completion of the square.




\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} is (b), a conjunction \texttt{∧} proved by \texttt{constructor}. The first half is \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}le} turned into \(0\le f(x)-f^\lambda(x)\) by \texttt{sub\_\allowbreak{}nonneg.\allowbreak{}mpr}; the second half is \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lower\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} rearranged by \texttt{linarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Uniform potential gaps and the displacement of marginal maximizers}{Uniform potential gaps and the displacement of marginal maximizers}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean}


Let \(U\) and \(V\) be real functions on the pair space \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times\mathbb R^{d_x}\) with \(V\le U\le V+\delta\), where \(U\) is continuous (and possibly nonsmooth) and \(V\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\). For a pair function \(W\) define the marginal integral and the marginal potential


\[k_W(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb R^{d_x}}e^{-W(\theta,x)}\,dx,\qquad\varphi_W(\theta)=-\log k_W(\theta).\]


A \emph{marginal maximizer} of \(W\) is a maximizer of \(k_W\), i.e. a maximizer of the marginal likelihood in the latent-variable model with joint density proportional to \(e^{-W}\). This section proves: \(k_U\) is positive, finite and continuous and has a maximizer; \(\varphi_V\le\varphi_U\le\varphi_V+\delta\); a marginal maximizer of \(U\) minimizes \(\varphi_U\); and any marginal maximizers \(\theta_U\) of \(U\) and \(\theta_V\) of \(V\) satisfy


\[\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|\le\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}{m}} .\]


The tools are the quadratic growth of a strongly convex function at its minimizer, monotonicity of the integral, a Gaussian majorant of \(e^{-V}\), dominated convergence, and the strong convexity of \(\varphi_V\) (a consequence of the Prékopa–Leindler inequality, proved in the IPLA development). No derivative of \(U\) and no expansion in \(\delta\) are used. With \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), \(U=g_1+g_2\) and \(\delta=\eta K^2/2\) this gives the smoothing bias \(\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\).



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.12]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.49]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Quadratic growth at the minimizer of a strongly convex function}{Quadratic growth at the minimizer of a strongly convex function}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(H\) be a real inner-product space, \(m\ge0\), \(F:H\to\mathbb R\) \(m\)-strongly convex, and \(z\in H\) a global minimizer of \(F\). Then for every \(x\in H\)


\[F(z)+\frac m2\|x-z\|^2\le F(x).\]


\textbf{Proof.} Fix \(x\) and put \(A=\frac m2\|x-z\|^2\ge0\) and \(G=F(x)-F(z)\ge0\) (minimality). We show \(A\le G\). Suppose instead \(G<A\); then \(A>G\ge0\). Put


\[b=\frac{A-G}{2A},\qquad\text{so that}\quad0<b\le\frac12,\qquad b\cdot2A=A-G.\qquad(1)\]


Strong convexity with weights \(1-b\) and \(b\) at the points \(z\) and \(x\) gives


\[F\bigl((1-b)z+bx\bigr)\le(1-b)F(z)+bF(x)-b(1-b)\frac m2\|x-z\|^2=F(z)+bG-b(1-b)A.\]


Minimality of \(z\) gives \(F(z)\le F((1-b)z+bx)\). Hence \(0\le bG-b(1-b)A\), and dividing by \(b>0\), \((1-b)A\le G\). By (1), \((1-b)A=A-bA=A-\frac{A-G}2=\frac{A+G}2\). Thus \(\frac{A+G}2\le G\), i.e. \(A\le G\), contradicting \(G<A\). Therefore \(A\le G\), which is the claim. \(\square\)




The argument uses only the strong-convexity inequality and global minimality; \(F\) need not be differentiable.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{strongConvex\_\allowbreak{}minimum\_\allowbreak{}growth}}
\leansource{\texttt{strongConvex\_\allowbreak{}minimum\_\allowbreak{}growth} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Perturbation.lean, lines 26–55}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ strongConvex\_minimum\_growth\ (F\ :\ H\ →\ ℝ)\ (m\ :\ ℝ)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ ≤\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ Set.univ\ m\ F)\ (z\ :\ H)}
\leanline{4}{(hz\ :\ ∀\ x,\ F\ z\ ≤\ F\ x)\ (x\ :\ H)\ :}
\leanline{4}{F\ z\ +\ m\ /\ 2\ *\ ‖x\ -\ z‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ F\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{strong\allowbreak{}Convex\_\allowbreak{}minimum\_\allowbreak{}growth} takes \texttt{hm : 0 \ensuremath{\le} m}, \texttt{hsc : Strong\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On Set.\allowbreak{}univ m F} and the minimality \texttt{hz : \ensuremath{\forall} x, F z \ensuremath{\le} F x}. With \texttt{A} and \texttt{G} as in the text, \texttt{suffices A \ensuremath{\le} G} reduces the goal, and \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}contra h} assumes the opposite. \texttt{b} is defined as in (1); \texttt{hb} and \texttt{hb1} are \(b>0\) and \(b\le1/2\). \texttt{hs} is the strong convexity inequality \texttt{hsc.\allowbreak{}2} with weights \(1-b\) and \(b\), and \texttt{hmin} is minimality at the convex combination. \texttt{hg} is \((1-b)A\le G\), obtained by \texttt{nlinarith}; \texttt{heq} is \(b\cdot2A=A-G\) (\texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}cancel\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}), and a final \texttt{nlinarith} derives the contradiction.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Comparison of the marginal integrals and the marginal potentials}{Comparison of the marginal integrals and the marginal potentials}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(U,V\) be real functions on the pair space and \(\delta\in\mathbb R\) with \(V(w)\le U(w)\le V(w)+\delta\) for every pair \(w\). Fix \(\theta\in\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item For the integrals \(M_W(\theta)=\int e^{-W(\theta,x)}\,dx\in[0,\infty]\) of the nonnegative functions \(e^{-W(\theta,\cdot)}\) (finite or not),
\[M_U(\theta)\le M_V(\theta)\le e^\delta M_U(\theta).\qquad(1)\]

\item If moreover \(U\) is continuous and \(V\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), then \(k_U(\theta)\) and \(k_V(\theta)\) are finite and positive, and
\[\varphi_V(\theta)\le\varphi_U(\theta)\le\varphi_V(\theta)+\delta.\qquad(2)\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) Since \(-U\le-V\) and the exponential is increasing, \(e^{-U(\theta,x)}\le e^{-V(\theta,x)}\) for every \(x\); monotonicity of the integral of nonnegative functions gives \(M_U(\theta)\le M_V(\theta)\). Since \(U\le V+\delta\), \(-V-\delta\le-U\), so \(e^{-V(\theta,x)}=e^\delta e^{-V(\theta,x)-\delta}\le e^\delta e^{-U(\theta,x)}\). Integrating and taking the finite constant \(e^\delta\) out of the integral gives \(M_V(\theta)\le e^\delta M_U(\theta)\).




(b) \emph{Integrability.} Since \(V\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex, \(V(w)\ge V(0)+\langle\nabla V(0),w\rangle+\frac m2\|w\|^2\), and by Young's inequality \(\langle\nabla V(0),w\rangle\ge-\|\nabla V(0)\|^2/m-\frac m4\|w\|^2\). Hence, with \(\|w\|^2=\|\theta\|^2+\|x\|^2\ge\|x\|^2\) for \(w=(\theta,x)\),


\[e^{-V(\theta,x)}\le C\,e^{-\frac m4\|x\|^2},\qquad C=\exp\Bigl(\frac{\|\nabla V(0)\|^2}{m}-V(0)\Bigr),\qquad(3)\]


and the right-hand side is integrable in \(x\) (a Gaussian function). So \(e^{-V(\theta,\cdot)}\) is integrable \cite[Section~3.9.2]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}. The function \(x\mapsto e^{-U(\theta,x)}\) is continuous, hence measurable, and bounded by \(e^{-V(\theta,x)}\); it is therefore integrable.




\emph{Positivity and comparison.} Both integrands are strictly positive and integrable, so \(k_U(\theta)>0\) and \(k_V(\theta)>0\). The pointwise inequalities of (a) and monotonicity of the integral give \(k_U(\theta)\le k_V(\theta)\le e^\delta k_U(\theta)\).




\emph{Logarithms.} The logarithm is increasing on \((0,\infty)\). From \(k_U\le k_V\), \(\log k_U(\theta)\le\log k_V(\theta)\), i.e. \(\varphi_V(\theta)\le\varphi_U(\theta)\). From \(k_V\le e^\delta k_U\), \(\log k_V(\theta)\le\delta+\log k_U(\theta)\), i.e. \(\varphi_U(\theta)\le\varphi_V(\theta)+\delta\). \(\square\)




The additive gap \(\delta\) passes unchanged from the potentials to the marginal potentials, because the logarithm is taken after the integration.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{marginalDensity\_\allowbreak{}sandwich}, \texttt{marginalPotential\_\allowbreak{}sandwich}}
\leansource{\texttt{marginalDensity\_\allowbreak{}sandwich} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Perturbation.lean, lines 59–77}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ marginalDensity\_sandwich\ \{dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}}
\leanline{4}{(U\ V\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ ℝ)\ (δ\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(hgap\ :\ ∀\ z,\ V\ z\ ≤\ U\ z\ ∧\ U\ z\ ≤\ V\ z\ +\ δ)}
\leanline{4}{(θ\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dθ))\ :}
\leanline{4}{IPLA.marginalDensity\ U\ θ\ ≤\ IPLA.marginalDensity\ V\ θ\ ∧}
\leanline{6}{IPLA.marginalDensity\ V\ θ\ ≤}
\leanline{8}{ENNReal.ofReal\ (Real.exp\ δ)\ *\ IPLA.marginalDensity\ U\ θ}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{marginalPotential\_\allowbreak{}sandwich} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Perturbation.lean, lines 82–117}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ marginalPotential\_sandwich\ \{dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}}
\leanline{4}{(U\ V\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ ℝ)\ (δ\ m\ :\ ℝ)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(hU\ :\ Continuous\ U)\ (hV\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ V)}
\leanline{4}{(hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ Set.univ\ m\ V)}
\leanline{4}{(hgap\ :\ ∀\ z,\ V\ z\ ≤\ U\ z\ ∧\ U\ z\ ≤\ V\ z\ +\ δ)}
\leanline{4}{(θ\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dθ))\ :}
\leanline{4}{IPLA.marginalPotential\ V\ θ\ ≤\ IPLA.marginalPotential\ U\ θ\ ∧}
\leanline{6}{IPLA.marginalPotential\ U\ θ\ ≤\ IPLA.marginalPotential\ V\ θ\ +\ δ}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The pair space is \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}Joint d\ensuremath{\theta} dx} and \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}pair \ensuremath{\theta} x} is the pair \((\theta,x)\). \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Density U \ensuremath{\theta}} is the lower Lebesgue integral \texttt{\ensuremath{\int}\ensuremath{{}^{-}} x, ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real (Real.\allowbreak{}exp (-\allowbreak{}U (pair \ensuremath{\theta} x))) \ensuremath{\partial}volume} with values in \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0\ensuremath{\infty}}, which is \(M_U(\theta)\); \texttt{volume} is Lebesgue measure on \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\). \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Integral U \ensuremath{\theta}} is the real (Bochner) integral \(k_U(\theta)\), and \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Potential U \ensuremath{\theta}} is \texttt{-\allowbreak{}Real.\allowbreak{}log (marginal\allowbreak{}Integral U \ensuremath{\theta})}, i.e. \(\varphi_U(\theta)\). The gap hypothesis is \texttt{hgap : \ensuremath{\forall} z, V z \ensuremath{\le} U z ∧ U z \ensuremath{\le} V z + \ensuremath{\delta}}.




\texttt{marginal\allowbreak{}Density\_\allowbreak{}sandwich} is (a): the first inequality is \texttt{lintegral\_\allowbreak{}mono} with the pointwise comparison \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real} and \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}exp\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}exp}; the second moves the constant into the integral with \texttt{lintegral\_\allowbreak{}const\_\allowbreak{}mul'} (allowed since \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}ne\_\allowbreak{}top}), then uses \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}exp\_\allowbreak{}add} and the same monotonicity.




\texttt{marginal\allowbreak{}Potential\_\allowbreak{}sandwich} is (b). \texttt{hi\allowbreak{}V} is the integrability of \(e^{-V(\theta,\cdot)}\) (\texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}integrable\_\allowbreak{}marginal\_\allowbreak{}weight}, which rests on (3)); \texttt{hi\allowbreak{}U} is obtained from it by \texttt{Integrable.\allowbreak{}mono'}, with measurability from continuity of \(U\) composed with \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}continuous\_\allowbreak{}pair}. \texttt{hp\allowbreak{}U} and \texttt{hp\allowbreak{}V} are positivity (\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}exp\_\allowbreak{}pos} and \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Integral\_\allowbreak{}pos}); \texttt{h\allowbreak{}UV} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}VU} are the two integral comparisons (\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono}, with \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}const\_\allowbreak{}mul} for the factor \(e^\delta\)). The conclusion uses \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}log\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}log}, and for the second inequality \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}log\_\allowbreak{}mul} and \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}log\_\allowbreak{}exp} to split \(\log(e^\delta k_U)=\delta+\log k_U\).
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.12.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.3.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.9.2]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrability, continuity and a maximizer of the original marginal}{Integrability, continuity and a maximizer of the original marginal}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-3}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(U\) be continuous, \(V\) differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), and \(V\le U\) on the pair space. Then:



\begin{enumerate}


\item for every \(\theta\), \(x\mapsto e^{-U(\theta,x)}\) is integrable on \(\mathbb R^{d_x}\);

\item \(k_U\) is continuous on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\);

\item \(U\) has a marginal maximizer: there is \(\theta^\star\) with \(\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}\,dx\le\int e^{-U(\theta^\star,x)}\,dx\) for all \(\theta\).

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) As in the previous step, \(e^{-U(\theta,\cdot)}\) is measurable and dominated by the integrable function \(e^{-V(\theta,\cdot)}\).




(b) We apply the dominated convergence theorem for parameter-dependent integrals: if \(f(\theta,\cdot)\) is measurable for each \(\theta\), \(|f(\theta,x)|\le h(x)\) for all \(\theta\) and \(x\) with \(h\) integrable, and \(\theta\mapsto f(\theta,x)\) is continuous for every \(x\), then \(\theta\mapsto\int f(\theta,x)\,dx\) is continuous. Take \(f(\theta,x)=e^{-U(\theta,x)}\) and \(h(x)=Ce^{-\frac m4\|x\|^2}\) with the constant \(C\) of (3) in the previous step: then \(f(\theta,x)\le e^{-V(\theta,x)}\le h(x)\) for all \(\theta\), \(h\) is integrable, and continuity of \(U\) gives continuity of \(\theta\mapsto f(\theta,x)\).




(c) \emph{Decay.} By (3) with the full norm \(\|w\|^2=\|\theta\|^2+\|x\|^2\), \(e^{-V(\theta,x)}\le Ce^{-\frac m4\|\theta\|^2}e^{-\frac m4\|x\|^2}\), so \(k_V(\theta)\to0\) as \(\|\theta\|\to\infty\) \cite[Section~3.9.4]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}. Since \(0\le k_U\le k_V\), also \(k_U(\theta)\to0\).




\emph{A maximum.} \(k_U(0)>0\), because the integrand is positive and integrable. By the decay there is \(R>0\) with \(k_U(\theta)<k_U(0)\) whenever \(\|\theta\|>R\). The closed ball of radius \(R\) is compact and \(k_U\) is continuous by (b), so \(k_U\) attains a maximum on it at some \(\theta^\star\); then \(k_U(\theta^\star)\ge k_U(0)>k_U(\theta)\) for \(\|\theta\|>R\), so \(\theta^\star\) is a global maximizer of \(k_U\).




\emph{Extended integrals.} For an integrable nonnegative function the integral in \([0,\infty]\) equals the real integral, so \(\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}\,dx=k_U(\theta)\) in both senses, and \(\theta^\star\) is a marginal maximizer. \(\square\)

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal}, \texttt{continuous\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal}, \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginalMaximizer}}
\leansource{\texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Perturbation.lean, lines 121–133}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integrable\_original\_marginal\ \{dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}}
\leanline{4}{(U\ V\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ ℝ)\ (m\ :\ ℝ)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(hU\ :\ Continuous\ U)\ (hV\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ V)}
\leanline{4}{(hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ Set.univ\ m\ V)\ (hgap\ :\ ∀\ z,\ V\ z\ ≤\ U\ z)}
\leanline{4}{(θ\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dθ))\ :}

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal} is (a), proved like \texttt{hi\allowbreak{}U} of the previous step, with the domination \(e^{-U}\le e^{-V}\) from \texttt{hgap : \ensuremath{\forall} z, V z \ensuremath{\le} U z}.




\texttt{continuous\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal} is (b): \texttt{continuous\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}dominated} is Mathlib's continuity theorem for parameter-dependent integrals, with the four obligations measurability, domination, integrability of the bound and continuity in the parameter. The bound is \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}exp (\ensuremath{\Vert}gradient V 0\ensuremath{\Vert} \textasciicircum{} 2 /\allowbreak{} m -\allowbreak{} V 0) * Real.\allowbreak{}exp (-\allowbreak{}(m /\allowbreak{} 4) * \ensuremath{\Vert}x\ensuremath{\Vert} \textasciicircum{} 2)}; domination is \texttt{h\allowbreak{}UV} followed by \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\_\allowbreak{}weight\_\allowbreak{}uniform\_\allowbreak{}bound} (inequality (3)), and integrability of the Gaussian bound is \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}integrable\_\allowbreak{}exp\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq}.




\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}original\_\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Maximizer} is (c). \texttt{hp} is \(k_U(0)>0\); \texttt{ht} is the decay \texttt{Filter.\allowbreak{}Tendsto (IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Integral U) (Filter.\allowbreak{}cocompact \_\allowbreak{}) (nhds 0)}, where the cocompact filter expresses \(\|\theta\|\to\infty\), proved by \texttt{squeeze\_\allowbreak{}zero} between \(0\) and \(k_V\) using \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}tendsto\_\allowbreak{}marginal\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}cocompact}. \texttt{he} states that eventually \(k_U(\theta)<k_U(0)\), and \texttt{Continuous.\allowbreak{}exists\_\allowbreak{}forall\_\allowbreak{}ge'} produces the global maximizer from continuity and this eventual bound. Finally \texttt{heq} identifies \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Density U a} with \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real (IPLA.\allowbreak{}marginal\allowbreak{}Integral U a)} (\texttt{of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}lintegral\_\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real}), so the real maximization gives the maximization in \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0\ensuremath{\infty}} required by \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}Is\allowbreak{}Marginal\allowbreak{}Maximizer}.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.26.4]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.11.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.12.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.3.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.9.2]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.9.4]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Minimality of the marginal potential and the displacement of maximizers}{Minimality of the marginal potential and the displacement of maximizers}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-4}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(U\) be continuous, \(V\) differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item If \(V\le U\) and \(\theta^\star\) is a marginal maximizer of \(U\), then \(\varphi_U(\theta^\star)\le\varphi_U(\theta)\) for every \(\theta\).

\item If \(V\le U\le V+\delta\), \(\theta_U\) is a marginal maximizer of \(U\) and \(\theta_V\) a marginal maximizer of \(V\), then
\[\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|^2\le\frac{2\delta}m,\qquad\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|\le\sqrt{\frac{2\delta}m}.\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) By the previous step, \(k_U(\theta)\) is finite and positive and equals the extended integral. The maximizer property gives \(k_U(\theta)\le k_U(\theta^\star)\), and since the logarithm is increasing on \((0,\infty)\), \(-\log k_U(\theta^\star)\le-\log k_U(\theta)\).




(b) Four facts are combined. By (a), \(\theta_U\) minimizes \(\varphi_U\); in particular


\[\varphi_U(\theta_U)\le\varphi_U(\theta_V).\qquad(1)\]


For the smooth strongly convex \(V\), a marginal maximizer minimizes \(\varphi_V\) \cite[Section~3.12.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}, and \(\varphi_V\) is \(m\)-strongly convex \cite[Section~3.12.3]{LangevinLean2026IPLA} (integration over the latent variable preserves strong log-concavity, by the Prékopa–Leindler inequality). The quadratic growth of the first step at the minimizer \(\theta_V\) gives


\[\varphi_V(\theta_V)+\frac m2\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|^2\le\varphi_V(\theta_U).\qquad(2)\]


By (2) of the second step at \(\theta_U\) and at \(\theta_V\),


\[\varphi_V(\theta_U)\le\varphi_U(\theta_U),\qquad\varphi_U(\theta_V)\le\varphi_V(\theta_V)+\delta.\qquad(3)\]


Chaining (2), the first inequality of (3), (1) and the second inequality of (3),


\[\frac m2\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|^2\le\varphi_V(\theta_U)-\varphi_V(\theta_V)\le\varphi_U(\theta_U)-\varphi_V(\theta_V)\le\varphi_U(\theta_V)-\varphi_V(\theta_V)\le\delta .\]


Multiplying by \(2/m>0\) gives the first bound. In particular \(2\delta/m\ge0\), and since the square root is increasing on \([0,\infty)\) and \(\|\theta_U-\theta_V\|\ge0\), taking square roots gives the second bound. \(\square\)




The hypothesis \(V\le U\le V+\delta\) itself forces \(\delta\ge0\). Only \(V\) needs to be differentiable; the minimality of \(\theta_U\) for \(\varphi_U\) is used only through (1).

Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.12.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.12.3]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.3.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\section{\texorpdfstring{Sharp Gibbs concentration by dilation}{Sharp Gibbs concentration by dilation}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SharpConcentration-lean}


Let \(F\) be continuous and \(q\)-strongly convex in dimension \(d\), with \(q>0\) and global minimizer \(z\). A contraction toward the minimizer compares the Gibbs partition with its unnormalized second moment. The left derivative at unit dilation gives the dimension-over-curvature bound. Gaussian domination ensures all integrals are finite, and only an explicit scalar expression is differentiated; no derivative of the potential is required.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The partition comparison under radial contraction}{The partition comparison under radial contraction}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpConcentration-lean-1}


Strong convexity and minimality give \(F(z+r(x-z))\le F(x)-\frac q2(1-r^2)\|x-z\|^2\) for \(0<r<1\). Exponentiate and use \(e^u\ge1+u\). The dilation \(x\mapsto z+r(x-z)\) has determinant \(r^d\), so integration gives

\[Z+\frac q2(1-r^2)M\le r^{-d}Z,\qquad Z=\int e^{-F},\quad M=\int\|x-z\|^2e^{-F}.\]

In the notation \(w(x)=e^{-F(x)}\), \(Z=\int w\), \(M=\int\|x-z\|^2w(x)\,dx\), and \(d=\dim H\), Gaussian domination first proves integrability of \(w\) and \(\|x-z\|^2w\). Strong convexity combined with quadratic growth at the mode gives

\[F(z+r(x-z))\le F(x)-\tfrac q2(1-r^2)\|x-z\|^2.\]

Using \(e^u\ge1+u\) after exponentiation gives

\[w(z+r(x-z))\ge w(x)+\tfrac q2(1-r^2)\|x-z\|^2w(x).\]

Indeed the strong-convexity segment from the minimizer to \(x\) contributes radial deficit \(q(1-r^2)\|x-z\|^2/2\). Inequality \(e^u\ge1+u\) turns it into a density-plus-moment bound. The determinant is \(r^d\), so the integral of the dilated density is \(r^{-d}Z\). This proves the partition comparison with all terms finite.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The limiting derivative and the normalized moment}{The limiting derivative and the normalized moment}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpConcentration-lean-2}


Multiply the partition comparison by \(r^d\) and define \(\Phi(r)=r^d[Z+(q/2)(1-r^2)M]\). It satisfies \(\Phi(r)\le Z=\Phi(1)\) for \(0<r<1\). The left difference quotients at one are nonnegative; differentiating the explicit scalar expression gives \(\Phi'(1)=dZ-qM\ge0\) and hence \(M\le dZ/q\). The partition \(Z\) is positive and finite by Gaussian domination. Normalization therefore gives

\[\int\|x-z\|^2\pi(dx)=M/Z\le d/q,\qquad\pi(dx)=Z^{-1}e^{-F(x)}dx.\]

The argument does not differentiate \(F\). Equivalently, \(qM\le dZ\) may be divided by positive \(Z\). The normalized second moment is \(M/Z\) and is bounded by \(d/q\). This permits the estimate for continuous nonsmooth strongly convex potentials without an integration-by-parts assumption.

\leanline{4}{(∫\ x,\ ‖x\ -\ z‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ∂gibbsMeasure\ F)\ ≤\ (Module.finrank\ ℝ\ H\ :\ ℝ)\ /\ q}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Here \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Z} proves \(Z>0\), and \texttt{hpart} identifies the extended partition function with \(\iota Z\). The density change in \texttt{heq} therefore gives \[\int\|x-z\|^2\,d\pi(x)=Z^{-1}M.\] Multiplication of the unnormalized inequality by the nonnegative \(Z^{-1}\), followed by \(Z^{-1}Z=1\), yields \(\int\|x-z\|^2d\pi\le d/q\). Positive normalization is proved from the potential assumptions, rather than supplied as a moment or probability premise.



Formal correspondence: sharp\_integral\_sq\_dist\_gibbs\_le : A continuous \(q\)-strongly convex Gibbs potential concentrates around its minimizer with sharp second moment at most \(\operatorname{dim}/q\). Every normalization and integrability fact used in the conclusion is proved from the stated inputs.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The left derivative giving the \(d/q\) bound}{The left derivative giving the d/q bound}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpConcentration-lean-3}


With \(Z,M,d\) from the partition comparison define

\[\Phi(r)=r^d\bigl[Z+\tfrac q2(1-r^2)M\bigr].\]

For \(0<r<1\) one has \(\Phi(r)\le Z=\Phi(1)\). Thus when \(-1<u<0\), the difference quotient \((\Phi(1+u)-\Phi(1))/u\) is nonnegative: its numerator and denominator are both nonpositive.



For negative \(u\) tending to zero, \(\Phi(1+u)-\Phi(1)\) and \(u\) retain these signs. The quotient converges to \(dZ-qM\) by differentiation of the explicit scalar expression. Its limit is nonnegative, giving the desired moment bound. The comparison only needs contractions \(r<1\) because their left derivative already determines the derivative inequality at \(r=1\).

Differentiate this explicit scalar polynomial, rather than differentiating \(F\): \[\Phi'(1)=dZ-qM.\] In Lean, \texttt{hinner} differentiates the bracket, \texttt{hder} applies the product rule, and \texttt{hnonneg} records the sign of the left difference quotient. Its limit is \(\Phi'(1)\), so \texttt{hbound} gives \(dZ-qM\ge0\). Since \(q>0\), divide to obtain \(M\le(d/q)Z\).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Sharp parameter concentration and the IPLA RMS bound}{Sharp parameter concentration and the IPLA RMS bound}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean}


Apply the continuous-potential concentration estimate to the parameter marginal, whose curvature has factor \(N\) from particle count. For a differentiable \(m\)-strongly convex pair potential \(U\) with \(m>0\), Prékopa–Leindler gives the marginal curvature. Its \(N\)-power target has curvature \(Nm\), so its parameter second moment is at most \(d_\theta/(Nm)\). This improves the equilibrium term in the earlier estimate while keeping the transport and discretization terms unchanged.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.19]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Sharp concentration of the parameter marginal}{Sharp concentration of the parameter marginal}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean-1}


For continuous \(a\)-strongly convex potential \(f\) with minimizer \(x_\star\), the dilation argument gives

\[
\int\|x-x_\star\|^2\,\pi(dx)\le\frac d a,
\qquad\pi(dx)\propto e^{-f(x)}dx.
\]

Only continuity, strong convexity, and a minimizing point are needed; no nonsmooth integration-by-parts formula is assumed. Prékopa–Leindler gives strong convexity of the parameter marginal potential. Raising the marginal likelihood to its \(N\)-th power multiplies that curvature by \(N\). Thus

\[
W_2(\Pi_{\lambda,N}^{\theta},\delta_{\theta_{\lambda,\star}})
\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}.
\]

This is the factor in the source’s equilibrium estimate. The earlier radius \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\) was a valid weaker bound.



The parameter density is proportional to \(M(\theta)^N\) and has negative log potential \(NF(\theta)\). Its curvature is \(Nm\), with minimizer \(\theta_*\). The sharp theorem in dimension \(d_\theta\) gives \(\int\|\theta-\theta_*\|^2d\Pi_N\le d_\theta/(Nm)\). In the equivalent notation, density \(e^{-N\Phi}\) has curvature \(Nm\), so dimension \(d_\theta\) gives second moment \(d_\theta/(Nm)\). The exact projection identity transfers that same moment to the full Gibbs law; its nonnegative square root is the equilibrium root mean square radius.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Gaussian particle estimate with sharp equilibrium radius}{The Gaussian particle estimate with sharp equilibrium radius}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean-2}


Assume \(N>0\), \(\gamma>0\), \(\gamma\le2/(m+L)\), independent Gaussian innovations, and \(L^2\) initialization. The scaled Gaussian Euler estimate, parameter projection, and the sharp equilibrium moment give

\[\operatorname{RMS}_n\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_N)+\frac{33}{20}\frac Lm\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

For any coupling of the current state and Gibbs target, projection does not increase quadratic cost. Minkowski adds the Gibbs parameter error about the center. Taking the coupling infimum gives transport plus the sharp radius, and substitution yields the displayed estimate. If the parameter dimension is zero its error is zero; if the state is a singleton its transport is zero. Thus the same inequality holds for arbitrary nonnegative parameter and latent dimensions.

The shared Gaussian ULA theorem controls its \(W_2\) error, while the parameter projection has norm at most one. The projected RMS therefore is at most the sharp equilibrium scale \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\) plus that transport error. The nonempty-dimensional case proves this combination directly; the final declaration splits off zero-dimensional cases so that no unspoken positive-dimension premise remains.

\texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}ipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}nonempty}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hn}, \texttt{hfinite}, \texttt{hs}, \texttt{hp}, \texttt{hmoment}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}iff}, \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}gibbs\allowbreak{}Target\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gibbs\allowbreak{}Measure}, \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}scaled\_\allowbreak{}bias\_\allowbreak{}eq}, \texttt{h\ensuremath{\gamma}.\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}projected\allowbreak{}RMSReal\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}sqrt\_\allowbreak{}integral}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}, \texttt{linarith}.



\texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}ipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms}: the cases are separated by \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hi : Nonempty (Fin d\ensuremath{\theta} ⊕ (Fin N \ensuremath{\times} Fin dx))}; the intermediate facts include \texttt{h\ensuremath{\theta}}, \texttt{hx}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{sharp\_\allowbreak{}ipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}nonempty}, \texttt{Nat.\allowbreak{}cast\_\allowbreak{}zero}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}zero}.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA: laws, moments and the error bound about the smoothed marginal maximizer}{MYIPLA: laws, moments and the error bound about the smoothed marginal maximizer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean}


Let \(g_1\) be a differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex pair potential with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, let \(g_2\) be convex and bounded below, and let \(\eta>0\). The smoothed pair potential is \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), where \(g_2^\eta\) is the Moreau envelope, and \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). By the previous sections \(V_\eta\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex, \(\nabla V_\eta\) is \(L_\eta\)-Lipschitz, and MYIPLA with the proximal map \(P_\eta\) of \(g_2\) is, iterate by iterate, the IPLA scheme for \(V_\eta\). This section turns these identities into statements about the laws \(\rho_n\) of the MYIPLA iterates on a probability space \((\Omega,Q)\).




We prove: \(\rho_n\) is the law of the IPLA iterate for \(V_\eta\); \(\rho_n\) is a probability measure whose parameter block has finite second moment for every \(L^2\) initialization and \(L^2\) innovations; and, for independent standard Gaussian innovations independent of the initialization and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\),


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta^\star_\eta\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V_\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}N}\,\sqrt\gamma,\]


where \(\theta_n\) is the parameter block of the \(n\)-th iterate, \(\theta^\star_\eta\) a maximizer of the smoothed marginal likelihood \(\theta\mapsto\int e^{-V_\eta(\theta,x)}\,dx\) and \(\pi_{N,V_\eta}\) the Gibbs target of the smoothed model. The next section replaces \(\theta^\star_\eta\) by the maximizer of the original nonsmooth model.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Curvature and gradient regularity of the smoothed potential}{Curvature and gradient regularity of the smoothed potential}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean-1}


\textbf{Definitions.} For pair potentials \(g_1,g_2\) and \(\eta>0\), the smoothed potential is \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), and for \(L_1\ge0\) the smoothed gradient constant is \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\).




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(E\) be a real normed space (in (b), a complete real inner-product space).



\begin{enumerate}


\item If \(g_1:E\to\mathbb R\) is \(m\)-strongly convex and \(e:E\to\mathbb R\) is convex, then \(g_1+e\) is \(m\)-strongly convex.

\item If \(g_1\) and \(e\) are differentiable, \(\nabla g_1\) is \(L_1\)-Lipschitz and \(\nabla e\) is \(L_2\)-Lipschitz, then \(\nabla(g_1+e)\) is \((L_1+L_2)\)-Lipschitz.

\end{enumerate}



In particular, if \(g_1\) is differentiable, \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and \(g_2\) is convex and bounded below on a finite-dimensional space, then \(V_\eta\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_\eta\)-Lipschitz gradient.




\textbf{Proof.} (a) Let \(x,y\in E\), \(a,b\ge0\), \(a+b=1\). Strong convexity of \(g_1\) and convexity of \(e\) give


\[g_1(ax+by)\le ag_1(x)+bg_1(y)-ab\frac m2\|x-y\|^2,\qquad e(ax+by)\le ae(x)+be(y).\]


Adding the two inequalities gives the \(m\)-strong convexity inequality for \(g_1+e\).




(b) By additivity of the gradient of differentiable functions (previous section), \(\nabla(g_1+e)=\nabla g_1+\nabla e\). By the triangle inequality,


\[\|\nabla(g_1+e)(x)-\nabla(g_1+e)(y)\|\le\|\nabla g_1(x)-\nabla g_1(y)\|+\|\nabla e(x)-\nabla e(y)\|\le(L_1+L_2)\|x-y\|.\]


For the final statement apply (a) and (b) with \(e=g_2^\eta\), which is convex and differentiable with \(\eta^{-1}\)-Lipschitz gradient by the Moreau envelope section, so \(L_2=\eta^{-1}\). \(\square\)

\leansource{\texttt{smoothedPotential} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MYIPLA.lean, lines 24–26}

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{smoothed\allowbreak{}Potential g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} g\ensuremath{{}_{2}} \ensuremath{\eta} x} is \texttt{g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} x + moreau g\ensuremath{{}_{2}} \ensuremath{\eta} x}. \texttt{smoothing\allowbreak{}Lipschitz L\ensuremath{{}_{1}} \ensuremath{\eta}} is the nonnegative real \texttt{L\ensuremath{{}_{1}} + \ensuremath{\eta}.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}NNReal\ensuremath{{}^{-1}}}, where \texttt{\ensuremath{\eta}.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}NNReal} is \(\max(\eta,0)\) as an element of \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0} and inversion in \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0} sends \(0\) to \(0\); \texttt{smoothing\allowbreak{}Lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}coe} states that for \texttt{h\ensuremath{\eta} : 0 < \ensuremath{\eta}} its real value is \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\) (by \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}NNReal\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}). Using a nonnegative real is required because \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With} takes its constant in \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0}.




\texttt{smoothed\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex} is (a). After \texttt{refine \ensuremath{\langle}convex\_\allowbreak{}univ, ?\_\allowbreak{}\ensuremath{\rangle}} and introduction of the points and weights, \texttt{h\ensuremath{{}_{1}}} is the strong convexity inequality \texttt{hsc.\allowbreak{}2} and \texttt{h\ensuremath{{}_{2}}} the convexity inequality \texttt{he.\allowbreak{}2}, and \texttt{linarith} adds them. No sign condition on \(m\) is needed. \texttt{smoothed\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} is (b): \texttt{hsum} is \(\nabla(g_1+e)=\nabla g_1+\nabla e\), obtained from \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}differentiable} by \texttt{funext}, and \texttt{h\ensuremath{{}_{1}}.\allowbreak{}add h\ensuremath{{}_{2}}} is the Mathlib fact that the sum of an \(L_1\)-Lipschitz and an \(L_2\)-Lipschitz map is \((L_1+L_2)\)-Lipschitz.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The MYIPLA laws are IPLA laws and have finite second moments}{The MYIPLA laws are IPLA laws and have finite second moments}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean-2}


\textbf{Setting.} Let \((\Omega,Q)\) be a probability space, \(Z_0:\Omega\to\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times(\mathbb R^{d_x})^N\) an initial state and \(\xi_0,\xi_1,\dots\) innovations, all square-integrable random vectors. For \(\omega\in\Omega\) let \(Z_n(\omega)\) be the MYIPLA chain started at \(Z_0(\omega)\) and driven by \((\xi_k(\omega))_k\). The \emph{law} of the \(n\)-th iterate is the pushforward measure \(\rho_n=Q\circ Z_n^{-1}\), \(\rho_n(A)=Q(Z_n\in A)\). Write \(\theta(z)\) for the parameter block of a state \(z\).




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\), let \(g_1,e\) be differentiable pair functions, \(P\) a map of the pair space and \(\eta\ne0\) with \(\nabla e=\eta^{-1}(I-P)\). Let \(\gamma\in\mathbb R\), \(L\ge0\), and assume that \(\nabla(g_1+e)\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz. Then for every \(n\):



\begin{enumerate}


\item \(\rho_n\) equals the law of the \(n\)-th IPLA iterate for the potential \(g_1+e\) with step \(\gamma\), started at \(Z_0\) and driven by the same innovations;

\item for every \(\vartheta\in\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\), \(\int\|\theta(z)-\vartheta\|^2\,\rho_n(dz)<\infty\).

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) By the last identity of the previous section, \(Z_n(\omega)\) coincides for every \(\omega\) with the IPLA iterate \(Y_n(\omega)\) for \(g_1+e\) driven by the same inputs. Equal maps have equal pushforwards, so \(\rho_n=Q\circ Y_n^{-1}\).




(b) Since \(\nabla(g_1+e)\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, the lifted gradient \(\nabla F_{g_1+e}\) is Lipschitz on the state space, hence of at most linear growth: \(\|\nabla F_{g_1+e}(z)\|\le\|\nabla F_{g_1+e}(0)\|+NL\|z\|\). The IPLA step \(z\mapsto z-\frac\gamma N\nabla F_{g_1+e}(z)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\,\xi\) therefore maps square-integrable random vectors to square-integrable random vectors, and by induction on \(n\) the iterate \(Y_n\) is square-integrable (and measurable). The parameter projection \(z\mapsto\theta(z)\) is linear with norm at most \(1\), so \(\theta(Y_n)-\vartheta\) is square-integrable on the probability space \((\Omega,Q)\), constants being square-integrable there. By the change-of-variables formula for pushforward measures,


\[\int\|\theta(z)-\vartheta\|^2\,\rho_n(dz)=\int_\Omega\|\theta(Y_n(\omega))-\vartheta\|^2\,Q(d\omega)<\infty .\ \square\]


No Gaussianity, independence or convexity is used here; only the Lipschitz gradient and the square-integrability of the inputs.

After rewriting with (a), \texttt{chain} is the \(L^2\) iterate; \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}map\_\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}iff} transfers the claim from the pushforward measure to the composition with \texttt{chain} (this is the change-of-variables step), and \texttt{(Lp.\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp chain).\allowbreak{}continuous\allowbreak{}Linear\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}comp IPLA.\allowbreak{}parameter\allowbreak{}CLM} followed by \texttt{.\allowbreak{}sub (mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}const \ensuremath{\theta})} proves square-integrability of \(\theta(Y_n)-\vartheta\). The linear-growth argument of the text is carried out in the construction of \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}lp\allowbreak{}Chain}, where each step is shown to map \(L^2\) to \(L^2\).
\end{leanbox}
