\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The error bound for a general smoothing with residual gradient}{The error bound for a general smoothing with residual gradient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean-3}


\textbf{Definitions.} The \emph{Gibbs target} of a pair potential \(V\) is the probability measure on the state space with density proportional to \(e^{-F_V(z)}\), \(F_V(z)=\sum_iV(J_iz)\); it is denoted \(\pi_{N,V}\). A \emph{marginal maximizer} of \(V\) is a point \(\theta^\star\in\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\) with \(k_V(\theta)\le k_V(\theta^\star)\) for every \(\theta\), where \(k_V(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb R^{d_x}}e^{-V(\theta,x)}\,dx\in[0,\infty]\). For probability measures \(\mu,\nu\) on the state space with finite second moments, \(W_2(\mu,\nu)=\inf_\pi\bigl(\int\|u-v\|^2\,\pi(du,dv)\bigr)^{1/2}\), the infimum over all couplings \(\pi\) of \(\mu\) and \(\nu\). The innovations are \emph{independent standard Gaussian, independent of the initialization} if every coordinate \(\xi_{k,a}\) has the law \(N(0,1)\), the family \((\xi_{k,a})_{k\ge0,\,a}\) of all coordinates is mutually independent, and \(Z_0\) is independent of the sequence \((\xi_k)_{k\ge0}\).




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\), \(m>0\), \(\eta>0\), \(\gamma>0\) and \(L_1\ge0\). Let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient; let \(e\) be differentiable and convex with \(\eta^{-1}\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let \(P\) be a map of the pair space with \(\nabla e=\eta^{-1}(I-P)\). Put \(V=g_1+e\), \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), and assume


\[\gamma\le\frac{2}{m+L_\eta}.\]


Let \(\theta^\star\) be a marginal maximizer of \(V\). Let \(Z_0,\xi_0,\xi_1,\dots\) be square-integrable random states with independent standard Gaussian innovations independent of the initialization, let \(\rho_n\) be the law of the \(n\)-th MYIPLA iterate and \(\theta_n\) its parameter block. Then for every \(n\ge0\)


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta^\star\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\,\sqrt\gamma .\]


\textbf{Proof.} \emph{Regularity of \(V\).} By the first step, \(V\) is \(m\)-strongly convex, and \(\nabla V\) is \((L_1+\eta^{-1})=L_\eta\)-Lipschitz; \(V\) is differentiable as a sum of differentiable functions.




\emph{Identification of the laws.} By the previous step (with \(L=L_\eta\)), \(\rho_n\) is the law of the \(n\)-th IPLA iterate for \(V\), with the same initialization and innovations.




\emph{The IPLA bound.} The IPLA estimate with sharp parameter concentration Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SharpIPLA-lean-2} applies to a differentiable \(m\)-strongly convex pair potential with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, \(m>0\), step \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L)\), a marginal maximizer and Gaussian innovations as above. Its hypotheses hold for \(V\) with \(L=L_\eta\), and its conclusion is the displayed inequality. In that estimate the three terms have the following origin: the lifted potential \(F_V\) is \(Nm\)-strongly convex with \(NL_\eta\)-Lipschitz gradient on a space of dimension \(d_\theta+Nd_x\), and the Gaussian Euler step with step \(\gamma/N\) contracts the Wasserstein distance to \(\pi_{N,V}\) by \(1-m\gamma\) per iteration up to a discretization error, which gives the second and third terms; the parameter marginal of \(\pi_{N,V}\) has second moment at most \(d_\theta/(Nm)\) about \(\theta^\star\), which gives the first term after projecting onto the parameter block. \(\square\)




The bound concerns the maximizer \(\theta^\star\) of the \emph{smoothed} marginal likelihood. The smoothing parameter enters through \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), both in the step restriction and in the discretization term: a smaller \(\eta\) forces a smaller step.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The theorem for the Moreau envelope}{The theorem for the Moreau envelope}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYIPLA-lean-4}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\), \(\eta>0\), \(L_1\ge0\), let \(g_1\) be differentiable with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let \(g_2\) be convex and bounded below on the (finite-dimensional) pair space. Let \(P_\eta\) be the proximal map of \(g_2\), \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), and let \(\rho_n\) be the law of the \(n\)-th MYIPLA iterate with proximal map \(P_\eta\), smoothing parameter \(\eta\), any step \(\gamma\in\mathbb R\), and square-integrable initialization and innovations. Then for every \(n\):



\begin{enumerate}


\item \(\rho_n\) is a probability measure;

\item \(\int\|\theta(z)-\vartheta\|^2\,\rho_n(dz)<\infty\) for every \(\vartheta\in\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\);

\item if in addition \(g_1\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\) with \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), \(\theta^\star_\eta\) is a marginal maximizer of \(V_\eta\), and the innovations are independent standard Gaussian and independent of the initialization, then
\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta^\star_\eta\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V_\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}N}\,\sqrt\gamma .\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} By the Moreau envelope section, under the stated hypotheses on \(g_2\) and \(\eta>0\): \(e=g_2^\eta\) is differentiable and convex, \(\nabla e=\eta^{-1}(I-P_\eta)\), and \(\nabla e\) is \(\eta^{-1}\)-Lipschitz (the residual is \(\eta^{-1}\)-Lipschitz). With \(P=P_\eta\) all hypotheses of the previous two steps that concern \(e\) and \(P\) are therefore satisfied, and \(\nabla(g_1+e)\) is \(L_\eta\)-Lipschitz by the first step.




(a) By the second step, \(\rho_n\) is the pushforward of the probability measure \(Q\) under a measurable map \(Y_n\), so \(\rho_n(\text{whole space})=Q(Y_n^{-1}(\text{whole space}))=Q(\Omega)=1\).




(b) is part (b) of the second step.




(c) is the previous step with \(e=g_2^\eta\) and \(P=P_\eta\). \(\square\)




All properties of the envelope used here are consequences of convexity and the lower bound of \(g_2\); none is assumed. Parts (a) and (b) need neither strong convexity, nor Gaussian innovations, nor a step restriction.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Changing the center of a projected quadratic error}{Changing the center of a projected quadratic error}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean}


Let \(\mu\) be a probability measure on a real normed space \(E\), \(T:E\to F\) a continuous linear map into a real normed space \(F\), and \(a\in F\). The projected quadratic error of \(\mu\) about the center \(a\) is


\[R_\mu(a)=\Bigl(\int_E\|Tx-a\|^2\,\mu(dx)\Bigr)^{1/2}\in[0,\infty],\]


with the convention \(R_\mu(a)=\infty\) when \(x\mapsto Tx\) is not measurable for \(\mu\). By Minkowski's inequality, \(R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|b-a\|\) for any two centers. Hence a finite error about one center implies a finite error about every center, and then the inequality holds between the ordinary square-root integrals. The result is used with \(T\) the projection onto the parameter block, to move the center from the smoothed marginal maximizer to the original one.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.48]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Minkowski's inequality for the change of center}{Minkowski's inequality for the change of center}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(\mu\) be a probability measure on \(E\), \(T:E\to F\) continuous and linear, and \(a,b\in F\). Then


\[R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|b-a\|\qquad\text{in }[0,\infty],\qquad(1)\]


and if \(R_\mu(b)<\infty\), then \(R_\mu(a)<\infty\).




\textbf{Proof.} For every \(x\), \(Tx-a=(Tx-b)+(b-a)\). Minkowski's inequality in \(L^2(\mu)\), which holds for functions with values in a normed space and with norms in \([0,\infty]\), gives


\[R_\mu(a)=\bigl\|(T\cdot-b)+(b-a)\bigr\|_{L^2(\mu)}\le\|T\cdot-b\|_{L^2(\mu)}+\|b-a\|_{L^2(\mu)},\]


where in the last term \(b-a\) denotes the constant function. Its \(L^2(\mu)\) norm is \(\bigl(\int\|b-a\|^2\,d\mu\bigr)^{1/2}=\|b-a\|\,\mu(E)^{1/2}=\|b-a\|\), because \(\mu(E)=1\). This proves (1). If \(R_\mu(b)<\infty\), the right-hand side of (1) is finite, hence so is \(R_\mu(a)\). If \(x\mapsto Tx\) is not measurable, both errors are infinite and (1) holds trivially. \(\square\)




For a finite measure of total mass \(M\) the constant term would be \(\sqrt M\,\|b-a\|\); the coefficient \(1\) uses \(M=1\). The inequality is proved before any finiteness is known, so finiteness about \(a\) is a conclusion, not a hypothesis.

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The quadratic error is \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}Hilbert.\allowbreak{}projected\allowbreak{}RMS \ensuremath{\mu} T b}, defined in the unadjusted Langevin development as \texttt{e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm (fun x \ensuremath{\mapsto} T x -\allowbreak{} b) 2 \ensuremath{\mu}}. Here \texttt{e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm f 2 \ensuremath{\mu}} is the \(L^2\) seminorm with values in \texttt{\ensuremath{\mathbb R}\ensuremath{\ge}0\ensuremath{\infty}} \(=[0,\infty]\); it equals \((\int^-\|f\|^2\,d\mu)^{1/2}\) for almost-everywhere strongly measurable \(f\) and \(\infty\) otherwise. The map \texttt{T : E \ensuremath{\to}L[\ensuremath{\mathbb R}] F} is a continuous linear map and \texttt{[Is\allowbreak{}Probability\allowbreak{}Measure \ensuremath{\mu}]} states \(\mu(E)=1\).




In \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMS\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{heq} is the identity of functions \(T\cdot-a=(T\cdot-b)+(b-a)\). After unfolding, \texttt{e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}le} with exponent \(2\ge1\) is Minkowski's inequality, and the \texttt{simpa} call evaluates the norm of the constant function with \texttt{e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm\_\allowbreak{}const'} (it equals \(\|c\|\,\mu(E)^{1/2}\)) and \texttt{of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}norm}, using \(\mu(E)=1\). \texttt{projected\allowbreak{}RMS\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}finite} applies \texttt{ne\_\allowbreak{}top\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}ne\_\allowbreak{}top} to (1): the right-hand side is finite because \texttt{hb} states \(R_\mu(b)\ne\infty\) and \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}ne\_\allowbreak{}top} states that \(\|b-a\|\) is finite.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.48.1]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The inequality for square-root integrals}{The inequality for square-root integrals}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(\mu\), \(T\), \(a,b\) be as in the previous step and assume \(R_\mu(b)<\infty\). Then both integrals below are finite and


\[\Bigl(\int\|Tx-a\|^2\,\mu(dx)\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\Bigl(\int\|Tx-b\|^2\,\mu(dx)\Bigr)^{1/2}+\|b-a\| .\]


\textbf{Proof.} By the previous step \(R_\mu(a)<\infty\) as well. The inequality (1) of the previous step is then an inequality between finite nonnegative numbers, hence an inequality of real numbers \(R_\mu(a)\le R_\mu(b)+\|b-a\|\). Since \(R_\mu(a)\) and \(R_\mu(b)\) are finite, the functions \(x\mapsto\|Tx-a\|^2\) and \(x\mapsto\|Tx-b\|^2\) are integrable and \(R_\mu(c)=\bigl(\int\|Tx-c\|^2\,d\mu\bigr)^{1/2}\) for \(c=a,b\). \(\square\)




The finiteness hypothesis is necessary for the statement to have its intended meaning: if the second moment about \(b\) were infinite, the integrals would not be finite and the displayed real numbers would not represent the quadratic errors.

\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.48.1]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.48.2]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.48.7]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\section{\texorpdfstring{MYIPLA about the marginal maximizer of the original nonsmooth model}{MYIPLA about the marginal maximizer of the original nonsmooth model}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean}


Let \(U=g_1+g_2\) be a pair potential on \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times\mathbb R^{d_x}\), where \(g_1\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient (\(m>0\)), and \(g_2\) is convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz. For \(\eta>0\) let \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\) be the smoothed potential and \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). The Moreau gap gives the two-sided comparison \(V_\eta\le U\le V_\eta+\eta K^2/2\). From it we derive that the original model has a marginal maximizer \(\theta_U\), i.e. a maximizer of \(\theta\mapsto\int e^{-U(\theta,x)}\,dx\), and that \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\) for every marginal maximizer \(\theta_\eta\) of the smoothed model.




Combined with the MYIPLA estimate of the previous section and Minkowski's inequality, this gives the main bound for MYIPLA relative to the original model: under Gaussian innovations independent of a square-integrable initialization and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\),


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V_\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}N}\,\sqrt\gamma .\]


The smoothing contribution is of order \(\sqrt\eta\). This replaces the smoothing term of Theorem 4.1 of arXiv:2406.14292v3, which was derived from a Taylor expansion and is of linear order in \(\eta\); the linear order is not established here. The discretization term depends on \(\eta\) through \(L_\eta\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The uniform comparison and existence of the original marginal maximizer}{The uniform comparison and existence of the original marginal maximizer}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} (a) Let \(E\) be a finite-dimensional real inner-product space, \(g_1:E\to\mathbb R\) any function, \(g_2:E\to\mathbb R\) convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz (\(K\ge0\)), and \(\eta>0\). Then for every \(z\in E\)


\[V_\eta(z)\le g_1(z)+g_2(z)\le V_\eta(z)+\frac{\eta K^2}{2},\qquad V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta.\qquad(1)\]


(b) On the pair space, let in addition \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\). Then \(U=g_1+g_2\) has a marginal maximizer: there is \(\theta_U\in\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\) with \(k_U(\theta)\le k_U(\theta_U)\) for all \(\theta\), where \(k_U(\theta)=\int_{\mathbb R^{d_x}}e^{-U(\theta,x)}\,dx\).




\textbf{Proof.} (a) By the Moreau envelope section, \(0\le g_2(z)-g_2^\eta(z)\le\eta K^2/2\). Adding \(g_1(z)\) to the three members of \(g_2^\eta(z)\le g_2(z)\le g_2^\eta(z)+\eta K^2/2\) gives (1).




(b) Fix any \(\eta>0\). The function \(U\) is continuous, because \(g_1\) is differentiable and \(g_2\) is Lipschitz. The comparison potential \(V_\eta\) is differentiable (sum of \(g_1\) and the differentiable envelope) and \(m\)-strongly convex (sum of \(g_1\) and the convex envelope), and \(V_\eta\le U\) by (1). These are exactly the hypotheses of the existence theorem for the original marginal maximizer Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-3}, which proves (b): the bound \(e^{-U}\le e^{-V_\eta}\) and a Gaussian majorant of \(e^{-V_\eta}\) make \(k_U\) finite, positive, continuous and tending to \(0\) as \(\|\theta\|\to\infty\), so \(k_U\) attains its maximum. \(\square\)




The smoothing parameter in (b) is auxiliary; any \(\eta>0\) provides the required smooth lower comparison potential.

The proof takes \texttt{h := moreau\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz hg\ensuremath{{}_{2}} hb h\ensuremath{\eta} h\allowbreak{}K z}, unfolds the smoothed potential, and proves both inequalities with \texttt{constructor <;> linarith}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The distance between the original and the smoothed marginal maximizers}{The distance between the original and the smoothed marginal maximizers}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(m>0\), let \(g_2\) be convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, and \(\eta>0\). If \(\theta_U\) is a marginal maximizer of \(U=g_1+g_2\) and \(\theta_\eta\) is a marginal maximizer of \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), then


\[\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}} .\]


\textbf{Proof.} By (1) of the previous step, \(V_\eta\le U\le V_\eta+\delta\) with \(\delta=\eta K^2/2\). As in the previous step, \(U\) is continuous and \(V_\eta\) is differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex. The marginal-center comparison Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Perturbation-lean-4} states that, under exactly these conditions, any marginal maximizers satisfy \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{2\delta/m}\). Its proof compares the marginal potentials \(\varphi_W=-\log k_W\): integrating \(e^{-V_\eta-\delta}\le e^{-U}\le e^{-V_\eta}\) over the latent variable gives \(\varphi_{V_\eta}\le\varphi_U\le\varphi_{V_\eta}+\delta\), the marginal potential \(\varphi_{V_\eta}\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with minimizer \(\theta_\eta\), and therefore


\[\frac m2\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|^2\le\varphi_{V_\eta}(\theta_U)-\varphi_{V_\eta}(\theta_\eta)\le\varphi_U(\theta_U)-\varphi_{V_\eta}(\theta_\eta)\le\varphi_U(\theta_\eta)-\varphi_{V_\eta}(\theta_\eta)\le\delta .\]


With \(\delta=\eta K^2/2\), \(2\delta/m=\eta K^2/m\). \(\square\)




The bound is of order \(\sqrt\eta\). No second derivative of \(U\) and no Taylor expansion of the envelope are used.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The MYIPLA error about the original marginal maximizer}{The MYIPLA error about the original marginal maximizer}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Theorem-lean-3}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \((\Omega,Q)\) be a probability space and \(N\ge1\). Let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and \(g_2\) convex, bounded below and \(K\)-Lipschitz, with \(L_1,K\ge0\). Let \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), and


\[\gamma\le\frac{2}{m+L_\eta}.\]


Let \(\theta_U\) be a marginal maximizer of \(U=g_1+g_2\) and \(\theta_\eta\) one of \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\). Let the initialization \(Z_0\) and innovations \(\xi_0,\xi_1,\dots\) be square-integrable random states such that all innovation coordinates are independent standard Gaussian and \(Z_0\) is independent of the innovation sequence. Let \(\rho_n\) be the law of the \(n\)-th MYIPLA iterate with proximal map \(P_\eta\) of \(g_2\), and \(\theta_n\) its parameter block. Then for every \(n\ge0\)


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V_\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}N}\,\sqrt\gamma .\]


\textbf{Proof.} \emph{Finiteness.} By the previous section, \(\rho_n\) is a probability measure and \(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2=\int\|\theta(z)-\theta_\eta\|^2\rho_n(dz)<\infty\).




\emph{Change of center.} By Minkowski's inequality in \(L^2(\rho_n)\) applied to \(\theta(z)-\theta_U=(\theta(z)-\theta_\eta)+(\theta_\eta-\theta_U)\), in the form proved in Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProjectionCenter-lean-2} (which requires the finite moment just established),


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_U\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}+\|\theta_\eta-\theta_U\|.\qquad(1)\]


\emph{The two terms.} By the previous step, \(\|\theta_\eta-\theta_U\|=\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\). By the MYIPLA estimate for the Moreau envelope of the previous section, applied with the smoothed marginal maximizer \(\theta_\eta\),


\[\Bigl(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\eta\|^2\Bigr)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi_{N,V_\eta})+\frac{33}{20}\,\frac{L_\eta}{m}\sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}N}\,\sqrt\gamma .\]


Inserting both bounds into (1) proves the claim. \(\square\)




The four terms are the smoothing bias, the equilibrium spread of the parameter marginal, the contracted initial transport distance, and the discretization error. Decreasing \(\eta\) decreases the first term but increases \(L_\eta\), hence the last term and the restriction on \(\gamma\). The existence of \(\theta_U\) was proved in the first step, and a marginal maximizer \(\theta_\eta\) of the smooth strongly convex potential \(V_\eta\) exists by \cite[Section~3.9.4]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; the theorem itself takes both points as given maximizers.

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}original} has the hypotheses of \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms} together with \texttt{h\allowbreak{}K : Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With K g\ensuremath{{}_{2}}}, the two points \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta}\allowbreak{}U \ensuremath{\theta}\ensuremath{\eta}} and their maximizer properties \texttt{hmax\allowbreak{}U} and \texttt{hmax\ensuremath{\eta}}. The law is \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws Q g\ensuremath{{}_{1}} (prox g\ensuremath{{}_{2}} \ensuremath{\eta}) \ensuremath{\eta} \ensuremath{\gamma} initial (fun k \ensuremath{\mapsto} noise k) n}, named \texttt{\ensuremath{\rho}} in the proof. The proof follows the text.



\begin{enumerate}


\item The instance \texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Probability\allowbreak{}Measure \ensuremath{\rho}} comes from \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Probability}, and \texttt{hmem}, the finite second moment about \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta}\ensuremath{\eta}}, from \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}mem\allowbreak{}Lp}.

\item \texttt{hmove} is (1): \texttt{sqrt\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}le} with the projection \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}parameter\allowbreak{}CLM}, centers \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta}\allowbreak{}U} and \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta}\ensuremath{\eta}}, and the finiteness \texttt{hmem.\allowbreak{}e\allowbreak{}Lp\allowbreak{}Norm\_\allowbreak{}ne\_\allowbreak{}top}; \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}rev} rewrites \(\|\theta_\eta-\theta_U\|\) as \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\).

\item \texttt{hdist} is \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}marginal\_\allowbreak{}center\_\allowbreak{}distance} and \texttt{hbound} is \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms} at the center \texttt{\ensuremath{\theta}\ensuremath{\eta}}.

\item \texttt{linarith} adds \texttt{hmove}, \texttt{hdist} and \texttt{hbound}.

\end{enumerate}



The source docstring records the comparison with Theorem 4.1 of arXiv:2406.14292v3: the present theorem is a finite-\(\eta\) statement whose smoothing term is \(\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\) and whose discretization constant depends explicitly on \(\eta\); it has no interpolation, entropy-production or convergence hypotheses.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{A distance–energy inequality for one proximal step}{A distance–energy inequality for one proximal step}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProximalEnergy-lean}


Let \(E\) be a real inner-product space, \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) convex, \(\eta>0\) and \(x\in E\), and let \(p\) be a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\) with parameter \(\eta\), i.e. a global minimizer of \(f(\cdot)+\|\cdot-x\|^2/(2\eta)\). If \(s\) is a subgradient of \(f\) at the input point \(x\), then for every comparison point \(a\in E\)


\[\|p-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta\bigl(f(x)-f(a)\bigr)+\eta^2\|s\|^2.\]


Thus one proximal step decreases the squared distance to any point \(a\) by \(2\eta\) times the energy excess \(f(x)-f(a)\), up to the error \(\eta^2\|s\|^2\). This is the deterministic inequality used in Lemma A.8 of Cordero Encinar, Crucinio and Akyildiz (arXiv:2406.14292v3); a bound \(\|s\|^2\le C\) turns the error into \(\eta^2C\). Any subgradient at \(x\) may be used. The inequality is applied pairwise to the particle updates in the next section and, with a random comparison point, in the energy estimate of the splitting scheme.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The energy inequality for an arbitrary proximal point}{The energy inequality for an arbitrary proximal point}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProximalEnergy-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be convex, \(\eta>0\), \(x,p,s,a\in E\). Assume that \(p\) is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\) with parameter \(\eta\) and that \(s\in\partial f(x)\), i.e. \(f(x)+\langle s,y-x\rangle\le f(y)\) for every \(y\in E\). Then


\[\|p-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta\bigl(f(x)-f(a)\bigr)+\eta^2\|s\|^2.\]


\textbf{Proof.} Put \(d=x-p\). Four facts are combined.




\emph{Support at the output.} Since \(f\) is convex and \(p\) is a proximal point, the residual \(\eta^{-1}d\) is a subgradient of \(f\) at \(p\). Testing it at \(a\) gives \(f(p)+\eta^{-1}\langle d,a-p\rangle\le f(a)\); multiplying by \(\eta>0\) and using \(a-p=-(p-a)\),


\[\eta f(p)-\langle d,p-a\rangle\le\eta f(a).\qquad(1)\]


\emph{Support at the input.} Testing \(s\in\partial f(x)\) at \(y=p\) and using \(p-x=-d\),


\[f(x)-\langle s,d\rangle\le f(p).\qquad(2)\]


\emph{Expansion of the distance.} Since \(x-a=d+(p-a)\),


\[\|x-a\|^2=\|d\|^2+2\langle d,p-a\rangle+\|p-a\|^2.\qquad(3)\]


\emph{A completed square.} Expanding \(\|d-\eta s\|^2\ge0\),


\[2\eta\langle s,d\rangle-\|d\|^2\le\eta^2\|s\|^2.\qquad(4)\]


Now, by (3), then (1) multiplied by \(2\), then (2) multiplied by \(2\eta>0\), and finally (4),


\[\begin{aligned}
\|p-a\|^2&=\|x-a\|^2-\|d\|^2-2\langle d,p-a\rangle\\
&\le\|x-a\|^2-\|d\|^2+2\eta\bigl(f(a)-f(p)\bigr)\\
&\le\|x-a\|^2-\|d\|^2+2\eta\bigl(f(a)-f(x)\bigr)+2\eta\langle s,d\rangle\\
&\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta\bigl(f(x)-f(a)\bigr)+\eta^2\|s\|^2. \qquad\square
\end{aligned}\]


Convexity enters only through the residual subgradient (1), and the subgradient \(s\) at the input only through (2) and (4). The point \(a\) is arbitrary and need not be related to \(x\) or \(p\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}}
\leansource{\texttt{isProxPoint\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ProximalEnergy.lean, lines 23–42}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ isProxPoint\_energy\_le\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{\{η\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hη\ :\ 0\ <\ η)\ \{x\ p\ s\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hp\ :\ IsProxPoint\ f\ η\ x\ p)\ (hs\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ x\ s)\ (a\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖p\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ ‖x\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -\ 2\ *\ η\ *\ (f\ x\ -\ f\ a)\ +\ η\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ ‖s‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le} has the hypotheses \texttt{hf : Convex\allowbreak{}On \ensuremath{\mathbb R} univ f}, \texttt{h\ensuremath{\eta} : 0 < \ensuremath{\eta}}, \texttt{hp : Is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point f \ensuremath{\eta} x p} and \texttt{hs : Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient f x s}, and an arbitrary comparison point \texttt{a : E}; the space need not be finite-dimensional.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{hres} is the residual support \texttt{subgradient\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point hf h\ensuremath{\eta} hp} tested at \texttt{a}; \texttt{hneg} and the simplification with \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}left} and \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}right} put it in the form of (1) divided by \(\eta\). \texttt{hscaled} multiplies it by \(\eta\) (\texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}left}), and \texttt{hinv}, \(\eta\eta^{-1}=1\) (\texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}inv\_\allowbreak{}cancel\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}), cancels the factor; the result is (1).

\item \texttt{hsub := hs p}, rewritten with \(p-x=-(x-p)\) and \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}right}, is (2).

\item \texttt{hdist} is (3), from \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real} after writing \(x-a=(x-p)+(p-a)\).

\item \texttt{hyoung} is \(\|(x-p)-\eta s\|^2\ge0\) expanded with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul}, \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos h\ensuremath{\eta}}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}right} and \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}comm}, which is (4).

\item \texttt{nlinarith}, supplied with (2) multiplied by \(\eta\), performs the final chain of inequalities.

\end{enumerate}
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The inequality for the proximal map and under a subgradient bound}{The inequality for the proximal map and under a subgradient bound}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProximalEnergy-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(E\) be finite-dimensional, \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) convex and bounded below, \(\eta>0\), \(x,s,a\in E\) with \(s\in\partial f(x)\), and let \(P_\eta\) be the proximal map of \(f\) with parameter \(\eta\). Then


\[\|P_\eta(x)-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta\bigl(f(x)-f(a)\bigr)+\eta^2\|s\|^2.\qquad(1)\]


If moreover \(\|s\|^2\le C\) for a real number \(C\), then


\[\|P_\eta(x)-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\eta\bigl(f(x)-f(a)\bigr)+\eta^2C.\qquad(2)\]


\textbf{Proof.} Under these hypotheses \(P_\eta(x)\) is a proximal point of \(f\) at \(x\) (existence of proximal points), so (1) is the previous step with \(p=P_\eta(x)\). Since \(\eta^2\ge0\), \(\|s\|^2\le C\) implies \(\eta^2\|s\|^2\le\eta^2C\), and (2) follows from (1). \(\square\)




A numerical bound on \(\|s\|^2\) controls only the last term; \(s\) must still be a subgradient at \(x\). For a convex \(K\)-Lipschitz function every subgradient satisfies \(\|s\|\le K\), so (2) holds with \(C=K^2\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient\_\allowbreak{}bound}}

\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b\ :\ ℝ,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{\{η\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hη\ :\ 0\ <\ η)\ \{x\ s\ :\ E\}\ (hs\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ x\ s)\ (a\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖prox\ f\ η\ x\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{‖x\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -\ 2\ *\ η\ *\ (f\ x\ -\ f\ a)\ +\ η\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ ‖s‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient\_\allowbreak{}bound} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ProximalEnergy.lean, lines 54–60}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_energy\_le\_of\_subgradient\_bound\ [FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b\ :\ ℝ,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{\{η\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hη\ :\ 0\ <\ η)\ \{x\ s\ :\ E\}\ (hs\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ x\ s)}
\leanline{4}{\{C\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hC\ :\ ‖s‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ C)\ (a\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖prox\ f\ η\ x\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{‖x\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -\ 2\ *\ η\ *\ (f\ x\ -\ f\ a)\ +\ η\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ C}

\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le} adds \texttt{[Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E]} and the lower bound \texttt{hb : \ensuremath{\exists} b : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}, \ensuremath{\forall} z, b \ensuremath{\le} f z}, which are the hypotheses of \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point}; its proof is the one-line application \texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le hf h\ensuremath{\eta} (prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point hf hb h\ensuremath{\eta} x) hs a}. \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient\_\allowbreak{}bound} adds \texttt{h\allowbreak{}C : \ensuremath{\Vert}s\ensuremath{\Vert} \textasciicircum{} 2 \ensuremath{\le} C}; it chains \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le} with the monotonicity step \(\eta^2\|s\|^2\le\eta^2C\), which \texttt{gcongr} proves from \texttt{h\allowbreak{}C} and \(\eta^2\ge0\).




The last remark of the mathematical text (subgradients of a \(K\)-Lipschitz convex function have norm at most \(K\)) is not part of these statements; the bounds on subgradients that are actually used are proved where they are needed.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{The energy inequality for averaged pairwise proximal outputs}{The energy inequality for averaged pairwise proximal outputs}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleEnergy-lean}


Fix \(N\ge1\) particles and dimensions \(d_\theta,d_x\). The pair space is \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times\mathbb R^{d_x}\), and the state space is \(\mathbb R^{d_\theta}\times(\mathbb R^{d_x})^N\) with points \(z=(\theta,z_1,\dots,z_N)\), Euclidean norm \(\|z\|^2=\|\theta\|^2+\sum_i\|z_i\|^2\), and physical particles \(x_i=\sqrt N z_i\). The \(i\)-th pair of a state is \(J_iz=(\theta,\sqrt Nz_i)\), and the lifted potential of a pair function \(g\) is \(F_g(z)=\sum_{i=1}^Ng(J_iz)\). Given any map \(P\) of the pair space, the \emph{pairwise map} \(\mathcal P\) applies \(P\) to each pair, averages the \(N\) parameter outputs and rescales the latent outputs:


\[(\mathcal Pz)_\theta=\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_\theta,\qquad(\mathcal Pz)_i=\frac{1}{\sqrt N}\bigl[P(J_iz)\bigr]_x .\]


This section proves \(\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\le N^{-1}\sum_i\|P(J_iz)-J_ia\|^2\) for every \(P\) (Jensen's inequality for the averaged parameter, exact scaling for the latent blocks), and combines it with the single-pair energy inequality of the previous section. For \(P=P_\lambda\), the proximal map of a convex, bounded-below pair function \(g\) with bounded subgradients, the result is


\[\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\le\|z-a\|^2-\frac{2\lambda}{N}\bigl(F_g(z)-F_g(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2C.\]


The averaged output is in general not a proximal point of \(F_g\) on the state space, so this inequality is proved directly; it is the deterministic energy estimate used for the PIPGLA splitting scheme.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Jensen's inequality for the averaged parameter}{Jensen's inequality for the averaged parameter}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ParticleEnergy-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item For real numbers \(b_1,\dots,b_N\),
\[N\Bigl(\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^Nb_i\Bigr)^2\le\sum_{i=1}^Nb_i^2.\qquad(1)\]

\item For every map \(P\) from the pair space to itself and all states \(z,a\),
\[\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl\|P(J_iz)-J_ia\bigr\|^2.\qquad(2)\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in \(\mathbb R^N\) applied to \((1,\dots,1)\) and \((b_1,\dots,b_N)\), \(\bigl(\sum_ib_i\bigr)^2\le N\sum_ib_i^2\). Dividing by \(N>0\) gives \(N\bigl(N^{-1}\sum_ib_i\bigr)^2=N^{-1}\bigl(\sum_ib_i\bigr)^2\le\sum_ib_i^2\).




(b) Write \(p_i=P(J_iz)=(p_{i,\theta},p_{i,x})\) and \(a=(a_\theta,a_1,\dots,a_N)\), so that \(J_ia=(a_\theta,\sqrt Na_i)\). By the definition of the Euclidean norm on the state space,


\[N\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2=N\Bigl\|\frac1N\sum_ip_{i,\theta}-a_\theta\Bigr\|^2+\sum_{i=1}^NN\Bigl\|\frac{1}{\sqrt N}p_{i,x}-a_i\Bigr\|^2.\qquad(3)\]


\emph{Parameter block.} For each parameter coordinate \(j\), the common comparison value can be moved inside the average: \(N^{-1}\sum_ip_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}=N^{-1}\sum_i(p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j})\). By (1) with \(b_i=p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\),


\[N\Bigl(\frac1N\sum_ip_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\Bigr)^2\le\sum_i\bigl(p_{i,\theta,j}-a_{\theta,j}\bigr)^2 .\]


Summing over \(j\) gives \(N\|N^{-1}\sum_ip_{i,\theta}-a_\theta\|^2\le\sum_i\|p_{i,\theta}-a_\theta\|^2\).




\emph{Latent blocks.} For each \(i\) and each latent coordinate \(j\), \(\sqrt N\bigl(N^{-1/2}p_{i,x,j}-a_{i,j}\bigr)=p_{i,x,j}-\sqrt Na_{i,j}\); squaring and using \((\sqrt N)^2=N\),


\[N\bigl(N^{-1/2}p_{i,x,j}-a_{i,j}\bigr)^2=\bigl(p_{i,x,j}-\sqrt Na_{i,j}\bigr)^2 .\]


Summing over \(j\) gives \(N\|N^{-1/2}p_{i,x}-a_i\|^2=\|p_{i,x}-\sqrt Na_i\|^2\), an equality.




Inserting both into (3),


\[N\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\le\sum_{i=1}^N\Bigl(\|p_{i,\theta}-a_\theta\|^2+\|p_{i,x}-\sqrt Na_i\|^2\Bigr)=\sum_{i=1}^N\|p_i-J_ia\|^2,\]


and dividing by \(N>0\) gives (2). \(\square\)




Only (1) is an inequality; the latent contributions are equalities. No property of \(P\) is used.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{pairwiseMap\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le}}
\leansource{\texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ParticleEnergy.lean, lines 22–29}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ average\_sq\_le\ (N\ :\ ℕ)\ (hN\ :\ 0\ <\ N)\ (b\ :\ Fin\ N\ →\ ℝ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(N\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ ((N\ :\ ℝ)⁻¹\ *\ ∑\ i,\ b\ i)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ ∑\ i,\ (b\ i)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{pairwiseMap\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ParticleEnergy.lean, lines 33–77}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ pairwiseMap\_sq\_dist\_le\ \{N\ dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}\ (hN\ :\ 0\ <\ N)}
\leanline{4}{(P\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)}
\leanline{4}{(z\ a\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖pairwiseMap\ P\ z\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{(∑\ i\ :\ Fin\ N,\ ‖P\ (IPLA.joint\ i\ z)\ -\ IPLA.joint\ i\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ /\ N}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The state space is \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}State N d\ensuremath{\theta} dx}, the Euclidean space \texttt{Euclidean\allowbreak{}Space \ensuremath{\mathbb R} (Fin d\ensuremath{\theta} ⊕ (Fin N \ensuremath{\times} Fin dx))} whose coordinates are indexed by parameter indices \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inl j} and latent indices \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inr (i, j)}; the pair space is \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}Joint d\ensuremath{\theta} dx}, with indices \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inl j} and \texttt{Sum.\allowbreak{}inr j}. \texttt{IPLA.\allowbreak{}joint i z} is \(J_iz\) (its latent coordinates are \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt N * z (Sum.\allowbreak{}inr (i, j))}), and \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map P z} is \(\mathcal Pz\); its coordinates are given by the definitional lemmas \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}parameter} and \texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}particle} of the previous section. The hypothesis \texttt{h\allowbreak{}N : 0 < N} is \(N\ge1\).




\texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le} is (1). It applies the Mathlib inequality \texttt{sum\_\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}card\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}card}, \(\bigl(\sum_ib_i/N\bigr)^2\le\sum_ib_i^2/N\) over \texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}univ} (a form of the Cauchy–Schwarz or Jensen inequality), multiplies it by \(N>0\) and simplifies.




\texttt{pairwise\allowbreak{}Map\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le} is (2), for an arbitrary map \texttt{P}.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{h\ensuremath{\theta} j} is the parameter inequality for coordinate \(j\); its \texttt{hid} moves the common comparison value inside the average (\texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}distrib}, \texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}const}), and \texttt{average\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le} finishes.

\item \texttt{hx i j} is the latent equality; \texttt{hid} is \(\sqrt N(N^{-1/2}p-a)=p-\sqrt Na\), squared with \texttt{congr\allowbreak{}Arg} and simplified with \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}pow} and \texttt{hs2} \((\sqrt N)^2=N\).

\item The goal is multiplied by \(N\) (\texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}iff\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}); \texttt{Euclidean\allowbreak{}Space.\allowbreak{}real\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}eq} writes the squared norms as coordinate sums, \texttt{Fintype.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}type} and \texttt{Fintype.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}prod\_\allowbreak{}type} split them into the parameter block and the double sum over particles and latent coordinates, which is (3). After \texttt{simp\_\allowbreak{}rw [hx]} the latent parts coincide, \texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}comm} exchanges the order of summation in the parameter part, and \texttt{add\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}add} with \texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}sum} applies \texttt{h\ensuremath{\theta}} coordinatewise.

\end{enumerate}
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Summing the pairwise proximal energy inequalities}{Summing the pairwise proximal energy inequalities}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ParticleEnergy-lean-2}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(N\ge1\), let \(g\) be a convex function on the pair space that is bounded below, let \(\lambda>0\), let \(z,a\) be states and \(C\in\mathbb R\). Assume that for every \(i\) there is a subgradient \(s_i\) of \(g\) at \(J_iz\) with \(\|s_i\|^2\le C\). Let \(\mathcal P\) be the pairwise map built from the proximal map \(P_\lambda\) of \(g\). Then


\[\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\le\|z-a\|^2-\frac{2\lambda}{N}\bigl(F_g(z)-F_g(a)\bigr)+\lambda^2C .\]


\textbf{Proof.} \emph{Pairwise inequalities.} For each \(i\), the energy inequality of the previous section (convexity and lower bound of \(g\), \(\lambda>0\), subgradient \(s_i\) at the input \(J_iz\) with \(\|s_i\|^2\le C\), comparison point \(J_ia\)) gives


\[\|P_\lambda(J_iz)-J_ia\|^2\le\|J_iz-J_ia\|^2-2\lambda\bigl(g(J_iz)-g(J_ia)\bigr)+\lambda^2C.\qquad(1)\]


\emph{The norm identity.} Each \(J_i\) is linear, so \(J_iz-J_ia=J_i(z-a)\). For any state \(w=(w_\theta,w_1,\dots,w_N)\),


\[\sum_{i=1}^N\|J_iw\|^2=\sum_{i=1}^N\bigl(\|w_\theta\|^2+N\|w_i\|^2\bigr)=N\|w_\theta\|^2+N\sum_{i=1}^N\|w_i\|^2=N\|w\|^2,\qquad(2)\]


because the parameter block occurs in each of the \(N\) pairs and each latent block occurs in one pair, multiplied by \(\sqrt N\).




\emph{Summation.} Summing (1) over \(i\) and using (2) with \(w=z-a\) and \(\sum_ig(J_iz)=F_g(z)\),


\[\sum_{i=1}^N\|P_\lambda(J_iz)-J_ia\|^2\le N\|z-a\|^2-2\lambda\bigl(F_g(z)-F_g(a)\bigr)+N\lambda^2C .\]


By (2) of the previous step applied to \(P=P_\lambda\), the left-hand side divided by \(N\) bounds \(\|\mathcal Pz-a\|^2\). Dividing the display by \(N>0\) proves the claim. \(\square\)




The minimizing property of \(P_\lambda\) is used only pair by pair, in (1). The averaged output \(\mathcal Pz\) is not, in general, a minimizer of \(w\mapsto F_g(w)+N\|w-z\|^2/(2\lambda)\) on the state space; an explicit two-particle example is given in Section~\ref{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean-4}. The inequality of this step is therefore proved for the averaged map itself. A subgradient of minimal norm, when available, is one admissible choice of \(s_i\).

\leanline{4}{(z\ a\ :\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ (C\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(hsub\ :\ ∀\ i\ :\ Fin\ N,\ ∃\ s\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx,}
\leanline{6}{IsSubgradient\ g\ (IPLA.joint\ i\ z)\ s\ ∧\ ‖s‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ C)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖pairwiseMap\ (prox\ g\ lam)\ z\ -\ a‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}{Reference drift moments uniform over smoothing scales}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean}


For \(F=f+e\), assume \(F\) is \(m\)-strongly convex, \(f,e\) are differentiable, \(\nabla f\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, and \(\|\nabla e\|\le K\). Sharp Gibbs concentration and the gradient identity at a global minimizer bound the smooth drift’s second moment using \(L,m,K\). Applied to a Lipschitz convex penalty and its envelope, the gradient-magnitude bound is uniform in the smoothing parameter and avoids its inverse-scale gradient Lipschitz constant.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.28]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Value Lipschitzness of the envelope}{Value Lipschitzness of the envelope}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean-1}


Let \(p=P_\lambda(y)\). At input \(x\) use competitor \(p+x-y\); its quadratic penalty equals that of \(p\) at \(y\). The \(K\)-Lipschitz bound on \(f\) changes its function value by at most \(K\|x-y\|\). Hence \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\), and exchanging \(x,y\) proves the absolute-value bound.



Explicitly the minimizer \(p=P_\lambda(y)\) supplies a competitor at \(x\) given by \(p+(x-y)\). Identity \(p+(x-y)-x=p-y\) makes the penalties equal, and the function increment is at most \(K\|x-y\|\). This gives \(f^\lambda(x)\le f^\lambda(y)+K\|x-y\|\). The inherited value Lipschitz constant is independent of \(\lambda\), unlike the gradient Lipschitz constant \(1/\lambda\). The next moment argument uses gradient magnitude rather than gradient variation.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 24–45}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_lipschitz\_of\_lipschitz\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)}
\leanline{4}{(hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)\ \{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)}
\leanline{4}{\{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ (moreau\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: moreau\_lipschitz\_of\_lipschitz : A Lipschitz potential has a Moreau envelope with the same Lipschitz constant.



\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{p}, \texttt{hmin}, \texttt{hdist0}, \texttt{hnorm}, \texttt{hdist}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With.\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}eq}, \texttt{dist\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{add\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}cancel\_\allowbreak{}left}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The total-potential minimizer and its smooth gradient}{The total-potential minimizer and its smooth gradient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean-2}


Positive strong convexity gives a coercive quadratic lower bound for \(F\). The radius \(2\|\nabla F(0)\|/m+1\) is large enough that the potential exceeds a fixed competitor outside the ball. Continuity and compactness give a global minimizer \(z\). Differentiability at that minimizer implies \(\nabla f(z)=-\nabla e(z)\) and thus \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\).



The vanishing total gradient is \(\nabla(f+e)=0\). The smooth gradient is therefore the negative perturbation gradient at this minimizing point, which gives \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\). Lipschitz growth of the smooth gradient can now be measured from this point and integrated using concentration of the total Gibbs law.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}, \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strongConvex\_\allowbreak{}minimum}, \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}smoothPart\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 48–52}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ gradient\_eq\_zero\_at\_minimum\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{z\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hz\ :\ ∀\ x,\ f\ z\ ≤\ f\ x)\ :\ gradient\ f\ z\ =\ 0}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strongConvex\_\allowbreak{}minimum} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 55–79}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ exists\_strongConvex\_minimum\ (f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ)\ (m\ :\ ℝ)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ f)\ (hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ univ\ m\ f)\ :}
\leanline{4}{∃\ z,\ ∀\ x,\ f\ z\ ≤\ f\ x}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}smoothPart\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 82–90}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ gradient\_smoothPart\_at\_minimum\ \{f\ e\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ f)\ (he\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ e)}
\leanline{4}{\{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hb\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖gradient\ e\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ))\ \{z\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hz\ :\ ∀\ x,\ f\ z\ +\ e\ z\ ≤\ f\ x\ +\ e\ x)\ :\ ‖gradient\ f\ z‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The strong-convexity lower bound forces the total potential above its value at zero outside a finite ball. A minimizer on the compact ball is therefore global; this is the radius construction in \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex\_\allowbreak{}minimum}. At a global minimizer of \(F=f+e\), the derivative vanishes, giving \(\nabla f(z)=-\nabla e(z)\). Thus a pointwise bound \(\|\nabla e\|\le K\) yields \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\). This is the only value of the smooth score at a specific point needed in the moment estimate.



Formal correspondence: gradient\_eq\_zero\_at\_minimum : A differentiable function has zero gradient at an actual global minimizer. exists\_strongConvex\_minimum : A smooth strongly convex real-valued function attains its minimum. gradient\_smoothPart\_at\_minimum : At a minimum of the sum, a bounded second gradient bounds the first gradient.



\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hmin}, \texttt{hlocal}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{hlocal.\allowbreak{}fderiv\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero}, \texttt{map\_\allowbreak{}zero}.



\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: the cases are separated by \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hx : x \ensuremath{\in} closed\allowbreak{}Ball (0 : E) R}; the intermediate facts include \texttt{R}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}R}, \texttt{hz0}, \texttt{hdiv}, \texttt{hnonneg}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{hz0.\allowbreak{}trans}, \texttt{mem\_\allowbreak{}closed\allowbreak{}Ball}, \texttt{not\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}nonneg\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos\_\allowbreak{}right}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}, \texttt{nlinarith}.



\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}smooth\allowbreak{}Part\_\allowbreak{}at\_\allowbreak{}minimum}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hg}, \texttt{hneg}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}differentiable}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}neg}.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.26.5]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The second moment of a bounded gradient}{The second moment of a bounded gradient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean-3}


The derivative of a differentiable \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function has operator norm at most \(K\), as follows by limits of directional difference quotients. Its gradient has the same norm. Thus a pointwise bound \(\|b(x)\|\le K\) implies \(\|b(Y)\|_{L^2}\le K\) on any probability space.



Indeed for value Lipschitz constant \(K\), every unit-direction difference quotient has absolute value at most \(K\). Taking derivatives and then the supremum over unit directions bounds the gradient. Integration gives \(\mathbb E\|b(Y)\|^2\le K^2\). Neither dimension nor an inverse smoothing scale occurs in this estimate.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, \texttt{lpDrift\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded}}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 93–98}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ gradient\_norm\_le\_of\_lipschitz\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}}
\leanline{4}{(hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :\ ‖gradient\ f\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{lpDrift\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 103–111}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ lpDrift\_norm\_le\_of\_bounded\ \{b\ :\ E\ →\ E\}\ \{L\ K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}}
\leanline{4}{(hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ b)\ (hb\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖b\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ))\ (Y\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖ULA.lpDrift\ P\ b\ hLip\ Y‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The derivative of a globally \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function has operator norm at most \(K\), and the Riesz identification preserves this norm, giving \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le K\). Separately, if \(\|b(x)\|\le K\) for every input, then the representative equality for \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift} gives \(\|b(Y(\omega))\|\le K\) almost everywhere. Since \(P\) has mass one, its \(L^2\) norm is at most \(K\). This explains why a state-space uniform bound transfers without a dimension or measure-mass factor.



Formal correspondence: gradient\_norm\_le\_of\_lipschitz : Global Lipschitzness bounds the actual gradient norm. lpDrift\_norm\_le\_of\_bounded : A pointwise bounded Lipschitz drift is bounded by the same constant in \(L^2\).



\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}: the proof is the application \texttt{simpa only [gradient, Linear\allowbreak{}Isometry\allowbreak{}Equiv.\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}map] using}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.



\texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded}: the intermediate fact is \texttt{hpoint}, in source order.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.13.2]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.13.3]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The smooth-gradient moment under the Gibbs law}{The smooth-gradient moment under the Gibbs law}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingMoments-lean-4}


The Lipschitz gradient estimate relative to the minimizing point gives \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\) and hence \(\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\le2L^2\|x-z\|^2+2K^2\). Sharp concentration gives \(\mathbb E_\pi\|x-z\|^2\le d/m\), so

\[\mathbb E_\pi\|\nabla f\|^2\le2L^2d/m+2K^2.\]

For a random vector \(Y\) with Gibbs law \(\pi\), the pushforward integral formula and nonnegative square root give \(\|\nabla f(Y)\|_2\le\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}\). The minimizing point is for the total potential, so the estimate \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\) contains the bounded perturbation gradient there. Squaring with \((a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2\) gives the integrable majorant whose expectation is bounded by \(2L^2d/m+2K^2\). The constants are uniform for all smoothing parameters satisfying the same gradient-magnitude bound.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{lpDrift\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}}
\leansource{\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 120–160}

\leanline{4}{\{L\ K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ (gradient\ f))}
\leanline{4}{(hb\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖gradient\ e\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ))\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ x,\ ‖gradient\ f\ x‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ∂ULA.gibbsMeasure\ (fun\ x\ =>\ f\ x\ +\ e\ x))\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{2\ *\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ ((Module.finrank\ ℝ\ E\ :\ ℝ)\ /\ m)\ +\ 2\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{lpDrift\_\allowbreak{}smooth\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingMoments.lean, lines 164–180}
\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(P\ :\ Measure\ Ω)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ P]}
\leanline{4}{\{f\ e\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (m\ :\ ℝ)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ f)\ (he\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ e)}
\leanline{4}{(hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ univ\ m\ (fun\ x\ =>\ f\ x\ +\ e\ x))}
\leanline{4}{\{L\ K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ (gradient\ f))}
\leanline{4}{(hb\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖gradient\ e\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ))}
\leanline{4}{(Y\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ (hlaw\ :\ P.map\ Y\ =\ ULA.gibbsMeasure\ (fun\ x\ =>\ f\ x\ +\ e\ x))\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖ULA.lpDrift\ P\ (gradient\ f)\ hLip\ Y‖\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{Real.sqrt\ (2\ *\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ ((Module.finrank\ ℝ\ E\ :\ ℝ)\ /\ m)\ +\ 2\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Let \(z\) minimize \(F=f+e\), with \(F\) \(m\)-strongly convex. From the previous stage, \(\|\nabla f(z)\|\le K\), and score Lipschitzness gives \(\|\nabla f(x)\|\le L\|x-z\|+K\). Consequently \[\|\nabla f(x)\|^2\le2L^2\|x-z\|^2+2K^2.\] The Gibbs second moment is integrable by strong convexity; the right side therefore supplies an integrable majorant for the score square. The facts \texttt{hbound} and \texttt{hgrad} discharge that prerequisite before \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono} is applied.



Sharp concentration yields \(\mathbb E_\pi\|X-z\|^2\le d/m\), so \(\mathbb E_\pi\|\nabla f(X)\|^2\le2L^2d/m+2K^2\). The next declaration transfers this actual Gibbs-law integral to any random variable with that law and takes its square root, obtaining the \(L^2\) drift bound used in the splitting reference.



Formal correspondence: integral\_sq\_smooth\_gradient\_le : A smoothing-uniform second moment for the smooth part of a Gibbs drift. \section{\texorpdfstring{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}{Lipschitz energy and the averaged lifted potential}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCoupling-lean}


Assume \(g\) is convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz, with \(\lambda>0\) and \(N>0\). Proximal optimality and the value bound give the pairwise energy inequality; Jensen transfers it to the averaged particle output. The replicated-pair norm identity also proves that \(G=N^{-1}\sum_i g(J_i z)\) has the same value Lipschitz constant. Neither argument requires the averaged output to minimize a shared-state objective.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The proximal energy inequality from value Lipschitzness}{The proximal energy inequality from value Lipschitzness}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCoupling-lean-1}


For \(p=P_\lambda(x)\), test the residual support at comparator \(a\) to bound \(f(p)-f(a)\). Apply \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\) in the expansion of \(\|x-a\|^2\). Nonnegativity of \((\|x-p\|-\lambda K)^2\ge0\) gives \(\|p-a\|^2\le\|x-a\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(a))+\lambda^2K^2\). Sum the pair inequalities, use Jensen for the common parameter, and divide by positive \(N\). The resulting full-state remainder is \(\lambda^2K^2\).



At the proximal point \(p\), the residual \(r=(x-p)/\lambda\) is a subgradient at \(p\). Its norm is at most \(K\) for a \(K\)-Lipschitz potential. The support at \(p\), bound \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\), and squared-distance expansion give

\[\|p-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2\lambda(f(x)-f(y))+\lambda^2K^2.\]

The required replacement of output energy by input energy is exactly \(f(x)-f(p)\le K\|x-p\|\). Completion of a square bounds the remaining displacement expression by \(\lambda^2K^2\). Thus value Lipschitzness supplies the needed numerical remainder without assuming a bounded input-subgradient selection.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The averaged potential’s value Lipschitz constant}{The averaged potential’s value Lipschitz constant}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCoupling-lean-2}


Put \(G(z)=F_g(z)/N\). Cauchy–Schwarz for the finite average and \(\sum_i\|J_i v\|^2=N\|v\|^2\) imply \(N^{-1}\sum_i\|J_i v\|\le\|v\|\). Applying the pairwise value Lipschitz estimate therefore gives

\[|G(z)-G(w)|\le\frac KN\sum_i\|J_i(z-w)\|\le K\|z-w\|.\]

The latent scaling \(N^{-1/2}\) makes the average pair squared distance equal to the state squared distance. Consequently the averaged physical-time potential \(G=F_g/N\) retains value Lipschitz constant \(K\). This bound ensures integrability of its values at square-integrable random states.

Let \(\bar f_N(z)=N^{-1}\sum_i f(A_i z)\). Cauchy–Schwarz and the replication identity give \[\frac1N\sum_i\|A_i v\|\le\left(\frac1N\sum_i\|A_i v\|^2\right)^{1/2}=\|v\|.\] Applying \(|f(a)-f(b)|\le K\|a-b\|\) to each pair and summing proves \(|\bar f_N(z)-\bar f_N(w)|\le K\|z-w\|\). The declarations separate the average norm estimate from the value estimate so that the particle scaling can be checked independently.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Contraction of the splitting recursion with common innovations}{Contraction of the splitting recursion with common innovations}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingContraction-lean}


Assume \(N>0\); let \(g_1\) be differentiable and \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_1\)-Lipschitz gradient, and let \(g_2\) be convex and bounded below. Suppose \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda>0\), and \(0\le\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). Jensen’s inequality transfers pairwise proximal nonexpansiveness to the averaged state map. In two updates driven by the same innovation, the additive perturbations cancel before proximity. The smooth gradient-step contraction therefore also bounds the full splitting update and its finite iterates.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.7]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.10]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Nonexpansiveness of the averaged pair map}{Nonexpansiveness of the averaged pair map}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingContraction-lean-1}


Jensen’s inequality for the averaged parameter difference and the exact latent squared scaling give

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}_P(z)-\operatorname{Pair}_P(w)\|^2\le N^{-1}\sum_i\|P(J_i z)-P(J_i w)\|^2.\]

If \(P\) is nonexpansive, each squared summand is at most \(\|J_i(z-w)\|^2\). Their sum is \(N\|z-w\|^2\), so the averaged map is nonexpansive.



Explicitly put \(A_i z=(\theta,\sqrt N z_i)\) and \(\Delta_i=P(A_i z)-P(A_i w)\). The parameter inequality is \(N\|\overline\Delta_\theta\|^2\le\sum_i\|\Delta_{i,\theta}\|^2\), while the latent differences are rescaled by \(N^{-1/2}\). Thus

\[\|\operatorname{Pair}P(z)-\operatorname{Pair}P(w)\|^2\le\frac1N\sum_i\|\Delta_i\|^2.\]

Nonexpansiveness of \(P\) and identity \(\sum_i\|A_i(z-w)\|^2=N\|z-w\|^2\) give the unit full-state Lipschitz bound. Positivity of \(N\) justifies division and rescaling. The argument compares two averaged outputs and does not assume that either minimizes a shared-state proximal objective.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Contraction with the same innovation}{Contraction with the same innovation}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingContraction-lean-2}


The lifted smooth potential has curvature \(Nm\) and gradient constant \(NL_1\). At step \(\gamma/N\) its gradient update contracts by \(1-m\gamma\ge0\). For the same innovation the noisy forward difference is this deterministic difference. Composition with the nonexpansive pairwise proximal map yields

\[\|T_\lambda(z,\xi)-T_\lambda(w,\xi)\|\le(1-m\gamma)\|z-w\|.\]

Induction gives factor \((1-m\gamma)^n\). Writing \(q=1-m\gamma\), the step restriction ensures \(q\ge0\). Starting from \(e_0=\|z-w\|\), the induction step is \(e_{k+1}\le q e_k\le q^{k+1}e_0\). Equivalently, with \(q=1-m\gamma\) the discrepancies satisfy \(e_{k+1}\le q e_k\) and are bounded at time \(n\) by \(q^n e_0\).



Only equality of the innovations is used, so this stability statement holds for deterministic sequences as well as Gaussian ones. Convergence to a Gibbs law additionally requires a stationary reference and a local-error estimate.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Integrated quadratic and convex-energy estimates}{Integrated quadratic and convex-energy estimates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean}


Apply the deterministic energy estimate to \(L^2\) random vectors. Lipschitz growth makes the scalar potential values integrable, and square integrability makes all squared-distance terms integrable. Weighted Young inequalities control the smooth local defect without losing the squared contraction. Integrated convex supports retain the perturbation-force pairing in a form that can be combined with the expected potential difference.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Weighted Young inequality and squared contraction}{Weighted Young inequality and squared contraction}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean-1}


For \(t>0\), expansion and \((t\|v\|-\|r\|)^2\ge0\) give

\[\|v+r\|^2\le(1+t)\|v\|^2+(1+t^{-1})\|r\|^2.\]

If \(0<t\le1\) and \(\|v\|\le(1-t)\|e\|\), the inequality \((1+t)(1-t)^2\le1-t\) leaves a squared contraction with coefficient one minus the parameter. The identity \(\|v+r+b\|^2=\|v+r\|^2+2\langle v+r+b,b\rangle-\|b\|^2\) separately retains the force cross term.



Choosing the Young parameter as \(mh\) gives \((1+mh)(1-mh)^2\le1-mh\) under \(mh\le1\), while multiplying the squared local defect by \(1+(mh)^{-1}\). Keeping the integrated perturbation force in its inner product permits its convex-energy contribution to combine with the proximal energy difference, rather than estimating each term by an unrelated norm bound.

\leanline{0}{theorem\ norm\_sum\_rearranged\ (v\ r\ b\ :\ H)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖v\ +\ r\ +\ b‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ =\ ‖v\ +\ r‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ 2\ *\ {\leanfallback ⟪}v\ +\ r\ +\ b,\ b{\leanfallback ⟫}\ -\ ‖b‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Expanding \(\|v+r\|^2\) and using \(2\langle v,r\rangle\le t\|v\|^2+t^{-1}\|r\|^2\) gives the weighted inequality. If \(\|v\|\le(1-t)\|e\|\), \(0<t\le1\), then \((1+t)(1-t)^2\le1-t\). This is the scalar inequality proved in \texttt{hf}; it keeps a linear contraction in the squared error instead of doubling the leading term. The final identity rearranges \(\|v+r+b\|^2\) with its cross term paired against the final error, the form needed for convex-energy cancellation.



Formal correspondence: norm\_add\_sq\_le\_weighted : A weighted quadratic triangle bound with an explicit positive weight. norm\_add\_sq\_le\_of\_contraction : The weighted estimate retains a linear contraction in the squared error. norm\_sum\_rearranged : Moving an integrated nonsmooth drift into a cross term avoids a Lipschitz constant for that drift in the consistency estimate.

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}, \texttt{nlinarith}.

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}, \texttt{nlinarith}.

\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrability of potential and quadratic terms}{Integrability of potential and quadratic terms}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean-2}


For globally \(K\)-Lipschitz scalar function \(f\), subtract \(f(0)\). The centered growth is at most \(K\|X\|\), so \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\) on a probability space. The \(L^2\) norm satisfies \(\|X\|_2^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\).



For observable \(f\), the direct bound is \(|f(X)|\le K\|X\|+|f(0)|\). Thus an \(L^2\) state gives \(f(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). Centering \(f\) justifies the composition estimate. The \(L^2\) inner product and identity \(\langle X,X\rangle=\|X\|^2\) give \(\|X\|_{L^2}^2=\int\|X(\omega)\|^2dP\). More generally, bound \(|f(x)|\le|f(0)|+K\|x\|\) makes composition with an \(L^2\) state an \(L^2\) function. Squared differences of \(L^2\) states are also integrable. Hence the potential and quadratic expectations below are finite, and linearity may be applied without subtraction of infinite quantities.

\end{leancode}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The integrated proximal energy inequality}{The integrated proximal energy inequality}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean-3}


Assume \(\|T(x)-y\|^2\le\|x-y\|^2-2h(f(x)-f(y))+C\) and Lipschitz maps \(T,f\). Substitute \(S,Y\in L^2\) pointwise on a probability-one representative event. The Lipschitz composition represents \(T(S)\) almost surely. Each term is integrable by the preceding growth and moment bounds. Integral monotonicity and linearity therefore give

\[\|T(S)-Y\|_2^2\le\|S-Y\|_2^2-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y))+C.\]

This is the same energy inequality in \(L^2\). The additive constant is unchanged because the source has mass one. The statement concerns the random output of the actual composed map, as identified by its almost-sure representative.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pointwise}}
\leansource{\texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pointwise} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingL2Energy.lean, lines 89–115}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ lp\_energy\_of\_pointwise\ \{T\ :\ E\ →\ E\}\ \{LT\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hT\ :\ LipschitzWith\ LT\ T)}
\leanline{4}{\{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hf\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ (h\ C\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(hpoint\ :\ ∀\ x\ y,\ ‖T\ x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -\ 2\ *\ h\ *\ (f\ x\ -\ f\ y)\ +\ C)}
\leanline{4}{(S\ Y\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖ULA.lpDrift\ P\ T\ hT\ S\ -\ Y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ ‖S\ -\ Y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -}
\leanline{6}{2\ *\ h\ *\ ((∫\ ω,\ f\ (S\ ω)\ ∂P)\ -\ ∫\ ω,\ f\ (Y\ ω)\ ∂P)\ +\ C}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Apply the deterministic premise at \((S(\omega),Y(\omega))\). The representative facts for \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Drift} and subtraction identify its left side almost everywhere with \(\|T(S)-Y\|^2\). The local facts \texttt{hn}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}S}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}Y} prove integrability of both quadratic norms and both potential values. Only then does \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}mono\_\allowbreak{}ae} integrate the inequality and distribute its finite terms: \[\|T(S)-Y\|_2^2\le\|S-Y\|_2^2-2h(\mathbb Ef(S)-\mathbb Ef(Y))+C.\] The constant remains \(C\) because \(P\) has mass one.



Formal correspondence: lp\_energy\_of\_pointwise : Lift a proved deterministic energy inequality to actual \(L^2\) random variables. This is an integration lemma; the particle instantiation below proves its pointwise premise from the genuine proximal minimization problem.



\texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pointwise}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hn}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}S}, \texttt{hf\allowbreak{}Y}, \texttt{hright}, \texttt{hi}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{Pi.\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}apply}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}add}, \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}integral}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Integrated convex support and the terminal-state difference}{Integrated convex support and the terminal-state difference}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingL2Energy-lean-4}


The support inequality for a differentiable convex perturbation gives \(\langle S-Y,\nabla e(Y)\rangle\le e(S)-e(Y)\) pointwise. Integrability permits the same inequality for the \(L^2\) inner product. For a third vector \(Z\), identity \(S-Z=(S-Y)+(Y-Z)\) leaves additional pairing \(\langle Y-Z,\nabla e(Y)\rangle_{L^2}\).



Explicitly, for convex differentiable \(e\) the inequality is \(\langle S-Y,\nabla e(Y)\rangle\le e(S)-e(Y)\). The gradient composition and state difference belong to \(L^2\) under the stated bounds, so Cauchy–Schwarz makes their pairing integrable. Integration consequently replaces the reference-force pairing by an expected potential difference, while retaining the endpoint-difference pairing whose future-noise part is shown to vanish separately.

\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(he\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ e)\ (hconv\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ e)}
\leanline{4}{\{L\ K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hgrad\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ (gradient\ e))\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ e)}
\leanline{4}{(S\ Y\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ :}
\leanline{4}{{\leanfallback ⟪}S\ -\ Y,\ ULA.lpDrift\ P\ (gradient\ e)\ hgrad\ Y{\leanfallback ⟫}\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{(∫\ ω,\ e\ (S\ ω)\ ∂P)\ -\ ∫\ ω,\ e\ (Y\ ω)\ ∂P}
\end{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(S\ Y\ Z\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ :}

\leanline{6}{(∫\ ω,\ e\ (S\ ω)\ ∂P)\ -\ (∫\ ω,\ e\ (Y\ ω)\ ∂P)\ +}

The pointwise supporting-plane inequality is \(e(S)-e(Y)\ge\langle\nabla e(Y),S-Y\rangle\). Potential integrability follows from value Lipschitzness; inner-product integrability follows from both vectors belonging to \(L^2\), with score Lipschitzness ensuring \(\nabla e(Y)\in L^2\). Integration gives the first declaration. For an endpoint \(Z\), write \(S-Z=(S-Y)+(Y-Z)\) and use linearity of the inner product. This produces the energy difference plus the reference-increment cross term, precisely the two contributions used in \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}cross\_\allowbreak{}energy\_\allowbreak{}le}.



Formal correspondence: lp\_convex\_gradient\_support : The actual convex gradient supports every square-integrable comparison variable after integrating the pointwise supporting-plane inequality. lp\_convex\_gradient\_cross : Endpoint comparison split into the convex energy difference and a reference-increment cross term, all in the genuine \(L^2\) inner product.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}{Local smooth drift defect and the future cross term}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProcess-lean}


Suppose the continuous Hilbert-valued reference satisfies \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\) with \(\|g(Y_t)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\sigma\sqrt t\). Its increments are bounded by the sum of a term linear in time and a term proportional to the square root of time. The smooth drift’s Lipschitz constant then bounds its integrated local defect. For a bounded perturbation observable, first use orthogonality of the future noise; the remaining drift pairing is linear in the remaining time.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The integrated smooth drift defect}{The integrated smooth drift defect}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProcess-lean-1}


The integral equation and the integral norm inequality give \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le Gt+\sigma\sqrt t\). If \(a\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, then for \(0\le t\le h\), \(\|a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)\|\le L(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)\). Integration over length \(h\) therefore yields

\[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h).\]

Equivalently, for solution \(Y_t-Y_0=-\int_0^t g(Y_s)ds+B_t\) with \(\|g(Y_s)\|\le G\) and \(\|B_t\|\le\nu\sqrt t\), one has \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le tG+\nu\sqrt t\). For smooth component \(a\) with Lipschitz constant \(L\), this gives

\[\left\|\int_0^h(a(Y_t)-a(Y_0))dt\right\|\le Lh(Gh+\nu\sqrt h).\]

The estimate replaces both time factors by their endpoint maxima on \(0\le t\le h\), using monotonicity of \(t\) and \(\sqrt t\), and multiplies by interval length. Thus from \(\|Y_t-Y_0\|\le Gt+\sigma\sqrt t\), multiplication by \(L\) controls the smooth force difference; for \(t\in[0,h]\) the factors \(t\) and \(\sqrt t\) are bounded by their endpoint values. Regularity may require Lipschitz continuity of the full drift, but only the smooth component’s constant enters this numerical bound.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}localDefect\_\allowbreak{}bound}}
\leansource{\texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}localDefect\_\allowbreak{}bound} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingProcess.lean, lines 23–47}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ splitting\_localDefect\_bound\ (a\ g\ :\ H\ →\ H)\ \{L\ Lg\ :\ ℝ≥0\}}
\leanline{4}{(ha\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ a)\ (\_hg\ :\ LipschitzWith\ Lg\ g)}
\leanline{4}{(Y\ B\ :\ ℝ\ →\ H)\ (\_hY\ :\ Continuous\ Y)\ (h\ :\ ℝ)\ (hh\ :\ 0\ ≤\ h)}
\leanline{4}{(G\ N\ :\ ℝ)\ (hG\ :\ 0\ ≤\ G)\ (hN\ :\ 0\ ≤\ N)}
\leanline{4}{(hbound\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ h,\ ‖g\ (Y\ t)‖\ ≤\ G)}
\leanline{4}{(hnoise\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ h,\ ‖B\ t‖\ ≤\ N\ *\ Real.sqrt\ t)}
\leanline{4}{(heq\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ h,\ Y\ t\ -\ Y\ 0\ =\ -(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ g\ (Y\ s))\ +\ B\ t)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖ULA.localDefect\ a\ Y\ h‖\ ≤\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ h\ *\ (G\ *\ h\ +\ N\ *\ Real.sqrt\ h)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: splitting\_localDefect\_bound : A coarse local smooth-drift defect bound uses only the smooth part's own Lipschitz constant and the total reference drift moment.

The \texttt{calc} block checks the successive equalities or inequalities in the argument. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{ring}, \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The future-drift pairing after noise orthogonality}{The future-drift pairing after noise orthogonality}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProcess-lean-2}


Subtract the reference equations at \(t,h\) to obtain

\[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\]

If \(\|b\|\le K\) and \(\langle b,B_h-B_t\rangle=0\), the noise pairing is zero. Cauchy–Schwarz on the remaining drift integral gives \(\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le(h-t)GK\), avoiding an order-\(\sqrt{h-t}\) bound.



Splitting the integral at \(t\) gives the same difference identity

\[Y_t-Y_h=\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds-(B_h-B_t).\]

Pairing with \(b\) and applying orthogonality gives

\[\langle Y_t-Y_h,b\rangle\le\left\|\int_t^h g(Y_s)ds\right\|\|b\|\le(h-t)GK.\]

In the application the inner product is in \(L^2\), so this cancellation is of the expected pairing. Subtraction at \(t\) and \(h\) leaves future drift integral of norm at most \((h-t)G\), hence remainder \((h-t)GK\). Brownian sample increments themselves do not vanish.

\leanline{4}{(h\ t\ :\ ℝ)\ (ht\ :\ 0\ ≤\ t)\ (hth\ :\ t\ ≤\ h)}
\leanline{4}{(G\ K\ :\ ℝ)\ (hG\ :\ 0\ ≤\ G)\ (\_hK\ :\ 0\ ≤\ K)}
\leanline{4}{(hbound\ :\ ∀\ s\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ h,\ ‖g\ (Y\ s)‖\ ≤\ G)}
\leanline{4}{(heq\ :\ ∀\ s\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ h,\ Y\ s\ -\ Y\ 0\ =\ -(∫\ r\ in\ 0..s,\ g\ (Y\ r))\ +\ B\ s)}
\leanline{4}{(b\ :\ H)\ (hb\ :\ ‖b‖\ ≤\ K)\ (horth\ :\ {\leanfallback ⟪}b,\ B\ h\ -\ B\ t{\leanfallback ⟫}\ =\ 0)\ :}
