\section{\texorpdfstring{Independence under grouping of random variables}{Independence under grouping of random variables}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-IndependenceHelpers-lean}


Independent source variables may be grouped into an initialized past and a future increment by factoring their joint distributions and reassociating product measures. The results below use joint independence of the specified blocks. Pairwise independence of three separate variables would not imply the required independence of one variable from a pair.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{An independent family with two indices}{An independent family with two indices}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-IndependenceHelpers-lean-1}


Assume \(X\perp Y\) and define \(Z_{\rm false}=X\) and \(Z_{\rm true}=Y\). To verify independence of this two-element family, consider its finite index subsets. The empty subset uses probability mass one, and each singleton gives an identity. For the subset of both indices the condition is exactly \(P(X\in A,Y\in B)=P(X\in A)P(Y\in B)\).



Thus two-variable independence is equivalent to the factorization required for the corresponding two-element independent family. No additional independence condition is introduced by this indexing.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{iIndepFun\_\allowbreak{}bool\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}indepFun}}
\leansource{\texttt{iIndepFun\_\allowbreak{}bool\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}indepFun} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}IndependenceHelpers.lean, lines 20–46}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ iIndepFun\_bool\_of\_indepFun\ \{Ω\ E\ :\ Type*\}}
\leanline{4}{[MeasurableSpace\ Ω]\ [MeasurableSpace\ E]\ \{μ\ :\ Measure\ Ω\}}
\leanline{4}{[IsProbabilityMeasure\ μ]\ \{X\ Y\ :\ Ω\ →\ E\}\ (h\ :\ IndepFun\ X\ Y\ μ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{iIndepFun\ (fun\ b\ :\ Bool\ ↦\ if\ b\ then\ Y\ else\ X)\ μ}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
An independent family is defined by factorization for every finite set of indices. For a Boolean index there are four possibilities: neither variable, only \(X\), only \(Y\), or both. The singleton identities are tautological; the empty family has probability one because \(\mu\) is a probability; the two-variable case is exactly the assumed independence of \(X\) and \(Y\). The two membership case splits in Lean exhaust these four possibilities explicitly.



Formal correspondence: iIndepFun\_bool\_of\_indepFun : Two independent random variables form an independent family indexed by Bool . The probability assumption supplies the empty-family identity.



\texttt{i\allowbreak{}Indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}bool\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}indep\allowbreak{}Fun}: the cases are separated by \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hf : false \ensuremath{\in} s}; the intermediate facts include \texttt{hs}, \texttt{hi}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{i\allowbreak{}Indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}inter\_\allowbreak{}preimage\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{Bool.\allowbreak{}forall\_\allowbreak{}bool}, \texttt{h.\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}inter\_\allowbreak{}preimage\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}mul}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Reassociation of a jointly independent triple}{Reassociation of a jointly independent triple}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-IndependenceHelpers-lean-2}


Assume measurable variables satisfy \(X\perp(Y,Z)\) and \(Y\perp Z\). Their triple law factors as \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\). Product reassociation gives \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\), while projection gives \(\mu_{(X,Y)}=\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y\). Hence \((X,Y)\perp Z\).



Indeed \(X\) is independent of the entire pair \((Y,Z)\), and \(Y\) and \(Z\) are independent. These hypotheses give \(\mu_X\otimes(\mu_Y\otimes\mu_Z)\), whose reassociation is \((\mu_X\otimes\mu_Y)\otimes\mu_Z\). This proves independence of \((X,Y)\) and \(Z\). The first two product marginals are the joint law of \((X,Y)\), so the conclusion is \((X,Y)\perp Z\). Equality of measurable pushforward measures proves the statement also for path-valued variables. The hypotheses are stronger than mere pairwise independence.

\end{leancode}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Orthogonality of future Brownian increments to past observables}{Orthogonality of future Brownian increments to past observables}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean}


Assume independent full scalar Brownian paths with measurable evaluations and initialization independent of the entire Brownian family. The integral solution is measurable with respect to initialization and the stopped noise path. A future Brownian increment is independent of this past and centered. Its expected inner product with any square-integrable Lipschitz observable of the reference state is therefore zero. This is an expectation identity, not a samplewise orthogonality claim.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.54]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.25]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.31]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independence of vector stopped past and shifted future}{Independence of vector stopped past and shifted future}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean-1}


For each scalar coordinate, the shifted future \(B_i(T+s)-B_i(T)\) is independent of the stopped past \(B_i(\min(t,T))\). Independence of the full coordinate paths gives joint independence across these coordinatewise pairs. Selecting and grouping the disjoint past and future indices therefore gives independence of the entire vector-valued past and future path families.



The stopped path records all coordinate values up to time \(t\); the shifted future records increments after \(t\). The resulting independence is between the sigma-algebras generated by these full path blocks. Consequently any measurable function of the entire past is independent of every measurable future increment. This uses full-path independence and Brownian independent increments, rather than unrelated pairwise independence assertions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The pointwise Brownian vector and its square-integrable representative}{The pointwise Brownian vector and its square-integrable representative}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean-2}


Define the pointwise vector noise by \(\mathcal B_t=\sqrt2(B(t_+)-B(0))\) with \(t_+=\max(t,0)\). The constructed \(L^2\) Brownian noise agrees almost surely with this expression. Each scalar evaluation is measurable, and finite vector assembly, subtraction, and scaling preserve measurability. Its Gaussian coordinate second moments prove square integrability.



The \(L^2\) class and the pointwise vector thus have the same laws and integrals. Independence and centering may be transferred by their almost-sure equality. The nonnegative-time definition specifies the domain of Brownian evaluation and does not change the positive-time process.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independence of the initialized past from future increments}{Independence of the initialized past from future increments}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean-3}


The stopped Brownian path is independent of the future increment. Initialization is independent of their joint pair. Reassociation of the product law therefore makes the pair consisting of initialization and stopped past independent of the future increment. Any variable measurable with respect to that initialized past inherits the same independence.



A variable only almost everywhere strongly measurable for the past admits a measurable representative with the same law. Almost-sure equality then transfers the conclusion to its \(L^2\) representative. The grouping relation is \((\text{initial},\text{past})\mid\text{future}\). Thus independence for the later reference observable follows from its past measurability and the input assumptions; it is not added as an independent assertion about the solution.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expected orthogonality from independence and centering}{Expected orthogonality from independence and centering}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean-4}


Scalar Brownian centering and commutation of finite coordinate projections with integration give \(\mathbb E B_t=0\), and subtraction and scaling give \(\mathbb E\mathcal B_t=0\). For independent \(L^2\) vectors \(X,W\), Cauchy–Schwarz proves integrability of the inner product. Independence and bilinear integration give \(\langle X,W\rangle_{L^2}=\langle\mathbb E X,\mathbb E W\rangle\).



When \(W\) is centered, the latter inner product of means is zero. Thus no norm bound on the other mean is needed: independence and centering give exact expected orthogonality.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Orthogonality for observables of the adapted integral solution}{Orthogonality for observables of the adapted integral solution}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOrthogonality-lean-5}


The integral-equation construction makes \(Y_t\) measurable for the initialized Brownian past. A Lipschitz observable \(b(Y_t)\) remains measurable for that past and belongs to \(L^2\). Independence of future increments and their zero mean imply

\[\langle b(Y_t),\mathcal B_h-\mathcal B_t\rangle_{L^2}=0,\qquad0\le t\le h.\]

For observable \(b(Y_t)\), both factors are in \(L^2\), so their pairing is integrable and

\[\mathbb E\langle b(Y_t),B_h-B_t\rangle=\left\langle\mathbb E b(Y_t),\mathbb E(B_h-B_t)\right\rangle=0.\]

The \(L^2\) inner product is this expectation. The state at time \(t\) depends only on initialization and noise up to \(t\). Composing with the drift observable preserves this dependence, while the increment over \([t,h]\) is independent and centered. This identity removes the contribution that an absolute norm bound would estimate at order \(\sqrt{h-t}\) in the subsequent energy comparison.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Expected energy comparison for the perturbation force}{Expected energy comparison for the perturbation force}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCrossEnergy-lean}


Let \(e\) be convex and differentiable, assume \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), and let \(Y_t\) have the stated stationary smoothed law, integral equation, and future-noise orthogonality. Assume the value Lipschitz bounds needed for integrability. Convex support bounds the force pairing by an envelope-energy difference. Stationarity identifies the time-dependent reference energy with the terminal energy, and the uniform gap then compares this with the original nonsmooth energy in the proximal estimate.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The expected uniform value-gap inequality}{The expected uniform value-gap inequality}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCrossEnergy-lean-1}


Suppose \(f(x)\le e(x)+\delta\) and both functions are Lipschitz. For \(L^2\) variable \(X\) their values are integrable, so integral monotonicity gives \(\mathbb E f(X)-\mathbb E e(X)\le\delta\). In the notation \(f\le e+\delta\) and an \(L^2\) state, the mass-one calculation is \(\mathbb Ef(X)\le\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta P(\Omega)=\mathbb Ee(X)+\delta\).



The subsequent comparison uses \(e\le f\) at the algorithm’s intermediate state and \(f\le e+\delta\) at the reference endpoint. The integrated gap is \(\delta\) because the source is a probability. All expected values are finite before subtraction, and no derivative of the original nonsmooth function is used.

\leanline{4}{(delta\ :\ ℝ)\ (hgap\ :\ ∀\ x,\ f\ x\ ≤\ e\ x\ +\ delta)\ (X\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ P)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ ω,\ f\ (X\ ω)\ ∂P)\ -\ (∫\ ω,\ e\ (X\ ω)\ ∂P)\ ≤\ delta}
\end{leancode}

\texttt{expectation\_\allowbreak{}gap\_\allowbreak{}le}: the intermediate fact is \texttt{hi}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{Pi.\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}apply}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}add}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The stationary convex-support and future-drift estimates}{The stationary convex-support and future-drift estimates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCrossEnergy-lean-2}


Convex support bounds \(\langle S-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\) by \(\mathbb E e(S)-\mathbb E e(Y_t)+\langle Y_t-Y_h,\nabla e(Y_t)\rangle\). Stationarity gives equality of \(\mathbb E e(Y_t)\) and \(\mathbb E e(Y_h)\). The future-noise estimate bounds the remaining drift pairing by \(hGK\). The gap \(e\le f\le e+\delta\) changes the difference of the two energies by at most \(\delta\). Integration therefore gives

\[2\left\langle S-Y_h,\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\right\rangle-2h(\mathbb E f(S)-\mathbb E f(Y_h))\le2h\delta+2h^2GK.\]

More explicitly, for \(b_t=\nabla e(Y_t)\) in \(L^2\) the support and future-increment bounds give

\[\langle S-Y_h,b_t\rangle\le\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_t)+(h-t)GK.\]

Stationarity replaces \(\mathbb Ee(Y_t)\) by \(\mathbb Ee(Y_h)\). Integration over \([0,h]\) is legitimate because \(b_t\) is continuous and the fixed inner-product functional commutes with the Bochner integral. A valid upper bound is \(h[\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_h)]+h^2GK\).



The decomposition \(S-Y_h=(S-Y_t)+(Y_t-Y_h)\) separates the convex support term from the future-increment term. Again \(\mathbb E e(Y_t)\) equals \(\mathbb E e(Y_h)\). At \(S\) use \(e\le f\), and at \(Y_h\) use \(f\le e+\delta\). Only one additive gap \(\delta\) remains. This is the energy comparison used to cancel the original potential difference in the splitting estimate.

The two value comparisons give \([\mathbb Ee(S)-\mathbb Ee(Y_h)]-[\mathbb Ef(S)-\mathbb Ef(Y_h)]\le\delta\). Multiply by \(2h\) and combine to obtain the displayed \(2h\delta+2h^2GK\) remainder. In Lean, \texttt{hpoint} is the pointwise-in-time estimate, \texttt{hi} its integral, and \texttt{hsum} the finite value-gap cancellation. The conclusion never substitutes \(\operatorname{Lip}(\nabla e)\) into this remainder.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The synchronous one-step splitting estimate}{The synchronous one-step splitting estimate}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOneStep-lean}


In the Hilbert space \(L^2\), suppose \(f_1\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient. Let a Lipschitz map \(T\) satisfy the proximal energy inequality for \(f\). Assume the stationary reference satisfies the integral equation for \(f_1+e\) and \(e\le f\le e+\delta\), together with the stated moment and orthogonality conditions. Subtraction from the numerical forward step decomposes the discrepancy into a contractive smooth difference, a local smooth defect, and an integrated envelope force. The energy comparison and weighted Young inequality then give the one-step squared-error bound.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The four local forcing terms}{The four local forcing terms}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOneStep-lean-1}


For \(m,h>0\), drift bound \(G\), noise coefficient \(\sigma\), perturbation-gradient bound \(K\), and potential gap \(\delta\), set

\[\mathcal F=(1+(mh)^{-1})[Lh(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)]^2+2h\delta+2h^2GK+h^2K^2.\]

The first term is the squared smooth local defect with Young factor \(1+(mh)^{-1}\). The other terms are, respectively, the finite potential-gap contribution, the reference drift paired with the bounded perturbation gradient, and the proximal quadratic remainder. These distinct estimates will bound the sum by a constant times the squared step.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{splittingCouplingForcing}}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingCouplingForcing} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingOneStep.lean, lines 19–21}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ splittingCouplingForcing\ (m\ h\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ delta\ :\ ℝ)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{(1\ +\ (m\ *\ h)⁻¹)\ *\ (L\ *\ h\ *\ (G\ *\ h\ +\ noise\ *\ Real.sqrt\ h))\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +}
\leanline{4}{2\ *\ h\ *\ delta\ +\ 2\ *\ h\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ G\ *\ K\ +\ h\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ K\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: splittingCouplingForcing : Explicit local forcing in the synchronous splitting proof.



\texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing} is a definition with defining expression \texttt{(1 + (m * h)\ensuremath{{}^{-1}}) * (L * h * (G * h + noise * Real.\allowbreak{}sqrt h)) \textasciicircum{} 2 +}. The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The error decomposition and energy comparison}{The error decomposition and energy comparison}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingOneStep-lean-2}


Define \(S=X-h\nabla f_1(X)+B_h\), \(v=(X-h\nabla f_1(X))-(Y_0-h\nabla f_1(Y_0))\), \(R=\int_0^h[\nabla f_1(Y_t)-\nabla f_1(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h\nabla e(Y_t)dt\). The shared noise and reference integral equation give \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\). Expand its square and integrate the energy inequality for \(T(S)\). The expected \(f\) difference cancels with the corresponding force pairing up to the cross-energy bound. Smooth-step contraction and weighted Young control \(\|v+R\|^2\), while the local estimate controls \(R\). Discarding \(-\|H\|^2\le0\) gives

\[\|T(S)-Y_h\|_2^2\le(1-mh)\|X-Y_0\|_2^2+\mathcal F.\]

The conditions \(m\le L\) and \(h\le2/(m+L)\) imply \(mh\le1\), as required in the weighted contraction estimate. In the shorter notation \(a=\nabla f_1\), \(b=\nabla e\), \(S=X-ha(X)+B_h\), \(v=[X-ha(X)]-[Y_0-ha(Y_0)]\), \(R=\int_0^h[a(Y_t)-a(Y_0)]dt\), and \(H=\int_0^h b(Y_t)dt\), the exact difference is \(S-Y_h=v+R+H\) and its squared rearrangement is

\[\|S-Y_h\|^2=\|v+R\|^2+2\langle S-Y_h,H\rangle-\|H\|^2.\]

This identity retains the integrated envelope force until its energy comparison is applied. The nonpositive square can then be dropped. Weighted Young leaves coefficient \(1-mh\) on the preceding squared error. The forcing is derived from these inequalities rather than assumed as a stochastic recurrence; the Gaussian reference construction supplies the stated reference premises.

\texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}one\_\allowbreak{}step\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}solution} names the lifted smooth and envelope drifts \texttt{a} and \texttt{b}, their sum \texttt{g}, the forward state \texttt{S}, the contracted difference \texttt{v}, the smooth defect \texttt{R} and the integrated envelope force \texttt{H}. \texttt{hdecomp} is their exact error decomposition; \texttt{hnorm} retains its envelope inner product in the squared norm. \texttt{hbase} inserts the proximal energy inequality. The contraction estimate \texttt{hv} and weighted estimate \texttt{hweighted} control the smooth part; \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Rbound} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Rweighted} bound its local defect. \texttt{hcross} invokes stationarity and the orthogonality premise to control the retained energy cross term. Unfolding \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing} collects precisely the four stated forcing terms.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The infimum of squared coupling estimates}{The infimum of squared coupling estimates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTransport-lean}


Assume \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\) and \(q,c\ge0\). Suppose \(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le q\operatorname{cost}(p)^2+c\) holds for every finite-cost coupling \(p\) of \(\mu,\nu\). The same coefficient and forcing then bound the output squared Wasserstein distance in terms of the input squared distance. The proof below treats zero coefficients separately and otherwise uses the greatest-lower-bound property of the input cost infimum.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.18]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.33]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The greatest-lower-bound argument}{The greatest-lower-bound argument}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTransport-lean-1}


If \(q=0\), choose one finite-cost input coupling. If the output square is at most \(c\), nonnegativity of the other term already proves the claim. Otherwise the coefficient is positive and one may set \(r=\sqrt{(W_2(\mu',\nu')^2-c)/q}\). Each finite coupling estimate implies \(r\le\operatorname{cost}(p)\), and infinite-cost couplings satisfy the same extended lower bound. Hence \(r\le\inf_p\operatorname{cost}(p)=W_2(\mu,\nu)\). Squaring and multiplying by \(q\) gives

\[W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\le qW_2(\mu,\nu)^2+c.\]

In the notation \(A=W_2(\mu',\nu')^2\), the hypothesis is \(A\le qC^2+c\) for all finite costs. The case \(q=0\) gives \(A\le c\) directly. The case \(A\le c\) follows using \(q\ge0\). Otherwise \(r=\sqrt{(A-c)/q}\) is real and positive, and all coupling costs are at least \(r\).



Equivalently, costs may approach the finite infimum from above; continuity of squaring makes their squared costs tend to \(W_2(\mu,\nu)^2\). The fixed output bound survives that limit. These arguments require neither an optimal coupling nor division by a coefficient without first proving its positivity. One finite-cost comparison also establishes output finiteness.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}finite\_\allowbreak{}couplings}}
\leansource{\texttt{wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}finite\_\allowbreak{}couplings} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingTransport.lean, lines 21–58}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ wasserstein2\_sq\_bound\_of\_finite\_couplings}
\leanline{4}{(μ\ ν\ μ'\ ν'\ :\ Measure\ E)\ (q\ c\ :\ ℝ)\ (hq\ :\ 0\ ≤\ q)\ (\_hc\ :\ 0\ ≤\ c)}
\leanline{4}{(hfinite\ :\ ULA.wasserstein2\ μ\ ν\ ≠\ ⊤)}
\leanline{4}{(hfamily\ :\ ∀\ p\ :\ ULA.Coupling\ μ\ ν,\ ULA.couplingCost\ p.joint\ ≠\ ⊤\ →}
\leanline{6}{ULA.wasserstein2Real\ μ'\ ν'\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ q\ *\ (ULA.couplingCost\ p.joint).toReal\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ c)\ :}
\leanline{4}{ULA.wasserstein2Real\ μ'\ ν'\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ q\ *\ ULA.wasserstein2Real\ μ\ ν\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ c}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The operation \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}i\allowbreak{}Inf} now proves \(r\le W_2(\mu,\nu)\) directly from the definition as an infimum. The finite-distance hypothesis justifies passing to real values. Squaring and multiplying by \(q\) gives \(A\le qW_2(\mu,\nu)^2+c\). This is why the proof does not need existence of an optimal transport plan.



Formal correspondence: wasserstein2\_sq\_bound\_of\_finite\_couplings : A uniform squared estimate for actual finite-cost couplings passes to the square of the genuine Wasserstein distance.



\texttt{wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}finite\_\allowbreak{}couplings}: the cases are separated by \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hq0 : q = 0}; the intermediate facts include \texttt{hqpos}, \texttt{hnum}, \texttt{r}, \texttt{hs}, \texttt{hreal}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{zero\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{zero\_\allowbreak{}add}, \texttt{trans\_\allowbreak{}eq}, \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Real}, \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}of\allowbreak{}Real}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}, \texttt{linarith}, \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.17.1]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.2]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.3]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.4]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.18.2]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\section{\texorpdfstring{A stationary coupling for the splitting transition}{A stationary coupling for the splitting transition}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCanonical-lean}


For splitting transition \(\mathcal T_h(x,\xi)=T(x-h\nabla f_1(x)+\sqrt{2h}\xi)\) and Gibbs reference potential \(F=f_1+e\), extend an arbitrary finite-cost initial coupling by an independent Brownian source. The Langevin solution started in the Gibbs marginal remains stationary. Its drift moments and independence from future increments supply the one-step comparison assumptions. The normalized Brownian endpoint gives the numerical transition its required independent Gaussian law.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.45]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The measurable transition and sum-gradient identity}{The measurable transition and sum-gradient identity}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCanonical-lean-1}


The Gaussian forward map is jointly continuous, and the following map \(T\) is Lipschitz. Their composition is measurable and defines the splitting transition. Differentiability of the two potential components gives \(\nabla(f_1+e)=\nabla f_1+\nabla e\).



Composition with square-integrable vectors preserves this identity almost surely, so the corresponding \(L^2\) drift maps add as \(L^2\) elements. Hence the reference drift used in the integral equation is the gradient of the stated sum potential.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{splittingUpdate}, \texttt{splittingUpdate\_\allowbreak{}measurable}, \texttt{lpDrift\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}add}}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingUpdate} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingCanonical.lean, lines 26–29}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ splittingUpdate\ (f₁\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ ι\ →\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(T\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ ι\ →\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ ι)\ (h\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(x\ z\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ ι)\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ ι\ :=}
\leanline{2}{T\ (x\ -\ h\ •\ gradient\ f₁\ x\ +\ Real.sqrt\ (2\ *\ h)\ •\ z)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingUpdate\_\allowbreak{}measurable} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingCanonical.lean, lines 31–37}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The stationary comparison from an arbitrary finite-cost coupling}{The stationary comparison from an arbitrary finite-cost coupling}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingCanonical-lean-2}


Let \(c\) be any finite-cost coupling of input law \(\mu\) and the Gibbs target. Its product with an independent Brownian probability space gives square-integrable initial variables with the prescribed marginals. The Langevin solution started at the target coordinate is stationary. Its adaptedness, future-increment independence, and moment bounds give

\[G=\sqrt{2L^2d/m+2K^2}+K,\qquad\sigma=\sqrt{2d}.\]

The normalized increment over \([0,h]\) is independent standard Gaussian noise for the numerical transition. The resulting output pair is a finite-cost coupling, and the one-step theorem gives

\[W_2(\mu\mathcal T_h,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+\mathcal F(m,h,L,G,\sigma,K,\delta).\]

More explicitly, the initial coupling \(c\) has reference marginal \(\pi\propto e^{-(f_1+e)}\) and gives initial variables \(X,Z\). The integral solution \(Y_t\) starts from \(Z\) and satisfies \(\operatorname{Law}(Y_t)=\pi\). Thus stationarity, Gaussian laws, moments, and orthogonality follow from the construction under the potential assumptions, rather than being extra assumptions of the final convergence theorem.

The bound on the total reference force is \[G=\sqrt{2L_1^2d/m+2K^2}+K,\] combining the proved smooth-score Gibbs moment with the uniformly bounded envelope score. The Brownian moment gives \(\|B_t\|_2\le\sqrt{2d}\sqrt t\). Adaptedness and future-increment independence prove \texttt{horth}; stationarity proves equality of the envelope expectations in \texttt{hstationary}. These facts discharge the analytic one-step theorem's stochastic inputs.



Finally, the normalized Brownian increment \(V\) has the standard Gaussian law and is independent of \(X\). The representative identity \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X'coe} identifies the updated random variable with \(T(X-h\nabla f_1(X)+\sqrt{2h}V)\); \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X'law} proves its law is the actual independent-Gaussian Markov step. The pair \((X',Y_h)\) therefore provides a finite transport coupling. This proves both output finiteness and the squared estimate for the original arbitrary \(c\).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Probability laws and second moments of the splitting recursion}{Probability laws and second moments of the splitting recursion}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean}


The splitting update is the composition of a noisy smooth-gradient map and the averaged pairwise proximal map. Assume convexity and a lower bound for \(g_2\), positive \(\lambda\), \(N>0\), and a globally Lipschitz smooth gradient. These hypotheses give measurable updates and propagation of finite second moments. Almost-sure equality identifies the pointwise and square-integrable recursions. Independent identically distributed innovations then determine the same finite-time law on any realization with the prescribed initial distribution.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.20]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurability of the splitting update}{Measurability of the splitting update}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean-1}


Proximal nonexpansiveness gives \(\operatorname{Lip}(\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\lambda})\le1\). The forward map



\((z,\xi)\mapsto z-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_{g_1}(z)+\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\)



is jointly continuous because its gradient is Lipschitz. Its composition with the continuous averaged proximal map is therefore measurable. Finite composition proves that each recursive state is a measurable function of initialization and innovations. The custom splitting recursion and the recursion using this same update agree by induction.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Square-integrable realization of the recursion}{Square-integrable realization of the recursion}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean-2}


Define the output law from the pointwise recursion. An \(L^2\) update is obtained by applying the Lipschitz proximal map to the \(L^2\) noisy forward step. Composition and addition in \(L^2\) give representatives equal almost surely to that pointwise composition. Its linear growth preserves square integrability, so every iterate belongs to \(L^2\).



At each finite step, intersect the probability-one events for the preceding representative equality and the update formula. Induction then proves agreement with the explicit recursion. These law and moment conclusions require neither Gaussian innovations nor equilibrium assumptions; square-integrable supplied innovations suffice.

The square-integrable update first applies the Euler drift/\allowbreak{}noise map and then the genuine pairwise proximal map. Both are Lipschitz, so the result is again an \(L^2\) random variable. The update representative theorem intersects the full-measure sets for these two operations and obtains the pointwise PIPGLA formula. An induction repeats this at each fixed finite time, replacing the previous representative by the pointwise iterate on their common full-measure event. The chain's pushforward law is therefore exactly \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws}, rather than merely a convenient auxiliary recursion.



Formal correspondence: pipglaLaws : The law of the actual PIPGLA state after a supplied innovation sequence. lpPipglaUpdate : The actual noisy forward step followed by the genuine pairwise proximal map, represented in \(L^2\). lpPipglaChain : Every iterate is an actual \(L^2\) random variable.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Probability normalization of the output law}{Probability normalization of the output law}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean-3}


Equality almost surely of the two realizations gives \(\rho_n=Q\circ X_n^{-1}\) for the constructed \(L^2\) iterate. Its measurable representative pushes the probability source forward to a probability law: the inverse image of the whole state space has \(Q\)-mass one.



Almost-sure replacement does not change a pushforward law. Thus normalization of the pointwise splitting law follows independently of its second-moment or transport estimates.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Equality of laws for independent identically distributed inputs}{Equality of laws for independent identically distributed inputs}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingLaw-lean-4}


Assume initialization is independent of the complete innovation sequence and the innovations are independent with common law \(\eta\). The joint input law is \(\mathcal L(X_0)\otimes\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). Its pushforward under the measurable finite recursion determines the output distribution.



In initial-law notation that input measure is \(\mu_0\otimes\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). The state at time \(n\) depends on finitely many innovation coordinates. Therefore two realizations with the same initial law and innovation law have identical output laws. In particular a product probability space may be used for the coupling proof without changing the distribution of the stated algorithm.

\leanline{4}{\{g₁\ g₂\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{L₁\ :\ ℝ≥0\}}
\leanline{4}{(hg₁\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ g₁)\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L₁\ (gradient\ g₁))}
\leanline{4}{(hg₂\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ Set.univ\ g₂)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b\ :\ ℝ,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ g₂\ z)}
\leanline{4}{\{lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ (γ\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(initial\ :\ Ω\ →\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ (noise\ :\ ℕ\ →\ Ω\ →\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)}

For a measurable update, the time-\(n\) state is a measurable function of initialization and the innovation path. Independence of initialization from that path makes their joint law a product; iid innovations make the path law \(\eta^{\otimes\mathbb N}\). Pushing this product through the finite recursion proves the canonical-law equality. The assumptions \texttt{hinit}, \texttt{hnoise}, \texttt{hind}, \texttt{hiid} and \texttt{hlaw} respectively supply these measurability, independence and distribution facts; none is a convergence-rate premise.



Formal correspondence: pipglaLaws\_eq\_canonical : Any independent iid realization has the canonical splitting law. This proves law transfer across coupling spaces without a convergence assumption.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Gaussian sequence representations of the splitting laws}{Gaussian sequence representations of the splitting laws}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingGaussianLaw-lean}


Mutual independence of the scalar standard Gaussian coordinates specifies an independent sequence of standard state-space Gaussian vectors. Independence of initialization gives a product law for all recursive inputs. The measurable splitting map therefore has the same output distribution whether the Gaussian sequence is represented by a scalar product measure, a vector product measure, or another realization with the same input laws.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.15]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.41]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independent standard Gaussian vectors from scalar coordinates}{Independent standard Gaussian vectors from scalar coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingGaussianLaw-lean-1}


The scalar coordinate distributions and their mutual independence give \(\xi_k\sim\mathcal N(0,I)\) as a full vector. Disjoint time-coordinate groups give independence across \(k\). Together with initialization independence, the innovation-sequence law is \(\mathcal N(0,I)^{\otimes\mathbb N}\).



Indeed every finite set of vector coordinates has the product of its scalar Gaussian laws. These finite-coordinate distributions determine the vector-sequence product measure. Applying the general independent-innovation recursion result identifies the finite-time splitting laws without requiring a stationary distribution.

\leanline{4}{pipglaLaws\ Q\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ lam)\ γ\ initial\ (fun\ k\ ↦\ noise\ k)\ n\ =}
\leanline{6}{ULA.chainLaw\ (pipglaUpdate\ g₁\ (prox\ g₂\ lam)\ γ)\ (Q.map\ initial)}
\leanline{8}{(Measure.infinitePi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ ℕ\ ↦\ stdGaussian\ (IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)))\ n}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The hypothesis \texttt{Gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations} specifies laws and independence of all scalar coordinates over all times, as well as independence of initialization from the whole path. \texttt{gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations\_\allowbreak{}vector\allowbreak{}Law} groups a finite set of independent standard scalar Gaussians into a standard state-space Gaussian; \texttt{gaussian\allowbreak{}Innovations\_\allowbreak{}vector\allowbreak{}Independent} groups the time slices into an independent vector family. The measurable recursion can then be pushed forward from the product of the initial law and this iid path law. Equality follows for the actual PIPGLA iterate at every finite time; it requires no invariant target.



Formal correspondence: pipglaLaws\_eq\_canonicalGaussian : The full iid vector Gaussian algorithm law follows from the original scalar-coordinate Gaussian hypotheses.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Equality of scalar and vector sequence representations}{Equality of scalar and vector sequence representations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingGaussianLaw-lean-2}


The map grouping scalar coordinates into time-indexed vectors is measurable. Its pushforward takes the scalar Gaussian product measure to the vector Gaussian product measure. Applying the same measurable recursion to these equal input distributions gives the same output law at time \(n\).



Consequently any two probability-space realizations with the same initial law and independent standard Gaussian sequence have equal splitting laws. The concrete representatives and the choice of auxiliary product space have no effect on this distributional conclusion.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}{Uniform Moreau gaps in scaled particle coordinates}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ParticleSmoothing-lean}


The lifted envelope gradient has norm at most \(NK\), and its uniform potential gap is at most \(N\eta K^2/2\). These follow by summing the single-pair value estimates and are different from gradient variation with constant involving \(1/\eta\). Write \(F_g(z)=\sum_i g(J_i z)\) with \(N>0\). If \(g\) is convex, bounded below, and \(K\)-Lipschitz, the Moreau value Lipschitz bound yields a gradient-magnitude estimate uniform over positive smoothing parameters.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The lifted value bound and envelope-gradient magnitude}{The lifted value bound and envelope-gradient magnitude}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ParticleSmoothing-lean-1}


The averaged potential has value Lipschitz constant \(K\). Multiplication by \(N\) gives \(\operatorname{Lip}(F_g)\le NK\). Since the envelope retains single-pair value constant \(K\), its lifted sum \(F_{g^\eta}\) has constant \(NK\). Differentiability therefore bounds its gradient norm by \(NK\) for every \(\eta>0\).



Equivalently multiplication by \(N\) gives lifted value constant \(NK\). The envelope’s single-pair constant \(K\) yields \(\|\nabla V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\|\le NK\). This uses the norm bound for the derivative of a Lipschitz function, rather than the gradient Lipschitz constant \(N/\eta\), and is consequently uniform as \(\eta\downarrow0\). In the averaged notation the constants are \(K\) and \(NK\) for the full sum, giving \(\|\nabla F_{g_2^\eta}\|\le NK\) independently of \(\eta\). Rapid spatial variation of the gradient does not change this bound on its magnitude.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz}, \texttt{potential\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}le}}

\leanline{4}{LipschitzWith\ ((N\ :\ ℝ≥0)\ *\ K)\ (@IPLA.potential\ N\ dθ\ dx\ g)}
\end{leancode}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The summed envelope gap and split-potential identity}{The summed envelope gap and split-potential identity}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ParticleSmoothing-lean-2}


Apply \(0\le g-g^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\) at each pair and sum the finite inequalities:

\[0\le F_g(z)-F_{g^\eta}(z)\le N\eta K^2/2.\]

Distributivity also gives the function identity \(F_{g_1+g_2^\eta}=F_{g_1}+F_{g_2^\eta}\). In nonsmooth-component notation the bound \(0\le g_2-g_2^\eta\le\eta K^2/2\) has \(N\) summands, so

\[0\le V_N^{(2)}-V_{N,\eta}^{(2)}\le N\eta K^2/2.\]

Expanding \(V_N(g_1+g_2^\eta)\) and distributing the sum gives \(V_N(g_1)+V_N(g_2^\eta)\). Each nonnegative gap is at most \(\eta K^2/2\); summing \(N\) such gaps proves the displayed total gap directly. Differentiating the sum identity, under the established regularity, separates the smooth and envelope reference forces in the splitting argument.

\end{leancode}

\section{\texorpdfstring{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}{A finite coefficient for the splitting error floor}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingRate-lean}


The explicit local forcing is at most \(mh^2C\) when its potential gap is at most \(hK^2/2\). Its coefficient uses curvature, smooth-gradient regularity, drift and noise moments, and the bounded perturbation-gradient magnitude. It contains no inverse envelope gradient constant. Thus the one-step squared forcing is \(O(h^2)\) under \(\delta\le hK^2/2\), as required for a transport error tending to zero with the step.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The explicit nonnegative coefficient}{The explicit nonnegative coefficient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingRate-lean-1}


For \(m>0\) and \(G,K\ge0\) define

\[C(m,L,G,\sigma,K)=\frac{4L^2}{m^2}\left(\frac{G^2}{m}+\sigma^2\right)+\frac2m(GK+K^2).\]

Every squared term is nonnegative and the denominators are positive, giving \(C\ge0\). Each summand of \(C\) is finite under \(m>0\) and the stated nonnegative magnitude bounds. The coefficient is not asserted optimal; its explicit dependence on dimension, curvature, and particle factors is retained in the quantitative estimate.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{splittingRMSConstant}, \texttt{splittingRMSConstant\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingRMSConstant} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingRate.lean, lines 17–18}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ splittingRMSConstant\ (m\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ :\ ℝ)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{4\ *\ L\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ m\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ (G\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ m\ +\ noise\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ +\ 2\ /\ m\ *\ (G\ *\ K\ +\ K\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingRMSConstant\_\allowbreak{}nonneg} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingRate.lean, lines 21–25}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ splittingRMSConstant\_nonneg\ \{m\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ :\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)\ (hG\ :\ 0\ ≤\ G)\ (hK\ :\ 0\ ≤\ K)\ :}
\leanline{4}{0\ ≤\ splittingRMSConstant\ m\ L\ G\ noise\ K}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: splittingRMSConstant : A finite coefficient for the squared splitting error floor. splittingRMSConstant\_nonneg : The coefficient is nonnegative under the actual moment-bound hypotheses.



\texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}RMSConstant} is a definition with defining expression \texttt{4 * L \textasciicircum{} 2 /\allowbreak{} m \textasciicircum{} 2 * (G \textasciicircum{} 2 /\allowbreak{} m + noise \textasciicircum{} 2) + 2 /\allowbreak{} m * (G * K + K \textasciicircum{} 2)}. The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.



\texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}RMSConstant\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}: the proof is the application \texttt{unfold splitting\allowbreak{}RMSConstant}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The quadratic-step bound on local forcing}{The quadratic-step bound on local forcing}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingRate-lean-2}


Assume \(h\le1/m\) and the stated gap bound. The estimates \((Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\le2G^2h^2+2\sigma^2h\le2h(G^2/m+\sigma^2)\) and \(h+1/m\le2/m\) bound the weighted smooth defect by \(4L^2h^2(G^2/m+\sigma^2)/m\). The gap contributes \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\). Adding \(2h^2GK+h^2K^2\) proves \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\), whose geometric sum has floor \(hC\).



For the expression \((Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\), apply \((u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2\) and \(h\le1/m\) to obtain

\[(Gh+\sigma\sqrt h)^2\le2h(G^2/m+\sigma^2).\]

The remaining weighted factor satisfies \((1+(mh)^{-1})h^2\cdot2h=2h^2(h+1/m)\le4h^2/m\). The gap bound is \(2h\delta\le h^2K^2\); addition of \(h^2K^2\) and \(2h^2GK\) gives exactly \(mh^2\,C\). Positivity \(m,h>0\) justifies all denominators.



The use of \((a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2\) separates drift and noise. Under \(h\le1/m\) the additional powers of \(h\) are bounded by curvature constants, leaving a coefficient times \(h^2\). The gap contributes \(h^2K^2\), and the sum is \(mh^2C\). Division by the later contraction gap \(mh\) gives a squared transport error linear in the step.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{splittingCouplingForcing\_\allowbreak{}le}}
\leansource{\texttt{splittingCouplingForcing\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}SplittingRate.lean, lines 29–78}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ splittingCouplingForcing\_le\ \{m\ h\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ delta\ :\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)\ (hh\ :\ 0\ <\ h)\ (hsmall\ :\ h\ ≤\ 1\ /\ m)}
\leanline{4}{(\_hG\ :\ 0\ ≤\ G)\ (\_hnoise\ :\ 0\ ≤\ noise)\ (\_hK\ :\ 0\ ≤\ K)}
\leanline{4}{(hdelta\ :\ delta\ ≤\ h\ *\ K\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2)\ :}
\leanline{4}{splittingCouplingForcing\ m\ h\ L\ G\ noise\ K\ delta\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{m\ *\ h\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ splittingRMSConstant\ m\ L\ G\ noise\ K}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing\_\allowbreak{}le} is the scalar estimate used after the stationary coupling has supplied its four forcing terms. \texttt{hlocal} bounds the squared increment by twice the sum of squares. \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Gscale} uses \texttt{hsmall} to remove one time power, and \texttt{hlocal'} collects that estimate. \texttt{hweight} allows multiplication by the Young weight. The identity \texttt{heq} and bound \texttt{htwice} control its coefficient; \texttt{hdelta'} inserts the potential gap. Finally \texttt{h\allowbreak{}C} expands the proposed constant and \texttt{hfirst} bounds the weighted smooth-error term. Unfolding \texttt{splitting\allowbreak{}Coupling\allowbreak{}Forcing} leaves precisely the scalar inequality stated above. The actual law recurrence is established separately in the splitting endpoint module.



Formal correspondence: splittingCouplingForcing\_le : With a gap of at most \(hK^2/2\), the derived one-step forcing is bounded by \(mh^2C\), where \(C\) is independent of the smoothing parameter.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Transport convergence for equal forward and proximal scales}{Transport convergence for equal forward and proximal scales}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean}


The stationary one-step coupling gives an estimate for each input coupling. Taking the coupling infimum makes it a recurrence for the Gaussian transition laws. At equal forward and proximal scales, the potential gap meets the required linear-step bound, and the forcing is quadratic in the step with a finite step-independent coefficient. Geometric summation and the particle scaling then give the stated squared transport estimate.




Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.15]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The one-step transport recurrence}{The one-step transport recurrence}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean-1}


The admissible step and \(m\le L\) give \(mh\le1\). For each finite-cost input coupling the stationary construction gives a finite output coupling with the same forcing. Taking the cost infimum yields

\[W_2(\rho_{k+1},\pi)^2\le(1-mh)W_2(\rho_k,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\]

The forcing is nonnegative because \(e\le f\le e+\delta\) implies \(\delta\ge0\). In the smooth-part notation, \(m\le L_1\) and step admissibility give \(0<mh\le1\), hence \(q=1-mh\ge0\). The transition estimate is

\[W_2(\mu P,\pi)^2\le qW_2(\mu,\pi)^2+\mathcal F.\]

The reference target is fixed during iteration at the chosen scale. One finite input coupling proves finite output transport, while the estimate for every coupling gives the infimum bound. The condition \(mh\le1\) ensures its preceding-error coefficient is nonnegative, permitting iteration.

A coupling from \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}finite\allowbreak{}Cost\_\allowbreak{}coupling} gives finite output transport. \texttt{wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}bound\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}finite\_\allowbreak{}couplings} then takes the initial-cost infimum, using the nonnegative contraction factor and forcing. The forcing sign follows from the nonnegative potential gap and its explicit formula. Thus an optimal coupling is unnecessary.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The finite-time geometric estimate}{The finite-time geometric estimate}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean-2}


Measurability preserves probability, and the one-step bound preserves finite distance to the target. Under \(\delta\le hK^2/2\) the forcing is at most \(mh^2C\). For \(q=1-mh\in[0,1)\), geometric summation gives

\[W_2(\rho_n,\pi)^2\le(1-mh)^nW_2(\rho_0,\pi)^2+hC.\]

The denominator is \(1-q=mh>0\). Explicitly \(\delta\le hK^2/2\) gives \(\mathcal F\le mh^2C\), so the recurrence \(E_{n+1}\le(1-mh)E_n+mh^2C\) has geometric error term \((mh^2C)/(mh)=hC\) and conclusion

\[E_n\le(1-mh)^nE_0+hC.\]

Cancellation is valid because \(m,h>0\). For \(E_{k+1}\le qE_k+mh^2C\) and \(q=1-mh\), the finite-sum formula is \(E_n\le q^nE_0+mh^2C(1-q^n)/(1-q)\). Identity \(1-q=mh>0\) bounds its added term by \(hC\). The same induction proves normalization and finiteness needed to reapply the transition estimate at every step.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The lifted particle coefficient}{The lifted particle coefficient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean-3}


Let \(d=d_\theta+Nd_x\) and substitute \(\bar m=Nm\), \(\bar L=NL_1\), \(\bar K=NK\), \(\sigma=\sqrt{2d}\), and

\[G_N=\sqrt{2(NL_1)^2d/(Nm)+2(NK)^2}+NK.\]

The resulting coefficient is \(C_N=C(Nm,NL_1,G_N,\sqrt{2d},NK)\). Its nonnegativity follows from \(N,m>0\) and the scalar coefficient inequality. Both lifted force constants have factor \(N\), and the moment uses dimension \(D=d_\theta+Nd_x\). This defines \(C_N\) with all particle dependence explicit before the forward step is varied. Independence of the step does not imply independence of particle count.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The equal-step PIPGLA estimate}{The equal-step PIPGLA estimate}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingEndpoint-lean-4}


For \(\lambda=\gamma>0\) take \(h=\gamma/N\), \(T=\operatorname{Pair}_{P_\gamma}\), and \(\pi=\Pi_{N,\gamma}\), the Gibbs law of \(g_1+g_2^\gamma\). The summed envelope gap \(N\gamma K^2/2\) equals \(h(NK)^2/2\), as required by the forcing estimate. Identification with the Gaussian transition laws then gives

\[W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2\le(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N.\]

Coefficient \(C_N\) does not depend on \(\gamma\), although the target itself uses envelope scale \(\gamma\). In detail the gap \(N\gamma K^2/2\), with \(h=\gamma/N\) and \(K_N=NK\), is exactly \(hK_N^2/2\). The contraction factor is \(1-m\gamma\) and the floor is \((\gamma/N)C_N\). These are substitutions into the proved recurrence for the output laws, which equal those of the supplied splitting recursion.

The identity \texttt{hupdate} and the Gaussian law rewrites identify its iterates with the actual PIPGLA laws; \texttt{hfactor} cancels the particle count in the contraction exponent.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}{Project splitting transport to the original parameter center}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProjection-lean}


Finite moments of the splitting recursion justify parameter root mean square errors about any deterministic center. Projection converts full-state transport to the smoothed Gibbs law into parameter error about its maximizer. The finite potential-gap estimate then changes that center to the original marginal maximizer. The resulting inequality retains the transport distance until its quantitative splitting estimate is substituted.




Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.21]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{State and projected second moments}{State and projected second moments}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProjection-lean-1}


The splitting law is the pushforward of its \(L^2\) iterate. Change of variables gives \(z\mapsto z\in L^2(\rho_n)\). Bounded parameter projection and subtraction of a constant give \(\theta(z)-a\in L^2(\rho_n)\) for every \(a\).



Indeed the \(L^2\) state is sent into \(L^2\) by the linear projection. A deterministic center is an \(L^2\) constant under a probability measure, so their difference has finite second moment. These moment conclusions hold for every positive proximal scale before the more restrictive convergence-step condition is imposed.

The law equals the pushforward of \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain}, so the identity observable under that law has a finite second moment. The continuous parameter projection preserves \(L^2\), and subtracting a constant center also preserves it because the law is a probability. These two declarations establish finiteness before the later comparison writes a real square-root integral.

\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.1.6]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Transport, equilibrium concentration, and center displacement}{Transport, equilibrium concentration, and center displacement}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingProjection-lean-2}


The smoothed target has sharp equilibrium radius \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\), and the uniform gap gives \(\|\theta_U-\theta_\eta\|\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\). Projection of a coupling and Minkowski’s inequality therefore yield

\[\operatorname{RMS}_{\rho_n}(\theta_U)\le\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta}).\]

For algorithm law \(\rho_n\) and reference \(\Pi_{N,\eta}\), finite second moments ensure finite transport. The intermediate estimate is

\[R_{\theta_\eta}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}.\]

Changing the center by its deterministic displacement gives

\[R_{\theta_U}(\rho_n)\le W_2(\rho_n,\Pi_{N,\eta})+\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}+\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}.\]

For each coupling the projection does not increase its quadratic cost, and the reference parameter error is bounded by \(\sqrt{d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Taking the coupling infimum gives the transport contribution. The center change is at most \(\sqrt{\eta K^2/m}\) by the finite smoothing gap. These operations use the same smoothed target, and the subsequent splitting transport theorem supplies the remaining numerical bound.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}{Sensitivity to the proximal parameter}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean}


For a convex bounded-below \(K\)-Lipschitz function, the proximal map satisfies \(\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|\) at positive scales. The bound follows by representing one proximal point as a proximal point at the other scale with a shifted input. Jensen transfers this bound to the averaged particle map without an extra particle factor. Combined with common-innovation contraction, it gives finite-time and uniform-iteration comparisons of splitting chains at two scales.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.15]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Value Lipschitzness from bounded supporting subgradients}{Value Lipschitzness from bounded supporting subgradients}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean-1}


Suppose a subgradient \(s_x\) is chosen at every point with \(\|s_x\|\le K\). Its support evaluated at \(y\) and Cauchy–Schwarz give \(f(x)-f(y)\le\langle s_x,x-y\rangle\le K\|x-y\|\). Exchange \(x,y\) to bound the absolute difference.



Thus a subgradient section bounded by \(K\) gives value Lipschitz constant \(K\). Every selected vector must satisfy the supporting inequality; the norm bound alone is insufficient. No derivative of the potential is required.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded\_\allowbreak{}subgradients}}
\leansource{\texttt{lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded\_\allowbreak{}subgradients} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ProxParameter.lean, lines 22–42}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ lipschitz\_of\_bounded\_subgradients\ \{E\ :\ Type*\}\ [NormedAddCommGroup\ E]}
\leanline{4}{[InnerProductSpace\ ℝ\ E]\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}}
\leanline{4}{(hsub\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ∃\ s,\ IsSubgradient\ f\ x\ s\ ∧\ ‖s‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ))\ :}
\leanline{4}{LipschitzWith\ K\ f}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: lipschitz\_of\_bounded\_subgradients : A bounded genuine subgradient at every input implies global Lipschitzness. In particular, the paper's uniformly bounded minimal section supplies this hypothesis, without adding a separate Lipschitz assumption to Assumption C1.



\texttt{lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}bounded\_\allowbreak{}subgradients}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hupper}, \texttt{hsupport}, \texttt{hinner}, \texttt{h}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}neg\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{Lipschitz\allowbreak{}With.\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}dist\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{dist\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}le.\allowbreak{}mpr}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}rev}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Comparison of proximal minimizers at two positive scales}{Comparison of proximal minimizers at two positive scales}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean-2}


Write \(q=P_\eta(x)\) and \(s=(x-q)/\eta\). The residual characterization gives \(s\in\partial f(q)\) and \(\|s\|\le K\). The same support makes \(q\) the \(\lambda\)-proximal point of \(q+\lambda s\). Nonexpansiveness at scale \(\lambda\) yields

\[\|P_\lambda(x)-q\|\le\|x-(q+\lambda s)\|=|\eta-\lambda|\|s\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

In residual notation \(s=(x-P_\eta x)/\eta\), the second minimizer is also the \(\lambda\)-proximal point at \(P_\eta x+\lambda s\). Its comparison input is \(x=P_\eta x+\eta s\), so their separation is the scale difference times \(\|s\|\). The uniform residual bound gives \(K|\lambda-\eta|\) for every pair of positive scales.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}sensitivity}}
\leansource{\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}sensitivity} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}ProxParameter.lean, lines 46–69}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_parameter\_sensitivity\ \{E\ :\ Type*\}\ [NormedAddCommGroup\ E]}
\leanline{4}{[InnerProductSpace\ ℝ\ E]\ [FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]}
\leanline{4}{\{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hf\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b\ :\ ℝ,\ ∀\ x,\ b\ ≤\ f\ x)}
\leanline{4}{\{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ f)\ \{lam\ eta\ :\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{(hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ (heta\ :\ 0\ <\ eta)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖prox\ f\ lam\ x\ -\ prox\ f\ eta\ x‖\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ |lam\ -\ eta|}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Let \(q=P_\eta(x)\) and \(s=(x-q)/\eta\). The optimality theorem gives \(s\in\partial f(q)\), and global value Lipschitzness gives \(\|s\|\le K\). Reversing the optimality characterization shows that the same \(q\) is \(P_\lambda(q+\lambda s)\). Nonexpansiveness at parameter \(\lambda\) therefore gives \[\|P_\lambda(x)-P_\eta(x)\|\le\|x-(q+\lambda s)\|=|\eta-\lambda|\|s\|\le K|\lambda-\eta|.\] The local identities \texttt{hq}, \texttt{hxs} and \texttt{hid} implement exactly these substitutions; both parameters must be positive to cancel their residual denominators.



Formal correspondence: prox\_parameter\_sensitivity : The actual proximal point changes by at most \(K|\lambda-\eta|\) when the positive smoothing parameter changes.



\texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}sensitivity}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{q}, \texttt{s}, \texttt{hs}, \texttt{hxs}, \texttt{hid}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}subgradient}, \texttt{add\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}cancel\_\allowbreak{}left}, \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}comm}, \texttt{hnonexp.\allowbreak{}trans}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}comm}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Uniform comparison of the averaged pair maps}{Uniform comparison of the averaged pair maps}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean-3}


Linearity in the supplied pair map gives \(\operatorname{Pair}_{P-Q}=\operatorname{Pair}_P-\operatorname{Pair}_Q\). If \(\sup_x\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\), the averaged-square inequality bounds every summand by \(c^2\) and hence the state norm by \(c\). Proximal scale sensitivity therefore transfers with bound \(K|\lambda-\eta|\).



Explicitly \(\operatorname{Pair}(P-Q)=\operatorname{Pair}(P)-\operatorname{Pair}(Q)\). Under \(\|P(x)-Q(x)\|\le c\), Jensen and latent scaling give squared state difference at most \(N^{-1}\sum_i c^2=c^2\). Taking nonnegative square roots gives \(c\); substitute \(c=K|\lambda-\eta|\). Every pair difference is bounded by \(c\), and the state square is bounded by \(N^{-1}\sum_i c^2=c^2\), so the same constant \(c\) applies despite the common-parameter average.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The finite-time and uniform scale-perturbation estimates}{The finite-time and uniform scale-perturbation estimates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean-4}


Insert an update at the first scale and the second current state. Common-innovation contraction controls the change of state, and proximal sensitivity controls the change of scale. Thus

\[e_{n+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_n+K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

Induction gives \(e_n\le(1-m\gamma)^ne_0+K|\lambda-\eta|\sum_{j<n}(1-m\gamma)^j\). For \(\gamma>0\), the step restriction gives \(0\le1-m\gamma<1\), so the geometric sum is at most \(1/(m\gamma)\).



With intermediate input \(w\) and scale \(\lambda\) the same triangle inequality gives

\[e_{k+1}\le(1-m\gamma)e_k+K|\lambda-\eta|.\]

Under \(\gamma>0\), factor \(1-m\gamma<1\) gives floor \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). More precisely the two update changes are bounded by \(q e_k\) and \(K|\lambda-\eta|\). Summing under \(q=1-m\gamma<1\) yields \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). A common initialization removes the transient term. Since the innovations only need to agree, no distribution or independence assumption enters this pathwise estimate.

\leanline{4}{(hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ g₂)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)\ (hmL\ :\ m\ ≤\ (L₁\ :\ ℝ))}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A scale comparison within the specified interval}{A scale comparison within the specified interval}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-ProxParameter-lean-5}


Suppose \(0<m\gamma<1\) and \(\gamma\le\lambda\le\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\). Subtraction gives \(|\lambda-\gamma|=\lambda-\gamma\le m\gamma^2/(1-m\gamma)\). Divide by positive \(m\gamma\) and multiply by \(K\) to obtain \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\le K\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\).



The width of the allowed scale interval is \(\gamma/(1-m\gamma)-\gamma=m\gamma^2/(1-m\gamma)\). Its lower endpoint \(\gamma\) makes the absolute difference from the reference scale equal to the nonnegative difference. Division by \(m\gamma>0\) gives the bound. The strict condition \(m\gamma<1\) is required because the denominator vanishes at equality.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}corridor\_\allowbreak{}floor}}

\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ prox\_parameter\_corridor\_floor\ (m\ γ\ lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (K\ :\ ℝ≥0)}
\leanline{4}{(hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)\ (hγ\ :\ 0\ <\ γ)\ (hmg\ :\ m\ *\ γ\ <\ 1)}
\leanline{4}{(hlo\ :\ γ\ ≤\ lam)\ (hhi\ :\ lam\ ≤\ γ\ /\ (1\ -\ m\ *\ γ))\ :}
\leanline{4}{(K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ |lam\ -\ γ|\ /\ (m\ *\ γ)\ ≤\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ γ\ /\ (1\ -\ m\ *\ γ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
In the corridor \(\gamma\le\lambda\le\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\), positivity of \(1-m\gamma\) gives \[0\le\lambda-\gamma\le\frac{m\gamma^2}{1-m\gamma}.\] Therefore \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\le K\gamma/(1-m\gamma)\). The Lean proof first removes the absolute value using the lower corridor inequality, then clears the two positive denominators. This term measures the additional error incurred by changing the proximal parameter away from the equal-step algorithm.



Formal correspondence: prox\_parameter\_corridor\_floor : In the paper's proximal-parameter corridor, the additional comparison floor against \(\eta=\gamma\) is of order \(\gamma\), with every denominator displayed.

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}{PIPGLA about the original center for every positive proximal scale}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTheorem-lean}


Assume the stated strong convexity and smoothness of \(g_1\), and convexity, a lower bound, and \(K\)-Lipschitz values for \(g_2\). Let \(N>0\), \(0<m\le L_1\), \(\lambda,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_1)\). Initialization belongs to \(L^2\) and all Gaussian innovation coordinates are mutually independent and independent of initialization. Compare the chain at its chosen proximal scale with the equal-step chain. Its transport bound, parameter concentration, and finite center displacement then give the error estimate relative to the original marginal maximizer with the scale-mismatch contribution retained.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The root mean square comparison of two scales}{The root mean square comparison of two scales}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTheorem-lean-1}


Use the same initial variable and innovations for the \(\lambda\) and \(\eta\) chains. Their uniform state separation is at most \(K|\lambda-\eta|/(m\gamma)\). Parameter projection is nonexpansive, and both chains have finite second moments. Minkowski therefore gives

\[\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(a)\le\operatorname{RMS}_\eta(a)+\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}.\]

This is an inequality for their \(L^2\) realizations. More explicitly, let \(X\) and \(Y\) be the \(L^2\) iterates at scales \(\lambda\) and \(\eta\). The pathwise bound is

\[\|X(\omega)-Y(\omega)\|\le c:=\frac{K|\lambda-\eta|}{m\gamma}\quad\text{almost surely}.\]

Its \(L^2\) norm is at most the same constant because the source has mass one. Projection followed by Minkowski adds the reference-center error. Pushforward integration identifies the two ordinary law integrals, both finite by the moment result.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}comparison}}

\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ pipgla\_parameter\_rms\_prox\_comparison}
\leanline{4}{\{Ω\ :\ Type*\}\ [MeasurableSpace\ Ω]\ (Q\ :\ Measure\ Ω)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ Q]}
\leanline{4}{\{N\ dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}\ (hN\ :\ 0\ <\ N)}

\leanline{4}{(hg₁\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ g₁)\ (hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ univ\ m\ g₁)}
\leanline{4}{(hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L₁\ (gradient\ g₁))}
\leanline{4}{(hg₂\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ g₂)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ g₂\ z)}
\leanline{4}{(hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ g₂)\ (hm\ :\ 0\ <\ m)\ (hmL\ :\ m\ ≤\ (L₁\ :\ ℝ))}

\leanline{6}{(K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ |lam\ -\ eta|\ /\ (m\ *\ γ)}

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{pipgla\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}comparison} constructs the two square-integrable chains \texttt{X} and \texttt{Y} on the same probability space and the comparison radius \texttt{c}. \texttt{hpoint} uses \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Chain\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}comparison\_\allowbreak{}bound} at common initial input, and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}XY} integrates the resulting pointwise bound. The centered projected vectors \texttt{A} and \texttt{B} satisfy \texttt{h\allowbreak{}AB}; projection contraction and the triangle inequality give \texttt{hn}. \texttt{hnorm} identifies each law-based root integral with its corresponding Hilbert-space norm using \texttt{pipgla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}lp\allowbreak{}Law}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}map} and \texttt{lp\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}integral}. Both proximal scales are positive; this comparison does not require the noises themselves to be independent Gaussian vectors.



The transient term vanishes because both initial states agree. The full-measure representative equalities for the two chains and their subtraction justify this substitution in \texttt{hpoint}. Since \(Q\) has total mass one, integrating the uniform bound gives \(\|X-Y\|_{L^2}\le c\); this is \texttt{h\allowbreak{}XY}.



Define \(A=P_\theta X-\theta\) and \(B=P_\theta Y-\theta\) as \(L^2\) variables. Linearity gives \(A-B=P_\theta(X-Y)\), and the projection norm bound gives \(\|A-B\|_2\le\|X-Y\|_2\). Thus \(\|A\|_2\le\|B\|_2+c\), the inequality \texttt{hn}. Finally, \texttt{hnorm} first identifies each actual law as the pushforward of its \(L^2\) iterate, then changes variables in the quadratic integral and uses \(\sqrt{\|A\|_2^2}=\|A\|_2\). All these norms are finite by construction. Substituting those two identities into \texttt{hn} proves the stated RMS comparison.



Formal correspondence: pipgla\_parameter\_rms\_prox\_comparison : Actual PIPGLA parameter RMS values at two positive proximal parameters are separated by the proved pathwise parameter-sensitivity floor.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}; \cite[Section~3.1.6]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The PIPGLA estimate about the original marginal maximizer}{The PIPGLA estimate about the original marginal maximizer}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-SplittingTheorem-lean-2}


Choose comparison scale \(\eta=\gamma\). The equal-step transport estimate and parameter projection relative to the original marginal maximizer give

\[\begin{aligned}\operatorname{RMS}_\lambda(\theta_U)\le{}&\frac{K|\lambda-\gamma|}{m\gamma}+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\&+\sqrt{(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})^2+\frac\gamma N C_N}.\end{aligned}\]

The explicit finite coefficient \(C_N\) is independent of the forward step. Every positive \(\lambda\) is allowed, with its displayed mismatch term. Convergence as \(\gamma\to0\) additionally requires control of that term. The coefficient is the proved one and does not reproduce the source’s printed heat-flow constants.



At reference scale \(\gamma\), the transport estimate gives the square root of the transient squared distance plus its floor. Sharp equilibrium concentration and the finite smoothing displacement add the other terms. For chosen scale \(\lambda\), common-innovation sensitivity gives \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\). Its constant bound has the same root mean square value under a probability source, giving the addition \(K|\lambda-\gamma|/(m\gamma)\). Thus allowance of all positive \(\lambda\) is distinct from a bound uniform as the forward step tends to zero. The preceding scale-interval estimate gives a simpler mismatch bound when that interval condition holds.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-noise cost}{Orthogonal masks and the exact removed-noise cost}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean}


Let \(P_\theta\) be the orthogonal projection onto the common parameter block and \(P_x=I-P_\theta\) the complementary latent projection. For standard Gaussian \(\xi\), the parameter projection has mean zero and squared moment \(d_\theta\). Coupling the latent-only and full-noise updates with the same Gaussian vector leaves exactly this omitted parameter block. Independence makes its mixed term with the preceding discrepancy vanish.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.10]{LangevinLean2026ULALSI}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Complementary parameter and latent projections}{Complementary parameter and latent projections}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean-1}


Define \(P_\theta(\theta,x)=(\theta,0)\) and \(P_x(\theta,x)=(0,x)\). These bounded linear maps preserve square integrability, so an \(L^2\) innovation remains an \(L^2\) random vector after either projection.



Coordinate evaluation gives \(P_\theta+P_x=I\), \(P_\theta P_x=0\), \(P_\theta^2=P_\theta\), and \(P_x^2=P_x\). Their ranges are therefore complementary orthogonal parameter and latent subspaces. Applying \(P_x\) to the noise removes its parameter component and leaves the deterministic drift unchanged, which is the update difference in the MYPGD comparison.

Denote the two maps by \(P_\theta\) and \(P_x=I-P_\theta\). On a scaled particle state, \(P_\theta(\theta,z_1,\ldots,z_N)=(\theta,0,\ldots,0)\), while \(P_x(\theta,z_1,\ldots,z_N)=(0,z_1,\ldots,z_N)\). The four coordinate lemmas establish these formulas directly; the subtraction defining \texttt{latent\allowbreak{}Noise\allowbreak{}CLM} is an equality of continuous linear maps, so all later compositions are measurable.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Projection norms and Gaussian moments}{Projection norms and Gaussian moments}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean-2}


The parameter projection satisfies \(\|P_\theta z\|^2=\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\) and hence \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\). Linearity of expectation gives \(\mathbb E P_\theta\xi=0\); the unit second moment of each standard Gaussian coordinate gives \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta\xi\|^2=d_\theta\).



In the coordinate expansion \(\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}z_j^2\), each summand is integrable because the Gaussian vector belongs to \(L^2\). The identities \(\mathbb E z_j^2=1\) and \(\mathbb E P_\theta Z=P_\theta\mathbb EZ=0\) therefore give \(\mathbb E\|P_\theta Z\|^2=d_\theta\) by a finite sum. No factor \(N\) occurs: the projection retains exactly \(d_\theta\) scalar coordinates and their variances sum to \(d_\theta\). The particle count enters only when this noise block is multiplied by amplitude \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The zero mixed expectation and omitted-noise variance}{The zero mixed expectation and omitted-noise variance}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDNoise-lean-3}


Let \(g\) be square-integrable and independent of standard Gaussian \(\xi\). Expansion of \(\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2\) is integrable term by term by Cauchy–Schwarz. Fubini and bilinearity identify the mixed expectation as \(\langle\mathbb E g,\mathbb E P_\theta\xi\rangle=0\), so

\[\mathbb E\|g+bP_\theta\xi\|^2=\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d_\theta.\]

The independence is between the fresh Gaussian and \(g\); the discrepancy \(g\) need not be centered. In particular \(\mathbb E\langle g,P_\theta\xi\rangle\) factors into the inner product of the two means, and the Gaussian mean is zero. Either sign of the removed parameter block therefore adds exactly \(b^2d_\theta\) to the squared error.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}parameterNoise\_\allowbreak{}sq}}
\leansource{\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}parameterNoise\_\allowbreak{}sq} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDNoise.lean, lines 87–130}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integral\_add\_parameterNoise\_sq}
\leanline{4}{\{A\ :\ Type*\}\ [MeasurableSpace\ A]\ (μ\ :\ Measure\ A)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ μ]}
\leanline{4}{\{N\ dθ\ dx\ :\ ℕ\}\ (g\ :\ A\ →\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ (hg\ :\ MemLp\ g\ 2\ μ)\ (b\ :\ ℝ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ p\ :\ A\ ×\ IPLA.State\ N\ dθ\ dx,\ ‖g\ p.1\ +\ b\ •\ parameterNoiseCLM\ p.2‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\leanline{6}{∂μ.prod\ (stdGaussian\ \_))\ =\ (∫\ x,\ ‖g\ x‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ∂μ)\ +\ b\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ (dθ\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
For a general \(g\in L^2(\mu)\), use the actual product law \(\mu\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\), and expand \[\|g(x)+bP_\theta z\|^2=\|g(x)\|^2+2b\langle g(x),P_\theta z\rangle+b^2\|P_\theta z\|^2.\] The facts \texttt{hgsq} and \texttt{hzsq} prove integrability of the two squares on the product; \texttt{hcross} proves integrability of the bilinear term, so splitting the integral is justified. Independence here is the product-measure construction itself: \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}prod\_\allowbreak{}bilin} factors the mixed integral as \(\langle\mathbb Eg,\mathbb E P_\theta Z\rangle=0\). Hence \(\mathbb E\|g+bP_\theta Z\|^2=\mathbb E\|g\|^2+b^2d_\theta\), with no assumption that \(g\) is centered.



Formal correspondence: integral\_add\_parameterNoise\_sq : Exact independent-noise identity, for an arbitrary square-integrable source. The mixed term vanishes by the actual product Gaussian law.

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{ring}.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{A coupling of latent-only and full Gaussian transitions}{A coupling of latent-only and full Gaussian transitions}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean}


Let \(r\)-Lipschitz map \(D\) define the common deterministic update. Compare \(D(x)+aP_x\xi\) and \(D(y)+a\xi\) using the same independent standard Gaussian vector. The omitted parameter block has a known variance, and its mixed expectation with the input discrepancy is zero. For probability input laws with finite second moments, this constructs a finite-cost coupling of the two transition laws.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.22]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.15]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Finite moments of Lipschitz images and finite coupling costs}{Finite moments of Lipschitz images and finite coupling costs}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean-1}


For map \(D\), the bound \(\|D(x)-D(0)\|\le r\|x\|\) and addition of its constant value at zero show preservation of \(L^2\). If two \(L^2\) vectors are defined on one probability space, their joint distribution is a coupling. Its quadratic cost bounds \(W_2\) and yields \(W_2(\mathcal L(X),\mathcal L(Y))^2\le\mathbb E\|X-Y\|^2\).



A joint realization \((X,Y)\) is thus an admissible candidate in the infimum defining \(W_2\). The same finite squared separation proves transport finiteness and the numerical upper bound. The map need not vanish at zero because its constant part has finite second moment under a probability measure.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{memLp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}comp}, \texttt{wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}pair}}
