\leanline{4}{\{X\ :\ A\ →\ E\}\ (hX\ :\ MemLp\ X\ 2\ P)\ :\ MemLp\ (fun\ x\ =>\ D\ (X\ x))\ 2\ P}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}integral\_\allowbreak{}pair} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MYPGDTransport.lean, lines 41–61}
\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{wasserstein2\ (P.map\ X)\ (P.map\ Y)\ ≠\ ⊤\ ∧}
\leanline{4}{wasserstein2Real\ (P.map\ X)\ (P.map\ Y)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ ∫\ x,\ ‖X\ x\ -\ Y\ x‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ∂P}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
A globally Lipschitz map \(D\) need not vanish at zero. The first lemma writes \(D(X)=[D(X)-D(0)]+D(0)\): the centered map preserves \(L^2\), and the constant belongs to \(L^2\) because the measure is a probability. For the transport lemma, convert \(X,Y\) to their \(L^2\) classes. Their joint realization is an admissible coupling of their pushforwards, so \[W_2(X_\#P,Y_\#P)^2\le\|X-Y\|_{L^2(P)}^2=\int\|X-Y\|^2dP.\] The representative equalities \texttt{hx}, \texttt{hy} prove that these pushforwards are the original laws, and the finite norm bound proves finite Wasserstein distance before squaring its real value.



Formal correspondence: memLp\_lipschitz\_comp : Globally Lipschitz maps preserve finite quadratic moments on a probability space. wasserstein\_sq\_le\_integral\_pair : Direct finite-cost transport estimate for an actual \(L^2\) pair.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Measurability and moments of the two Gaussian transitions}{Measurability and moments of the two Gaussian transitions}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean-2}


The transition maps are \(T_x(u,\xi)=D(u)+aP_x\xi\) and \(T(u,\xi)=D(u)+a\xi\). Continuity of \(D\), linearity of \(P_x\), and continuity of addition make them jointly measurable. On \(\mu\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\) both summands belong to \(L^2\).



The squared norm of a sum is at most twice the sum of squared norms, so both output distributions have finite second moments. This integrability also justifies expansion and Fubini in the subsequent exact mixed-term calculation. The product distribution explicitly separates the input from the fresh noise.

The sum is in \(L^2\); \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}map\_\allowbreak{}measure\_\allowbreak{}iff} then transfers this fact to the output law. \subsection{\texorpdfstring{Extension of an input coupling by independent Gaussian noise}{Extension of an input coupling by independent Gaussian noise}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTransport-lean-3}


Given any finite-cost input coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), extend it independently to \(c\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\). The output marginals are the latent-only and full Gaussian transitions. Their difference is \(D(x)-D(y)-aP_\theta\xi\). The zero mixed-term identity gives squared cost \(\mathbb E_c\|D(x)-D(y)\|^2+a^2d_\theta\), at most \(r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\) by Lipschitz continuity.



The first marginal uses law \(\mu\) and latent Gaussian noise, while the second uses \(\nu\) and full Gaussian noise. In random-vector notation the difference is \(D(X)-D(Y)-aP_\theta\xi\). Independence gives mean square at most \(r^2\mathbb E\|X-Y\|^2+a^2d_\theta\). Thus the same bound holds for every finite-cost input coupling, which permits the Wasserstein infimum without choosing an optimal coupling.

\leanline{6}{(markovStep\ (fullGaussianUpdate\ D\ a)\ (stdGaussian\ \_)\ ν)\ ≠\ ⊤\ ∧}

\leanline{6}{(markovStep\ (latentGaussianUpdate\ D\ a)\ (stdGaussian\ \_)\ μ)}
\leanline{6}{(markovStep\ (fullGaussianUpdate\ D\ a)\ (stdGaussian\ \_)\ ν)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{(r\ :\ ℝ)\textasciicircum{}2\ *\ (couplingCost\ c.joint).toReal\textasciicircum{}2\ +\ a\textasciicircum{}2*(dθ\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Start from any input coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), and work on \(c\otimes\mathcal N(0,I)\). Define \(F=D(X)+aP_xZ\) and \(H=D(Y)+aZ\). The facts \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Flaw} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Hlaw} check separately that their marginals are the desired two Markov transitions; this ensures the constructed pair is a coupling of the actual algorithms.



The cancellation in \texttt{hid} is \[F-H=D(X)-D(Y)-aP_\theta Z.\] By the product Gaussian identity, its mean square is \(\mathbb E_c\|D(X)-D(Y)\|^2+a^2d_\theta\). If \(D\) is \(r\)-Lipschitz, the first term is bounded by \(r^2\mathbb E_c\|X-Y\|^2\). Finally \texttt{hnorm} identifies the latter integral with the square of the coupling cost. All three random variables are proved square integrable before any of these integral identities is used.



Formal correspondence: latent\_full\_markov\_coupling\_sq : Actual latent-noise and full-noise one-step laws admit the stated squared coupling bound. The independent centered parameter noise supplies exactly \(a^2d_\theta\), and has no mixed contribution.



\texttt{latent\_\allowbreak{}full\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}coupling\_\allowbreak{}sq}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{Q}, \texttt{P}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X}, \texttt{X}, \texttt{hn}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{c.\allowbreak{}fst}, \texttt{h.\allowbreak{}comp\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}map}, \texttt{coe\allowbreak{}Fn\_\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Lp.\allowbreak{}fun\_\allowbreak{}comp}, \texttt{add\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}add}, \texttt{le\_\allowbreak{}rfl}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Iterated finite-particle noise-removal comparison}{Iterated finite-particle noise-removal comparison}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean}


Consider finite-second-moment latent-only and full-noise chains with the same deterministic \(r\)-contractive map \(D\) and noise amplitude \(a\). The one-step coupling gives a squared Wasserstein recurrence. From a common initial law, geometric summation bounds their separation uniformly over finite iteration counts. For the smoothed gradient map this yields an explicit radius depending on parameter dimension and particle count.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The transition estimate after the coupling infimum}{The transition estimate after the coupling infimum}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean-1}


For every finite-cost input coupling the transition estimate is \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2\operatorname{cost}(c)^2+a^2d_\theta\). Finite input second moments give \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), so taking the coupling infimum yields \(W_2(T_x\mu,T\nu)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta\).



The additive term \(a^2d_\theta\) is independent of the chosen coupling. Since \(W_2(\mu,\nu)<\infty\), the infimum is finite and the equivalent kernel notation gives

\[W_2(\mu P_x,\nu P)^2\le r^2W_2(\mu,\nu)^2+a^2d_\theta.\]

The infimum replaces the squared input cost by \(W_2(\mu,\nu)^2\), with nonnegative coefficient \(r^2\). One finite input coupling already proves a finite output cost. Thus neither finiteness nor the recurrence assumes attainment of the optimal coupling.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The geometric comparison from a common initial law}{The geometric comparison from a common initial law}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean-2}


Measurable transitions preserve probability, and their linear-growth moment bounds preserve \(L^2\) at every finite iteration. Starting both chains from \(\mu\) gives zero initial transport by the diagonal coupling. Under \(0\le r<1\), geometric summation yields \(W_2(\rho_n^x,\rho_n)^2\le a^2d_\theta/(1-r^2)\).



For recurrence \(E_{k+1}\le r^2E_k+a^2d_\theta\) with zero initial value, induction gives \(E_k\le a^2d_\theta\sum_{j<k}r^{2j}\). The condition \(r<1\) makes \(1-r^2\) positive, so the finite geometric sum is bounded by its infinite-series value. This proves the stated uniform bound while retaining finite moments at each iteration.

Let \(E_k=W_2(\mu_k^x,\mu_k^{\mathrm{full}})^2\), with the same initial law. The two inductions \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Mm} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Im} propagate finite second moments, while \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Mp} and \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Ip} propagate probability mass. Therefore the one-step theorem applies at every \(k\), giving \(E_{k+1}\le r^2E_k+a^2d_\theta\). A diagonal realization proves \(E_0=0\). Since \(0\le r<1\), the affine-recurrence sum yields \[E_k\le a^2d_\theta\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}r^{2j}\le\frac{a^2d_\theta}{1-r^2}.\] This recurrence is derived from the transitions, rather than a hypothesis of this theorem.

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{nlinarith}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Contraction of the smoothed gradient step}{Contraction of the smoothed gradient step}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGD-lean-3}


The smoothed gradient step contracts by \(r=1-m\gamma\) under \(0<m\le L_\eta\) and \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\). For each input coupling, use a common standard Gaussian vector for the latent-only and full transitions. The exact omitted-noise variance gives

\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_{k+1},\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_{k+1})^2
 \le r^2 W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_k,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_k)^2
       +\frac{2\gamma d_\theta}{N}.
\]

Take the input coupling infimum at each step. From the same initial law, \(0\le r<1\) and \(1-r^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\ge m\gamma\) give

\[
W_2(\rho^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n,\rho^{\mathrm{IPLA}}_n)^2
 \le\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}.
\]

For \(V_\eta=g_1+g_2^\eta\), the envelope results give curvature \(m\) and gradient constant \(L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\). Lifting gives \(Nm,NL_\eta\), so the step \(\gamma/N\) has contraction \(r=1-m\gamma\). Restriction \(0<\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\) and \(m\le L_\eta\) imply \(0\le m\gamma\le1\) and thus \(0\le r<1\).



With \(r=1-m\gamma\) and \(a^2=2\gamma/N\), the denominator in the geometric sum is \(1-r^2=m\gamma(2-m\gamma)\). Inequality \(m\gamma\le1\) bounds it below by \(m\gamma>0\). Cancellation of the physical step gives the squared radius \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\). This is an upper bound on the difference between two finite-particle laws. It asserts neither that removing noise worsens the algorithm nor that a mean-field limit has been taken.

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYPGD recursion and transition laws}{MYPGD recursion and transition laws}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean}


MYPGD retains the Moreau residual drift and latent Gaussian noise of MYIPLA while removing its parameter Gaussian component. Equality of the update maps identifies the expanded proximal recursion with the masked-noise Euler recursion. The independent Gaussian-sequence assumptions then identify its laws with the corresponding iterated Markov transition, to which the finite-particle noise comparison applies.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.33]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.41]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The recursion with latent-projected innovations}{The recursion with latent-projected innovations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean-1}


Replace the innovation \(\xi\) in MYIPLA by \(P_x\xi\). This defines MYPGD. The recursion is MYIPLA driven by sequence \((P_x\xi_k)_k\) because initialization and successor updates agree.



The deterministic terms are identical at every step; only the supplied innovation is projected. Induction therefore proves equality of the pointwise chains before any distributional assumptions are made. Their laws are subsequently the measurable pushforwards of this same recursion.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The common deterministic smoothed gradient map}{The common deterministic smoothed gradient map}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean-2}


Let \(F_\eta=\sum_i(g_1+g_2^\eta)(\theta,x_i)\) and \(h=\gamma/N\). The residual-envelope identity gives the update

\[
X_{k+1}=X_k-h\nabla F_\eta(X_k)+\sqrt{2h}\,P_x\xi_k.
\]

The parameter coordinate has no additive Gaussian term, while each physical latent coordinate has coefficient \(\sqrt{2\gamma}\). Finite-second-moment initialization and independent standard Gaussian coordinates, independent of initialization, define the transition laws. The unused parameter Gaussians may remain on the probability space for comparison with MYIPLA; no initial independent-particle assumption is required.



The bound \(\operatorname{Lip}(I-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_\eta)\le1+|\gamma/N|\,NL_\eta\) ensures continuity and measurable finite-moment updates without imposing contraction. In particular the deterministic map is \(D(z)=z-(\gamma/N)\nabla F_\eta(z)\), and the physical latent noise coefficient is \(\sqrt{2\gamma}\). The stricter step restriction is required only for the quantitative contractive comparison.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Markov transition from independent Gaussian coordinates}{The Markov transition from independent Gaussian coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDAlgorithm-lean-3}


Mutual independence of all scalar Gaussian innovations gives independent standard vector innovations, independent of initialization. Consequently the two supplied chains have the Markov transitions \(D+aP_x\xi\) and \(D+a\xi\).



The common deterministic map is \(D(z)=z-(\gamma/N)\nabla V_{N,\eta}(z)\). MYPGD adds \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}P_x\xi\) and MYIPLA adds \(\sqrt{2\gamma/N}\xi\). At time \(k\) the current state is a measurable function of initialization and preceding innovations, so the next Gaussian vector is independent of it. Projection gives the latent-only kernel; retention of the full vector gives the MYIPLA kernel. Induction identifies their supplied laws with the iterated transition laws used in the comparison theorem.

\section{\texorpdfstring{MYPGD error about the original marginal maximizer}{MYPGD error about the original marginal maximizer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean}


Assume the potential, smoothing, initialization, and Gaussian conditions of the MYIPLA theorem relative to the original marginal maximizer, together with \(m\le L_\eta=L_1+\eta^{-1}\), \(N>0\), \(m,\eta,\gamma>0\), and \(\gamma\le2/(m+L_\eta)\). The MYPGD latent-only transition and the MYIPLA full transition have a finite-particle Wasserstein comparison. Parameter projection adds its radius to the MYIPLA estimate, yielding comparison, smoothing, equilibrium, initial-transport, and discretization contributions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The squared transport comparison of the two laws}{The squared transport comparison of the two laws}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean-1}


Both recursions have the same smoothed deterministic map and initial pushforward. The initial representative belongs to \(L^2\), so this law has a finite second moment. Identification of their Gaussian transition laws allows the iterated comparison theorem to give finite transport and \(W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})^2\le2d_\theta/(Nm)\).



The squared comparison radius is \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\). All output moments follow from the finite-moment transition bounds. The comparison therefore applies to the stated algorithm laws, independently of the particular probability space used to realize the innovations.

It applies \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}myipla\_\allowbreak{}markov\_\allowbreak{}transport\_\allowbreak{}sq}, the transition-law coupling theorem containing the centered-noise cancellation and geometric recurrence. The two rewrites \texttt{mypgd\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws} and \texttt{myipla\allowbreak{}Laws\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}markov\allowbreak{}Laws} identify these transition iterates with the actual algorithms under \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Gaussian}. Its conclusion includes finite transport before the ordinary real squared-distance bound. The following declaration \texttt{mypgd\_\allowbreak{}parameter\_\allowbreak{}rms\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}myipla} applies projection to that finite bound.



Formal correspondence: mypgd\_myipla\_actual\_transport\_sq : The actual MYPGD and MYIPLA laws have the proved finite transport bound. Their common full Gaussian input is used only to identify the two different Markov transitions; the MYPGD output has latent-only noise.

Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.17.3]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.4]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The parameter-error comparison under projection}{The parameter-error comparison under projection}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean-2}


The parameter projection has operator norm at most one. Projection of a coupling and Minkowski’s inequality give \(\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm PGD}(\theta)\le W_2(\rho_n^{\rm PGD},\rho_n^{\rm IPLA})+\operatorname{RMS}_{\rm IPLA}(\theta)\). The finite comparison distance and finite MYIPLA moment prove finiteness of the MYPGD parameter moment as well. The square root of the squared comparison estimate is \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\).



In root mean square notation the inequality is

\[R_{\mathrm{MYPGD}}(\theta_*)\le R_{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}(\theta_*)+W_2(\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYPGD}},\rho_n^{\mathrm{MYIPLA}}).\]

The squared last term is at most \(2d_\theta/(Nm)\), and nonnegative square roots bound it by \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Equivalently, minimizing the projected coupling estimate gives the added radius \(\sqrt{2d_\theta/(Nm)}\). Substitution of the MYIPLA theorem relative to the original marginal maximizer retains its finite smoothing displacement, sharp equilibrium radius, initial transport term, and discretization term.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The MYPGD bound about the original marginal maximizer}{The MYPGD bound about the original marginal maximizer}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MYPGDTheorem-lean-3}


Apply the projected transport inequality to the two algorithm laws and substitute the MYIPLA estimate relative to the original marginal maximizer. With \(L_\eta=L_1+1/\eta\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\left(\mathbb E\|\theta^{\mathrm{PGD}}_n-\theta_\star\|^2\right)^{1/2}
\le{}&\sqrt{\frac{2d_\theta}{Nm}}
 +\sqrt{\frac{\eta K^2}{m}}+\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm}}\\
&+(1-m\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\eta})\\
&+\frac{33}{20}\frac{L_\eta}{m}
 \sqrt{d_x+\frac{d_\theta}{N}}\sqrt\gamma.
\end{aligned}
\]

The result is finite under the stated assumptions. It is a finite-particle version related to the source’s Appendix B theorem: its initial transient is measured from the smoothed Gibbs target and it adds a conservative noise-comparison radius. It does not reproduce the source’s mean-field metric, constants, or stronger Taylor smoothing claim. The remaining discretization coefficient contains \(L_1+1/\eta\). Thus the estimate retains its dependence on smoothing scale and does not assert a bound for a path supremum.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Wasserstein regularity of the integrated parameter force}{Wasserstein regularity of the integrated parameter force}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean}


For pair potential \(V\) with \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, define the parameter force by \(b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\mu(dx)\). Finite latent second moments and linear gradient growth make this a Bochner integral. Comparing the integrands on arbitrary couplings gives a bound in quadratic Wasserstein distance. Parameter projection is a contraction, so the joint-gradient regularity constant also controls this force.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.21]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.22]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expectation differences for a Lipschitz observable}{Expectation differences for a Lipschitz observable}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean-1}


For a Lipschitz observable \(\phi\), subtract \(\phi(0)\). Its centered growth is bounded by \(K\|x\|\), giving \(\phi(X)\in L^2\subset L^1\). For any finite-cost coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\), the norm-of-integral inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz give

\[\left\|\int\phi\,d\mu-\int\phi\,d\nu\right\|\le K\left(\int\|x-y\|^2dc\right)^{1/2}.\]

The infimum of coupling costs gives \(K W_2(\mu,\nu)\). The coupling integral identity is

\[\int\varphi\,d\mu-\int\varphi\,d\nu=\int[\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)]\,dc(x,y).\]

Integrability includes the observable’s value at zero. Its Lipschitz constant bounds the integrand difference by the pair distance, and \(\|\cdot\|_1\le\|\cdot\|_2\) bounds its first moment by its quadratic cost. The input second moments make the infimum finite; no optimal coupling is required.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{memLp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}observable}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2}}
\leansource{\texttt{memLp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}observable} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldDrift.lean, lines 34–44}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ memLp\_lipschitz\_observable\ (P\ :\ Measure\ Ω)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ P]}
\leanline{4}{\{φ\ :\ E\ →\ F\}\ \{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hφ\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ φ)}
\leanline{4}{\{X\ :\ Ω\ →\ E\}\ (hX\ :\ MemLp\ X\ 2\ P)\ :\ MemLp\ (fun\ ω\ =>\ φ\ (X\ ω))\ 2\ P}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldDrift.lean, lines 50–108}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integral\_lipschitz\_wasserstein2}
\leanline{4}{(μ\ ν\ :\ Measure\ E)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ μ]\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ ν]}
\leanline{4}{(hμ\ :\ MemLp\ (fun\ x\ :\ E\ =>\ x)\ 2\ μ)\ (hν\ :\ MemLp\ (fun\ x\ :\ E\ =>\ x)\ 2\ ν)}
\leanline{4}{\{φ\ :\ E\ →\ F\}\ \{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hφ\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ φ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖(∫\ x,\ φ\ x\ ∂μ)\ -\ ∫\ x,\ φ\ x\ ∂ν‖\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{(K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ ULA.wasserstein2Real\ μ\ ν}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
The nonzero value \(\varphi(0)\) must be retained when proving integrability. Write \(\varphi(X)=[\varphi(X)-\varphi(0)]+\varphi(0)\). The first term is in \(L^2\) because its Lipschitz map vanishes at zero; the second is a constant in \(L^2\) on a probability space. This proves \texttt{mem\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}observable}, including the offset.



For the transport estimate, take any coupling \(c\) of \(\mu,\nu\). Its marginal identities pull the given second moments onto its two coordinate random variables. Applying the observable lemma to both coordinates proves their \(L^2\) membership and hence their Bochner integrability. The fact \texttt{hid} then changes variables using those marginals and combines the two integrals:



The successive inequalities in \texttt{hfamily} are the integral triangle inequality, the probability-space comparison \(\|F\|_1\le\|F\|_2\), and the pointwise Lipschitz bound \(\|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\|\le K\|x-y\|\). They give the same upper bound \(K\,\operatorname{cost}(c)\) for every coupling. Taking the infimum in the extended nonnegative reals proves \texttt{hbound}. Finite input second moments already imply finite \(W_2\), so the product \(KW_2\) is finite and the final conversion to real numbers preserves the inequality.



Formal correspondence: memLp\_lipschitz\_observable : A Lipschitz observable with a nonzero offset still preserves finite second moments on a probability space. integral\_lipschitz\_wasserstein2 : Actual expectations of a Lipschitz observable are Lipschitz in \(W_2\). Both input probability laws have finite second moment; infinity is never removed by an unguarded real conversion.

\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein2}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hfinite}, \texttt{hfamily}, \texttt{hfst}, \texttt{hbound}, \texttt{hr}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{c.\allowbreak{}fst}, \texttt{hh.\allowbreak{}comp\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}map}, \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Real\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{ENNReal.\allowbreak{}coe\_\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Real}, \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}wasserstein2\allowbreak{}Real}. The \texttt{calc} block checks the successive equalities or inequalities in the argument.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The parameter block of the joint gradient}{The parameter block of the joint gradient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean-2}


The orthogonal projection \(P_\theta\) keeps the parameter coordinates and satisfies \(\|P_\theta z\|\le\|z\|\) because their squared norm is a subsum of the full squared norm. Define \(\phi_\theta(x)=P_\theta\nabla V(\theta,x)\) and integrate this observable against the latent law.



Projection preserves the joint-gradient Lipschitz and linear-growth bounds. Finite latent second moment implies finite first moment, so the resulting parameter force is a finite Bochner expectation. It is the integrated parameter block of the specified potential gradient, rather than an independently assumed vector field.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The two-coordinate gradient perturbation bound}{The two-coordinate gradient perturbation bound}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean-3}


Decomposing the pair difference into parameter and latent inclusions gives \(\|(\theta,x)-(\theta',x')\|\le\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|\). The joint-gradient Lipschitz estimate followed by the parameter contraction gives

\[\|\phi_\theta(x)-\phi_{\theta'}(x')\|\le L(\|\theta-\theta'\|+\|x-x'\|).\]

For \(\theta=\theta'\) the latent observable is \(L\)-Lipschitz. Fixing the latent coordinate similarly gives the separate parameter bound. The sum on the right comes from the triangle inequality on the two orthogonal coordinate inclusions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Separate parameter and probability-law perturbations}{Separate parameter and probability-law perturbations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldDrift-lean-4}


For differentiable \(V\) define

\[
b_V(\theta,\mu)=\int \nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mu(dx).
\]

Assume \(\nabla V\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz and \(\mu,\nu\) have finite second moments. Linear growth gives integrability. Comparison on a finite-cost coupling, Cauchy–Schwarz, and the cost infimum then give

\[
\|b_V(\theta,\mu)-b_V(\theta',\nu)\|
 \le L\bigl(\|\theta-\theta'\|+W_2(\mu,\nu)\bigr).
\]

For split envelope \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\) the gradient constant is \(L_1+1/\eta\). The source’s \(1/\eta\) constant is the full-envelope constant and is not in general sufficient for this split potential. Moreover the present estimate is in quadratic Wasserstein distance, a weaker conclusion than the source’s first-Wasserstein assertion. It does not alone establish the later propositions cited there.



For arbitrary coupling \((X,Y)\), the \(L\)-Lipschitz joint gradient gives \(\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)-\nabla_\theta U(\vartheta,y)\|\le L(\|\theta-\vartheta\|+\|x-y\|)\). Integration and Cauchy–Schwarz on \(\mathbb E\|X-Y\|\) yield

\[\|b(\theta,\mu)-b(\vartheta,\nu)\|\le L\|\theta-\vartheta\|+LW_2(\mu,\nu).\]

The Moreau substitution gives \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\). Alternatively insert intermediate force \(b_V(\theta',\mu)\): its parameter change is at most \(L\|\theta-\theta'\|\) and its law change at most \(LW_2(\mu,\nu)\). Their sum proves the claim with constant \(L_1+1/\eta\) for split smoothing.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Mean-field integral equations in the space of square-integrable states}{Mean-field integral equations in the space of square-integrable states}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean}


On probability space \(Q\) let \(H=L^2(Q;\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x})\). A potential \(V\) with globally \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient induces a Lipschitz composition map on this Hilbert space. A bounded linear operator replaces only its parameter component by expectation. Picard’s theorem constructs the integral solution with a continuous additive input. Projection gives a deterministic parameter equation coupled to the law of the latent component when the initial parameter is deterministic and the input is latent-only.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.25]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Expectation as a bounded linear map}{Expectation as a bounded linear map}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-1}


Under a probability measure, \(\|X\|_{L^1}\le\|X\|_{L^2}\). The norm-of-integral inequality therefore gives \(\|\mathbb E_Q X\|\le\|X\|_2\). Bochner integration is linear on these integrable variables, defining continuous linear map \(\mathcal E:H\to E\) with operator norm at most one.



Continuity makes expectation commute with continuous time integrals. This fact will identify the deterministic parameter equation after projecting the joint Hilbert-space solution.

\leanline{8}{exact\ integral\_smul\ a\ X\ \}}
\leanline{4}{1\ (fun\ X\ =>\ by}
\leanline{6}{change\ ‖∫\ ω,\ X\ ω\ ∂Q‖\ ≤\ 1\ *\ ‖X‖}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate projections and parameter inclusion}{Coordinate projections and parameter inclusion}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-2}


Define latent projection \(P_x(\theta,x)=x\) and parameter inclusion \(I_\theta(\theta)=(\theta,0)\). Coordinate expansion gives \(\|(\theta,0)\|^2=\|\theta\|^2\), so \(I_\theta\) is an isometry. The latent projection is a contraction because its squared norm discards the nonnegative parameter coordinates.



Both maps are bounded and linear, and their pointwise actions preserve these properties on square-integrable random vectors. They consequently commute with the Bochner time integrals in the projected equations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The latent projection on random joint states}{The latent projection on random joint states}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-3}


Set \(M_x=I-I_\theta P_\theta\). The parameter block of this map is zero, while its latent block equals the original latent block. Squared-norm expansion gives \(\|M_xz\|^2\le\|z\|^2\). Thus the mask is a contraction on the joint space and on \(L^2\).



In particular \(M_x\) preserves square integrability and continuity of an \(L^2\)-valued input. Applied to the Brownian input it removes every parameter coordinate at all times.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The parameter-averaging operator}{The parameter-averaging operator}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-4}


Define the bounded linear averaging operator by

\[\mathcal M X=\operatorname{const}(I_\theta P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)+M_xX.\]

The norm of each summand is at most \(\|X\|_2\), so \(\|\mathcal M X\|_2\le2\|X\|_2\). Linearity gives \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal M)\le2\). This sufficient constant is not asserted optimal.



The operator sends a joint state \((\Theta,X)\) to \((\mathbb E\Theta,X)\), where the first component is represented as a constant random vector. Only the parameter component is averaged. The latent force remains a random function of its current latent state, as required for the mean-field system.

\leanline{4}{(jointParameterInclusionCLM.comp\ (jointParameterCLM.comp\ (lpExpectationCLM\ Q)))\ +}
\leanline{4}{jointLatentMaskCLM.compLpL\ 2\ Q}
\end{leancode}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The mean-field drift and finite-horizon integral solution}{The mean-field drift and finite-horizon integral solution}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-5}


Set \(\mathcal A_V(X)=\mathcal M(\nabla V(X))\). Composition with the Lipschitz gradient preserves \(L^2\) and has constant \(L\). The averaging bound gives \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal A_V)\le2L\). For a continuous \(H\)-valued input \(B\) satisfying \(B_0=0\) and any \(T\ge0\), Picard’s theorem gives continuous \(Y\) with \(Y_0=Y^{\rm in}\) and

\[Y_t-Y^{\rm in}=-\int_0^t\mathcal A_V(Y_s)ds+B_t,\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The complete space is \(H\). Writing \(E=\mathbb R^{d_\theta+d_x}\), expectation is a bounded linear map \(L^2(Q;E)\to E\). Constant inclusion maps \(E\) back into \(H=L^2(Q;E)\). Composing these maps with the parameter projection averages the parameter component; the latent component is left unchanged. The coordinate maps are bounded. Linear growth makes gradient composition everywhere defined on \(L^2\), so the averaged drift has constant at most \(2L\). Subtracting the continuous additive input reduces the equation to a nonautonomous globally Lipschitz integral equation. This proves finite-horizon existence and the displayed identity from the stated input and potential assumptions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The averaging operator in coordinates}{The averaging operator in coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-6}


On representatives the averaging operator has formula

\[(\mathcal M X)(\omega)=(\mathbb E_Q P_\theta X,\,P_xX(\omega)).\]

Its parameter projection is \(\operatorname{const}(P_\theta\mathbb E_QX)\), and its latent projection is \(P_xX\). The constant inclusion contributes only the first block and the latent mask only the second. These almost-sure identities give equalities in \(L^2\). Hence the averaging operator gives exactly the expected parameter component and leaves latent randomness unchanged.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The integrated parameter-gradient formula}{The integrated parameter-gradient formula}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-7}


Define the continuous map \(a_V(X)=P_\theta\mathbb E_Q\nabla V(X)\) on \(H\). The averaged drift has blocks \(\operatorname{const}(a_V(X))\) and \(P_x\nabla V(X)\). If \(P_\theta X=\operatorname{const}(\theta)\), the state satisfies \(X=(\theta,P_xX)\) almost surely. Commuting parameter projection through expectation and applying the pushforward integral formula gives

\[a_V(X)=\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta,x)\,\mathcal L_Q(P_xX)(dx).\]

Indeed a deterministic parameter block identifies the state as \((\theta,P_xX)\). Evaluating and projecting the joint gradient then gives the marginal integral defining \(b_V\). The latent law is the pushforward of the constructed state, rather than an independently specified measure curve.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The deterministic parameter and latent integral equations}{The deterministic parameter and latent integral equations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-8}


Assume initial parameter \(\theta_0\) is constant and \(P_\theta B_t=0\). Projection commutes with the time integral, giving \(P_\theta Y_t=\operatorname{const}(\theta_t)\) where \(\theta_t=\theta_0-\int_0^t a_V(Y_s)ds\). Continuity of the primitive proves continuity of \(\theta\). Define \(\mu_t=\mathcal L_Q(P_xY_t)\) and substitute the force identity:

\[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t b_V(\theta_s,\mu_s)ds,\qquad P_xY_t-P_xY_0=-\int_0^tP_x\nabla V(Y_s)ds+P_xB_t.\]

The latent equality is in \(L^2\). The parameter input is zero and the parameter drift is constant as a random vector, so its time integral is constant as well. Thus the deterministic path \(\theta_t\) is derived from the joint solution. In terms of its latent law \(\mu_t\), the equation is

\[\theta_t-\theta_0=-\int_0^t\left[\int\nabla_\theta V(\theta_s,x)\,d\mu_s(x)\right]ds.\]

Latent projection gives the drift-plus-noise equation in the display. Both equations use the same constructed process and its latent distribution.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The continuous latent-only Brownian input}{The continuous latent-only Brownian input}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-9}


Apply the latent mask to the coordinate Brownian \(L^2\) input. Gaussian second moments show continuity in \(L^2\) and a zero initial value. Its parameter projection vanishes identically.



Indeed the \(L^2\) norm of a Brownian increment is proportional to the square root of its time length, proving \(L^2\) continuity. A bounded linear mask preserves continuity and the zero initial value. These facts establish the continuous additive-input hypotheses of the integral equation by construction.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Brownian input and split-envelope specialization}{Brownian input and split-envelope specialization}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldProcess-lean-10}


The latent Brownian input satisfies the preceding input conditions, so the finite-horizon theorem gives the Brownian-driven \(L^2\) solution. For \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\), proximal calculus supplies gradient constant \(L_1+\eta^{-1}\). This proves continuous \(L^2\) integral equations with the deterministic parameter equation.



Specifically the potential \(g_1+g_2^\eta\) has constant \(L_1+1/\eta\), and substitution yields an \(L^2\)-valued continuous solution. These statements concern mean-square integral equations. They do not assert a pathwise strong stochastic differential equation or a Fokker–Planck theorem, which require further arguments.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}{Exact iid variance and the empirical parameter force}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean}


For \(N>0\), independent centered square-integrable Hilbert-valued vectors have zero mixed expected inner products. Expanding the finite squared sum and integrating termwise therefore gives an exact \(1/N\) variance factor for their sample mean. The finite product measure supplies the required independence. Applying the identity to the parameter-gradient observable gives the empirical mean-field force fluctuation.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The centered off-diagonal expectations}{The centered off-diagonal expectations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean-1}


View the supplied \(L^2\) functions as \(L^2\) vectors. Cauchy–Schwarz makes each pairwise inner product integrable, so finite expansion gives

\[\mathbb E\left\|\sum_iX_i\right\|^2=\sum_{i,j}\mathbb E\langle X_i,X_j\rangle.\]

For \(i\ne j\), independence and zero means give \(\langle\mathbb E X_i,\mathbb E X_j\rangle=0\). The diagonal terms are \(\mathbb E\|X_i\|^2\). For distinct indices \(i\ne j\), centering alone would not remove covariance; independence is required for this factorization. Products of \(L^2\) norms are integrable, which justifies the complete termwise identity.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}memLp}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}centered\_\allowbreak{}independent}}
\leansource{\texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}memLp} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldVariance.lean, lines 28–34}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integrable\_inner\_of\_memLp\ \{X\ Y\ :\ Ω\ →\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hX\ :\ MemLp\ X\ 2\ P)\ (hY\ :\ MemLp\ Y\ 2\ P)\ :}
\leanline{4}{Integrable\ (fun\ ω\ =>\ {\leanfallback ⟪}X\ ω,Y\ ω{\leanfallback ⟫})\ P}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}centered\_\allowbreak{}independent} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldVariance.lean, lines 38–62}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integral\_norm\_sum\_sq\_centered\_independent}
\leanline{4}{\{ι\ :\ Type*\}\ [Fintype\ ι]\ (X\ :\ ι\ →\ Ω\ →\ E)}
\leanline{4}{(hX\ :\ ∀\ i,\ MemLp\ (X\ i)\ 2\ P)\ (hind\ :\ iIndepFun\ X\ P)}
\leanline{4}{(hmean\ :\ ∀\ i,\ (∫\ ω,X\ i\ ω\ ∂P)=0)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ ω,\ ‖∑\ i,\ X\ i\ ω‖\textasciicircum{}2\ ∂P)\ =\ ∑\ i,\ ∫\ ω,\ ‖X\ i\ ω‖\textasciicircum{}2\ ∂P}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Expand the Hilbert square as \(\|\sum_iX_i\|^2=\sum_{i,j}\langle X_i,X_j\rangle\). Each mixed product is integrable by the \(L^2\) inner-product bound, which justifies moving the finite double sum through the integral. For \(i\ne j\), independence factors \(\mathbb E\langle X_i,X_j\rangle\) into \(\langle\mathbb EX_i,\mathbb EX_j\rangle=0\). For \(i=j\), it is \(\mathbb E\|X_i\|^2\). The local facts \texttt{hint}, \texttt{hcross} and \texttt{hexpand} establish these prerequisites and identities separately. Centering without independence would leave the off-diagonal covariances.



Formal correspondence: integrable\_inner\_of\_memLp : Every inner product of two \(L^2\) random variables is integrable. integral\_norm\_sum\_sq\_centered\_independent : Centered independent Hilbert-valued samples have exactly additive second moments. Independence is used for each mixed term.

\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}centered\_\allowbreak{}independent}: the cases are separated by \texttt{by\_\allowbreak{}cases hij : i=j}; the intermediate facts include \texttt{hint}, \texttt{hcross}, \texttt{h}, \texttt{hexpand}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{ite\_\allowbreak{}true}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}self\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq}, \texttt{Finset.\allowbreak{}sum\_\allowbreak{}comm}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}finset\allowbreak{}Sum}, \texttt{integrable\_\allowbreak{}finset\allowbreak{}Sum}.
\end{leanbox}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The variance of an independent sample mean}{The variance of an independent sample mean}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean-2}


Let \(N>0\) and let \(\phi\) be square-integrable under probability \(\mu\). On \(\mu^{\otimes N}\) the coordinate samples are independent. Center \(\phi\) by its expectation, expand the squared sample mean, and cancel its off-diagonal pairings. This gives

\[
\mathbb E_{\mu^{\otimes N}}
 \left\|\frac1N\sum_{i=1}^N\phi(X_i)-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\right\|^2
 =\frac1N\mathbb E_\mu\|\phi-\mathbb E_\mu\phi\|^2.
\]

The root mean square sampling factor is \(N^{-1/2}\). This variance identity is an ingredient in particle comparison, not by itself a propagation-of-chaos theorem. Exchangeability alone would not cancel covariances.



Indeed let \(C_i=\phi(X_i)-\mathbb E\phi\). Its average is \(N^{-1}\sum_iC_i\), the expected squared sum is \(N\mathbb E\|C_1\|^2\), and the averaging prefactor is \(N^{-2}\). Their product is \(N^{-1}\mathbb E\|C_1\|^2\). Taking square roots gives \(1/\sqrt N\). Independence here comes from the finite product construction, rather than an assumption about interacting particle states.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{empiricalAverage}, \texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}variance}, \texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}rms}}

\leanline{4}{(φ\ :\ A\ →\ E)\ (x\ :\ Fin\ N\ →\ A)\ :\ E\ :=\ (N\ :\ ℝ)⁻¹\ •\ ∑\ i,φ\ (x\ i)}
\leanline{0}{\strut}
\leanline{0}{section\ ProductVariance}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}variance} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldVariance.lean, lines 80–124}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ iid\_empirical\_variance\ \{N\ :\ ℕ\}\ (hN\ :\ 0<N)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ x\ :\ Fin\ N\ →\ A,\ ‖empiricalAverage\ φ\ x-(∫\ a,φ\ a\ ∂μ)‖\textasciicircum{}2}
\leanline{6}{∂Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ μ))\ =}
\leanline{6}{(∫\ a,‖φ\ a-(∫\ b,φ\ b\ ∂μ)‖\textasciicircum{}2\ ∂μ)/(N\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}rms} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldVariance.lean, lines 127–131}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ iid\_empirical\_rms\ \{N\ :\ ℕ\}\ (hN\ :\ 0<N)\ :}
\leanline{4}{Real.sqrt\ (∫\ x\ :\ Fin\ N\ →\ A,‖empiricalAverage\ φ\ x-(∫\ a,φ\ a\ ∂μ)‖\textasciicircum{}2}
\leanline{6}{∂Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ μ))\ =}
\leanline{4}{Real.sqrt\ (∫\ a,‖φ\ a-(∫\ b,φ\ b\ ∂μ)‖\textasciicircum{}2\ ∂μ)/Real.sqrt\ (N\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
On the explicit product probability \(P=\mu^{\otimes N}\), set \(C(a)=\varphi(a)-\mathbb E_\mu\varphi\) and \(X_i(\omega)=C(\omega_i)\). Measure-preserving coordinate evaluation proves that each \(X_i\) has the same finite second moment, zero mean and law, and the product construction proves their joint independence. The exact centered-mean identity is \[\frac1N\sum_i\varphi(\omega_i)-\mathbb E\varphi=\frac1N\sum_iX_i(\omega).\] Thus the previous variance identity gives \[\mathbb E_P\left\|\frac1N\sum_i\varphi(\omega_i)-\mathbb E\varphi\right\|^2=\frac{\mathbb E_\mu\|\varphi-\mathbb E\varphi\|^2}{N}.\] In the code, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}Xi}, \texttt{hind}, \texttt{hmean}, \texttt{hvar} and \texttt{hid} verify each of these assertions. Taking the nonnegative square root gives the RMS version.



Formal correspondence: empiricalAverage : The empirical mean on the explicitly specified finite sample space. iid\_empirical\_variance : The actual iid finite product gives the exact centered empirical variance. All moments are Bochner integrals, including the mean used for centering. iid\_empirical\_rms : The same exact identity written as an RMS sampling error.



\texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Average} is a definition with defining expression \texttt{(N : \ensuremath{\mathbb R})\ensuremath{{}^{-1}} \ensuremath{\bullet} ∑ i,\ensuremath{\varphi} (x i)}. The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.



\texttt{iid\_\allowbreak{}empirical\_\allowbreak{}variance}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hn}, \texttt{C}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}C}, \texttt{hsum}, \texttt{hid}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{h\ensuremath{\varphi}.\allowbreak{}integrable}, \texttt{Fintype.\allowbreak{}card\_\allowbreak{}fin}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}const\_\allowbreak{}mul}, \texttt{nsmul\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}mul}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}.

\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The empirical parameter-gradient variance}{The empirical parameter-gradient variance}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldVariance-lean-3}


Assume \(N>0\), fix \(\theta\), and put \(\phi(x)=\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)\). Joint-gradient Lipschitzness gives affine growth of this observable in its latent input, so it is square-integrable under every finite-second-moment law \(\mu\). Its expectation is \(b_U(\theta,\mu)\). The independent-sample identity therefore gives

\[\mathbb E\left\|\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X_i)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\right\|^2=\frac1N\mathbb E\|\nabla_\theta U(\theta,X)-b_U(\theta,\mu)\|^2.\]

This is the exact variance of the empirical parameter force. Its finite variance is derived from the gradient and latent-moment assumptions rather than postulated as an additional sampling-error bound.

\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{\{U\ :\ IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{L\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ (gradient\ U))}
\leanline{4}{(θ\ :\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dθ))}
\leanline{4}{(μ\ :\ Measure\ (EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dx)))\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ μ]}
\leanline{4}{(hμ\ :\ MemLp\ (fun\ x\ =>\ x)\ 2\ μ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∫\ x\ :\ Fin\ N\ →\ EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dx),}
\leanline{6}{‖empiricalAverage\ (parameterGradient\ U\ θ)\ x-meanFieldDrift\ U\ θ\ μ‖\textasciicircum{}2}
\leanline{6}{∂Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ μ))\ =}
\leanline{6}{(∫\ x,‖parameterGradient\ U\ θ\ x-meanFieldDrift\ U\ θ\ μ‖\textasciicircum{}2\ ∂μ)/(N\ :\ ℝ)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
For \(\varphi(x)=\nabla_\theta U(\theta,x)\), the parameter projection is nonexpansive and the full score is \(L\)-Lipschitz, so \(\varphi\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz in the latent variable. A latent law with finite second moment therefore makes this observable square integrable. Its Bochner mean is exactly \texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Drift}, so the general iid identity applies without assuming a separate variance bound for this particular gradient observable.



Formal correspondence: iid\_parameterGradient\_variance : Exact iid empirical parameter-gradient variance for the actual joint potential. The copies are independent under the displayed finite product.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The particle drift on a product of square-integrable coordinate spaces}{The particle drift on a product of square-integrable coordinate spaces}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean}


Use a finite product of coordinate \(L^2\) spaces to describe the physical particle system. Specifically let \(H_N=L^2(P;\Theta)\times\prod_{i<N}L^2(P;X)\) with \(\|z\|=\max(\|z_\theta\|_2,\max_i\|z_i\|_2)\). Reconstruct a joint pair from the common parameter and each latent coordinate, then evaluate its gradient. Averaging the parameter forces and retaining each latent force gives a Lipschitz drift with constant independent of particle count. The maximum norm directly bounds each coordinate without exchangeability assumptions.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Coordinate inclusions and square-integrable contractions}{Coordinate inclusions and square-integrable contractions}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean-1}


Define the latent inclusion \(I_x(x)=(0,x)\). Adding zero parameter coordinates gives \(\|I_xx\|=\|x\|\), and latent projection has norm at most one. For a pointwise linear contraction \(C\), integration of \(\|C(X)\|^2\le\|X\|^2\) gives \(\|C\circ X\|_2\le\|X\|_2\).



Thus coordinate inclusion and projection are contractions also in \(L^2\). They introduce no dimension factor in reconstruction of the random pair. The maximum product norm consequently controls every parameter and tagged latent-coordinate difference.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Reconstruction of a random joint pair}{Reconstruction of a random joint pair}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean-2}


Set \(J(\Theta,X)=I_\theta\Theta+I_xX\). Its representative is \((\Theta(\omega),X(\omega))\) almost surely. The bounded linear inclusions and triangle inequality give

\[\|J(\Theta,X)-J(\Theta',X')\|_2\le\|\Theta-\Theta'\|_2+\|X-X'\|_2.\]

The coordinate \(L^2\) differences bound the reconstructed pair difference. Its representative is exactly \((\Theta(\omega),X(\omega))\), so evaluation of a joint potential or gradient on this Hilbert-space pair agrees with evaluation at the intended parameter and latent vectors.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The empirical particle drift}{The empirical particle drift}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean-3}


Apply \(\nabla V\) to the reconstructed pair \(J(\Theta,X_i)\) for each particle. Define the empirical parameter drift and the individual latent drifts by

\[\mathcal A_N(z)=\left(\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta V(\Theta,X_i),\ (\nabla_xV(\Theta,X_i))_i\right).\]

The representative formulas follow from composition and the finite-sum identity. Only the parameter components are averaged, with division justified by \(N>0\). This is the interacting physical-particle drift, retaining the common random parameter; no mean-field expectation is substituted in this definition.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Particle-count-independent Lipschitzness and the integral solution}{Particle-count-independent Lipschitzness and the integral solution}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleDrift-lean-4}


Every coordinate difference is bounded by the maximum product distance, so pair reconstruction and gradient regularity give \(\|\nabla V(J_i z)-\nabla V(J_i w)\|_2\le2L\|z-w\|\). Averaging the \(N\) parameter terms and dividing by \(N>0\) preserves this bound. The maximum latent-coordinate norm preserves it as well. Hence \(\operatorname{Lip}(\mathcal A_N)\le2L\) independently of \(N\). Picard gives continuous \(Z\) satisfying \(Z_t-Z_0=-\int_0^t\mathcal A_N(Z_s)ds+B_t\) for continuous input with \(B_0=0\).



The state consists of one parameter \(L^2\) vector and finitely many latent \(L^2\) vectors. For particle \(i\), reconstruction gives \(\|\operatorname{pair}(\theta,x_i)-\operatorname{pair}(\vartheta,y_i)\|_2\le\|\theta-\vartheta\|_2+\|x_i-y_i\|_2\le2\|z-w\|\). The \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient therefore bounds its force difference by \(2L\|z-w\|\). Averaging cancels factor \(N\) and gives constant \(2L\). Equivalently the reconstruction factor two is multiplied by \(L\); averaging \(N\) parameter bounds and taking the maximum of latent bounds leaves \(2L\). The finite-horizon integral equation follows from completeness and global drift Lipschitzness.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Independent copies and the empirical parameter-force residual}{Independent copies and the empirical parameter-force residual}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean}


Independent copies of the mean-field state give an empirical drift residual with exact \(N^{-1/2}\) norm factor. Starting with a joint random state on \(Q\), use the finite product \(P=Q^{\otimes N}\) and its coordinate pullbacks. When the reference parameter is deterministic, all copies have the same parameter while their latent components are independent. The copy maps are bounded and linear, so they commute with the reference integral equation. All latent drift residuals cancel; only the centered empirical parameter force remains.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The measure-preserving coordinate copy map}{The measure-preserving coordinate copy map}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean-1}


For product coordinate \(\omega_i\) define \(C_iX=X\circ\omega_i\). Evaluation preserves the reference measure, so pullback is an \(L^2\) isometry and a bounded linear map. Its representative equals \(X(\omega_i)\) almost surely. Coordinate substitution gives \(C_i(CX)=C(C_iX)\) for bounded linear \(C\), and constants satisfy \(C_i(\operatorname{const}x)=\operatorname{const}x\).



The evaluation \(\omega\mapsto\omega_i\) has pushforward \(Q\); hence integration of \(\|X(\omega_i)\|^2\) gives the original second moment. Pullback is consequently an isometry on \(L^2\). These pointwise and integral identities justify copying the projected reference equations.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The centered empirical fluctuation norm}{The centered empirical fluctuation norm}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean-2}


For \(N>0\) define \(\mathcal C X=X-\operatorname{const}(\mathbb EX)\) and \(\Delta_N(X)=N^{-1}\sum_iC_iX-\operatorname{const}(\mathbb EX)\). Finite-sum representative identities identify the latter with the centered sample mean. Independence on the product space gives

\[\|\Delta_N(X)\|_{L^2(P)}^2=\frac1N\|\mathcal C X\|_{L^2(Q)}^2,\qquad\|\Delta_N(X)\|_2=\frac{\|\mathcal C X\|_2}{\sqrt N}.\]

The pullback \(X\mapsto X(\omega_i)\) preserves \(Q\) and is a linear isometry. Thus the centered copy sum is an \(L^2\) vector represented by that sample mean. The exact variance statement is

\[\|\text{empirical fluctuation}\|_2^2=\frac{\|X-\mathbb EX\|_2^2}{N},\]

Taking nonnegative square roots gives factor \(N^{-1/2}\). The centered force norm is divided by \(\sqrt N\). This uses independence of reference copies; the later interacting particle states are not assumed independent.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The reference particle state with deterministic parameter}{The reference particle state with deterministic parameter}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean-3}


Define the reference particle state by

\[\mathcal R_N(X)=\left(\operatorname{const}(\mathbb E P_\theta X),\ (P_xC_iX)_i\right).\]

Bounded linear maps commute with expectation, and a constant has its original value as expectation under a probability measure. Thus if \(P_\theta X=\operatorname{const}\theta\), the parameter component of \(\mathcal R_N(X)\) is \(\operatorname{const}\theta\).



The latent coordinates are independent coordinate copies, while the deterministic parameter is unchanged. The construction is a bounded linear map, which permits passage through the reference time integral.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Reconstruction and drift composition for copied pairs}{Reconstruction and drift composition for copied pairs}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean-4}


Recombining the parameter and latent projections recovers the original \(L^2\) vector. For deterministic parameter this gives \(J_i\mathcal R_N(X)=C_iX\). Composition of the copied state with a Lipschitz pointwise drift is the same as copying the composition, since both representatives equal \(b(X(\omega_i))\) almost surely.



For the gradient, this common representative is \(\nabla V(X(\omega_i))\). Hence each reconstructed reference pair has precisely the copied joint drift. This equality causes the latent components to cancel in the particle-minus-reference drift.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The empirical parameter-force drift residual}{The empirical parameter-force drift residual}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCopies-lean-5}


Apply the particle drift to \(\mathcal R_N(X)\) and compare it with \(\mathcal R_N(\mathcal A_V(X))\). Reconstruction and gradient commutation identify all latent components, leaving

\[\mathcal A_N(\mathcal R_NX)-\mathcal R_N(\mathcal A_VX)=\left(\Delta_N(P_\theta\nabla V(X)),0\right).\]

The maximum product norm and sample-mean variance identity give residual norm \(\|\mathcal C(P_\theta\nabla V(X))\|_2/\sqrt N\). In detail, latent coordinate \(i\) combines with the deterministic parameter to recover joint copy \(i\). The parameter discrepancy is

\[\frac1N\sum_i\nabla_\theta V(\theta,X_i)-\mathbb E\nabla_\theta V(\theta,X).\]

It is the empirical parameter force minus its expectation. Its norm is the centered single-copy force norm divided by \(\sqrt N\), equivalently \(\|\mathcal C(P_\theta\nabla V(X))\|_2/\sqrt N\). This exact residual supplies the sampling forcing used in the comparison theorem.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Grönwall comparison with an extra continuous noise input}{Grönwall comparison with an extra continuous noise input}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldForcing-lean}


A common sampling factor gives an \(N^{-1/2}\) comparison for an integral drift defect and an additional continuous input. In a Banach space let \(F\) be \(L\)-Lipschitz. Compare two integral equations with common input \(B\), additional input \(A\) in one equation, and reference drift \(G\). Subtracting the additional input before applying Grönwall yields a differentiable remainder without differentiating either noise path.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.43]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The difference after subtraction of the additional input}{The difference after subtraction of the additional input}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldForcing-lean-1}


Let \(L\ge0\) and \(T\ge0\), and define \(\Psi(L,T)=\int_0^T e^{L(T-s)}ds\). Thus \(\Psi(0,T)=T\), and \(\Psi(L,T)=(e^{LT}-1)/L\) for \(L>0\). Assume \(\|A_t\|\le a\), \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C\), and

\[X_t-x=-\int_0^tF(X_s)ds+B_t+A_t,\qquad Y_t-x=-\int_0^tG_sds+B_t.\]

Put \(D_t=X_t-Y_t-A_t\). Subtraction cancels the common input, and the Lipschitz and defect bounds give \(\|D_t\|\le\int_0^t[L\|D_s\|+La+C]ds\). Grönwall then yields \(\|X_t-Y_t\|\le(C+La)\Psi(L,T)+a\) on \([0,T]\).



The continuous inputs \(B,A\) may be Brownian inputs in \(L^2\). Defining \(D_t=X_t-Y_t-A_t\) cancels \(B\) and \(A\) algebraically and leaves

\[D_t=-\int_0^t[F(X_s)-G_s]ds,\quad D_0=0.\]

Continuity of the integrand makes this remainder differentiable with derivative \(-F(X_t)+G_t\). Equivalently, for \(D=X-Y-A\), the remainder \(D\) is differentiable although \(A\) need not be. Since \(X-Y=D+A\), its derivative is bounded by \(L\|D\|+La+C\). Grönwall bounds \(D\), and restoring \(A\) adds \(a\). This proves the estimate using only continuous integral equations.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation}}
\leansource{\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldForcing.lean, lines 22–81}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integral\_forcing\_perturbation}
\leanline{4}{(F\ :\ H\ →\ H)\ (L\ :\ ℝ≥0)\ (hF\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ F)}
\leanline{4}{(x\ :\ H)\ (B\ A\ G\ X\ Y\ :\ ℝ\ →\ H)}
\leanline{4}{(hA\ :\ Continuous\ A)\ (hG\ :\ Continuous\ G)\ (hX\ :\ Continuous\ X)\ (hY\ :\ Continuous\ Y)}
\leanline{4}{(T\ :\ ℝ)\ (hT\ :\ 0\ ≤\ T)\ (C\ a\ :\ ℝ)\ (hC\ :\ 0\ ≤\ C)\ (ha\ :\ 0\ ≤\ a)}
\leanline{4}{(hAbound\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ ‖A\ t‖\ ≤\ a)}
\leanline{4}{(hdefect\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ ‖F\ (Y\ t)\ -\ G\ t‖\ ≤\ C)}
\leanline{4}{(hXe\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ X\ t\ -\ x\ =\ -(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ F\ (X\ s))\ +\ B\ t\ +\ A\ t)}
\leanline{4}{(hYe\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ Y\ t\ -\ x\ =\ -(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ G\ s)\ +\ B\ t)\ :}
\leanline{4}{∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ ‖X\ t\ -\ Y\ t‖\ ≤\ gronwallBound\ 0\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ (C\ +\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ a)\ T\ +\ a}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
If \(F\) is \(L\)-Lipschitz, \(\|A_t\|\le a\) and \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C\), then \[\|D'_t\|\le L\|D_t\|+C+La.\] The right-derivative Grönwall theorem gives \(\|D_t\|\le\operatorname{gronwallBound}(0,L,C+La,T)\) uniformly on \([0,T]\). Adding back \(A_t\) produces the final \(+a\). In Lean, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}DI} identifies the differentiable integral remainder, \texttt{hderiv} proves its derivative, and \texttt{hb} establishes this exact scalar forcing.



Formal correspondence: integral\_forcing\_perturbation : Actual integral equations with common input, an additional continuous input, and a uniformly controlled reference-force defect satisfy this finite-horizon bound. Neither a derivative of the input nor an error recurrence is assumed.



\texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{D}, \texttt{I}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}DI}, \texttt{ht\allowbreak{}A}, \texttt{htri}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{sub\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}iff\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}add}, \texttt{ht.\allowbreak{}1.\allowbreak{}trans}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}neg}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X.\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{sub\_\allowbreak{}zero}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The comparison with a common inverse sampling factor}{The comparison with a common inverse sampling factor}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldForcing-lean-2}


The zero-initial-error Grönwall bound is \(C\Psi(L,T)\), where \(\Psi\) is defined above. It is linear in \(C\), including when \(L=0\). Let \(r>0\) and suppose that \(\|A_t\|\le a/r\) and \(\|F(Y_t)-G_t\|\le C/r\) throughout the interval. Substitution in the preceding bound gives

\[\|X_t-Y_t\|\le\frac{(C+La)\Psi(L,T)+a}{r}.\]

Indeed, the differential inequality for the remainder has forcing \(C/r+La/r=(C+La)/r\). Grönwall multiplies this forcing by \(\Psi(L,T)\), and restoring the additional input contributes \(a/r\). For \(r=\sqrt N\), with \(N>0\), the resulting coefficient is independent of \(N\) whenever \(C,L,a,T\) are independent of \(N\).

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{gronwallBound\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}div}, \texttt{integral\_\allowbreak{}forcing\_\allowbreak{}perturbation\_\allowbreak{}scaled}}

\texttt{gronwall\allowbreak{}Bound\_\allowbreak{}zero\_\allowbreak{}div}: the proof is the application \texttt{unfold gronwall\allowbreak{}Bound}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.

\section{\texorpdfstring{The parameter Brownian input at finite particle count}{The parameter Brownian input at finite particle count}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldNoise-lean}


The common parameter Brownian input has amplitude \(\sqrt{2/N}\). A coordinate pullback on the reference product space gives its exact \(L^2\) norm, bounded on a finite horizon by a coefficient times \(N^{-1/2}\). On \(Q^{\otimes N}\) with \(N>0\), use copy zero of the supplied \(d_\theta\)-dimensional Brownian block. Its amplitude is \(\sqrt{2/N}\), matching the empirical-force fluctuation scale.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.32]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.8]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The pulled-back and scaled parameter Brownian vector}{The pulled-back and scaled parameter Brownian vector}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldNoise-lean-1}


Product coordinate evaluation preserves the reference measure, so each pulled-back scalar path retains its Brownian law. Define \(A_t=N^{-1/2}\sqrt2(B_t^\theta-B_0^\theta)\) as an \(L^2\) vector. The Brownian second-moment construction gives \(L^2\) continuity and zero value at time zero.



The coordinate numbered zero exists under \(N>0\). Its Langevin-scaled noise \(\sqrt2W_t\), multiplied by \(N^{-1/2}\), gives the parameter input. Fixed scaling preserves the \(L^2\) continuity and zero initial value. Equivalently the finite-dimensional Brownian vector is multiplied by \(\sqrt{2/N}\); its inherited \(L^2\) continuity verifies the additive-input hypotheses rather than assuming them for an unspecified process.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Its exact second moment and horizon bound}{Its exact second moment and horizon bound}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldNoise-lean-2}


The sum of parameter-coordinate second moments is \(d_\theta t\). Taking square roots gives

\[\|A_t\|_2=\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt t}{\sqrt N}\le\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt T}{\sqrt N},\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

Each scalar increment has variance \(t\). Thus its Langevin vector has squared \(L^2\) norm \(2d_\theta t\). Scaling by \(N^{-1/2}\) yields

\[\|A_N(t)\|_2=\frac{\sqrt{2d_\theta}\sqrt t}{\sqrt N}.\]

Monotonicity bounds the time factor by \(\sqrt T\). In coordinate form the squared norm is \((2/N)\sum_{j=1}^{d_\theta}\mathbb E|B_t^j-B_0^j|^2=2d_\theta t/N\). For \(t\le T\) its root gives horizon coefficient \(\sqrt{2d_\theta T}\), independent of \(N\). Substitution of the constructed Brownian probability space gives an explicit example with these distributions and moments.

\end{leancode}

\section{\texorpdfstring{A finite-horizon comparison with independent mean-field copies}{A finite-horizon comparison with independent mean-field copies}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean}


Construct one continuous mean-field solution and, on each finite product source, its independent copies. Their particle-drift discrepancy is the centered empirical parameter force. A compact-horizon bound on the single-copy force and the parameter Brownian moment both have the inverse-square-root particle factor. Grönwall therefore bounds every tagged \(L^2\) error. For a globally \(L\)-Lipschitz gradient, the coefficient is fixed before particle count is chosen, and the conclusion is uniform over time after taking \(L^2\) norms.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A finite horizon bound on the centered reference force}{A finite horizon bound on the centered reference force}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean-1}


Define \(\mathcal C_V(X)=P_\theta\nabla V(X)-\operatorname{const}\mathbb E[P_\theta\nabla V(X)]\). Gradient composition is Lipschitz, projection is bounded linear, and expectation is continuous, so this centered-force map is continuous. For continuous path \(Y_t\) on compact interval \([0,T]\), its norm has finite bound \(C\ge0\). The constant \(C\) depends on the reference and horizon and not on \(N\).



Indeed the reference is continuous in \(L^2\). Its centered force is continuous on \([0,T]\) and bounded there before any \(N\)-particle product space is constructed. This quantifier order gives a single coefficient for all particle counts.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The copied integral equation and comparison coefficient}{The copied integral equation and comparison coefficient}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean-2}


The reference-copy operator \(\mathcal R_N\) is bounded and linear, so it commutes with the Bochner time integral. Its application to the mean-field equation gives an exact copied equation. Define

\[C_T=2\big[(C+2Lb)\Psi(2L,T)+b\big].\]

Here \(C\) bounds the single-reference centered force, and \(b/\sqrt N\) bounds the extra parameter input. The empirical-residual identity controls the difference between copied mean-field drift and particle drift. The factor two bounds the sum of the parameter and one latent-coordinate error by twice the maximum product norm.

The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The particle integral solution and independent-sample residual}{The particle integral solution and independent-sample residual}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean-3}


For each \(N>0\), global Lipschitzness gives particle solution \(Z\) with copied latent input and continuous additional parameter input \(A\) bounded by \(b/\sqrt N\). The copied reference residual has norm \(\|\mathcal C_V(Y_t)\|_2/\sqrt N\le C/\sqrt N\). Apply the forcing comparison with drift constant \(2L\). Each coordinate error is bounded by the maximum product error, so

\[\|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\le C_T/\sqrt N,\qquad0\le t\le T.\]

The reference centered force has bound \(C\) on \([0,T]\). For input \(A_N\) the residual bound is \(C/\sqrt N\) and the added-input bound is \(\|A_N\|\le b/\sqrt N\). Equivalently these are \(C/\sqrt N\) and \(b/\sqrt N\) for a \(2L\)-Lipschitz particle equation. The common initial states make the initial discrepancy zero. The coefficient is independent of \(N\) and the conclusion retains the constructed integral equation and independent copied initialization.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The comparison with the specified Brownian inputs}{The comparison with the specified Brownian inputs}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticles-lean-4}


Continuity of the centered reference force on \([0,T]\) gives a finite \(C\ge0\) such that

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\|
 \nabla_\theta V(Y_t)-\mathbb E_Q\nabla_\theta V(Y_t)\|_2\le C.
\]

This \(C\) is chosen independently of \(N\). The common latent noise cancels on subtraction; subtract the extra parameter input as well before applying Grönwall to the remaining drift integral. Define

\[
\Psi(a,T)=\begin{cases}(e^{aT}-1)/a,&a>0,\\T,&a=0,\end{cases}
\qquad
C_T=2\big[(C+2Lb)\Psi(2L,T)+b\big].
\]

For each \(N>0\) the construction gives a particle solution with the prescribed independent initialization, and for every tag \(i\),

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\left(
 \|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\right)
 \le\frac{C_T}{\sqrt N}.
\]

For \(V=g_1+g_2^\eta\) use gradient constant \(L=L_1+1/\eta\). The coefficient may depend on \(\eta\), so uniformity in smoothing is not asserted. The Brownian construction gives \(b=\sqrt{2d_\theta T}\) and jointly independent parameter and latent paths, which supply the intended noise amplitudes in the retained integral equations.



The supremum is taken after the \(L^2\) norm. Proposition C.2, equation (45), instead bounds the expectation of the supremum of the unsquared parameter and latent errors. That quantity and the supremum of the \(L^2\) error norms are not generally ordered. The proved result here is the displayed supremum of the \(L^2\) norms.

It constructs the deterministic-parameter mean-field solution with \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}lp\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}equations}. \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}comparison\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}solution} then supplies the general particle comparison. For each positive particle count the final specialization uses \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise}, its continuity and zero initial value, and \texttt{empirical\allowbreak{}Parameter\allowbreak{}Noise\_\allowbreak{}bound}. Thus the forcing radius is proved for the stated Brownian inputs rather than assumed.
\end{leanbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Independent Brownian coordinates for the particle input}{Independent Brownian coordinates for the particle input}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldBrownianFamily-lean}


On \(Q^{\otimes N}\), select the common parameter Brownian coordinates from copy zero and latent coordinate \((i,j)\) from copy \(i\). Independence of source rows and scalar paths gives independence of all selected full paths. Parameter and latent coordinate tags are disjoint, so the first source row may provide both parameter noise and the first particle’s latent noise without repeating any scalar coordinate.



Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.32]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.8]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{An injective selection of source coordinates}{An injective selection of source coordinates}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldBrownianFamily-lean-1}


Send parameter index \(j\) to \((0,\mathrm{parameter}\ j)\) and latent index \((i,j)\) to \((i,\mathrm{latent}\ j)\). Different coordinate tags separate the two kinds. Within either kind equality of selected indices implies equality of original indices, so the selection is injective.



In coordinate notation parameter index \(j\) selects \((0,\theta_j)\), while \((i,j)\) selects \((i,x_j)\). Evaluate the corresponding source Brownian path in the selected copy. A latent coordinate for particle \(i\) comes from copy \(i\). Even when that copy is zero, its latent tag differs from every parameter tag. Thus no scalar Brownian path is reused.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The Brownian law of each selected coordinate}{The Brownian law of each selected coordinate}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldBrownianFamily-lean-2}


For each selected coordinate, product evaluation preserves the source measure. Composition with its supplied scalar path therefore preserves the standard pre-Brownian law. Every time evaluation remains measurable by composition.



These facts establish the individual process distributions and measurability. Independence of their full paths follows separately from the joint source-family argument.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Independence of the selected full paths}{Independence of the selected full paths}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldBrownianFamily-lean-3}


The product measure makes the full source rows independent, and within each row the scalar coordinate paths are jointly independent. Flattening the two indices gives a jointly independent family indexed by \((i,j)\). The injective selection therefore retains independence.



This is independence of complete selected paths, including the common parameter block and the first latent block from row zero. It is stronger than independence at a single time. The constructed Brownian source supplies the row laws and the coordinate-path independence required for this construction.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{particleBrownianFamily\_\allowbreak{}paths\_\allowbreak{}independent}, \texttt{canonical\_\allowbreak{}particleBrownianFamily}}

\end{leancode}

First pull each whole Brownian row from the source to one coordinate of the finite product; distinct rows are independent by the product measure. Within a row the supplied coordinate paths are independent, and measure-preserving evaluation retains their joint law. The helper \texttt{i\allowbreak{}Indep\allowbreak{}Fun\_\allowbreak{}uncurry'} flattens these two levels into a jointly independent family indexed by all copy/\allowbreak{}coordinate pairs. Finally, precomposition with the injective index map selects the intended subfamily and preserves independence. This proves independence of full coordinate paths, including the parameter path and the first particle's latent path; it does not assert independence of interacting particle states.



Formal correspondence: particleBrownianFamily\_paths\_independent : Full coordinate-path independence is derived on the actual finite product. There is no assumption of independent particles after interaction. canonical\_particleBrownianFamily : The concrete canonical Gaussian time-indicator construction supplies all selected scalar Brownian laws and their full-path independence.

\texttt{canonical\_\allowbreak{}particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family}: the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}pre\allowbreak{}Brownian}, \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}paths\_\allowbreak{}independent}, \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}Canonical\allowbreak{}Brownian.\allowbreak{}process\_\allowbreak{}measurable}, \texttt{measurable\_\allowbreak{}pi\_\allowbreak{}apply}, \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}Canonical\allowbreak{}Brownian.\allowbreak{}coordinate\_\allowbreak{}paths\_\allowbreak{}independent}.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Initialization independent of the Brownian family}{Initialization independent of the Brownian family}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldInitialSource-lean}


Let \(\mu\) be a latent probability law with finite second moment and let \(\theta_0\) be a deterministic parameter. Use \(Q=\mu\otimes Q_B\), where \(Q_B\) carries the constructed joint Brownian coordinates. The first coordinate gives the latent initialization and the second gives independent full Brownian paths. The pair initialization is square-integrable and retains the prescribed parameter and latent law. Thus the input assumptions for the mean-field and particle equations follow from these initial data.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The product measure and Brownian evaluations}{The product measure and Brownian evaluations}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldInitialSource-lean-1}


The probability space has measure \(\mu\otimes Q_B\). Every Brownian path depends only on the second coordinate, whose projection preserves its source measure. Its scalar Brownian laws, full-coordinate independence, and measurable evaluations are therefore unchanged. The mass identity is \(Q(\Omega)=\mu(X)Q_B(\Omega_B)=1\cdot1=1\).



Coordinate evaluation measurability also gives measurability of the grouped full paths. Adding the independent latent factor does not change their joint pushforward distribution.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The square-integrable pair initialization}{The square-integrable pair initialization}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldInitialSource-lean-2}


The map \(x\mapsto(\theta_0,x)\) is measurable and nonexpansive as a function of its latent input. The first product coordinate has the supplied finite second moment. Hence \((\theta_0,X_0)\) is square-integrable and defines an \(L^2\) initialization.



The initialized pair \((\theta_0,X_0)\) has squared norm \(\|\theta_0\|^2+\|X_0\|^2\). Its parameter term is a finite constant and its latent term has finite expectation. Thus it is an element of the joint \(L^2\) space with the prescribed coordinates.

\end{leancode}

\leanline{4}{MemLp\ (fun\ ω\ =>\ IPLA.pair\ θ\ ω.1)\ 2\ (meanFieldInitialSource\ dθ\ μ)}

\leanline{4}{Lp\ (IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ 2\ (meanFieldInitialSource\ dθ\ μ)\ :=}
