\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The prescribed marginals and full-noise independence}{The prescribed marginals and full-noise independence}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldInitialSource-lean-3}


The pair representative equals \((\theta_0,\omega_1)\) almost surely. Its parameter projection is \(\operatorname{const}\theta_0\), and its latent law is \(\mu\). Initialization is a function of the first product coordinate, whereas every Brownian path is a function of the second. Their full families are therefore independent.



For the pointwise pair \((\theta_0,x)\) this factorization is immediate from the product measure. It retains parameter \(\theta_0\) and latent law \(\mu\). The first coordinate still has that law and parameter \(\theta_0\) is deterministic. Replacing the initialization by an almost-surely equal square-integrable representative preserves both its law and independence from the complete Brownian family.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Particle comparison from a prescribed initial latent law}{Particle comparison from a prescribed initial latent law}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCanonical-lean}


Let \(\mu\) have finite second moment, let \(\theta_0\) be deterministic, assume \(\nabla V\) is globally Lipschitz, and fix \(T\ge0\). The product with the constructed Brownian source supplies independent initialization and all required noise coordinates. One mean-field reference is then constructed, followed by a particle integral solution for every \(N>0\). Their retained equations and the common finite-horizon bound give the timewise mean-square comparison.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Brownian coordinate laws and full-path independence}{Brownian coordinate laws and full-path independence}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCanonical-lean-1}


The initialized Brownian probability space has measurable standard scalar Brownian coordinates, independent as complete paths and independent of initialization. A finite product gives independent copies of the full source. Injective selection of coordinate tags gives the common parameter block and each particle’s latent block without repeating a scalar path.



Therefore all individual Brownian distributions, full-family independence, and initialization independence required in the comparison follow from the product construction, including the case where row zero supplies both parameter and first-particle latent coordinates with different tags.

\leanline{4}{(μ\ :\ Measure\ (EuclideanSpace\ ℝ\ (Fin\ dx)))\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ μ]\ :}
\leanline{4}{(∀\ a\ :\ Fin\ dθ\ ⊕\ (Fin\ N\ ×\ Fin\ dx),}
\leanline{6}{IsPreBrownianReal\ (particleBrownianFamily\ hN\ (@meanFieldInitialBrownian\ dθ\ dx)\ a)}
\leanline{8}{(Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ meanFieldInitialSource\ dθ\ μ)))\ ∧}
\leanline{4}{iIndepFun\ (fun\ a\ ω\ t\ =>\ particleBrownianFamily\ hN\ (@meanFieldInitialBrownian\ dθ\ dx)\ a\ t\ ω)}
\leanline{6}{(Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ meanFieldInitialSource\ dθ\ μ))}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: initialized\_particleBrownianFamily : Actual interacting-particle Brownian coordinates on the iid initialized canonical source: every coordinate is standard Brownian and all full paths are independent, including common-parameter and first-particle coordinates.



\texttt{initialized\_\allowbreak{}particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family}: the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Family\_\allowbreak{}pre\allowbreak{}Brownian}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A common reference and the particle-count-uniform comparison}{A common reference and the particle-count-uniform comparison}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldCanonical-lean-2}


Use initial pair \((\theta_0,X_0)\) on \(\mu\otimes Q_B\). Its finite second moment and deterministic parameter were established above. The Brownian integral construction gives continuous \(Y,\theta\) and finite centered-force bound \(C\ge0\). For every \(N>0\) it then gives continuous particle solution \(Z^N\) with independent copied initialization. The inputs are \(\sqrt2 B^{x,i}\) in latent coordinates and \(\sqrt{2/N}B^\theta\) in the parameter, and

\[\sup_{0\le t\le T}\big(\|\Theta_t^N-\theta_t\|_2+\|X_t^{i,N}-X_t^i\|_2\big)\le\frac{C_T}{\sqrt N}.\]

The coefficient is fixed before \(N\) is chosen. This is a supremum of \(L^2\) norms. For every positive \(N\)-the integral equations and deterministic reference-parameter equation remain part of the conclusion, and the uniform quantity is \(\sup_{t\le T}\|Z_t-R_t\|_{L^2}\).



For each \(N>0\) copy the already constructed reference, add parameter noise with amplitude \(\sqrt{2/N}\), and solve the empirical drift equation. The residual and forcing comparison gives \(C_T/\sqrt N\). The same reference-dependent \(C_T\) works for all counts. The construction does not assert an expectation of a samplewise time supremum.

\section{\texorpdfstring{One-time Wasserstein convergence for tagged particles}{One-time Wasserstein convergence for tagged particles}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleLaw-lean}


At each fixed time the tagged particle pair and its reference copy are defined on the same probability space. Both have finite second moments, and the measure-preserving copy has exactly the original mean-field law. Their joint distribution is consequently an admissible finite-cost coupling. The coordinate \(L^2\) estimate bounds its cost and yields the one-time quadratic Wasserstein rate for the constructed particle process.


\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The unchanged law of a measure-preserving copy}{The unchanged law of a measure-preserving copy}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleLaw-lean-1}


The copy representative is \(X(\omega_i)\) almost surely. Product evaluation pushes \(Q^{\otimes N}\) to \(Q\). Composition of pushforwards therefore gives \(\mathcal L_{Q^{\otimes N}}(C_iX)=\mathcal L_Q(X)\).



Almost-sure replacement of representatives preserves this law. Thus the reference coordinate in the tagged coupling has precisely the original mean-field distribution.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{iidCopyCLM\_\allowbreak{}law}}
\leansource{\texttt{iidCopyCLM\_\allowbreak{}law} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldParticleLaw.lean, lines 21–36}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ iidCopyCLM\_law\ \{Ω\ E\ :\ Type*\}\ [MeasurableSpace\ Ω]}
\leanline{4}{[NormedAddCommGroup\ E]\ [NormedSpace\ ℝ\ E]\ [MeasurableSpace\ E]\ [BorelSpace\ E]}
\leanline{4}{[SecondCountableTopology\ E]\ (Q\ :\ Measure\ Ω)\ [IsProbabilityMeasure\ Q]}
\leanline{4}{\{N\ :\ ℕ\}\ (i\ :\ Fin\ N)\ (X\ :\ Lp\ E\ 2\ Q)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ Q)).map\ (iidCopyCLM\ Q\ i\ X)=Q.map\ X}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: iidCopyCLM\_law : An actual iid \(L^2\) copy has exactly the original law.



\texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Copy\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}law}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{P}, \texttt{heval}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}X}, \texttt{hh}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{Measure.\allowbreak{}map\_\allowbreak{}congr}, \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Copy\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}coe}, \texttt{Lp.\allowbreak{}aestrongly\allowbreak{}Measurable}, \texttt{hh.\allowbreak{}symm}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The coupling of the tagged pair and its reference copy}{The coupling of the tagged pair and its reference copy}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleLaw-lean-2}


On the product space couple particle pair \((\Theta^N,X_i^N)\) with the corresponding copy of \((\theta,X)\). Both belong to \(L^2\), so their quadratic coupling cost is finite. Pair reconstruction and Minkowski give

\[W_2(\mathcal L(\Theta^N,X_i^N),\mathcal L(\theta,X))\le\|\Theta^N-\theta\|_2+\|X_i^N-X_i\|_2.\]

The particle pair \((\Theta_t^N,X_t^{i,N})\) and copied reference have the required marginal laws. Their \(L^2\) moments justify the quadratic cost, whose root is bounded by the sum of coordinate \(L^2\) differences. For latent coordinate \(i\), the same reconstruction estimate in \(L^2\) gives the displayed inequality. Transport, as the infimum over couplings, is no larger. Its finiteness is thus proved before real conversion.

\leansource{\texttt{tagged\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}le} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldParticleLaw.lean, lines 40–62}

\leanline{4}{(hθ\ :\ jointParameterCLM.compLp\ Y=Lp.const\ 2\ Q\ θ)}
\leanline{4}{(Z\ :\ MeanFieldParticleState\ (Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ Q))\ N\ dθ\ dx)\ (i\ :\ Fin\ N)\ :}
\leanline{4}{ULA.wasserstein2}
\leanline{6}{((Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ Q)).map\ (lpJointPair\ \_\ Z.1\ (Z.2\ i)))\ (Q.map\ Y)\ ≠\ ⊤\ ∧}
\leanline{4}{ULA.wasserstein2Real}
\leanline{6}{((Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ Q)).map\ (lpJointPair\ \_\ Z.1\ (Z.2\ i)))\ (Q.map\ Y)\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{‖Z.1-Lp.const\ 2\ (Measure.pi\ (fun\ \_\ :\ Fin\ N\ =>\ Q))\ θ‖+}
\leanline{6}{‖Z.2\ i-(iidMeanFieldCopiesCLM\ Q\ (N\ :=\ N)\ Y).2\ i‖}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{tagged\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}le} defines \texttt{X} as the tagged particle pair and \texttt{R} as its reference independent copy. \texttt{ULA.\allowbreak{}wasserstein2\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}Lp\_\allowbreak{}cost} and its real version bound transport by this explicit square-integrable coupling. \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Copy\allowbreak{}CLM\_\allowbreak{}law} identifies the reference marginal with the original one-time mean-field law. \texttt{lp\allowbreak{}Joint\allowbreak{}Pair\_\allowbreak{}difference\_\allowbreak{}le} bounds the coupling cost by the parameter and tagged-latent errors, and \texttt{iid\allowbreak{}Mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Copies\_\allowbreak{}joint} identifies the corresponding copied pair. Finiteness is returned before the numerical bound.
\end{leanbox}


Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.17.12]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.13]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.3]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.17.4]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}; \cite[Section~3.1.1]{LangevinLean2026IPLA}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The one-time law estimate for the specified particle equation}{The one-time law estimate for the specified particle equation}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldParticleLaw-lean-3}


The pair \((\Theta_t^N,X_t^{i,N})\) has finite second moment, and its reference copy has the law of \(Y_t=(\theta_t,X_t)\). The explicit coupling and coordinate comparison give

\[
W_2\!\left(\mathcal L(\Theta_t^N,X_t^{i,N}),\mathcal L(\theta_t,X_t)\right)
 \le\frac{C_T}{\sqrt N},\qquad 0\le t\le T.
\]

For every positive \(N\), tag, and time, the conclusion retains the empirical-gradient integral equation, independent copied initialization, and copied latent Brownian input plus scaled parameter Brownian input. Thus the law estimate refers to those specified processes.



At each fixed time the coupling proves finite \(W_2\) and bound \(C_T/\sqrt N\), uniformly over the stated interval. This is convergence of tagged one-time laws. A pathwise strong stochastic equation, expected path-supremum bound, or gradient-flow partial differential equation is not asserted by this result.

\texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Input} combines the copied latent noise with the empirical parameter noise. \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}canonical\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}law\_\allowbreak{}comparison} obtains the constructed mean-field and particle solutions from \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}canonical\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}comparison}. For each positive particle count, it rewrites the integral equation using \texttt{particle\allowbreak{}Brownian\allowbreak{}Input}. \texttt{tagged\_\allowbreak{}particle\_\allowbreak{}wasserstein\_\allowbreak{}le} then converts the pathwise root second-moment comparison into finite transport for every tagged pair at each time. Its final bound is the same finite-horizon inverse-square-root particle estimate. It does not infer a time-uniform comparison or an invariant law.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Strong convexity of the Moreau envelope of a strongly convex function}{Strong convexity of the Moreau envelope of a strongly convex function}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-StrongMoreau-lean}


Let \(E\) be a finite-dimensional real inner-product space, \(m\ge0\), \(\lambda>0\), and let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be \(m\)-strongly convex and bounded below. This section proves that the Moreau envelope \(f^\lambda(x)=\min_p\bigl(f(p)+\|p-x\|^2/(2\lambda)\bigr)\) is \(m/(1+m\lambda)\)-strongly convex. The proof tests the minimization that defines \(f^\lambda(ax+by)\) with the convex combination \(aP_\lambda(x)+bP_\lambda(y)\) of the two proximal points, and combines the strong-convexity deficit of \(f\) with the deficit of the quadratic penalty by completing a square. Differentiability of \(f\) is not used.




The result is used when the whole strongly convex potential is smoothed, as in PIPULA. When only a convex summand \(g_2\) is smoothed and an \(m\)-strongly convex \(g_1\) is kept, the sum \(g_1+g_2^\lambda\) keeps the modulus \(m\), because \(g_2^\lambda\) is convex.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Two quadratic identities}{Two quadratic identities}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-StrongMoreau-lean-1}


\textbf{Claim.} Let \(E\) be a real inner-product space and \(u,v\in E\).



\begin{enumerate}


\item If \(a,b\ge0\) and \(a+b=1\), then
\[\|au+bv\|^2=a\|u\|^2+b\|v\|^2-ab\|u-v\|^2.\qquad(1)\]

\item If \(m\ge0\) and \(\lambda>0\), then
\[\frac{m}{1+m\lambda}\|v\|^2\le m\|u\|^2+\frac{\|u-v\|^2}{\lambda}.\qquad(2)\]

\end{enumerate}



\textbf{Proof.} (a) Expanding the squared norms by bilinearity of the inner product,


\[\|au+bv\|^2=a^2\|u\|^2+2ab\langle u,v\rangle+b^2\|v\|^2,\qquad ab\|u-v\|^2=ab\|u\|^2-2ab\langle u,v\rangle+ab\|v\|^2.\]


Hence \(a\|u\|^2+b\|v\|^2-ab\|u-v\|^2=a(1-b)\|u\|^2+2ab\langle u,v\rangle+b(1-a)\|v\|^2\), and since \(1-b=a\) and \(1-a=b\), this equals \(\|au+bv\|^2\).




(b) Put \(c=1+m\lambda\). Since \(m\ge0\) and \(\lambda>0\), \(c\ge1>0\). We show


\[m\|u\|^2+\frac{\|u-v\|^2}{\lambda}-\frac{m}{c}\|v\|^2=\frac{\|cu-v\|^2}{\lambda c}.\qquad(3)\]


Multiply the left side by \(\lambda c>0\) and expand \(\|u-v\|^2=\|u\|^2-2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2\):


\[\begin{aligned}
m\lambda c\|u\|^2+c\bigl(\|u\|^2-2\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2\bigr)-m\lambda\|v\|^2
&=c(1+m\lambda)\|u\|^2-2c\langle u,v\rangle+(c-m\lambda)\|v\|^2\\
&=c^2\|u\|^2-2c\langle u,v\rangle+\|v\|^2=\|cu-v\|^2 .
\end{aligned}\]


Dividing by \(\lambda c\) gives (3). The right-hand side of (3) is nonnegative, because it is a square divided by the positive number \(\lambda c\); this is (2). \(\square\)




For \(v=cu\) the right-hand side of (3) vanishes, so equality holds in (2) and the constant \(m/(1+m\lambda)\) cannot be replaced by a larger one.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}convexCombo\_\allowbreak{}sq}, \texttt{weighted\_\allowbreak{}quadratic\_\allowbreak{}lower}}
\leansource{\texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}convexCombo\_\allowbreak{}sq} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}StrongMoreau.lean, lines 21–29}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ norm\_convexCombo\_sq\ (u\ v\ :\ E)\ \{a\ b\ :\ ℝ\}\ (ha\ :\ 0\ ≤\ a)\ (hb\ :\ 0\ ≤\ b)}
\leanline{4}{(hab\ :\ a\ +\ b\ =\ 1)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖a\ •\ u\ +\ b\ •\ v‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ =\ a\ *\ ‖u‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ b\ *\ ‖v‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ -}
\leanline{6}{a\ *\ b\ *\ ‖u\ -\ v‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{weighted\_\allowbreak{}quadratic\_\allowbreak{}lower} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}StrongMoreau.lean, lines 32–46}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ weighted\_quadratic\_lower\ \{m\ lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hm\ :\ 0\ ≤\ m)\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)}
\leanline{4}{(u\ v\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{m\ /\ (1\ +\ m\ *\ lam)\ *\ ‖v‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ m\ *\ ‖u‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ ‖u\ -\ v‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ lam}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}convex\allowbreak{}Combo\_\allowbreak{}sq} is (1) with the hypotheses \texttt{ha : 0 \ensuremath{\le} a}, \texttt{hb : 0 \ensuremath{\le} b} and \texttt{hab : a + b = 1}. The proof rewrites with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}abs} and \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}nonneg} (here the signs of \(a\) and \(b\) are used to remove \(|a|\) and \(|b|\)), \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}pow}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}left}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}right} and \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}sq\_\allowbreak{}real}; then \texttt{obtain rfl := eq\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}eq hab} substitutes \(a=1-b\) and \texttt{ring} verifies the resulting polynomial identity.




\texttt{weighted\_\allowbreak{}quadratic\_\allowbreak{}lower} is (2) under \texttt{hm : 0 \ensuremath{\le} m} and \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam}. \texttt{hden} is \(c>0\); \texttt{heq} is identity (3), proved by expanding both squared norms, extracting the factor \(c\) with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul} and \texttt{abs\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}pos hden}, and finishing with \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} and \texttt{ring}; \texttt{hpos} is the nonnegativity of the right side of (3) (\texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}nonneg}); rewriting \texttt{hpos} with \texttt{← heq} and \texttt{linarith} give (2).
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The envelope of an \(m\)-strongly convex function is \(m/(1+m\lambda)\)-strongly convex}{The envelope of an m-strongly convex function is m/(1+mλ)-strongly convex}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-StrongMoreau-lean-2}


Recall that \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) is \emph{\(m\)-strongly convex} if for all \(x,y\in E\) and all \(a,b\ge0\) with \(a+b=1\),


\[f(ax+by)\le af(x)+bf(y)-ab\,\frac m2\,\|x-y\|^2.\]


For \(m=0\) this is convexity; for \(m\ge0\) an \(m\)-strongly convex function is convex.




\textbf{Claim.} Let \(E\) be finite-dimensional, \(m\ge0\), \(\lambda>0\), and let \(f:E\to\mathbb R\) be \(m\)-strongly convex and bounded below. Then \(f^\lambda\) is \(m_\lambda\)-strongly convex, where


\[m_\lambda=\frac{m}{1+m\lambda}.\]


\textbf{Proof.} Since \(m\ge0\), \(f\) is convex; together with the lower bound and finite dimension this gives the proximal map \(P_\lambda\) and the envelope \(f^\lambda\) of the previous sections. Let \(x,y\in E\), \(a,b\ge0\) with \(a+b=1\), and put \(z=ax+by\), \(p=P_\lambda(x)\), \(q=P_\lambda(y)\). Since \(P_\lambda(z)\) minimizes \(Q_{\lambda,z}\), the competitor \(ap+bq\) gives


\[f^\lambda(z)\le f(ap+bq)+\frac{\|ap+bq-z\|^2}{2\lambda}.\qquad(4)\]


Since \(ap+bq-z=a(p-x)+b(q-y)\), identity (1) of the previous step with \(u=p-x\) and \(v=q-y\), for which \(u-v=(p-q)-(x-y)\), gives


\[\|ap+bq-z\|^2=a\|p-x\|^2+b\|q-y\|^2-ab\,\|(p-q)-(x-y)\|^2.\]


Strong convexity of \(f\) at \(p,q\) gives \(f(ap+bq)\le af(p)+bf(q)-ab\frac m2\|p-q\|^2\). Inserting both into (4) and regrouping,


\[f^\lambda(z)\le a\Bigl(f(p)+\frac{\|p-x\|^2}{2\lambda}\Bigr)+b\Bigl(f(q)+\frac{\|q-y\|^2}{2\lambda}\Bigr)-\frac{ab}{2}\Bigl(m\|p-q\|^2+\frac{\|(p-q)-(x-y)\|^2}{\lambda}\Bigr).\]


The first two brackets are \(f^\lambda(x)\) and \(f^\lambda(y)\). By (2) of the previous step with \(u=p-q\) and \(v=x-y\), the last bracket is at least \(m_\lambda\|x-y\|^2\); since \(ab\ge0\), the last term is at most \(-\frac{ab}{2}m_\lambda\|x-y\|^2\). Therefore


\[f^\lambda(ax+by)\le af^\lambda(x)+bf^\lambda(y)-ab\,\frac{m_\lambda}{2}\,\|x-y\|^2 .\]


This is \(m_\lambda\)-strong convexity of \(f^\lambda\). \(\square\)




No derivative of \(f\) appears in the argument. For \(m=0\) the claim reduces to convexity of the envelope.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}strongConvex}}
\leansource{\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}strongConvex} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}StrongMoreau.lean, lines 49–84}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ moreau\_strongConvex\ [FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}}
\leanline{4}{\{m\ lam\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hm\ :\ 0\ ≤\ m)\ (hsc\ :\ StrongConvexOn\ univ\ m\ f)}
\leanline{4}{(hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ :}
\leanline{4}{StrongConvexOn\ univ\ (m\ /\ (1\ +\ m\ *\ lam))\ (moreau\ f\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
\texttt{Strong\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On univ m f} is Mathlib's strong convexity on the whole space: it unfolds to \texttt{Uniform\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On} with modulus \(r\mapsto\frac m2r^2\), a structure whose first component is convexity of the domain and whose second component is the displayed inequality for weights \(a,b\ge0\), \(a+b=1\).




\texttt{moreau\_\allowbreak{}strong\allowbreak{}Convex} assumes \texttt{[Finite\allowbreak{}Dimensional \ensuremath{\mathbb R} E]}, \texttt{hm : 0 \ensuremath{\le} m}, \texttt{hsc : Strong\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On univ m f}, the lower bound \texttt{hb} and \texttt{hlam : 0 < lam}, and concludes \texttt{Strong\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On univ (m /\allowbreak{} (1 + m * lam)) (moreau f lam)}.



\begin{enumerate}


\item \texttt{hf} is convexity of \(f\): \texttt{hsc.\allowbreak{}mono hm} weakens the modulus from \(m\) to \(0\), and \texttt{strong\allowbreak{}Convex\allowbreak{}On\_\allowbreak{}zero} identifies \(0\)-strong convexity with convexity.

\item \texttt{refine \ensuremath{\langle}convex\_\allowbreak{}univ, fun x \_\allowbreak{} y \_\allowbreak{} a b ha hb' hab => ?\_\allowbreak{}\ensuremath{\rangle}} introduces the two points and the weights. \texttt{hmin} is (4): \texttt{prox\_\allowbreak{}is\allowbreak{}Prox\allowbreak{}Point} at \texttt{a \ensuremath{\bullet} x + b \ensuremath{\bullet} y} tested at \texttt{a \ensuremath{\bullet} p + b \ensuremath{\bullet} q}. \texttt{hs} is the strong convexity of \(f\) at \(p,q\).

\item \texttt{heq} is \(ap+bq-z=a(p-x)+b(q-y)\); the rewrite with \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}convex\allowbreak{}Combo\_\allowbreak{}sq (p -\allowbreak{} x) (q -\allowbreak{} y) ha hb' hab} expands \(\|ap+bq-z\|^2\), and \texttt{hd} rewrites \((p-x)-(q-y)\) as \((p-q)-(x-y)\).

\item \texttt{hw} is \texttt{weighted\_\allowbreak{}quadratic\_\allowbreak{}lower} with \(u=p-q\), \(v=x-y\), and \texttt{hww} multiplies it by \(ab\ge0\). \texttt{hscale}, \texttt{hmin'} and \texttt{hweight} multiply the relevant inequalities by \(2\lambda\) or \(\lambda\); \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp} clears the denominators, and \texttt{nlinarith} combines everything as in the final display.

\end{enumerate}
\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{PIPULA for the full envelope and its smoothed marginal maximizer}{PIPULA for the full envelope and its smoothed marginal maximizer}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-PIPULA-lean}


PIPULA applies the full-potential pairwise proximal map and then adds Gaussian noise. The residual identity makes it Euler Langevin for the full Moreau envelope at the same scale. The envelope has reduced curvature \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)\). Assume the original potential \(U\) is \(m\)-strongly convex and bounded below, with \(m,\gamma>0\). No global Lipschitz assumption is imposed on \(U\). The resulting estimate concerns the marginal maximizer of \(U^\gamma\) and retains the scale dependence of the generic Euler error.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The full-envelope curvature and admissible step}{The full-envelope curvature and admissible step}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-PIPULA-lean-1}


Set \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)>0\). The inequality \(\gamma m_\gamma=m\gamma/(1+m\gamma)\le1\) gives \(\gamma(m_\gamma+\gamma^{-1})\le2\), equivalently \(\gamma\le2/(m_\gamma+\gamma^{-1})\). Positivity of the denominators justifies the multiplications and divisions.



More specifically, \(m\gamma/(1+m\gamma)<1\) implies \(\gamma m_\gamma+1\le2\). Since the envelope gradient constant is \(1/\gamma\), this is precisely the required Euler restriction. The contraction factor is \(1-m_\gamma\gamma=1/(1+m\gamma)>0\). These facts follow from strong convexity of the original potential and envelope regularity, without a global Lipschitz bound on the original potential’s values.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{fullSmoothingModulus}, \texttt{fullSmoothingModulus\_\allowbreak{}pos}, \texttt{pipula\_\allowbreak{}admissible\_\allowbreak{}step}}
\leansource{\texttt{fullSmoothingModulus} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}PIPULA.lean, lines 26–26}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ fullSmoothingModulus\ (m\ γ\ :\ ℝ)\ :\ ℝ\ :=\ m\ /\ (1\ +\ m\ *\ γ)}
\end{leancode}

Formal correspondence: fullSmoothingModulus : Strong convexity after smoothing the entire potential. pipula\_admissible\_step : With smoothing equal to the time step, the envelope always meets the proved Gaussian ULA step restriction.



\texttt{full\allowbreak{}Smoothing\allowbreak{}Modulus} is a definition with defining expression \texttt{m /\allowbreak{} (1 + m * \ensuremath{\gamma})}. The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.



\texttt{full\allowbreak{}Smoothing\allowbreak{}Modulus\_\allowbreak{}pos}: the proof is the application \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}pos hm (by positivity)}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.



\texttt{pipula\_\allowbreak{}admissible\_\allowbreak{}step}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hd}, \texttt{hmod}, \texttt{ht}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}div\_\allowbreak{}assoc}, \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}le\_\allowbreak{}iff\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}add}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}inv\_\allowbreak{}cancel\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}, \texttt{h\ensuremath{\gamma}.\allowbreak{}ne'}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}, \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Equality of the proximal-noise and envelope Euler laws}{Equality of the proximal-noise and envelope Euler laws}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-PIPULA-lean-2}


In the MYIPLA update set \(g_1=0\) and \(\eta=\gamma\). Proximal optimality gives \(P_\gamma=I-\gamma\nabla U^\gamma\), so the resulting recursion is IPLA for \(U^\gamma\).



The pointwise identity \(P_\gamma(v)=v-\gamma\nabla U^\gamma(v)\), applied to every pair, gives the scaled Euler drift. Adding the same Gaussian vector after proximity gives the PIPULA update. Equality at initialization and at each successor proves equality of all finite iterates; their measurable pushforwards therefore agree as probability laws.

The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The proved estimate about the smoothed marginal center}{The proved estimate about the smoothed marginal center}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-PIPULA-lean-3}


The update specialization \(g_1=0\) and \(\lambda=\gamma\) identifies PIPULA with full-envelope IPLA. The source’s claim about error relative to the original marginal maximizer requires additional analysis: its proximal inner-product deficit \(\mu\) gives envelope-gradient monotonicity coefficient \(\mu/\gamma\) because the residual is divided by \(\gamma\). A scale-uniform discretization estimate and the source’s rate relative to the original marginal maximizer are not established by substituting smoothness \(1/\gamma\) into a generic Euler bound.



The proved estimate instead assumes a bounded-below \(m\)-strongly convex full potential. For \(\gamma>0\) its envelope curvature is \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)\) and its gradient constant is \(1/\gamma\). The generic error term is \((33/20)(1/(\gamma m_\gamma))\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma\), which diverges as \(\gamma\downarrow0\). It therefore does not reproduce the claimed rate \(O(\sqrt\gamma)\) relative to the original marginal maximizer.



Write \(m_\gamma=m/(1+m\gamma)\), let \(\Pi_{N,\gamma}\) be the envelope Gibbs law, and let \(\theta_\gamma\) be its marginal maximizer. Sharp concentration and the Gaussian particle estimate give

\[\left(\mathbb E\|\theta_n-\theta_\gamma\|^2\right)^{1/2}\le\sqrt{\frac{d_\theta}{Nm_\gamma}}+(1-m_\gamma\gamma)^nW_2(\rho_0,\Pi_{N,\gamma})+\frac{33}{20}\frac{\gamma^{-1}}{m_\gamma}\sqrt{d_x+d_\theta/N}\sqrt\gamma.\]

The theorem uses gradient constant \(\gamma^{-1}\). For a separately denoted envelope scale, the curvature is \(m_\eta=m/(1+m\eta)\) rather than \(m\) and the gradient constant is \(1/\eta\); the required restriction is \(\gamma\le2/(m_\eta+1/\eta)\). At equal proximal and Euler scales, the preceding algebra proves it automatically. The equilibrium radius uses this reduced curvature, and the error coefficient uses \((1/\gamma)/m_\gamma\). Consequently the valid finite-step estimate is about the smoothed center and does not prove vanishing error as \(\gamma\downarrow0\). A uniform conclusion relative to the original marginal maximizer would require a stronger envelope analysis.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Exact algebraic corrections and an averaged-proximity counterexample}{Exact algebraic corrections and an averaged-proximity counterexample}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean}


The examples below establish specific algebraic facts used to qualify the source arguments: the sign of a quadratic proximal expansion, scaling of a squared norm, the inverse-step factor in residual monotonicity, and failure of shared-state proximal optimality for averaged pairwise outputs. The convergence results use the finite potential-gap and summed energy inequalities proved above. Each example has only the scope stated for its own assumptions.


\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The second-order sign in the quadratic proximal expansion}{The second-order sign in the quadratic proximal expansion}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean-1}


The proximal equation \(p+\lambda\nabla g_2(p)=x\) gives a positive second-order coefficient in the scalar quadratic example:

\[
\operatorname{prox}_{u^2/2}^\lambda(x)
=\frac{x}{1+\lambda}
=x-\lambda x+\lambda^2x-\frac{\lambda^3x}{1+\lambda}.
\]

The negative coefficient printed in the source leaves error at least \(\lambda^2\) at \(x=1\) and therefore cannot be its third-order expansion. The quadratic is not globally value Lipschitz, so this calculation diagnoses the displayed algebra rather than refuting a full theorem under that assumption.



Almost-everywhere twice differentiability and the stated moment condition also do not provide the uniform integrable third-order remainder needed for the claimed order \(O(\lambda)\) for displacement of the original marginal maximizer. The proved finite-gap result gives \(\sqrt{\lambda K^2/m}\); this difference in established scope does not prove the stronger assertion false. For \(f(u)=u^2/2\) the minimizer equation is \((1+\lambda)p=x\). Division gives the exact resolvent and its positive \(\lambda^2x\) term with the displayed third-order remainder.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{quadratic\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}minimizes}, \texttt{quadratic\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}expansion}, \texttt{negative\_\allowbreak{}second\_\allowbreak{}order\_\allowbreak{}residual} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{quadratic\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}minimizes} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 20–31}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ quadratic\_prox\_minimizes\ (x\ lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (hlam\ :\ 0\ <\ lam)\ (u\ :\ ℝ)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(x\ /\ (1\ +\ lam))\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2\ +\ (x\ /\ (1\ +\ lam)\ -\ x)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ (2\ *\ lam)\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{u\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ 2\ +\ (u\ -\ x)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ /\ (2\ *\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{quadratic\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}expansion} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 36–39}

\leanline{0}{theorem\ quadratic\_prox\_expansion\ (x\ lam\ :\ ℝ)\ (hlam\ :\ 1\ +\ lam\ ≠\ 0)\ :}
\leanline{4}{x\ /\ (1\ +\ lam)\ =\ x\ -\ lam\ *\ x\ +\ lam\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ x\ -\ lam\ \textasciicircum{}\ 3\ *\ x\ /\ (1\ +\ lam)}
\end{leancode}

\leanline{4}{1\ /\ (1\ +\ lam)\ -\ (1\ -\ lam\ -\ lam\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ =\ lam\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ (2\ +\ lam)\ /\ (1\ +\ lam)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{negative\_\allowbreak{}second\_\allowbreak{}order\_\allowbreak{}residual\_\allowbreak{}ge\_\allowbreak{}sq} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 48–52}

Formal correspondence: quadratic\_prox\_minimizes : The proximal point of \(u\mapsto u^2/2\) is \(x/(1+\lambda)\). The difference of objectives is a nonnegative square; no differentiability of an argmin map is assumed. quadratic\_prox\_expansion : The second-order coefficient in the proximal expansion has a positive sign. This exact identity diagnoses the opposite sign in v1 equation (25) and v3 equation (22). negative\_second\_order\_residual : The negative second-order term printed in v1 equation (25) /\allowbreak{} v3 equation (22) leaves an error of order \(\lambda^2\), already for the scalar quadratic.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Squared scaling and the unit recurrence ratio}{Squared scaling and the unit recurrence ratio}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean-2}


Multiplication of a vector by \(N\) multiplies its squared norm by \(N^2\), by norm homogeneity and squaring. Separately, an affine recurrence with unit ratio has finite sum \(n\) and therefore a linear-in-iteration additive bound, rather than a bounded geometric sum. These identities determine the particle powers and the strict positive-denominator condition needed in an energy or transport estimate.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling}, \texttt{linear\_\allowbreak{}squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling\_\allowbreak{}fails}, \texttt{endpoint\_\allowbreak{}denominator\_\allowbreak{}zero} and 1 more}
\leansource{\texttt{squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 56–59}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ squared\_gradient\_scaling\ \{E\ :\ Type*\}\ [NormedAddCommGroup\ E]}
\leanline{4}{[NormedSpace\ ℝ\ E]\ (N\ :\ ℝ)\ (g\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{‖N\ •\ g‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ =\ N\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ *\ ‖g‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{linear\_\allowbreak{}squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling\_\allowbreak{}fails} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 63–64}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ linear\_squared\_gradient\_scaling\_fails\ :}
\leanline{4}{¬\ ((2\ *\ (1\ :\ ℝ))\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ 2\ *\ 1)}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{endpoint\_\allowbreak{}denominator\_\allowbreak{}zero} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 69–73}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ endpoint\_denominator\_zero\ (μ\ γ\ :\ ℝ)}
\leanline{4}{(hγ\ :\ γ\ ≠\ 0)\ (hden\ :\ 1\ -\ μ\ *\ γ\ ≠\ 0)\ :}
\leanline{4}{1\ -\ (γ\ /\ (1\ -\ μ\ *\ γ))\ *\ (1\ -\ μ\ *\ γ)\ /\ γ\ =\ 0}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{endpoint\_\allowbreak{}error\_\allowbreak{}floor\_\allowbreak{}fails} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 77–78}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ endpoint\_error\_floor\_fails\ :}
\leanline{4}{¬\ ((1\ :\ ℝ)\ ≤\ 0\ +\ 1\ /\ (1\ -\ 1))}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Norm homogeneity gives \(\|Ng\|^2=N^2\|g\|^2\). The numerical case \(N=2,\|g\|=1\) disproves linear squared-gradient scaling. Substituting \(\lambda=\gamma/(1-\mu\gamma)\) gives \(1-q=0\). Lean’s total real division has \(1/0=0\); the numerical certificate \(1\not\le0+1/(1-1)\) shows why an unguarded formula at that endpoint cannot express an error floor.



Formal correspondence: squared\_gradient\_scaling : Scaling a gradient by \(N\) scales its squared norm by \(N^2\), not \(N\). This corrects v1 Corollary A.5; v3 Corollary A.12 already uses this scaling. linear\_squared\_gradient\_scaling\_fails : A numerical witness to the invalid \(NC\) bound from the valid \(C\) bound when the gradient is multiplied by \(N\). endpoint\_denominator\_zero : The geometric-series denominator is zero at the upper endpoint allowed in v1 Corollaries A.4--A.6 and v3 Corollaries A.11--A.13. A finite error floor requires a strict inequality. endpoint\_error\_floor\_fails : At \(q=1\), the recurrence \(e(n+1)=e(n)+1\) has linear growth. Interpreting real division by zero cannot rescue the printed error floor.



\texttt{squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling}: the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}smul}, \texttt{Real.\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}abs}, \texttt{mul\_\allowbreak{}pow}, \texttt{sq\_\allowbreak{}abs}.



\texttt{linear\_\allowbreak{}squared\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}scaling\_\allowbreak{}fails}: the proof is the application \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}num}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.



\texttt{endpoint\_\allowbreak{}denominator\_\allowbreak{}zero}: the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}cancel\ensuremath{{}_{0}}}, \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}self}.



\texttt{endpoint\_\allowbreak{}error\_\allowbreak{}floor\_\allowbreak{}fails}: the proof is the application \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}num}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The inverse-step factor in residual monotonicity}{The inverse-step factor in residual monotonicity}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean-3}


Assume \(\langle x-y,p(x)-p(y)\rangle\le(1-\mu)\|x-y\|^2\) and define the residual difference by \(R(x)-R(y)=\gamma^{-1}[(x-y)-(p(x)-p(y))]\). For \(\gamma>0\), expansion gives \(\langle x-y,R(x)-R(y)\rangle\ge(\mu/\gamma)\|x-y\|^2\). Hence the strong-monotonicity coefficient is \(\mu/\gamma\) rather than \(\mu\).



Explicitly the inner product expands as \(\langle x-y,R(x)-R(y)\rangle=\gamma^{-1}(\|x-y\|^2-\langle x-y,p(x)-p(y)\rangle)\). Substitution of the proximal deficit bounds it below by \((\mu/\gamma)\|x-y\|^2\). Division by \(\gamma\) in the residual definition is the source of this inverse-step factor.

\leanline{4}{(p\ :\ E\ →\ E)\ (μ\ γ\ :\ ℝ)\ (hγ\ :\ 0\ <\ γ)}
\leanline{4}{(hB\ :\ ∀\ x\ y,\ inner\ ℝ\ (x\ -\ y)\ (p\ x\ -\ p\ y)\ ≤\ (1\ -\ μ)\ *\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)}
\leanline{4}{(x\ y\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{(μ\ /\ γ)\ *\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{inner\ ℝ\ (x\ -\ y)\ (γ⁻¹\ •\ (x\ -\ p\ x)\ -\ γ⁻¹\ •\ (y\ -\ p\ y))}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}

\texttt{proximal\_\allowbreak{}displacement\_\allowbreak{}strong\_\allowbreak{}monotonicity}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{hid}, \texttt{h}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}smul\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{real\_\allowbreak{}inner\_\allowbreak{}self\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}norm\_\allowbreak{}sq}, \texttt{div\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}mul\_\allowbreak{}inv}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{nlinarith}.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A counterexample to shared-state proximal optimality}{A counterexample to shared-state proximal optimality}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Corrections-lean-4}


Take \(g(\theta,x)=|\theta-x|\) and proximal scale one. The pair inputs \((1,0)\) and \((1,4)\) have proximal minimizers \((1/2,1/2)\) and \((2,3)\). Averaging their parameter components gives \((5/4,1/2,3)\). The shared-state objective is

\[\tfrac12(|\theta-x_1|+|\theta-x_2|)+\tfrac12(\theta-1)^2+\tfrac14(x_1^2+(x_2-4)^2).\]

The competitor \((1,1,3)\) has strictly smaller objective, as follows from direct absolute-value evaluation. Each pair was allowed its own proposed parameter, whereas the shared objective permits only one. Averaging the proposals need not solve that common minimization problem.



In particular the averaged output has value \(51/32\) and the feasible comparator has value \(3/2=48/32\). This strict comparison disproves the shared-state proximal identity in general. The splitting proof instead sums the valid single-pair energy inequalities and applies Jensen’s inequality to the parameter average.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{pairAbsObjective}, \texttt{pairAbs\_\allowbreak{}first\_\allowbreak{}prox}, \texttt{pairAbs\_\allowbreak{}second\_\allowbreak{}prox} and 2 more}
\leansource{\texttt{pairAbsObjective} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 98–99}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ pairAbsObjective\ (θ\ x\ θ₀\ x₀\ :\ ℝ)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{|θ\ -\ x|\ +\ ((θ\ -\ θ₀)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ (x\ -\ x₀)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ /\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{pairAbs\_\allowbreak{}first\_\allowbreak{}prox} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 102–107}
\begin{leancode}

\end{leancode}

\begin{leancode}

\end{leancode}

\leanline{2}{(|θ\ -\ x₁|\ +\ |θ\ -\ x₂|)\ /\ 2\ +}
\leanline{4}{((θ\ -\ 1)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ (x₁\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ +\ (x₂\ -\ 4)\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ /\ 2)\ /\ 2}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{averaged\_\allowbreak{}pair\_\allowbreak{}prox\_\allowbreak{}not\_\allowbreak{}lifted\_\allowbreak{}minimizer} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}Corrections.lean, lines 126–128}

\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: pairAbsObjective : The pair objective for the convex, globally Lipschitz penalty \(g_2(\theta,x)=|\theta-x|\) with smoothing parameter one. pairAbs\_first\_prox : At pair input \((1,0)\), the true proximal point is \((1/2,1/2)\). pairAbs\_second\_prox : At pair input \((1,4)\), the true proximal point is \((2,3)\). sharedAbsObjective : The two-particle lifted objective in physical particle coordinates. The quadratic penalty uses precisely the scaled state norm with \(N=2\). averaged\_pair\_prox\_not\_lifted\_minimizer : Averaging the two true pairwise proximal outputs gives parameter \(5/4\) and particles \((1/2,3)\). A feasible competitor \((1,1,3)\) has strictly smaller lifted objective. Thus the pairwise average cannot be a lifted proximal minimizer, even for a convex globally Lipschitz penalty.

The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Bounded fields and positive global strong monotonicity}{Bounded fields and positive global strong monotonicity}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-PIPULAConsistency-lean}


A globally bounded vector field on a nontrivial real Hilbert space cannot have a positive global strong-monotonicity constant. The Moreau gradient of a globally Lipschitz convex function is bounded. Hence the conjunction of full-potential value Lipschitzness and a positive global proximal-residual deficit is inconsistent. The conclusion concerns that literal conjunction of assumptions; it does not apply to a decomposition in which only the nonsmooth summand is Lipschitz.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Incompatibility of boundedness and positive global monotonicity}{Incompatibility of boundedness and positive global monotonicity}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-PIPULAConsistency-lean-1}


Assume \(\|R(x)\|\le K\) and \(c\|x-y\|^2\le\langle x-y,R(x)-R(y)\rangle\) with \(c>0\). Cauchy–Schwarz and the triangle inequality give \(c\|x-y\|^2\le2K\|x-y\|\). A nonzero direction can be scaled to have length greater than \(2K/c\), contradicting this bound.



In the alternative notation \(\|b(x)\|\le K\) and \(\langle b(x)-b(y),x-y\rangle\ge q\|x-y\|^2\) with \(q>0\) imply \(q\|x-y\|\le2K\) for every distinct pair. Explicitly choose separation \(r>2K/c\). Strong monotonicity bounds the inner product below by \(cr^2\), while boundedness bounds it above by \(2Kr\). Division by \(r\) gives \(cr\le2K\). A nontrivial space has arbitrarily large such separations \(r\), so the assumptions cannot both hold. The nontrivial-space qualification is essential.

\leanline{4}{(R\ :\ E\ →\ E)\ (c\ K\ :\ ℝ)\ (hc\ :\ 0\ <\ c)\ (hK\ :\ 0\ ≤\ K)}
\leanline{4}{(hbound\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖R\ x‖\ ≤\ K)}
\leanline{4}{(hmono\ :\ ∀\ x\ y,\ c\ *\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2\ ≤\ {\leanfallback ⟪}x\ -\ y,\ R\ x\ -\ R\ y{\leanfallback ⟫})\ :\ False}
\end{leancode}

The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{field\_\allowbreak{}simp}, \texttt{linarith}, \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The obstruction for a globally bounded proximal residual}{The obstruction for a globally bounded proximal residual}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-PIPULAConsistency-lean-2}


Consider the reading of the source reduction in which \(g_1=0\) also applies the value-Lipschitz assumption to \(U=g_2\). Then \(U\) is globally \(K\)-Lipschitz, so its Moreau gradient \(R_\gamma=\gamma^{-1}(I-P_\gamma)\) satisfies \(\|R_\gamma(x)\|\le K\). A positive proximal deficit gives

\[
\frac{\mu}{\gamma}\|x-y\|^2
 \le\langle x-y,R_\gamma(x)-R_\gamma(y)\rangle
 \le2K\|x-y\|.
\]

The preceding obstruction contradicts these simultaneous global conditions on a nontrivial space. If the Lipschitz assumption instead concerns the nonsmooth term in an independent decomposition \(U=g_1+g_2\), it does not make the full \(U\) Lipschitz and the contradiction does not follow.



The residual \(R_\gamma=(I-P_\gamma)/\gamma\) is bounded in the first reading, while the deficit makes it strongly monotone with coefficient \(\mu/\gamma\). Under \(\mu>0\) these are incompatible. This is a conditional consistency result about the specified assumptions, not a general refutation of the source corollary. The separate full-potential PIPULA theorem assumes strong convexity and a lower bound without global value Lipschitzness.

\leanline{4}{[FiniteDimensional\ ℝ\ E]\ [Nontrivial\ E]}
\leanline{4}{\{U\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ (hU\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ U)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ x,\ b\ ≤\ U\ x)}
\leanline{4}{\{K\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hK\ :\ LipschitzWith\ K\ U)\ (μ\ γ\ :\ ℝ)\ (hμ\ :\ 0\ <\ μ)\ (hγ\ :\ 0\ <\ γ)}
\leanline{4}{(hB\ :\ ∀\ x\ y,\ {\leanfallback ⟪}x\ -\ y,\ prox\ U\ γ\ x\ -\ prox\ U\ γ\ y{\leanfallback ⟫}\ ≤}
\leanline{6}{(1\ -\ μ)\ *\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ \textasciicircum{}\ 2)\ :\ False}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: lipschitz\_full\_potential\_not\_proximal\_deficit : Under a literal full-potential globally Lipschitz reading, B1 cannot hold with positive deficit in positive dimension. The separate PIPULA theorem therefore does not assume global Lipschitzness of the full strongly convex \(U\).

\end{leanbox}
\section{\texorpdfstring{Scalar affine recurrences for the proposed splitting coefficients}{Scalar affine recurrences for the proposed splitting coefficients}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-Recurrence-lean}


Assume a scalar affine inequality with the coefficients specified below. Nonnegative multiplication and induction give its finite geometric sum. A strict ratio below one gives a uniform error bound; ratio one gives only the finite-horizon linear bound. These are conditional scalar conclusions. A stochastic one-step estimate must be proved separately before this recurrence can describe an algorithmic error.




Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.15]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The proposed coefficients and their sign conditions}{The proposed coefficients and their sign conditions}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Recurrence-lean-1}


Define

\[q=\frac{\lambda(1-\mu\gamma)}\gamma,\qquad c=\lambda\left(\frac{2\gamma LD}{N}+\lambda C\right).\]

The stated sign assumptions give \(q,c\ge0\). Under \(\gamma>0\) and \(\mu\gamma<1\), multiplication through positive denominators gives \(q<1\iff\lambda<\gamma/(1-\mu\gamma)\). The strict inequality is needed for the geometric denominator. The subgradient contribution is \(\lambda^2C\) and has no further division by \(N\).



Equivalently let \(q=\lambda(1-\mu\gamma)/\gamma\) and \(c=2\lambda\gamma LD/N+\lambda^2C\). Positivity of \(\gamma\) and \(1-\mu\gamma\) makes \(q<1\) equivalent to \(\lambda<\gamma/(1-\mu\gamma)\). Division by \(1-q\) is consequently permitted only under that strict condition; the signs of \(q,c\) follow separately. In particular \(q<1\) fails at the limiting scale, where \(q=1\). Only the Gaussian part of the forcing contains \(1/N\), so its particle scaling differs from the subgradient part.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The finite geometric bound for an assumed recurrence}{The finite geometric bound for an assumed recurrence}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Recurrence-lean-2}


Assume \(e_{n+1}\le q e_n+c\) and \(q\ge0\). Repeated substitution gives \(e_n\le q^ne_0+c\sum_{j<n}q^j\). If \(q<1\), the finite geometric identity gives \(e_n\le q^ne_0+c(1-q^n)/(1-q)\). Under \(c\ge0\) the tail term \(-cq^n/(1-q)\) is nonpositive, so \(e_n\le q^ne_0+c/(1-q)\).



The induction is over \(n\). Multiply the preceding bound by nonnegative \(q\) and add \(c\). When \(q<1\) the geometric denominator is positive; when \(c\ge0\) its negative tail may be discarded. Each statement keeps the original recurrence as an explicit hypothesis.

These three declarations instantiate the shared affine-recurrence lemmas with the displayed corrected coefficients. They are scalar consequences of a supplied recurrence, independently of the later stochastic theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The unit ratio and the equal-scale formula}{The unit ratio and the equal-scale formula}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-Recurrence-lean-3}


When \(q=1\), induction gives \(e_n\le e_0+nc\). Under \(\lambda=\gamma\) and the nonzero-denominator assumptions, algebra instead gives

\[\frac c{1-q}=\frac\gamma\mu\left(\frac{2LD}{N}+C\right).\]

The \(C\) contribution is unscaled by particle count. At unit ratio the finite sum is \(n\), giving \(e_n\le e_0+nc\) rather than a bounded geometric error. Under equal positive scales and strict contraction, cancellation in \(c/(1-q)\) produces the displayed identity. This scalar computation does not establish that a stochastic error satisfies the assumed recurrence.

At \(q=1\), retain the finite sum: the induction gives \(e_n\le e_0+nc\). For \(\lambda=\gamma\), cancellation in the strict regime gives \[\frac{c}{1-q}=\frac\gamma\mu\left(\frac{2LD}{N}+C\right).\] The \(C\) term is not divided by \(N\): its numerator was \(\lambda^2 C\), whereas the Gaussian contribution was \(2\lambda\gamma LD/N\). The nonzero assumptions in \texttt{splitting\_\allowbreak{}bias\_\allowbreak{}equal\_\allowbreak{}steps} justify exactly these algebraic cancellations.



Formal correspondence: splitting\_recurrence\_endpoint : At the noncontracting endpoint, retain the linear finite-horizon bound. splitting\_bias\_equal\_steps : When \(\lambda=\gamma\), the corrected error floor takes this particularly simple form, with no \(1/N\) multiplying \(C\).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Finite smoothing comparison for mean-field integral equations}{Finite smoothing comparison for mean-field integral equations}}
\label{proof-module-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean}


Assume additionally that the convex penalty is differentiable with Lipschitz gradient. Its proximal residual then equals the original gradient evaluated at the proximal point. The finite proximal displacement bounds the difference between original and envelope gradients by a constant times the smoothing scale. Construct both mean-field integral solutions with identical initial state and additive input. The input cancels on subtraction, and Grönwall bounds the supremum over time of their mean-square norms.




Earlier results used here: \cite[Section~3.43]{LangevinLean2026ULASC}.
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Equality of the subgradient and gradient at a differentiability point}{Equality of the subgradient and gradient at a differentiability point}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean-1}


If \(s\) is a supporting subgradient, then \(x\mapsto f(x)-\langle s,x\rangle\) has a global minimum at \(p\). Differentiability makes its derivative zero, giving \(s=\nabla f(p)\). Applied to the proximal residual and envelope-gradient formula, this gives \(\nabla f^\eta(x)=\nabla f(P_\eta(x))\).



Equivalently, a support \(s\) at \(p\) minimizes \(f(x)-\langle s,x\rangle\) at \(p\), so \(s=\nabla f(p)\). The conclusion is an equality at the minimizing point, not an asymptotic approximation.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{IsSubgradient.\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient}, \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}prox}}
\leansource{\texttt{IsSubgradient.\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldSmoothing.lean, lines 29–43}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ IsSubgradient.eq\_gradient\ \{f\ :\ E\ →\ ℝ\}\ \{p\ s\ :\ E\}}
\leanline{4}{(hs\ :\ IsSubgradient\ f\ p\ s)\ (hf\ :\ DifferentiableAt\ ℝ\ f\ p)\ :\ s\ =\ gradient\ f\ p}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}prox} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldSmoothing.lean, lines 46–52}
\begin{leancode}

\leanline{4}{(hconv\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ f)\ \{η\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hη\ :\ 0\ <\ η)\ (x\ :\ E)\ :}
\leanline{4}{gradient\ (moreau\ f\ η)\ x\ =\ gradient\ f\ (prox\ f\ η\ x)}
\end{leancode}

Formal correspondence: IsSubgradient.eq\_gradient : A supporting subgradient of a differentiable function is its actual gradient. gradient\_moreau\_eq\_gradient\_prox : The actual Moreau score is the original smooth score at the true proximal point.



\texttt{Is\allowbreak{}Subgradient.\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}gradient}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{F}, \texttt{hmin}, \texttt{h}, \texttt{hd}, \texttt{hg}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{inner\_\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}right}, \texttt{map\_\allowbreak{}sub}, \texttt{Pi.\allowbreak{}sub\_\allowbreak{}def}, \texttt{Inner\allowbreak{}Product\allowbreak{}Space.\allowbreak{}to\allowbreak{}Dual\_\allowbreak{}apply\_\allowbreak{}apply}, \texttt{sub\_\allowbreak{}eq\_\allowbreak{}zero.\allowbreak{}mp}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{linarith}.

\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The finite gradient difference under smoothing}{The finite gradient difference under smoothing}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean-2}


The gradient \(\nabla f\) is \(L_2\)-Lipschitz, and \(P_\eta\) is nonexpansive. Their composition therefore has constant \(L_2\) independently of \(\eta\). The displacement bound \(\|x-P_\eta(x)\|\le\eta K\) then gives

\[\|\nabla f(x)-\nabla f^\eta(x)\|\le L_2\|x-P_\eta(x)\|\le\eta L_2K.\]

Optimality and differentiability identify \(\nabla f^\eta(x)=\nabla f(P_\eta x)\). When \(\nabla f\) is \(L_2\)-Lipschitz and \(f\) is \(K\)-Lipschitz, the same argument gives

\[\|\nabla f^\eta(x)-\nabla f(x)\|\le L_2\|P_\eta x-x\|\le\eta L_2K.\]

The envelope gradient is \(\nabla f\circ P_\eta\). Its gradient constant is bounded by \(L_2\), which can be smaller than the generic \(1/\eta\) bound. Comparison with \(\nabla f(x)\) costs at most \(L_2\|P_\eta x-x\|\le\eta L_2K\). This finite uniform defect supplies the forcing in the integral-equation comparison.

\leanline{4}{(hconv\ :\ ConvexOn\ ℝ\ univ\ f)\ (hb\ :\ ∃\ b,\ ∀\ z,\ b\ ≤\ f\ z)}
\leanline{4}{(hf\ :\ Differentiable\ ℝ\ f)\ \{η\ :\ ℝ\}\ (hη\ :\ 0\ <\ η)}
\leanline{4}{\{L\ :\ ℝ≥0\}\ (hLip\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ (gradient\ f))\ :}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Component regularity from the sum and the smooth term}{Component regularity from the sum and the smooth term}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean-3}


If \(U=f+g\) and \(f\) are differentiable, then \(g=U-f\) is differentiable. Gradient linearity gives \(\nabla g=\nabla U-\nabla f\). Taking differences at two points and the triangle inequality yields \(\operatorname{Lip}(\nabla g)\le L_U+L_f\).



Thus the constant \(L_2\) may be supplied from regularity of the original sum and the known smooth component. This gives a justified \(L_2\) without adding an unstated differentiability assumption on the remaining component.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{component\_\allowbreak{}differentiable\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}sum}, \texttt{component\_\allowbreak{}gradient\_\allowbreak{}lipschitz\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}sum}}

\leanline{4}{Differentiable\ ℝ\ g}
\end{leancode}

\texttt{component\_\allowbreak{}differentiable\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}sum}: the proof is the application \texttt{convert hfg.\allowbreak{}sub hf using 1}. It specializes that result with the hypotheses and objects in the exact statement.

\end{leanbox}
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Comparison with common additive input}{Comparison with common additive input}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean-4}


Let integral solutions with drifts \(g,k\) share additive input \(B\) and have initial states \(x,y\). Assume one drift is globally Lipschitz and \(\sup_z\|g(z)-k(z)\|\le\varepsilon\). Subtraction cancels the input. Adding and subtracting one drift at the other state bounds the integrand by \(L\|Y-Z\|+\varepsilon\). Integral Grönwall gives

\[\|Y_t-Z_t\|\le e^{Lt}\|x-y\|+\varepsilon\Psi(L,t),\qquad\Psi(L,t)=\begin{cases}(e^{Lt}-1)/L,&L\ne0,\\t,&L=0.\end{cases}\]

No differentiation of \(B\) is needed. The bounded defect is integrated with factor \(\Psi\). The definition of \(\Psi\) includes zero Lipschitz constant, so the formula requires no division by zero.

\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ integral\_drift\_perturbation}
\leanline{4}{(g\ k\ :\ H\ →\ H)\ (L\ M\ :\ ℝ≥0)\ (hg\ :\ LipschitzWith\ L\ g)\ (hk\ :\ LipschitzWith\ M\ k)}
\leanline{4}{(ε\ :\ ℝ)\ (hdefect\ :\ ∀\ x,\ ‖g\ x\ -\ k\ x‖\ ≤\ ε)}
\leanline{4}{(x\ y\ :\ H)\ (B\ Y\ Z\ :\ ℝ\ →\ H)\ (hY\ :\ Continuous\ Y)\ (hZ\ :\ Continuous\ Z)}
\leanline{4}{(T\ :\ ℝ)\ (hT\ :\ 0\ ≤\ T)}
\leanline{4}{(hYe\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ Y\ t\ -\ x\ =\ -(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ g\ (Y\ s))\ +\ B\ t)}
\leanline{4}{(hZe\ :\ ∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ Z\ t\ -\ y\ =\ -(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ k\ (Z\ s))\ +\ B\ t)\ :}
\leanline{4}{∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ ‖Y\ t\ -\ Z\ t‖\ ≤\ gronwallBound\ ‖x\ -\ y‖\ (L\ :\ ℝ)\ ε\ t}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Formal correspondence: integral\_drift\_perturbation : Two actual integral equations driven by common additive input differ by the Grönwall response to a uniform drift defect. No derivative of the input is used, and no error recurrence is assumed.

The \texttt{calc} block checks the successive equalities or inequalities in the argument.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The finite smoothing estimate for the two integral systems}{The finite smoothing estimate for the two integral systems}}
\label{report-step-ProximalLangevin-MeanFieldSmoothing-lean-5}


Assume \(g_2\) is differentiable with \(L_2\)-Lipschitz gradient, and retain convexity, a lower bound, and value Lipschitz constant \(K\). The supporting residual equals the gradient at the proximal minimizer:

\[
\nabla g_2^\eta(v)=\nabla g_2(P_\eta(v)).
\]

Nonexpansiveness of \(P_\eta\) preserves constant \(L_2\), and \(\|v-P_\eta(v)\|\le\eta K\) gives

\[
\|\nabla g_2(v)-\nabla g_2^\eta(v)\|\le\eta L_2K.
\]

If instead regularity is stated for \(U=g_1+g_2\) with constant \(L_U\) and for \(g_1\) with constant \(L_1\), the difference argument supplies \(L_2=L_U+L_1\).



Construct both Hilbert-space solutions with common initial state and continuous additive input. The averaged drifts differ uniformly by at most \(2\eta L_2K\). Their pair-gradient constant is at most \(L_1+L_2\), independent of \(\eta\). The averaging operator gives the corresponding factor two. Grönwall yields

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}\|Y_t-Z_t\|_{L^2}
 \le 2\eta L_2K\,\Psi(2(L_1+L_2),T),
\qquad
\Psi(a,T)=\begin{cases}(e^{aT}-1)/a,&a>0,\\T,&a=0.\end{cases}
\]

For deterministic initial parameter and latent-only Brownian input these are the mean-field systems described above. The finite bound is linear in \(\eta\). More explicitly the averaged drift constant is \(2(L_1+L_2)\) and its uniform difference is \(2\eta L_2K\). The supremum is outside the \(L^2\) norm. Proposition C.3 instead bounds the expectation of the supremum of squared errors. The square root of that quantity bounds the supremum of the \(L^2\) error norms. A bound on the expectation of the supremum of squared errors is not established here.

\begin{leanbox}{Lean: \texttt{meanFieldSmoothingConstant}, \texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}meanField\_\allowbreak{}smoothing\_\allowbreak{}comparison}}
\leansource{\texttt{meanFieldSmoothingConstant} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldSmoothing.lean, lines 169–172}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{def\ meanFieldSmoothingConstant\ (L₁\ L₂\ K\ :\ ℝ≥0)\ (T\ :\ ℝ)\ :\ ℝ\ :=}
\leanline{2}{2\ *\ (L₂\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ (K\ :\ ℝ)\ *\ gronwallBound\ 0\ (2\ *\ ((L₁\ :\ ℝ)\ +\ (L₂\ :\ ℝ)))\ 1\ T}
\leanline{0}{\strut}
\leanline{0}{section\ MeanField}
\end{leancode}
\leansource{\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}meanField\_\allowbreak{}smoothing\_\allowbreak{}comparison} \quad ProximalLangevin/\allowbreak{}MeanFieldSmoothing.lean, lines 187–249}
\begin{leancode}
\leanline{0}{theorem\ exists\_meanField\_smoothing\_comparison}
\leanline{4}{(initial\ :\ Lp\ (IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ 2\ Q)}
\leanline{4}{(B\ :\ ℝ\ →\ Lp\ (IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ 2\ Q)\ (hB\ :\ Continuous\ B)\ (hB0\ :\ B\ 0\ =\ 0)}
\leanline{4}{(T\ :\ ℝ)\ (hT\ :\ 0\ ≤\ T)\ :}
\leanline{4}{∃\ (hU\ :\ LipschitzWith\ (L₁\ +\ L₂)\ (gradient\ (fun\ x\ =>\ g₁\ x\ +\ g₂\ x)))}
\leanline{6}{(hV\ :\ LipschitzWith\ (L₁\ +\ L₂)\ (gradient\ (smoothedPotential\ g₁\ g₂\ η)))}
\leanline{6}{(Y\ Z\ :\ ℝ\ →\ Lp\ (IPLA.Joint\ dθ\ dx)\ 2\ Q),}
\leanline{6}{Continuous\ Y\ ∧\ Continuous\ Z\ ∧\ Y\ 0\ =\ initial\ ∧\ Z\ 0\ =\ initial\ ∧}
\leanline{6}{(∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ Y\ t\ -\ initial\ =}
\leanline{8}{-(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ lpMeanFieldDrift\ Q\ (fun\ x\ =>\ g₁\ x\ +\ g₂\ x)\ hU\ (Y\ s))\ +\ B\ t)\ ∧}
\leanline{6}{(∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ Z\ t\ -\ initial\ =}
\leanline{8}{-(∫\ s\ in\ 0..t,\ lpMeanFieldDrift\ Q\ (smoothedPotential\ g₁\ g₂\ η)\ hV\ (Z\ s))\ +\ B\ t)\ ∧}
\leanline{6}{∀\ t\ ∈\ Icc\ 0\ T,\ ‖Y\ t\ -\ Z\ t‖\ ≤\ η\ *\ meanFieldSmoothingConstant\ L₁\ L₂\ K\ T}
\end{leancode}
\leanlabel{How the Lean proof follows the argument}
Both systems are constructed with the same continuous additive input. Subtracting their equations cancels that input and leaves a differentiable integral error. One drift contributes its global Lipschitz constant times the current difference; the other contributes the uniform Moreau score defect just proved. Grönwall then bounds the actual process difference by a coefficient times \(\eta\) on the finite horizon. The component-regularity declarations justify the smoothness constants of each summand from the stated assumptions on the regular sum before this perturbation argument is applied.



Formal correspondence: meanFieldSmoothingConstant : Explicit finite-horizon coefficient for the mean-field \(L^2\) smoothing error. The Grönwall response is \(T\) at zero smoothness and otherwise \(\frac{\exp(2(L_1+L_2)T)-1}{2(L_1+L_2)}\). exists\_meanField\_smoothing\_comparison : Constructed original and smoothed mean-field processes share their actual initial variable and continuous additive input. Their \(L^2\) distance, uniformly over the finite horizon, is bounded by \(\eta\) times an explicit \(\eta\)-independent constant. This is \(\sup_t\|\operatorname{error}_t\|_{L^2}\), not a pathwise supremum inside an expectation.



\texttt{mean\allowbreak{}Field\allowbreak{}Smoothing\allowbreak{}Constant} is a definition with defining expression \texttt{2 * (L\ensuremath{{}_{2}} : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}) * (K : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}) * gronwall\allowbreak{}Bound 0 (2 * ((L\ensuremath{{}_{1}} : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}) + (L\ensuremath{{}_{2}} : \ensuremath{\mathbb R}))) 1 T}. The right-hand side specifies the object used in the mathematical statement.



\texttt{exists\_\allowbreak{}mean\allowbreak{}Field\_\allowbreak{}smoothing\_\allowbreak{}comparison}: the intermediate facts include \texttt{h\allowbreak{}U}, \texttt{h\allowbreak{}V}, \texttt{U}, \texttt{heq}, \texttt{hpt}, in source order; the source steps apply or rewrite \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}add\_\allowbreak{}of\_\allowbreak{}differentiable}, \texttt{gradient\_\allowbreak{}moreau\_\allowbreak{}defect\_\allowbreak{}le}, \texttt{norm\_\allowbreak{}zero}, \texttt{hpt.\allowbreak{}trans}, \texttt{htime.\allowbreak{}trans\_\allowbreak{}eq}. The remaining scalar rearrangements are checked by \texttt{ring}, \texttt{nlinarith}.
\end{leanbox}
